# Thin wall
Will's first impression of the neighbor across the hall was that ze was unusually helpful and unusually hard to talk to, and that neither fact seemed to trouble zir in the least.

It was moving day. Will had a third-floor walk-up, more boxes than sense, and a back that had already filed a complaint. He was halfway through the books when a tall figure crossed the lot, lifted a box out of his arms without a word, and carried it up the stairs. Then came back for the next one. Ze was big — taller than Will, broad enough that the boxes rode like nothing in zir grip — with one brown eye and one blue, and ze met every opening Will offered with the smallest possible amount of speech.

A name, on the third trip: Kyu. A nod. The last box set down inside Will's door with a neatness that didn't square with how little ze seemed to care about the rest of it.

"Thank you. Seriously," Will said. "At least let me buy you—"

"It's fine."

And ze was already gone, back across the hall, the door shut, before Will found the line that usually worked. He wasn't worried. People were the thing he was good at; it was most of his job. Cold neighbor, helpful hands, no small talk — he'd have zir warmed up by the weekend.

It was close to midnight when the noise started.

Will was shelving plates when he heard it: once, muffled, through the wall behind the cupboards. He stopped. Told himself it was the pipes. New building, strange sounds, nothing more. Then it came again, lower, and gained a second voice, and then a rhythm, steady against what could only be the wall his kitchen shared with the apartment opposite. He stood there with a plate in each hand and arrived, slowly, at two conclusions: the walls here were closer to paper than plaster, and his quiet, helpful neighbor was having a considerably better night than he was.

He finished the kitchen. He went to bed. He did not sleep especially well.

The next morning the building put them at the mailboxes together, because the building had no mercy. Kyu looked unfairly well-rested. Will looked like a man running on six hours of pretending not to hear things.

He meant to leave it alone. He got his box open, got his mail out, and then heard himself say, with the delicacy of someone defusing a device, "So. Last night. These walls are — thinner than you'd expect."

Kyu looked over. No embarrassment, not a trace. Closer to the face of someone who'd just remembered a small errand left undone.

"Right. Meant to mention that when you moved in." Ze closed the little brass door. "Floor already knows. You'll get used to it." A beat, held a fraction too long. Then the smile came up, slow and easy and far too satisfied with itself. "Or you won't. Either's fine."

Ze left. Will stayed where he was, holding his mail, working through several responses, each of which arrived too late and then left again. By the time anything usable surfaced, the lobby was empty and Kyu was somewhere up the stairs, and Will understood that this was his life now: across the hall from the most shameless person he'd ever met — one who would broadcast zir evenings through the wall without a flicker of apology, and who would also, in total silence, carry his boxes and shut his door gently on the way out.

---
# The strange neighbor
The thing that broke Will's brain first wasn't the noise. It was that nobody else seemed to mind it.

He'd assumed, naturally, that he was living next to a problem the whole building tolerated through gritted teeth. So he went fishing. Casual, neighborly. He mentioned to the woman in 3B — seventy if she was a day, watering plants in the hall — that the walls were, ha, a little thin, weren't they.

"Oh, Kyu?" Her whole face softened like he'd brought up a grandchild. "Such a lovely one. So helpful. And so" — here she paused, selecting the word with the care of a sommelier — "full of youthful energy."

She winked. Will would think about that wink for days.

It was the same everywhere. The man in 2A called zir a gem. The couple downstairs said the building had gotten safer since ze moved in. Nobody — not one person — had ever complained, which meant Will was either the only one with ears or the only one without a personality flaw he hadn't yet identified. He began to suspect a cult. He began to suspect he was the only sane man in a building that had collectively agreed to find the floor-three sound situation charming.

And then, slowly, against his will, he started to understand them. Because Kyu was kind. Aggressively, infuriatingly kind. Kind in a way that left Will perpetually in debt and never once given a chance to settle up.

His sink backed up; he came home to find it draining, no note, the wrench gone from wherever ze'd produced it. He locked himself out; Kyu was somehow already in the stairwell with the spare the super hadn't told Will existed. Every minor catastrophe of his life resolved itself in a single tidy beat the moment ze materialized, fixed it, and left before Will could assemble the word _thank_ into a usable shape. He started keeping a mental tally of favors owed. He stopped when it crossed double digits and threatened his self-image.

The pasta was the turning point.

It was a simple dish. Will had watched a video. He had bought the correct ingredients with his own two hands. He had also, somewhere between the boiling and the everything-else, produced a smell that could only be described as _industrial,_ and a pot whose contents had bonded chemically to the steel — and then his smoke alarm, a coward, began to scream.

The knock came before the screaming stopped. Of course it did.

Kyu took one look past him at the kitchen, said nothing, walked in, killed the heat, opened the window, fanned the alarm into silence with a dish towel, and surveyed the wreckage with the grim patience of a paramedic at a scene that did not, technically, require this much equipment.

"You burned water," ze said.

"There was pasta in the water."

"There was." Ze was already running the pot under the tap, where it hissed in protest. "Allegedly."

Twenty minutes later there was edible food on Will's table, made from his own ingredients by his own neighbor, and Will — standing there useless in his own kitchen — opened his mouth to deliver, at last, a _proper_ thank-you. A real one. Heartfelt. Composed.

"It's fine," Kyu said, and was gone.

That should have been the end of it. It was, in fact, the beginning of something worse, because the building had a nervous system and the building had now decided that Will Could Not Cook. He never learned the route the information took. He only knew that within the week, 3B was leaving a covered dish at his door "in case, dear," and 2A pressed a Tupperware of stew into his hands in the elevator with the gentle pity reserved for the recently widowed, and the downstairs couple started saying things like "we made too much again" with the worst poker faces he had ever seen.

He was being fed. By an entire apartment complex. Like a stray. Because his neighbor had rescued one pot of pasta and apparently filed a report with the whole floor.

Will gave up around the second month. There was no winning this. There was only living here — across the hall from someone the entire building adored, who would not let him fail at a single thing and would not let him say thank you for any of it, and who, most nights, somewhere past eleven, reminded the third floor at length and at volume exactly how _full of youthful energy_ ze really was.

He got used to the noise, in the end. That was the part that worried him.


---

# The toilet paper incident
It was 11:40 on a Tuesday when Will ran out of toilet paper, and there was nothing open, and there was exactly one door on the floor he could knock on.

It was also, by his now-finely-tuned sense of the building's schedule, right about the hour that door got busy. He stood in his own bathroom debating it longer than the situation deserved, lost the debate to biology, and crossed the hall, and knocked fast before he could think better of it.

The faint sounds beyond the door stopped. A pause. Then it opened, and Kyu stood there in a shirt pulled on a few seconds ago and not fully buttoned, hair wrecked, flushed, looking at him with the patience of someone whose evening has been mildly interrupted and who feels nothing about it whatsoever.

"I'm so sorry," Will said. "I ran out of toilet paper. I will never ask you for anything again."

Ze disappeared, came back, put a full roll in his hands. And then — because of course — ze stepped past him into his apartment and glanced toward the bathroom, confirming the emergency was what he'd said it was, before ze'd consider the matter closed.

That was when Will got the liquor off zir, warm and close. And glancing past zir shoulder, he saw the apartment opposite sitting open and empty. No one there. No second pair of shoes.

"Is your, uh—" He aimed for casual and missed by a continent. "Did your — company leave already?"

"No company." Kyu said it without a flicker, the way ze said everything. "Just me. Drink makes it easier."

Will's face did something complicated and very warm. "Sorry — that's none of my business, I shouldn't have—"

"It's a little your business." Ze tipped zir head, and the smile came up, slow and far too pleased. "You seem awfully interested in me, neighbor. Want to do something about it, or you gonna stand there holding my toilet paper all night?"

Something in Will's head, after two months, just went.

He kissed zir. Roll still in one hand, the other already in that wrecked hair, not careful about any of it — and Kyu made a low, satisfied sound and kissed him back just as hard, fisting the front of his shirt and pulling him in.

The door got kicked shut. The toilet paper hit the floor and rolled off somewhere to die. They went backward through the apartment in a mess of mouths and hands, into the counter, off the wall, clothes leaving in stages, until they made it to the bedroom and Will stopped tracking the route entirely.

It was, the third floor would later concede, a memorable night. Notably louder than the usual broadcast — impressive, given the standard — except this time the noise had no wall to travel through, because for once it was coming from both apartments at once.

3B slept right through it. The building had called this weeks ago.

---
# The morning after
Will woke up somewhere he had to reconstruct from context clues.

Wrong ceiling. Wrong light, coming from the wrong side. A weight across his back that turned out, on inspection, to be an arm — heavy, warm, and very much not his own. The events of the previous night reassembled themselves in his head one piece at a time, each piece worse and better than the last, and Will lay there and accepted the situation with the calm of a man who has decided that panicking can wait until after coffee.

Kyu was already awake. Of course ze was. Propped on one elbow, looking down at him with the lazy, unhurried interest of someone watching a kettle they know is about to boil.

"Morning," ze said.

"Morning," Will managed, into the pillow.

"You drool."

"I do not."

"You do. It's all over my pillow. Honestly kind of a lot." Ze did not move zir arm. "Don't worry about it. Adds character."

Will rolled over, which put them face to face, which he immediately understood to be a tactical error, because Kyu's hair was a catastrophe and the brown eye and the blue one were both fixed on him at close range and ze looked obscenely good for someone who'd been doing the things ze'd been doing six hours ago. Will had imagined, in the abstract, that the morning after would involve some negotiation of awkwardness. Kyu appeared to be experiencing no awkwardness at all and had, characteristically, left the entire allotment for him.

"So," Will said.

"So."

"That happened."

"It did." Ze stretched, unbothered, taking up an unreasonable amount of the bed. "Loudly, even. Whole floor's very proud of you. First time you've contributed."

"I'd like to die now, please."

"Too late. You're famous." Ze yawned and finally lifted the arm off him, which Will refused to admit he missed. "There's coffee if you want it. Don't touch the machine, you'll break it. Sit there and look pretty, I'll do it."

And ze got up and went, padding out toward the kitchen like this was an ordinary Wednesday, leaving Will alone in the rumpled wreck of the bed with the slow realization that the strange kind neighbor who fixed his sink and fed him and would not let him say thank you had now also extended that exact treatment — _I'll do it, sit there, don't worry about it_ — to the morning after sleeping with him.

He stared at the ceiling. The wrong ceiling.

From the kitchen, the sound of the coffee machine being operated by the only person permitted to operate it. And then, raised, mild, entirely without shame: "By the way, you left your shirt in the hallway. And one sock in the bathroom. Don't know how. Impressive, really."

Will pulled the wrong pillow over his face and stayed there a while.

It smelled like zir. He didn't move.

---
# The part where they don't talk about it
They went back to being neighbors, which Will found both reasonable and faintly insulting.

Nobody had said the word _relationship._ Nobody had said any words, really. By unspoken agreement it had been a one-time thing — a thin-walls thing, a late-night accident with very good follow-through — and Will told himself he was fine with that, because he was a grown man with a company to run and could not, in good conscience, be deciding whether he wanted to _date his neighbor_ over the question of whether the sex had been worth the social risk.

The sex had been worth the social risk. Will was clear on that point. He was clear on it more often than he'd have liked.

What he was not clear on was anything else, because Kyu, in daylight, gave him absolutely nothing to work with. Ze was exactly as ze'd always been. Not warmer. Not cooler. Still the strange neighbor who lifted his groceries without asking and called him an idiot for forgetting his umbrella and left too much food at his door with no note. The morning after had changed precisely nothing about how ze treated him, which Will found, on alternating days, either a relief or a small personal affront. He'd slept with the most unbothered person alive. Ze had not been bothered.

So Will did the only thing left to do, which was notice zir. More than before. Against his own advice.

It crept up on him. The way ze rolled zir sleeves to the elbow doing something one-handed that should've taken two. The dry little nothing-comments ze dropped in the elevator that took Will until the third floor to realize were jokes. The fact that ze was, frankly, getting _better-looking_ by the week, which made no sense, ze hadn't changed, and Will was forced to conclude the variable was him.

And the wall, of course. The wall kept reporting.

Except now, lying in the dark with the familiar muffled soundtrack coming through, Will did not put the pillow over his head. He'd lost the moral standing for that. Now he lay there and — to his ongoing horror — _pictured_ it. Pictured zir, specifically, with a clarity that the memory unfortunately supported in full. Pictured zir under him, over him, that infuriating smug mouth doing something other than teasing for once. He'd surface from it flushed and annoyed and, on the worst nights, discover his own body had cast a firm and humiliating vote on the matter while he wasn't looking.

He was a CEO. He was nearly thirty. He was lying in bed with a problem like a teenager because his neighbor was loud and he had a good memory.

It stopped being only the wall.

There were dinners now. He couldn't have said how they started — one of them had too much food, which was always zir, or his fridge was empty, which was always him — and they'd end up at one table or the other, eating, not talking much, Kyu stealing off his plate without asking and Will having stopped minding. Ordinary. Domestic, even. The kind of evening two normal neighbors might have.

And then, often enough that _often_ had quietly become the word for it, nobody would say anything, and somehow the dishes would still be sitting out the next morning, and Will would wake up on the wrong side of the hall with the wrong ceiling overhead and that arm across his back again, and zir voice telling him, mildly, that he'd left a sock somewhere improbable.

He kept meaning to decide what this was.

He kept, instead, ending up in that bedroom.

---
# The friends

Will's friends were there for the early days. They were, in fact, the primary audience for the saga of the strange neighbor — the man who carried record crates without strain and then turned out to be the loudest person on the third floor; the smug elevator encounter; the umbrella, the food, the door that stuck. For weeks Will brought them fresh dispatches. *You will not believe what ze did now.* They came to think of the neighbor as a recurring character in a show they didn't have to watch, only hear about.

And then the dispatches stopped.

It took them a while to notice the absence, the way you don't notice a clock until it stops ticking. But eventually someone — it was Lena, it was always Lena — squinted across the café table and said, "Hey, whatever happened with your neighbor? The war. How's the war going?"

And Will, who could talk his way through anything, who led with feeling, who had never in his life been at a loss for words — *hesitated.*

It was a fatal hesitation. It lasted maybe half a second. The whole table saw it.

"Oh my god," Lena said, delighted, leaning in. "Oh my *god.*"

"It's not—" Will started.

"The war's *over,* isn't it." Marco grinned now. "There was a *treaty.*"

"You guys—"

"A *peace accord.*"

"Can we not—"

"He went *quiet* about the neighbor," Lena announced to the table, like a prosecutor resting her case, "which means it's not annoyance anymore, which means—" she gasped theatrically, "—Will. *Will.* Are you—"

"I'm getting another coffee," Will said, standing, ears scarlet, and the entire table erupted, and his fate was sealed. Within ten minutes a plan formed. They were coming over. They were going to see, with their own eyes, the strange neighbor who rendered their most talkative friend *speechless.* Will tried every exit. There was no exit. This was what he got for two months of dispatches.

---

The visit, when it came, went about as well as Will feared, which was to say catastrophically and also somehow fine.

His friends arrived loud and nosy and barely seated before they angled, peering out his window toward the door across the hall, asking pointed questions Will deflected with diminishing success. And then — because the universe had a sense of timing — there was a knock, and Will's door (still unlocked, he'd given up) opened, and Kyu leaned in to return a dish, and the entire room went silent.

Because here was the thing the dispatches never adequately conveyed: the strange neighbor was *attractive.* Distractingly so. Will watched the realization land on all three of his friends' faces in real time — the wrecked black hair, the mismatched eyes, the loose unbothered way ze filled the doorway — and watched them understand, instantly, the entire situation, and his ears began to burn before anyone said a word.

"Hi," Kyu said, flat, to the room of strangers. Unbothered. Set the dish on the counter. "You're loud. Heard you from across the hall."

"That's rich," Will muttered, "coming from you," and immediately regretted it, because Lena's head swiveled like a hawk's.

"Coming from *you*?" she repeated. "Why's that, exactly?"

And Kyu — Kyu, who never hid a single thing in zir life, who broadcast zir private life to three floors without a flicker of shame — looked at Lena, perfectly calm, and opened zir mouth to answer the bait *completely honestly*, and Will felt his soul leave his body—

But ze caught it. Caught Will's face — the genuine mortification, not the playful kind — in that fast, total way ze read everything. And something shifted. The blunt answer that would've detonated the room got set down, gently, unused.

"Walls are thin," Kyu said instead, with a small shrug. A pause, just long enough. Then the corner of zir mouth tipped up, and ze added, dry, to the riveted table: "He snores. Terrible. You have my sympathies, living next door." A beat. "Kidding."

It was a mercy and a deflection in one, and it was the first time Will ever saw zir bother to be *tactful* — to clock his discomfort and choose, deliberately, to spare it. The friends laughed, the tension broke, Kyu made a graceful exit before they could regroup, and Will sat there with a strange warm thing happening in his chest that had nothing to do with how good the man looked in a doorway.

(The friends, for the record, waited approximately four seconds after the door closed before turning on him as one. *He's gorgeous. You absolute liar. "Strange neighbor." You've been holding out on us.* Will defended nothing. There was nothing to defend.)

---

Later, after they went — after the wine cleared and the apartment quieted — Will crossed the hall and knocked. Kyu opened, eyebrow raised.

"I wanted to say sorry," Will said. "For— back there. You could tell I was uncomfortable, and you covered for me, and I made you feel like you had to hide something you've never hidden once in your life. That's not— you didn't do anything wrong. I'm not ashamed of— of whatever this is. I just panicked. So. Sorry."

Kyu looked at him. The flat thing wasn't on zir face. Just the blue eye and the brown one, and something quieter behind them.

"You came over to apologize," ze said slowly, "for me being polite. Once. By accident."

"...When you put it that way it sounds stupid."

"It is stupid." But ze stepped back, holding the door, which was the closest ze came to the word *come in.* "Your friends think I'm hot."

"My friends are correct, and I hate them."

The corner of zir mouth. "Stay for dinner. Tell me which one's Lena. She seemed sharp."

And Will stepped inside, and the door closed, and the apology — like most things between them — quietly resolved into food and proximity and the things neither of them said straight.

---
# Late morning
Will woke up wrapped around Kyu, deeply comfortable, one leg thrown over zir like he owned the place, and for a few warm seconds everything was perfect.

Then he saw the clock.

What followed was not dignified. Will went from horizontal to vertical to halfway dressed in the time it takes most people to register an alarm, while Kyu, woken by the chaos, watched from the pillow with the serene amusement of a person who sets zir own hours and answers to no one.

"You could just be late," ze offered, helpful.

"I run the company."

"So be late impressively. They can't fire you."

"That's not—" Will hopped into one shoe. "Where's my— I had a—" He gave up locating anything, kissed zir once on autopilot, registered halfway out the door that he'd done that, chose not to examine it, and ran.

He made it. Barely, breathless, but he made it, sliding into the building with four minutes to spare and the satisfaction of a man who has defeated time itself.

The satisfaction lasted until his assistant looked at him.

It was a particular look. It started at his collar, traveled up, paused, and arrived somewhere between _alarmed_ and _thrilled._ Will excused himself to the nearest reflective surface and conducted an emergency review of the situation, and the situation, he discovered, was a three-part catastrophe.

One: there was a mark on his neck. High, dark, entirely unhideable, in a spot no collar would ever reach. He had run through a populated lobby wearing it like a press release.

Two — and this took him a moment, because it had felt right when he grabbed it — the shirt was not his. It was close. Same color family. But it was a size too big in the shoulders, it smelled distractingly of his neighbor, and the cut was unmistakably _not Will's,_ which several people had apparently already clocked and were too professional to mention and too human not to discuss later.

Three, and worst, fatal: the folder. The one with the materials for the ten o'clock meeting. The meeting that was now ninety minutes out. The folder was sitting, he knew with total certainty, on Kyu's kitchen counter, exactly where he had not picked it up because he had been busy not being late.

There was precisely one solution, and it ended his life.

He called Kyu.

"Miss me already," ze answered.

"I need a favor. A big one. I left the Henderson folder on your counter, it's blue, it's by the fruit bowl, and I need it for a ten o'clock or I am genuinely finished. Can you — please—"

A pause that lasted a year. "You want me to bring it."

"To the office. Yes. I'm so sorry. I'll owe you forever."

"You already owe me forever. This is just interest." A rustle. The sound of zir locating it on the first try, because of course. "What's the address."

He gave it. He hung up. He spent the next forty minutes trying to project the calm authority of a man whose neck was not advertising his weekend, and he was just beginning to relax when reception called up to say that someone was here for him.

Will came down to find Kyu standing in the lobby of his very corporate, very glass company — tall, unhurried, dressed like ze'd rolled out of bed because ze had, holding a blue folder, drawing the eye of every single person on the ground floor with the absolute indifference of someone who has never once cared who was looking. The receptionist had forgotten how phones worked. Two people by the elevator had stopped talking.

"Folder," Kyu said, handing it over. Then ze looked, pointedly, at Will's collar — at the mark ze had personally put there — and then at the shirt, zir own shirt, hanging off Will's frame in front of the assembled staff, and the slow smug thing climbed onto zir face in full view of everyone Will employed.

"Nice shirt," ze said.

"Thank you for the folder," Will said, through his teeth, dying.

"Suits you. Bit big." Ze patted him once on the shoulder, turned, and strolled back out through the doors, leaving Will standing in his own lobby holding the folder in the borrowed shirt with the mark on his neck, in front of a workforce that had just received, free of charge and in high definition, the full and unambiguous announcement that the boss had someone new.

By lunch the whole building knew. By Monday, so did the satellite office.

Will did not bother to deny any of it. There was, at that point, no surviving evidence to suppress.

---
# The pasta

It was a perfectly ordinary trip to the supermarket right up until Will rounded the end of an aisle and nearly rammed his cart into his neighbor's.

Kyu was there with a basket — the careless, efficient shopping of someone who'd done this ten thousand times — and ze looked at Will, and then, with the unhurried inevitability of a man who had nothing better to do, looked *into Will's cart.* The eyes did a slow, assessing pass over the contents. Pasta. Tomatoes. Garlic. Cream. The damning evidence of ambition.

"You're going to burn the pasta," Kyu said. Flat. Certain. A weather report.

"I am *not.*"

"You are. For sure." Ze nodded at the cart like it had already happened. "I've heard it through the wall. The smoke alarm has a particular sound."

The thing was, Will *had* burned pasta. More than once. The smoke alarm incident that started this whole chapter of his life was, in fact, pasta-adjacent. The evidence against him was substantial and a reasonable man would have conceded the point. Will was not, in that moment, a reasonable man.

"This time is different," he announced, with the doomed confidence of a man about to lose. "Watch. I've been practicing. You just wait."

The corner of Kyu's mouth tipped up — slow, smug, *delighted* in that flat economical way — and ze said, "Okay." Just that. "I'll wait." And there was something in how ze said it that Will was too prideful to examine.

He examined it twenty minutes later, when they'd walked home together and he was unlocking his door and Kyu — without invitation, without comment, without the slightest flicker of hesitation — strolled past him into his apartment, settled onto his couch, stretched out, and *waited.* Literally. Sat in Will's living room with the patient, comfortable air of a man who had been promised dinner and intended to collect.

"...What are you doing," Will said.

"Waiting." Kyu got comfortable. "You said watch. I'm watching." A beat, and the smug thing deepened. "Cook, neighbor. I'm hungry."

And Will — trapped now, fully hoisted by his own petard, with the most attractive and insufferable man on the third floor reclining on his couch as a live audience — had no choice but to cook the pasta.

It went exactly how you'd think.

It started fine. It always started fine. That was the cruelty of pasta — it lulled you. Will was doing *great,* the water was boiling, the garlic was in, he could feel Kyu watching him from the couch and he was *showing off,* narrating his own competence, "see, this is— you just have to watch the heat, it's not hard, I don't know why you—"

The garlic turned. Not gold. Past gold. The fast brown of garlic that had decided to leave this earth, and the smell hit, and Will made a noise and grabbed for the pan and the pasta water chose that exact moment to boil over with a violent hiss, and suddenly there was too much happening at once, two crises, three, the sauce he hadn't started, the alarm beginning its familiar ascent toward—

"Stop," Kyu said.

ze was already up. Already crossing the kitchen with that unhurried precision, the same way ze had opened the window the first night, the same way ze did everything — like the emergency was interesting but not urgent, like ze had never once been rushed by anything in zir life. Ze took the wooden spoon out of Will's hand. Nudged him aside with a hip. Killed the heat under the garlic, rescued what could be rescued, dropped the temperature on the pasta with a practiced flick, and within thirty seconds the kitchen had gone from disaster to calm under zir hands.

"Watch," Kyu said — gently, this time, turning Will's own word back on him without any cruelty in it at all. "Heat's too high the whole way. You panic, you crank it. Low and slow." Ze tipped the garlic, showed him the colour. "There. That's where you stop. Before you think you should."

And Will stood at his neighbor's shoulder in his own kitchen and watched Kyu cook — competent, easy, sleeves shoved up, fixing what Will had broken without a single *I told you so,* which was somehow worse than if ze'd gloated, because the gloating he could have fought and *this* he could not — and felt the warm dangerous thing in his chest get a little harder to ignore.

"You let me start it," Will accused quietly, "knowing I'd fail."

"Yeah." Not even a little sorry. Ze plated the pasta — two plates, Will noticed; ze made enough for both without being asked, the silent provision that had been happening since the very first container at his door. "Faster than arguing. You had to see it." A glance, sidelong, the smug softening into something realer. "Now you'll remember. Low and slow."

Will poured two glasses and sat down and let himself be cooked for in his own kitchen, defeated, fed, and — he was beginning to suspect this was the actual point of the entire arrangement, had been for a while now — completely, hopelessly comfortable about it.

---
# You deserve better

Lately Will had started thinking about it seriously, which was itself the most alarming development of all.

Because it had started as an accident. A wall, a roll of toilet paper, a bad decision with excellent results. He'd spent weeks telling himself it was casual, telling himself he hadn't decided anything — and somewhere in there the telling had stopped working. He liked this. He liked the dinners and the rescues and the borrowed shirts. He liked, God help him, the smug bastard confidence of zir, the way Kyu could make him want to dig a hole and climb into it and still leave him wanting to come back the next night. He'd stopped trying to talk himself out of it. He could do this forever, was the thing he kept arriving at. He wouldn't mind doing this forever.

He was turning that over, comfortable with it for once, when he came home one evening to the sound of voices and laughter and clinking glasses coming through Kyu's door.

Friends. Kyu had friends over. _Kyu had friends_ was a sentence Will needed a second with.

He let himself be waved in — the door was open, the welcome automatic, the group loose and easy and several drinks deep. And Kyu was right in the middle of it, exactly as ze always was, neither warmer nor cooler, trading dry shots with people who clearly went back years. Will stood at the edge of it with a glass someone pressed into his hand and felt, without warning, something small and ugly turn over in his chest. He didn't have a name for it that he was willing to use. The ease of them. The closeness. The history he wasn't in.

It got worse fast. Because these people knew. All of them. They knew Kyu was — there was no polite phrasing — extremely relaxed about sex, a fact Will had somehow filed as _private,_ as _his,_ conveniently forgetting that the entire thing between them had begun with how loud ze was through a shared wall. And they knew about Will, too. Knew he was one of the people on that particular roster.

"You're the neighbor," one of them said, grinning, sizing him up. "The CEO. Oh, this one's gone, look at his face." And then, jovial, to Will directly, lifting a glass: "Word of advice, man — zir great, zir a catch, but don't actually _fall_ for zir. Not worth it. Take it from people who—"

The table laughed. Kyu kicked the speaker's chair, unbothered, grinning. "I'm sitting right here, you know."

Will did not laugh.

"You shouldn't talk about your friend like that," he said, and the room cooled a degree, surprised. Then he turned to Kyu, and heard himself, in a voice he didn't fully recognize, say: "You're drunk. Come on. Come with me."

And he took zir hand and pulled zir up and walked zir out — past the stalled conversation, past the raised eyebrows, out the open door and across the hall into his own apartment, leaving every person in that room blinking after him. Including, for once, Kyu.

He sat zir down on his couch. Went to the kitchen. Made a tall glass of lemon water, the kind you drink so the morning hurts less, and put it in zir hands.

Kyu looked at the glass, then at him. "Why'd you do that."

"Because they shouldn't talk about you that way."

"They're not wrong, though." Said flatly, no self-pity in it, just fact. "Plenty of evidence."

"I don't care about the evidence." Will sat down across from zir. "You deserve better than that. Than people who'd say it to your face and laugh."

Kyu was quiet. Quiet in a way ze rarely went — not the easy silence ze used as a weapon, something stiller than that. Ze turned the glass once in zir hands.

"Better," ze repeated. "Like what."

"Like someone who'd take you home and make sure you drank water."

A longer silence. Then, not looking at him, careful: "Like you?"

"Yeah." Will didn't hesitate. "Like me."

And Kyu let out a short breath, half a laugh, and finally looked up — and the smug thing was there, but it was thinner than usual, something else showing under it that ze didn't bother hiding for once.

"Huh," ze said. "Here I thought you'd never say it. Took you long enough." The mouth curved. "_Arrogant_ of you, honestly. Making me wait around like—"

Will leaned across and kissed zir, which shut zir up neatly, the lemon water saved at the last second by zir free hand. Ze made a low sound against his mouth that might have been a laugh and might have been _finally,_ and didn't pull away, and for once had nothing smug left to say.

---
# The morning after the war ended
Will woke up first, which never happened.

That was the first sign the world had tilted. Every other morning he'd surfaced to find Kyu already up, already smug, already three sips into Will's coffee with an opinion about his sleeping habits. This morning the apartment was silent and the body next to him was still out cold, face down, dead to everything, one arm flung across Will's chest like it had been dropped there from a height.

Right. The wine. The whole bottle, plus whatever ze'd had before Will dragged zir across the hall.

Will lay there a while, looking at the ceiling — his own ceiling, for once, his own bed — and let last night reassemble itself. The party next door. The friend telling him not to fall for zir. His own voice, strange and certain, _come with me._ The lemon water. _You deserve better._ _Like you?_ _Like me._ He'd said it. Out loud, on purpose, sober. And ze'd said it back, more or less, in that sideways way ze had where the realest thing got smuggled out under a joke.

So that was done now. That was a thing that had happened and could not be un-happened.

He felt great about it. He felt like a man who'd won something. He was still feeling great about it when Kyu made a low, wounded noise into the pillow and came back to life one degree at a time.

"You're awake," Will said, pleasantly.

"Don't." The voice was wrecked. "Whatever it is. Don't."

"I didn't say anything."

"You're _thinking_ loud. You've got a — face on. I can feel the face." Kyu pried one eye open, the brown one, and squinted at the window like it had personally betrayed zir. "Why is it bright. Make it stop being bright."

And here was the thing Will understood, with slow and rising delight, as he watched the most unbothered person on the third floor lose a fistfight with a sunbeam: that for the first time since he'd moved in, _he_ was the one in working order. Ze was hungover. Ze was miserable. Ze was, at this moment, entirely at his mercy.

He got up. He was generous about it. He went to the kitchen and came back with water and two painkillers and set them down on the nightstand with a care he'd learned, frankly, from zir.

"Drink that."

Kyu eyed the glass like it might be a trick. "What is this."

"Water. The thing you put in me last night, after the lemon, when you were busy being _kind._" Will sat on the edge of the bed, insufferably calm. "Turnabout. Drink it."

A long pause. Then Kyu hauled zirself up onto one elbow with the dignity of a felled tree, took the glass, drank the water, took the pills, and lay back down — and there was a beat, a real one, where ze didn't say anything smug at all, just looked at Will with the brown eye and the blue one from very close range, both of them apparently registering that the grammar had reversed and neither quite knowing what to do about it.

"You took care of me," Kyu said. It came out almost suspicious.

"I did."

"I don't — that's not how this works." Genuinely thrown, for once, off the script ze'd run on Will for months. "_I_ do that. That's my—"

"Yeah, well." Will brushed a wrecked strand of black hair off zir forehead, didn't make a thing of it, found the gray-white ones threaded in and didn't make a thing of that either. "You said it back last night. _Like me._ Comes with conditions. One of them is you have to let me get you water when you've poisoned yourself with an entire vineyard."

Kyu was quiet. Will watched zir decide how to handle being cared for, watched the discomfort of it move across a face that handled almost nothing as discomfort — and then watched zir do the only thing ze knew how to do with a feeling that big, which was bury it under the nearest available smirk.

"So we're together now," Kyu said, hoarse, recovering, the corner of the mouth coming up by degrees. "And the first thing you do with it is boss me around in my hangover."

"In _my_ apartment. You're in my bed. I bossed you here last night, you came real quiet." Will was enjoying this more than was strictly fair. "You're easy when you're drunk, you know that? Pulled you off your own couch by the wrist and you just _went._"

"I was being polite." Ze pulled the blanket over zir face. From under it, muffled, hungover, and entirely without shame: "Don't get used to it. I'm taking the wrist thing back the second the room stops moving."

"Sure you are." Will stayed where he was, hand resting in zir hair, the morning quiet around them in a new way — no wall to perform for, nothing to deny anymore, just the two of them and a glass of water and the fact, now spoken and standing in the room, that this was a thing they were doing on purpose. "Go back to sleep. I'll make coffee."

A pause from under the blanket. Then, grudging, small, the realest thing ze'd say all morning:

"...You don't know how to use the machine."

"Then get up and supervise."

And ze did, eventually — wincing, swearing, wrapped in Will's blanket, standing over Will's shoulder in Will's kitchen to make sure he didn't ruin the one thing ze trusted no one else to do right. Which was, Will was learning, simply how this person said the soft thing out loud.

---
# The announcement
The announcement happened because Will decided to host, which his friends would later identify as the moment he lost control of his own life.

The logic was sound on paper. Both their worlds knew, separately, that something was going on; nobody had ever put them in one room and said the word out loud. So Will would have everyone over, feed them, and make it official like an adult. His apartment was the smaller of the two, which was the first miscalculation. The second was assuming his friends and Kyu's friends would not immediately recognize each other as the same species.

They did. It took about a minute. Lena clocked the loudest of Kyu's friends across the room — the one who'd told Will not to fall for zir and then caught a kick to the shin for it — and the two of them locked eyes with the mutual recognition of predators at the same watering hole, and within ten minutes the two groups had fused into a single intelligence agency, pooling everything they had.

"Wait, wait — the _folder._ He came to the office? In your shirt?"

"Walked into reception. Asked for him by name. Then announced the hickey to the entire lobby—"

"WILL."

"I'm getting more chairs," Will said, to nobody, and went to get more chairs.

Kyu, for the record, was having the time of zir life. Ze sat at the middle of the table doing almost nothing, which was zir whole gift — letting the room build the case while ze supplied the single most damning detail at exactly the right moment, flat, unbothered, never raising zir voice over the noise because ze never had to.

"It wasn't a stove fire the _first_ time," ze offered, mild, into a lull. "First time it was a window. He didn't know how to open a window."

"It was _smoke,_" Will called from the kitchen. "There was an alarm. It was a real situation—"

"He stood there holding two oven mitts," Kyu told the table, "like they'd help."

The table lost it. Will gave up on the chairs.

And somewhere in the middle of it — wine going around, both groups now functionally one group, somebody three drinks deep relitigating the toilet paper — Will decided he wasn't going to wait for a clean moment, because there wasn't going to be one, and a clean moment was never how anything between them had gone anyway.

He tapped his glass. The table half-quieted, suspicious.

"Since you all clearly already know my entire life," he said, "I want to actually say it. On purpose. Out loud." He looked at Kyu, and he wasn't pink, wasn't hedging, steady as the night he'd hauled zir off a couch by the wrist. "Kyu and I are together. Properly. Not a wall thing, not whatever you've all been calling it. Together. That's the announcement. Thank you, you can go back to ruining me."

A beat of silence.

Then Lena burst into tears, Kyu's friends started _applauding_, the shinned one stood up and yelled "I CALLED IT, I called it the second ze defended your honor with a _kick_—" and the whole table came apart into noise and toasts and Lena demanding to know why nobody told her to wear better mascara to this.

And through all of it Will was watching Kyu, because Kyu hadn't said anything yet, and there was a thin old reflex of worry — too public, too many feelings in too small a room for the one person who couldn't say things—

But ze just stood. Let the noise come down on its own, which it did, because the whole table wanted to hear what ze'd say. Ze lifted zir glass. And said, dry, to the room:

"He left out the part where he kidnapped me. Off my own couch. In front of half of you." A pause, perfectly weighted, everyone leaning in. "But, yeah." The corner of the mouth. And then — plain, no fight in it, no scramble for the upper hand, just set down on the table where everyone could see it, which from zir cost more than a speech would from anyone else — "He's mine. I'm keeping him."

The room detonated.

And in the wreckage of it, while both their worlds hollered and toasted and Lena openly wept, Kyu leaned down close to Will's ear, under the noise, just for him: "That's how you make an announcement, neighbor. You went too long. I'd have lost the room."

"You did not do it better."

"I made your friend cry and I did it sitting down." Ze straightened up, serene, reaching past him to steal the wine. "Drink your drink. We're together now. Everyone heard. No takebacks."

Will drank his drink. There were, at that point, far too many witnesses for takebacks anyway — and across the hall, through the wall the whole saga had been conducted through, the third floor got to hear, for once, the sound of an entire room being happy about something, which made a nice change from the usual broadcast.

---
# A quiet night
The last of them left slowly, the way good parties end — in waves, with hugs in the doorway, Lena going last and stage-whispering _I'm so happy for you_ loud enough that Kyu heard it from the couch and looked unbearably pleased about it.

And then it was just the two of them and the battlefield: glasses everywhere, a table that had seated too many people, the particular wreckage of a room that had been very full and was now very quiet.

Will started collecting glasses, because Will was the kind of person who collected glasses. He got about four into it before a hand closed over his wrist and lifted the stack right back out of his grip.

"That can wait."

"It'll congeal," Will said. "Wine does that, it gets—"

"It'll congeal whether you do it now or not." Kyu set the glasses down with a deliberateness that made Will go still, because there was a different note in zir voice now, low and unhurried and pointed entirely at him. Ze stepped in, and Will found himself backed gently into the counter — _the_ counter, the burnt-pasta counter, the counter where roughly every disaster in his life had begun — and the smug had cooled into something warmer and a great deal more focused. "I just watched you stand up at your own table and tell two rooms full of people you're keeping me."

"You said you were keeping _me_—"

"Both true." Zir hand came up to the side of his neck, thumb finding the spot that had caused him so much professional embarrassment. Ze wasn't really arguing. "You were sure of yourself tonight. Out loud. In front of everyone." The voice dropped another degree. "I liked it."

Will didn't go pink — or he did, but it was the good kind now, the kind that had stopped being fear weeks ago. He held zir gaze instead of finding the floor. "You like when I'm sure of myself."

"I like when you're sure of _me,_" Kyu said, quiet, and that one slipped out from under the heat the way the real things always did with zir — never declared, always smuggled, gone before you could point at it.

So Will kissed zir. Not the frantic, stumbling version of the early nights, all teeth and disbelief — they were past that, past the wall, past pretending. Slow. Certain. He got a hand in the front of zir shirt and walked them backward off the counter, and for once Kyu _let_ zirself be led, going where he steered, which was its own quiet confession: the most unbothered person on the third floor handing over the wheel because ze trusted whose hands it was in.

They navigated toward the bedroom by feel and long practice. Kyu walked them into the doorframe — zir fault, again, a fixed law of their physics — and didn't apologize, again, and Will laughed into zir mouth and felt zir grin against his.

"For the record," he managed, somewhere in the dark, clothes already losing the argument, "the walls in this place are still way too thin."

"Good." Said against his skin, without a shred of shame, pulling him down with zir. "Whole floor signed the treaty. Let them hear it's official."

And the rest of it the wall took care of announcing.

It was, by any honest accounting, the loudest the third floor had carried in a long time — both of them, no pretense left to keep quiet for, a _quiet night_ in name only — and not one door on the hall so much as creaked in complaint. The saga was common knowledge by now. Everyone had watched it coming through the drywall for months, from the first mortified elevator ride to this. If the price of two people finally stopping the act was one more loud night, the building had long since decided, without ever holding a vote, that it was more than fair.

In the morning there'd be coffee, made in Will's kitchen by the one person allowed to touch the machine, who increasingly lived there in everything but lease. The glasses would have congealed exactly as advertised. Neither of them would care even slightly.

But that was the morning. Tonight there was just the two of them, and a wall too thin to hide a single thing, and — at last — no reason left in the world to be quiet about being happy.

---
# The clothing problem
Moving in together came with a learning curve, and the steepest part of it caught Will completely off guard:

Kyu's shamelessness wasn't just how ze talked. It was how ze lived. And it turned out that at home, with the doors locked and the heat on, Kyu saw no compelling argument for clothes.

It wasn't a bit. That was the thing Will could never quite get a handle on — a bit he could have braced for. This was just sincerity. Home was home. Clothes were for elsewhere. So ze cooked and read and wandered the apartment in whatever ze happened to have on, which was frequently nothing, with the flat unconcern of a person who had genuinely never spent one second worrying about a body that gave them no reason to.

Will worked from home.

"I'm trying to do something meaningful here," he said, on a Tuesday, from his desk in the corner. Facing the wall. On purpose.

"So do it." Kyu didn't look up from zir book. Sprawled across the couch behind him.

"I can't. You're—" He gestured over his shoulder without turning, because he knew what turning cost. "Put something on. Please. One item."

"Why."

"You know why."

The book lowered. He heard it happen — the slow rustle, the smug arriving ahead of the words. "Say it," Kyu said. "You're good with words. Whole job's words. Describe the problem, neighbor."

"I hate you."

"You told two rooms you were keeping me." The mouth had gone up at the corner; he didn't have to look. "No dress code at home. You signed."

He lost every round after that. He tried the bedroom; ze wandered in for a charger and stayed. He tried working around zir three jobs, but zir schedule was weather, not clock, and ze came home from the gym especially uninterested in being clothed, and there went the afternoon.

"This is a hostile work environment," Will said.

"This is my apartment."

"It's _our_ apartment, and I have a deadline—"

"You have a deadline." Kyu unfolded off the couch and crossed the room and draped over the back of his chair, chin on his shoulder, killing the deadline by proximity. "You moved in with someone who looks like me. And you want to do a spreadsheet about it."

"Some of us have jobs—"

"I have three."

"—jobs we do with clothes on—"

Ze laughed against his ear, and the spreadsheet died, and Will pushed back from the desk and turned around at last, because there was no winning this and there never had been.

"Fine. You win. It can wait."

"It can always wait," Kyu said, already pulling him toward the couch, smug and warm and his — and Will went, and didn't say the rest of it out loud, because he didn't have to. Ze was barefoot and unbothered and entirely at home in a way Will had never once seen zir be anywhere else, and that said it fine on its own.

The deadline waited a long time.

---
# The tomato vendor
Will had gotten good. That was the dangerous part.

Months in, he knew zir tells — knew the half-second before a tease landed, knew which silences were loaded, knew the exact tilt of the head that meant a knockout was coming. He could see them now. And seeing them, he'd made the rookie mistake of assuming he could block them.

He could not. He could only see the truck before it hit him.

Saturday, the farmers' market, Will feeling untouchable. He'd slept well. He looked good. Kyu was beside him being effortlessly the most looked-at person in a three-stall radius, and Will, riding the confidence ze claimed to like so much, decided today was the day he finally won one in public. He slid an arm around zir waist, leaned in, pitched it low and smooth, a line he'd been saving.

"You know everyone here's staring at you."

"I know." Kyu didn't even slow down, picking through tomatoes. "They've been staring since I walked in. You're a little behind."

"I meant—" Will recalibrated. "I meant they're all wondering who's lucky enough to take you home."

That got zir to look up. A flicker of something. Will felt the brief, glorious lift of a man who has finally, _finally_ landed one — and then Kyu set the tomatoes down, turned, looked at him with the brown eye and the blue one, and said, perfectly level, loud enough for the tomato man and the people behind him:

"You are. You did. Twice last night. You couldn't walk straight this morning."

The lift died. Will's whole face went up like a struck match. Somewhere to his left, a stranger choked on a sample.

"_Kyu—_"

"You started it." Ze went back to the tomatoes, serene, victorious, not a hair out of place. "You wanted them wondering. Now they're not wondering. That's better, right? Closure."

Will stood in the middle of the market, scarlet, holding a paper bag of nothing, having walked directly and confidently into it the way he did every single time. He'd seen it coming. He'd _watched_ it come. He had a full second of warning and zero ability to do anything with it, because that was the thing nobody warned you about loving the smuggest person alive: knowing all zir moves didn't make them land softer. It just meant you got to appreciate the craftsmanship on the way down.

"I hate you," he said, into the bag.

"You brought me to the market and told strangers you were lucky." Kyu handed him the tomatoes to carry, already moving to the next stall. "You really don't."

---
# Will's first win
Here was the thing about being shameless: it only worked as armor against embarrassment. And Will had figured out, somewhere in the first month, that embarrassment was never the move. You couldn't fluster someone who'd announce zir own sex life to a tomato vendor. Shame slid right off zir.

So he stopped trying to embarrass zir and started trying to _take zir apart,_ and that — that, ze had no defense for.

Because Will was good. Not loud-good, not show-off good. _Attentive_ good. The kind of good that came from actually paying attention — to breath, to where zir hands went tight, to the exact half-second a clever remark died in zir throat. Everywhere else in the world Kyu read Will faster than Will could think. In here Will turned it around, watched zir like ze was the puzzle for once, and used everything he saw.

It usually started with Kyu still winning. Smug, in control, narrating, because narrating was zir whole thing.

"You're so _careful,_" ze said one night, lazy, amused, propped up watching him like this was a performance staged for zir benefit. "Look at you. Very thorough. Did you take a—"

Will did something unhurried and precise, and the sentence stopped.

Just stopped. Mid-word. Kyu's breath caught on whatever the rest of it was going to be, and Will glanced up, all innocence, and said, "Sorry — did you take a _what?_"

"...Nothing."

"No, finish it. You were making a point." He did the thing again, slower, watching zir face, and watched the smug _fracture_ — watched the unbeatable, unbotherable, never-flustered menace of the third floor lose the thread entirely, head going back, a sound coming out of zir that had nothing performed in it at all. Not the broadcast version ze did for the floor. The real one. The one ze couldn't dress up.

That was the whole game, and Will had mastered it: patience. Kyu's entire arsenal was composure, the easy unbothered _nothing-touches-me,_ and Will had learned exactly how to dismantle it one deliberate piece at a time and refuse — _refuse_ — to be rushed.

"You could go faster," Kyu managed, which was as close to _please_ as ze would ever come.

"I could." He didn't.

"Will."

"You're usually so chatty." Even, unbothered, turning zir own register back on zir. "Where'd all the words go?"

And Kyu — who had a comeback for everything, who had never once in recorded history been left without the last word — could not assemble one. Tried. Got a syllable out, lost it. Fisted a hand in the sheets and gave up on dignity entirely, because there was no version of shameless that helped here, no smug that could survive being known this precisely and handled this well, and Will had found the one room in the world where ze didn't get to win.

He pushed zir right up to the edge of it and held zir there, attentive, merciless, _enjoying_ it — the closest he ever got to even — until ze finally broke and said his name like it cost zir something, no irony left in it, no upper hand, just Will's name and nothing else.

He let zir have it then. Generous in victory.

Afterward, Kyu lay there wrecked, staring at the ceiling, working to reassemble the composure ze wore like skin everywhere else. Will watched, smug for once himself, insufferably pleased.

"Don't," Kyu said, hoarse.

"I didn't say anything."

"You've got a _face_ on."

"You make a face on me," Will said serenely, "and then you tell strangers about me at the market. This is the one place I'm in charge. Let me have it."

A long silence. Kyu, still catching zir breath, conceded the only way ze knew how — which was not at all in words, just rolling over and pulling him back down, which was its own admission: that here, and pretty much only here, the neighbor had zir number, and ze'd stopped pretending otherwise.

"...Floor heard that one," ze muttered into his shoulder, the last flag planted on a lost field.

"Good," Will said, and meant it.

---
# The bar
Will's friends wanted to see the bar. This was inevitable. From the moment they'd learned the legendary neighbor _tended a bar_ three nights a week, a pilgrimage was only a question of scheduling.

So they went — Will, Lena, the whole loud pack, plus a couple of Kyu's friends who materialized because they always did. Will told himself he was relaxed about it. He was not relaxed about it. He'd never seen ze work.

Bar-Kyu, it turned out, was a different animal than home-Kyu. Same dryness, same unbothered ease, but switched _on_ — owning the space behind the counter, three drinks going at once, trading flat one-liners with regulars who clearly came as much for zir as for the liquor. Will took a stool at the end and got handed something amber and perfect he hadn't ordered, and spent a while just watching the man be good at one of the three things he was good at, and felt his chest do something embarrassing about it.

Lena caught him at it instantly.

"You've got the face."

"I don't have a face."

"You're watching him pour a beer like it's a sunset." She leaned past him and announced, to the assembled table, "Will's doing the _face,_" and Will would have defended himself except Kyu — not looking up, building a gin and something — said mildly, "He does it a lot. You get used to it," and the whole table howled, and Will lost that round before it started, the way he lost all the public ones.

It kept going like that. Every opening Will found, Kyu closed, flat and easy, never raising zir voice over the noise of the room because ze never had to. The friends fed zir material. Ze fielded all of it. Will watched his own evening turn, methodically, into a roast of Will, conducted by the person he lived with, in front of everyone, with the home advantage of an audience that adored zir.

But Will had a card now. That was the thing none of them knew. Will had spent weeks learning the one place ze couldn't win, and he was, for once, holding it.

He waited. Let zir get comfortable. Let the table get a few more drinks in. And when Kyu was mid-pour — actually working, hands busy, fully _on_ — Will set down his glass, leaned his forearms on the bar, caught zir eye across it, and said it quiet. Pitched under the noise, just for zir, pleasant as anything:

"Careful. You keep showing off out here, you know exactly how that goes for you when we get home."

Nothing in the words a stranger could've flagged. Everything in them aimed.

And Kyu — unshakeable, never-flustered, in-zir-own-house-behind-zir-own-bar Kyu — _missed the pour._

Barely. A splash over the rim of the glass, a half-second hitch in those competent hands, recovered almost before it happened. But Will saw it. Kyu knew Will saw it. And the look that came across the bar at him was pure, outraged disbelief — _not here. that's a home thing. you don't bring that here._

Will picked his drink back up and sipped it, serene.

"You spill something?" Lena asked, missing all of it.

"Slipped," Kyu said.

"Slipped," Will agreed, innocent as a choirboy.

Kyu wiped the bar down, recovered, went back to the regulars — but there was a new wariness in how ze looked at him after that, sidelong, recalculating, the look of someone who'd just watched their opponent walk a weapon out of the room it was supposed to stay in. The market, the office, the friends — those ze'd always have. But the one front Will held, the one ze'd quietly accepted was lost behind locked doors, had just proven it could _travel,_ and now no public table was entirely safe ground anymore.

It was the best night out they'd had. Both friend-groups shut the place down. Kyu comped half their drinks and pretended ze hadn't.

On the walk home, the pack scattering off into the dark, a little drunk and pleased with the world, Kyu bumped zir shoulder into Will's. Said it grudging, almost impressed:

"Dirty move. The bar thing."

"Learned from the best."

A pause. The corner of the mouth, but no fight in it. "Yeah. You did." And then, because ze could never let a flag stay planted: "Enjoy it. You've got, what, until we're through the door?"

"Worth it," Will said.

It was.

---
# The princess carry
The princess carry started, like most things between them, as Kyu winning an argument.

The first time was practical. Will rolled his ankle stepping wrong off a curb, and before he could mount any kind of dignified limp, Kyu simply bent and scooped him up — one arm under his knees, one behind his back — and kept walking. On a public street. In front of strangers and the entire third floor's worth of potential witnesses.

"Put me _down,_" Will hissed, going scarlet. "People are looking—"

"You can't walk."

"I can _limp_—"

"Slower," Kyu said, not breaking stride, "and you'll complain the whole way. This is faster." And ze carried him the three blocks home like he weighed nothing, while Will died approximately forty times and tried to hide his face in zir shoulder, which only made the whole thing look more like the cover of a paperback, which Lena would absolutely hear about and which Will would absolutely never live down.

That was the beginning. And for a while it ran exactly the way ze wanted it to, because Kyu — who'd discovered that hauling Will around bridal-style was both genuinely efficient and a bottomless source of his flustered mortification — did it constantly. Stairs. Puddles. Across the apartment for no reason at all. "You looked like you were about to walk somewhere," ze'd explain, holding him a foot off the floor. "Couldn't allow it." And Will would squawk and flush and protest, every time, which was the whole point. The carry was a delivery system. The payload was Will's embarrassment.

Except embarrassment was the one thing Kyu had taught him, personally and at length, never to bother with.

It happened gradually. Will didn't clock it changing. But there came an ordinary afternoon when Kyu swept him up off the couch to relocate him to the kitchen — the hundredth time, the move so routine ze was already braced for the usual flailing — and Will just... looped his arms around zir neck. Settled in. Got _comfortable._ And said, serene:

"Yeah, no, I'm into this. Carry on."

Kyu nearly dropped him.

Because Will not protesting wasn't in the script. The carry worked because Will squirmed. And here he was draped in zir arms like a cat finding a sunbeam, fully at ease, openly _enjoying it,_ and the entire mechanism short-circuited on the spot.

"You're supposed to complain," Kyu said, suspicious.

"I've decided I like it." Will laced his hands behind zir neck. "You're strong, I'm comfortable, the view's good from up here. Genuinely, why was I ever embarrassed? This is the best seat in the house. Do it more."

"That's not—" and Kyu, for once, was the one off-balance, "—that defeats the—"

"The bit?" Will smiled up at zir, sweet and merciless, deploying the exact weapon ze'd run on him a hundred times — refusing to fluster, taking the shameless option, out-confidencing the master at zir own craft. "Turns out the trick was just to enjoy it as much as you do. Funny how that works."

And Kyu stood there in the middle of the living room holding a deeply, infuriatingly content boyfriend, looking down at him with the slow betrayed realization that the lesson had taken too well — that Will had been studying zir shamelessness this whole time and had started turning it back, and that there was now no version of carrying him that embarrassed him anymore, because he'd simply decided not to be embarrassed, which was the single most Kyu thing a person could do, and ze'd taught it to him with zir own two hands.


"I've created a monster," Kyu said.

"You've created a *passenger,*" Will corrected, getting comfortable. "Kitchen, please. I'm hungry. Chop chop."

And Kyu — defeated, charmed, holding the one person who's ever beaten zir at zir own game — sighs the sigh of someone who got exactly what they deserved, and carries him to the kitchen anyway.

---
# The surname

Will learned zir surname the way you learn most small true things about a person — by accident, early, off a piece of mail.

It was still the strange first stretch, the loud-neighbor-becoming-something-else phase, and ze had left a stack of zir mail on Will's counter after a dinner that had, as they did by then, drifted into the bedroom. Will was clearing up the next morning, ze already gone to a shift, and he picked up the top envelope to move it and stopped.

_Kyu Valentine._

He read it twice. He turned it over as if the back might explain. It did not.

Because it was the least likely surname imaginable to be attached to that specific person. He'd spent weeks now learning ze — the flat delivery, the deflection, the way ze couldn't take a compliment, the way the softest thing ze'd ever said was "had extra" about food ze'd clearly made on purpose. The single least romantic-_declaring_ human he'd ever met. And ze was, legally, on paper, in official ink, a _Valentine._

He was still holding the envelope, grinning, when ze got back from the shift.

"You're going through my mail."

"You're a _Valentine._"

Ze didn't even pause taking off zir jacket. "That's my name. Names are just names."

"Your _surname_ is Valentine. You." He held the envelope up like evidence. "The person who calls flowers unnecessary. The person who told me dinner was 'fine.' You're named after the most romantic day of the year, and you're — you're the _opposite_ of it. That's the funniest thing I've ever learned about anyone."

"It's a coincidence of paperwork," ze said, flat, entirely unbothered, taking the envelope out of his hand. "It doesn't mean anything. My name being Valentine is exactly as meaningful as your name being — whatever forgettable thing your name is."

"Reeves."

"Reeves. See. Boring. No one makes a face about _Reeves._" Ze set the mail down. "You're going to be weird about this. I can see it. You've got the face. You're filing it away to be insufferable about later."

"I'm _absolutely_ filing it away." He was delighted, and made no attempt to hide it. "I'm going to be insufferable about this for the rest of my life. You know that, right? You've handed me the single best piece of ammunition a person could have. A _Valentine_ who can't say 'I love you.'"

"I can say plenty of things—"

"You said 'had extra' about a meal you cooked from scratch specifically for me—"

"_That's not the same as_—" Ze stopped, because it was, a little, and ze wasn't going to lose this by continuing. "You're going to make the joke every February, aren't you."

"Every February. Probably other months too. I'll find excuses." He was radiant. "'As a Valentine, you should really be more—' I have _years_ of this planned already. You've doomed yourself. This is a life sentence."

And ze — surnamed, of all the possible names, _Valentine,_ saddled with it long before either of them had any idea where the two of them were going — looked at the delighted man across the counter and understood, correctly, that ze had just handed a naturally romantic person the perfect lever, and that he would pull it forever.

"...I hate that you know now," ze said, flatly.

"Too late." He kissed the top of zir head on the way past. "It's in the file. Been in the file since the envelope. _Valentine._"

"It's a curse."

"It's a _little_ bit a curse," Will agreed, already gone, already insufferable, "and I'm keeping it."

---
# The jealous
Will had watched zir get looked at plenty. He had never sat through a whole shift of zir getting _worked._

The guy slid in around ten — good-looking, confident in the pre-installed way, the kind who'd clearly never had a night not go his direction. A drink. A comment. A laugh held a beat too long. Then the lean, and the voice dropping into that register, and the line about the eyes, the two colors, _I could look at those all night._

Kyu clocked it on the first sentence. Of course ze did; ze clocked everything. And ze didn't lose half a step. Ze'd fielded this a thousand times across a thousand shifts and the deflection ran on rails — warm enough to keep the tip, flat enough to give nothing, hands never stopping, every line answered with one that sounded like an answer and wasn't. _Genetic thing._ Lime into the well. _Long shift, yeah._ Glass to the rack. The guy thought he was getting somewhere. He was buying drinks at a wall and being walked, gently, nowhere. It was its own kind of craft, the bar equivalent of a bouncer who never has to raise zir voice.

The guy didn't notice he was being handled. They never did.

Will noticed. Will, end of the bar, crossword abandoned, watched the whole smooth machine of it run — and felt something hot and ugly start uncoiling in his chest that he had absolutely not signed up for. He knew that smug. He got a version of it aimed at him most days. Watching it get pointed, flat and professional and _empty,_ at a stranger should have reassured him. It did the opposite.

He set the pen down. Got up. Slid onto the stool beside the guy, slung an arm along the back of it, and looked at Kyu.

"Almost done? Thought we'd take the long way home."

_We._ _Home._ Set down clean on the bar.

Kyu looked over and read his face — the whole territorial set of it — in that instant, total way ze read him and nothing else in the room. And the smug that came up then was a different animal from the one the guy had been getting. Same mouth, more behind it.

"Ten minutes," ze said to Will. Then, to the guy, mild, no edge, just the practiced close: "He's the one I go home to. You've been at this twenty minutes, I didn't want to waste your night." A nod at the glass. "That one's on me. No hard feelings."

The guy found somewhere else to be.

Kyu planted both forearms on the bar in front of Will, fully online now.

"You were jealous."

"I was being friendly."

"You sat down like hired security." Delighted. "I had him handled before you were off your stool. I always do. Comes with the apron."

"He was persistent."

"They're all persistent. It's a bar." Ze flicked a lime wedge at his chest. A beat. "Didn't need a flag planted, though. Could've sat there and watched me not care."

"And if I hadn't?"

"Then you'd have watched me not care," Kyu said, like it was obvious, untying the apron. The smug clicked back into place like armor going on. "But you got up. So." Ze came around the bar, took the pen out of his hand, pocketed it. "Long way home."

"You're filing this. I can see you filing this."

"Jealous Will. Good look." Already steering him toward the door. "Thinking I'll let the next one talk a little longer. For science."

"Absolutely not."

"Get your book, neighbor."

---
# 
Will got to thinking, on the long way home, about the first weeks. About how _cold_ he'd decided ze was — the box-carrier who wouldn't make small talk, the flat face in the elevator. He'd built a whole theory of zir around it.

He'd had it backwards. He could see that now.

Because that wasn't cold. That was just zir _face_ — the resting set of it, flat and unbothered, the same one ze wore stocking limes and reading on the couch and listening to Will talk. It didn't mean anything. He'd filed it as a closed door and it had only ever been a face. Half the things he'd read as zir keeping distance, looking back, weren't. The elevator. The smug, that first morning. Ze hadn't been brushing him off. Ze'd been—

"You're thinking loud again," Kyu said, beside him.

Will glanced over. And got caught looking, the way he still did — at the line of zir jaw under the streetlight, the wrecked black hair, the mismatched eyes, the whole unfair arrangement of zir. Pretty wasn't the word a person was supposed to reach for, ze'd hate it, but it was the one that arrived. Pretty. And, in the flat unguarded resting-face quiet of the walk, something close to _cute,_ which ze'd hate more.

Kyu looked back at him. Held it.

And Will watched something land behind zir eyes — watched zir catch him caught, watched the read happen, fast and total — and then the corner of zir mouth started to go, slow, into the smug, the real one, the one that had never once meant _cold_ and that Will had spent months learning to translate.

He knew that smile now. He knew exactly what it cost him.

"Don't," Will said.

"You were staring."

"I'm allowed. You live with me."

"You went all soft about it, too." Ze stopped walking. Turned to face him on the empty street, in no hurry, the smug arriving in full. "You've been doing it all night. The jealous thing, the bodyguard thing, and now you're out here looking at me like—" the mouth tipped further, the trap closing, "—we got home an hour ago."

"That's not—"

"It's the long way for a reason, neighbor." And there it was, that smile aimed at _him,_ the version no stranger at a bar would ever get, the one that had been there since the elevator if he'd only known how to read it. "You started something at my bar. Walked all the way home with it. You're not going to make it through the door, are you."

Will was, distantly, aware that he was in trouble.

He was also aware that he'd been in trouble since a man carried his record crates up three flights without being asked — that the cold neighbor had never been cold, that the door had never been closed, that he'd just needed the months to learn the face.

"...Walk faster," he said.

Kyu laughed, low, and took his hand, and did not walk faster at all.

---
# The office
Will's morning was going well right up until reception called to say he had a visitor, no appointment, who'd given zir name as _his neighbor._

The office remembered the neighbor. The office had not forgotten a single detail of the folder incident — the borrowed shirt, the mark on his neck, the front-desk announcement that had toured the satellite branch by Monday. So when Kyu strolled out of the elevator onto Will's floor, unhurried, wrecked black hair and mismatched eyes and the easy presence that turned an open-plan office into an audience, work simply stopped at every desk ze passed. Will watched it happen from the glass wall of his own office like a wave coming toward him.

This time there was no folder. This time the intent was written all over zir.

"Hi," ze said, letting zirself in, closing the door with a click that several onlookers absolutely clocked. "Busy?"

"Extremely. I have a two o'clock." Will did not get up. Did not trust himself to get up. "Why are you here. You're never here. You actively avoid the concept of _here._"

"Was in the area." Ze was not in the area. Ze took an unhurried tour of his office instead — ran a finger along the bookshelf, examined the city view, drifted, with the slow inevitability of weather, around behind his desk. "Nice office. Very you. Lot of glass." A beat. "Everyone can see in."

"They can," Will said, with feeling, "see in."

"Mm." And ze settled a hip onto the edge of his desk, right beside him, close, looking down with the resting flat face that he now knew meant the exact opposite of nothing, and Will understood with total clarity that he was not getting anything done for the foreseeable future, and that ze knew it, and that ruining his afternoon was the entire and only reason ze had come.

"I have a _deck,_" he tried.

"You have a deck." Ze reached over, unhurried, and turned his monitor a few degrees away, like clearing a table. "Tragic. Tell me about the deck."

"Kyu."

"You took the home win," ze said, mild, conversational, leaning in just slightly, voice dropping under the open-plan hum to a register pitched for exactly one person. "And the bar, that dirty little move with the pour. Two fronts. I let you have both." The corner of the mouth. "This one's mine. You're at work, you can't make a sound, there's forty people through that glass, and you have a two o'clock you're going to walk into looking like _that._" Ze tilted zir head, taking in the color already climbing his neck. "And there's nothing you can do, because you can't win out here. Out here I just have to sit on your desk."

Will's pen had stopped moving some time ago.

"This is a hostile work environment," he said, weakly, the same words, no force left in them.

"This is your _office._" Ze smiled, the real one, the trouble one. "I'd never. People can see in." A pause, deadly. "Better keep it together, then. You've got a two o'clock."

And ze stayed exactly there — a hip on his desk, close enough to be a problem, doing nothing a single onlooker could name, fully content to spend zir afternoon dismantling his one entirely without lifting a finger, because ze'd found the one arena Will held and walked straight into it to remind him that everywhere outside the bedroom, the score was still hers.

Will did not get through the deck.
