He did, somehow, get through the two o'clock — flushed, distracted, the conference room politely pretending not to notice — while his neighbor waited in his glass office with zir feet up on his desk, in full view of his entire staff, who had by now thoroughly updated the group chat.

---
# 
The group chat, for the record, had been a quiet and professional place before Will's neighbor started visiting.

It did not stay that way. By 2:15 it had a working title — most votes for **boss's situation (ongoing)** — and a frankly impressive pace.

> **reception (Dana):** zir here again **Dana:** no folder this time **Dana:** just. here. closed his door **accounting (Mateusz):** closed it CLOSED it or closed it closed it **Dana:** clicked it. i heard the click **Mateusz:** 🫡 **Priya, design:** zoom in on the glass i need eyes **Priya:** ze is SITTING on his desk **Priya:** ON it. like a cat. boss looks like he's seen god **Mateusz:** is he ok he keeps not moving his pen **Dana:** he's been on the same slide for nine minutes **Lena (??? how is Lena in this chat):** which slide **Mateusz:** who added lena **Lena:** i have my ways. WHICH SLIDE **Priya:** lena this is an internal company chat **Lena:** and that's an internal company crush so we're even. tell me everything

By the time Will surfaced for his two o'clock — pink, distracted, smoothing his collar in a way that fooled no one — the chat had assembled a full incident report, two grainy long-lens photos taken through the glass, a poll (_are they together / obviously / has been for months you absolute children_), and a running theory thread titled _the SHIRT incident — a retrospective_, in which Mateusz from accounting had reconstructed the entire folder morning from memory and was being hailed as a primary source.

> **Priya:** he's going into the conference room. he's the color of a tomato **Dana:** ze waved at him through the glass. zir got zir FEET on his desk now **Mateusz:** a king. a menace. i would die for zir **Lena:** ☝️ correct. ok somebody screenshot the feet thing and send it to me directly, it's for science **Priya:** science being **Lena:** i'm going to put it on a cake

When Will got back to his office, his neighbor was still there, still feet-up, looking insufferably comfortable. Across the floor, forty people snapped their eyes back to their monitors at exactly the same suspicious moment.

"Your staff like me," Kyu observed.

"My staff are _working._"

"Your staff took a photo. Two. The design one's got a good angle." Ze didn't move zir feet. "You should get the glass frosted, neighbor. Or don't. I'm starting to like the audience."

Will sat down, put his face in his hands, and did not ask what the cake was for. He found out at the holiday party. It was a very good cake.

---
# The lazy night

It happened on an ordinary night, which was the only kind it could have.

Late, on the couch. Kyu had zir head in Will's lap, stretched out, the resting face gone soft the way it only went here, doors locked, no one to be on for. Will had a hand in zir hair without thinking about it. He'd noticed weeks ago that ze let him. Hadn't said anything, because saying something was the fastest way to make it stop.

His fingers moved through the black, the grey-white strands threaded in it, and he felt the long exhale, the weight of zir sinking further into his lap.

He kept stroking, slow, the way you'd settle a cat — and ze leaned into it. Pressed up into his hand. Wordless.

"You know I'm crazy about you," Will said. Quiet. No joke behind it. "Right."

"Mm."

"Look at me." He didn't stop the hand. "I'm not doing the bit. No bar, no scorekeeping. Just saying it. You're it for me. I don't want a long way home with anyone else."

He felt zir reach for the out — the half-second where the smug came up, where ze'd find the angle and turn it back into a game ze could win.

He kept his hand moving. "Don't make it a joke. Take it."

Kyu went still. The mouth opened, found nothing, closed.

Ze didn't run.

After a long moment, eyes shut, ze turned zir face the smallest degree into his palm — and let him see zir do it. "...Yeah," ze said, hoarse. "You too. Obviously." Then, smaller: "Keep doing the hair thing."

Will kept doing the hair thing.

Ze lay there, eyes closed, and let him, and didn't reach for the smug again. The wall carried nothing that night. There was nothing to carry.

---
# Movie night
It started small, which was the only way Will ever got away with anything.

Movie night. The two of them on the couch, lights off, Kyu sprawled into his side the way ze ran aground there most evenings. And somewhere in the first act Will let his hand drift — under the hem of zir shirt, flat against the warm skin of zir back, no agenda. Just resting there.

Kyu didn't look away from the screen. "Smooth."

"I'm watching the movie."

"You're not watching anything." But ze didn't move his hand. Settled, if anything, a little closer into it.

So it stayed. And the next movie night the hand started there, already under the shirt, like it had earned the spot — and got braver. Mapped the line of zir spine, slow, idle, while Will kept his eyes fixed on the screen and his face composed in a performance of innocence neither of them was buying.

"You missed the whole scene," Kyu observed.

"Riveting film."

"You don't know a single character's name."

"It's a slow burn." His hand traced higher, fingertips along a shoulder blade. Kyu exhaled through zir nose, the soft almost-laugh, and tipped zir head back against the cushion — but ze didn't push him off. Never pushed him off. The teasing was just narration. The hand was allowed.

Each time after that it pushed its luck a little further. He'd start innocent and drift bold, and ze'd clock every escalation out loud — _bold tonight,_ _that's not your back,_ _you missed the twist, there was a whole twist_ — flat and dry and amused, the running commentary of someone fully aware they were being slowly undone and electing, for reasons of zir own, to let it happen.

Tonight Will didn't even pretend to start at the back.

"Movie's good," he said, hand already sliding up under the front of zir shirt, splaying warm and certain across zir stomach, his eyes still dutifully on the screen.

A beat. Kyu looked down at the hand, then sideways at him, the corner of zir mouth going.

"You're not even subtle anymore."

"Subtlety wasn't working." His thumb moved, unhurried. "You kept narrating it."

"I'll keep narrating." But zir voice had dropped a register, and ze still wasn't moving him, was in fact going pliant under the hand in a way that gave the whole thing away. "Bold. Very bold. Hand's really going on a journey, neighbor—"

"Shh." Will turned his head at last, abandoning the pretense of the film entirely. "I'm watching the movie."

Kyu was already pulling him down. "You're really not."

The movie played to an empty couch about four minutes later.

---
# Meeting with parents
Will thought about it for two weeks before he asked, which Kyu clocked immediately, because Will thinking about something for two weeks had a smell.

"You're doing the two-week face," ze said, over dinner. "The one where you've decided something and you're building a runway before you say it. Just say it."

"My parents want to meet you."

Kyu didn't look up from zir plate. "Okay."

"That's it? _Okay?_" Will had prepared for resistance. He had counterarguments loaded. "They're — my dad's very formal. My mother has _opinions._ It's a whole dinner, there's an actual seating thing, it's not—" He stopped. "You're not nervous at all."

"Why would I be nervous."

"Most people—"

"I'm not most people, and I don't get nervous meeting people who already have to like me." Ze finally glanced up, flat, certain. "You told them you're keeping me. Same as the friends. They can adjust or they can adjust. Either way I'm coming to dinner." A beat. "What do I wear. There's clearly a wardrobe answer you want and you're afraid to say it."

"...Something with sleeves would be great."

"Sleeves. Noted."

Will spent the drive over quietly bracing for catastrophe. He'd seen what happened when Kyu answered a loaded question honestly; he'd watched it detonate a table of his own friends. His parents' dinner table had _linen._ There were multiple forks. He had visions.

The visions did not survive contact with reality, because Kyu — sleeves on, hair as tamed as it got, which was not very — turned out to be _devastating_ in exactly the wrong way for Will's blood pressure. Not by performing. By doing the opposite. Ze answered every question his father lobbed with the same flat, total honesty ze gave everyone, and it landed in that formal dining room like a novelty.

"And what is it you do?" his father asked, in the tone of a man conducting an interview.

"Three things. Bartend, coach boxing, freelance." Ze buttered a roll. "Whichever pays that week."

Will waited for the temperature to drop. It didn't. His father, who Will had never once seen impressed by a non-answer, seemed almost thrown by the lack of a sales pitch — by someone at his table with nothing to prove to him.

His mother was worse. His mother was a problem. His mother asked how they'd met, and Will opened his mouth to deploy the sanitized version, and Kyu said, "Thin walls. I'm loud, he complained, one thing led to another," and Will died a small death — but his _mother laughed._ Really laughed, delighted, the laugh she saved for people who surprised her, which was almost no one.

It went like that all night. Every place Will braced for the wheels to come off, they didn't. Kyu didn't charm anyone, which was the thing — didn't try, wouldn't have known how. Ze was just entirely, immovably zirself in a room built to make people perform, and it read, somehow, as the most confident thing anyone at that table had ever seen. His mother was gone by dessert. His father shook zir hand at the door and held it a beat too long, the closest that man came to approval.

In the car, Will exhaled for what felt like the first time in four hours.

"They loved you."

"People usually do." Kyu was already reclining the seat. "You were terrified the whole time. Vibrating. I could feel it from across the table."

"You told my mother we met because you're _loud._"

"She asked. I answered. She thought it was funny, because it is." Ze cracked one eye at him, the smug climbing back on now the sleeves had done their duty. "You know what your problem is, neighbor. You keep thinking I'm going to embarrass you. I never embarrass you." A pause, perfectly weighted. "You do that. You embarrass you. I just stand there being honest and watch you turn colors."

Will couldn't argue with it, which was the whole trouble with zir, so he drove and said nothing.

"For the record," Kyu added, settling in, eyes closing, entirely at ease in the passenger seat of a life ze'd walked into and made zirself at home in, "your mother slipped me her number. Said to call if you're ever being difficult." The corner of the mouth. "I like her. We're going to be a problem for you."

"Please don't team up with my mother."

"Too late. It's in the file."

---
# Handsome
It slipped out on a Tuesday. That was the thing Will would hold onto: not a big moment, not a lead-up. Just a Tuesday.

He'd come out of the bedroom dressed for some work thing, fixing a cuff, not really paying attention — and Kyu, passing through with coffee, glanced over and said, offhand, the way ze said the temperature, "You look good. Handsome." And kept walking to the couch.

Will stopped fixing the cuff.

"What did you just say."

"That you're going to be late." Kyu was already opening a book.

"You said I was handsome."

"I said you look presentable. Don't spiral."

But it was out, and it could not be put back, and Will had spent months on the losing end of every public exchange this person had ever handed him, and he recognized, with the pure joy of a man who has been waiting, that the balance of the entire universe had just tipped.

"You think I'm handsome," Will said, wonderingly.

"I think you're going to miss your meeting."

"You — the smuggest person alive, the person who made a tomato vendor blush, the person who put my feet-on-desk humiliation on a _cake_ — you just called me handsome, unprompted, on a Tuesday." He sat down on the arm of the couch, radiant. "Say it again."

"No."

"Just once more. For the file."

Kyu did not say it again. Kyu had, in fact, made a rare and grievous tactical error, and knew it, and the flat face was doing an admirable job of pretending otherwise — but Will had _seen_ the eye contact break for half a second, and half a second was all he needed, and he was never, ever going to let this go.

He did not let it go.

He brought it up at breakfast the next morning ("morning, handsome — oh, sorry, that's your line"). He brought it up at the bar in front of zir regulars ("ze thinks I'm handsome, ze told me, it's a whole thing"). He brought it up during the movie, at the gym, in the passenger seat, each time with the insufferable delight of a man cashing a single chip he'd waited the entire relationship to win.

"You've mentioned it," Kyu said, flatly, on day four, "eleven times."

"You _counted._ That means it got to you." Will was thriving. "One offhand compliment and I've had four of the best days of my life. Do you see what you did. Do you see the power you handed me."

"I'll take it back."

"You can't take it back. It's said. There are no takebacks, you told me that yourself, at the party, in front of witnesses—"

Kyu looked at him for a long moment. And then, because ze could never quite let Will hold a win clean, the corner of zir mouth went up, slow, and ze leaned in and said, mild:

"Fine. You're handsome. Very. I look at you and I forget what I was doing sometimes, it's a real problem, some mornings I lose whole minutes." A beat, watching the grin freeze on his face as ze escalated straight past him. "I tell my friends. I told your mother. There's a word for how you look in the grey shirt and I'm not going to say it in daylight." Ze tilted zir head, victorious. "That what you wanted? Or is the one word easier to carry around."

Will, scarlet, thoroughly out-maneuvered on his own turf, said nothing.

"Thought so." Kyu went back to zir book, smug restored, order returned to the universe. "Handsome. Now you're overloaded. Couldn't even take the whole sentence." The mouth tipped. "You're easy, neighbor. One compliment and you fold. I've known since the elevator."

---
# 
"You're easy, neighbor," Kyu said, going back to zir book. "One compliment and you fold."

Will took the book out of zir hands and set it on the table.

"Say the word."

"No."

"You said there was a word. For the grey shirt. You wouldn't say it in daylight." He was already moving, unhurried, crowding zir back into the couch cushions, and there was nothing flustered in him now — this was the other Will, the one that only came out with the door locked, patient and certain and done losing. "It's not daylight much longer. Say it."

"You don't get to—" Kyu started, which was the wrong sentence, because it implied Will was asking, and Will was past asking. He got a hand along zir jaw, tipped zir face up, and kissed zir slow — the way that already, always, took the smug apart faster than anything else in his arsenal — and felt the comeback dissolve unspoken against his mouth.

He didn't rush the rest. That was the whole weapon, and he knew it now. Everywhere else in the world Kyu was faster than him, read him quicker, won cleaner. Here Will had one advantage and it was _patience,_ and he used it like a scalpel — got zir out of the grey shirt slow, deliberate, mouth following his hands down the line of zir throat, zir collarbone, taking his time in a way that had nothing careless in it at all.

"This the part," Kyu managed, still going for dry, "where you—"

Will bit down, gently, at the join of neck and shoulder, and the sentence broke in half.

He mapped zir like that. Unhurried. Reading the body the way ze read his face — completely, faster than ze could hide it — cataloguing every hitch of breath, every place zir hands went tight in the couch, filing it all away and using it. When ze arched up wanting more he gave less. When a clever remark started to surface he closed it with his mouth or his teeth or a slow drag of his hand until it died in zir throat, until the running commentary the menace of the third floor narrated the whole world with simply ran _out._

"You could go faster," Kyu said, and it came out wrecked, half a request, as close to _please_ as ze got.

"I could." He didn't. He dragged his mouth lower instead, down the flat of zir stomach, felt the muscle jump under his lips, felt zir breath go ragged above him. "You spent four days running your mouth. The market. Your regulars. The cake, my mother." He looked up, deliberate, from between zir thighs. "Every front out there is yours. I stopped fighting for them. But you gave me one word and got smug about it—" he pressed a kiss to the inside of zir knee, then higher, then higher, watching zir come apart in increments, "—and now you're going to lie here and let me take it back."

And then he stopped talking, and put his mouth on zir, and _there_ went the last of it.

Whatever Kyu had been building to say left in a sound instead — sharp, involuntary, nothing performed in it, none of the broadcast register ze did for the wall. The real one. Ze fisted a hand in Will's hair and Will let zir, worked zir slow and thorough and _merciless,_ refusing every push toward faster, keeping zir right at the edge where the composure lived and then dismantling it a piece at a time. Ze was loud — ze was always loud, that was the whole origin of them — but this was different, unguarded, the flat unbotherable menace reduced to gasping helpless want with a hand knotted in Will's hair and zir hips chasing his mouth and no smug left anywhere in zir at all.

"_Will_—" Zir voice cracked on it.

He hummed against zir, unhurried, felt zir shudder. Pulled back just enough to speak, breath ghosting over slick skin. "You're usually so chatty." Even. Devastating. Turning zir own register back on zir. "Where'd all the words go?"

"I'm going to _kill_ you—"

"After." And he went back to it, and this time he didn't tease, this time he took zir apart on purpose, fast where he'd been slow, relentless where he'd been patient — one hand pinning zir hip down when it bucked, the other pushing up under zir thigh, holding zir open and _there_ and unable to get away from what he was doing to zir. Ze broke fast after that. Ze couldn't not. Will felt it build in the shake of zir thighs and the frantic pull of the hand in his hair and the way the sounds climbed past anything the wall had ever carried, and he stayed exactly with it, exactly there, gave zir no mercy and no pause and nowhere to hide from how good it was—

Ze came with his name in zir mouth and nothing dressed over it. No irony, no upper hand, no armor. Just _Will,_ helpless, wrecked, the loudest and least guarded ze had ever let anyone hear zir — and he worked zir through every second of it, slow now, gentling, until ze was shaking and spent and boneless under him with one arm thrown over zir own face.

Will sat back on his heels. Wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. Looked at the state of zir — flushed, undone, chest heaving, the flat menace of the third floor comprehensively taken apart on their own couch — and felt smug for once in his entire life, insufferably, radiantly smug.

"Don't," Kyu said, from under zir arm, hoarse.

"I didn't say anything."

"You've got a _face_ on. I can feel it."

"You make a face on me," Will said serenely, crawling back up the length of zir, "and then you tell strangers about me at the market. This is the one room I win. Let me have it."

Kyu moved zir arm. Looked up at him, mismatched eyes still hazed, and there was no comeback there — genuinely none, the first time all week ze'd been left without the last word. Ze looked, for once, like someone who'd lost and couldn't summon it in zirself to mind.

"...Yeah," ze admitted, rough. "Fine. You win that one." A beat, the smug trying and failing to reassemble. "The _one._"

"I'll take the one." Will kissed zir, let zir taste what ze'd done to zir. "I'm not done, by the way."

Kyu made a sound that was half a laugh and half a groan and pulled him down by the back of the neck. "Thank god," ze said, against his mouth, entirely without shame. "Floor's barely heard anything yet."

They fixed that.

---
# 
Kyu did not sulk. Kyu had never sulked. Sulking required caring enough about losing to show it, and caring enough to show it was a thing that had happened to other people.

Which was why it took Will most of a day to identify what he was looking at.

The signs were subtle, because everything about zir was subtle. Ze was quieter than usual. Ze answered in slightly fewer words. Ze made zir coffee and Will's coffee and set Will's down a fraction harder than necessary. Ze was, when Will tried to catch zir eye across the kitchen, extremely interested in the middle distance.

"Are you—" Will started, and stopped, because the word felt insane in his mouth. "Are you _sulking?_"

"No."

"You're sulking."

"I don't sulk." Ze said it to the coffee. "I'm busy."

"You're reading the same page you were reading twenty minutes ago. Upside down for part of it." Will was starting to enjoy this more than was survivable. "This is about last night."

"It's not about anything."

But it was, transparently, entirely about last night — about being taken apart that thoroughly, about the one-voice broadcast, about waking up on the losing end of the only front ze'd ever conceded and discovering ze did not, it turned out, have a graceful setting for _thoroughly beaten._ The smug ran on winning. Ze'd never had to find out what was under it when ze lost, because ze never lost, and now ze had, and the thing under it was this: a tall, unbotherable menace pretending very hard to read a book, radiating a wounded dignity ze had absolutely no framework for.

Will crossed the kitchen and leaned on the counter beside zir, grinning like a man witnessing a miracle.

"You're pouting."

"I will end you."

"You've handled every single thing life has thrown at you with total indifference. Jealous men, my entire company, my mother. Nothing lands on you. And _this_—" he was delighted, incandescent, "—one good night and you can't look at me. This is the thing that got through."

Kyu finally looked at him, flat, betrayed, mismatched eyes narrowed. "You were _smug_ about it this morning. You did the face. My face. You put it on and pointed it at me."

"I learned from the best."

"That's the _problem._" And there it was — ze actually said it, aggrieved, the closest thing to a genuine complaint Will had ever heard leave zir mouth. "You keep taking my things. The shameless thing, the not-embarrassed thing, the — you took the _bar,_ you took the desk back, and now you're—" ze gestured, disgusted, at all of Will, "—you're _good_ at the one thing I can't out-talk, and you _know_ it now, and you're going to be insufferable forever."

"Yeah," Will agreed, warmly. "I am."

Kyu went back to glaring at the upside-down book.

And Will, who had spent this entire relationship on the losing end of every public exchange, who had been announced-about at his own office and cake'd and out-smugged in front of his own mother, looked at the sulking menace on the other stool and felt something he could only describe as _religious._

He kissed the top of zir head. Ze allowed it, radiating grievance.

"For the record," he said, "you're cute when you sulk."

"I don't sulk."

"You're doing it right now. It's adorable. I'm putting it in the file."

"There's no file."

"There's a file. You started it, at the market, months ago. It's very full now." He refilled zir coffee, because he'd learned the grammar from zir and provision was how you said the soft thing. "Drink that. Sulk if you want. You've earned one. First one of your life, we should mark the occasion."

Kyu took the coffee. Sulked into it. Did not, Will noticed, move away from the counter, or the hand, or him — which was, from zir, the entire white flag.

"...You're still insufferable," ze muttered.

"I know. You're keeping me. In front of witnesses." Will bumped zir shoulder. "No takebacks."

---
# The niece
The information arrived in the wrong order, which was how information about Kyu always arrived.

It started with a phone call Will only half-heard from the kitchen — Kyu's end, clipped, unbothered: "Yeah. When. Fine. No, that's fine. I've got her." Then, a pause, and a faint note of offense: "What do you mean _don't teach her anything._"

Will drifted in. Kyu was leaning against the wall, phone to zir ear, wearing an expression of genuine grievance.

"I would be an _excellent_ influence," ze was saying. "I don't get to be the cool one? I'm the coolest one. I'm the coolest person she knows and she hasn't even met me yet—" A pause. "_Fine._ No tricks. I'll teach her nothing. She'll leave dumber than she arrived, happy?" Ze hung up, aggrieved. "My brother thinks I'm a bad influence."

Will was standing very still in the middle of the kitchen.

"Your _brother._"

"Mm. Has to work Saturday, no sitter." Kyu was already opening the fridge, moving on. "I'm taking his kid for the day. She's six. Don't worry, I'll behave, apparently."

"You have a _brother._"

Kyu glanced back, catching the tone. "...Yeah?"

"You have a _sibling._" Will heard his own voice climbing. "In all these months. The friends, the bar, my parents, the whole — you never once mentioned you have a _brother._"

"You never asked." Ze said it exactly the way ze'd said everything, all the way back — flat, honest, no wall in it, just the truth sitting there where it had always been for anyone who'd thought to look. Ze considered. "Siblings, technically. Plural. Two. I'm the oldest."

"You're the—"

"Oldest of three." Kyu found the thing ze was looking for in the fridge and shut the door. "Why is your face doing that."

"You're the _oldest of three,_" Will repeated, at a volume that surprised them both. "You? You are somebody's _responsible older sibling?_ You?? The person who put my humiliation on a cake? The one who tells tomato vendors about our sex life? _You raised children??_"

"I didn't _raise_ them, I'm not their—" Ze paused, reconsidered, an old flat honesty surfacing. "Okay, a little. Some. Our parents weren't there. It's not a thing." A shrug that Will was learning to read as _it was very much a thing and I'm not discussing it._ "Point is I've kept a six-year-old alive before. Repeatedly. I'm good at it. My brother's just precious about the tricks."

"What tricks. What _tricks,_ Kyu."

"Nothing dangerous." The corner of the mouth started to go. "Last time I taught the middle one to pick a basic lock. He was nine. It's a _life skill._ My brother didn't speak to me for a month, completely unreasonable—"

"You taught a _child_ to pick locks."

"A _basic_ one. Cheap padlock. Builds fine motor control." Ze was thriving now, watching Will unravel. "You're going to be so weird about Saturday. She's going to love me and you're going to stand in the corner having a crisis about it. I can see it already." Ze leaned in, smug, delighted, the whole thing turned entirely to zir advantage the way it always was. "_Uncle Kyu._ Get used to it, neighbor. I come with people. There's a whole set."

Will sat down.

"How many more of these," he said faintly. "How many more things are you just going to — how deep does this _go._"

"Guess you'll have to keep asking." Kyu patted his cheek on the way past, insufferable, gone to plan a day with a six-year-old ze was absolutely going to teach something zir brother would hate. "That's the fun part. You've had me this long and you still don't know the half of it."

---
# 

Her name was Mai, she was six, and she arrived at 8 a.m. with a backpack shaped like a frog and the specific unimpressed expression of a child who has been told to be good.

She looked Kyu up and down. Kyu looked back, equally appraising. Will watched two people negotiate.

"Dad says you're trouble," Mai announced.

"Your dad's a snitch," Kyu said. "You want breakfast?"

They were allies inside ten minutes. Will didn't fully see how it happened — he went to make coffee and came back and they had simply _agreed_ on something, the way ze agreed on things with people, no visible warmth deployed, just a flat mutual recognition that they were going to get along. Mai ate an enormous breakfast, fast, the way ze did, and Kyu clocked it and said nothing and gave her more, and Will felt something quiet turn over in his chest that he decided not to examine before nine in the morning.

"Who's that," Mai said, pointing a fork at Will.

"That's Will. He lives here. He can't cook."

"I _can_ cook—"

"He burns pasta," Kyu told her, confidentially. "Water, too. We don't let him near the stove."

Mai regarded Will with open pity. Will accepted that this was now his standing with a six-year-old and moved on.

The day was, by any external measure, wholesome. They went to the park. Kyu pushed the swing too high and Mai shrieked with delight and demanded higher. They got ice cream. Kyu bought the biggest one available without being asked and handed it over like it was tactical resupply. Will, trailing along, kept waiting for the shameless menace of the third floor to surface in some age-inappropriate way and kept not seeing it — saw instead someone patient and unhurried and quietly, competently _good_ at this, catching Mai by the hood before she ran into a bike lane without so much as a change in expression, tracking her through a crowd with the same total attention ze aimed at everything ze'd decided to keep an eye on.

Then Will turned around at the playground to find the two of them crouched together over the padlock on the park's supply shed, heads bent, Kyu's voice low and instructional.

"—so you feel for the pin. Don't look. _Feel._ You'll know when it—"

"KYU."

"We're learning," Kyu said, not looking up. "Fine motor skills."

"That's the _park's_ shed—"

"Which is why it's a good one to practice on. Nothing in it but cones." Ze guided Mai's small hands, unbothered. "You're doing great. Ignore him. He gets like this."

Mai popped the lock. Her face lit up like Christmas. She looked up at Kyu with the pure, incandescent adoration of a child who has just been handed forbidden knowledge by the coolest adult alive, and Kyu looked down at her with the flat, deeply satisfied expression of someone who has successfully corrupted the next generation, and Will understood, with a sinking heart, that he had lost. That whatever crisis he'd been planning to have about _Uncle Kyu_ was moot, because it was already over, because these two had found each other and there was nothing to be done.

"Re-lock it," Will said weakly. "Please. Before someone—"

"Already teaching her that part. Putting it back is harder than opening it. That's the real skill." Kyu ruffled Mai's hair. "Don't tell your dad."

"Don't tell Dad," Mai agreed solemnly, a co-conspirator for life.

By the time Kyu's brother came to collect her, Mai was asleep on the couch against Kyu's side, frog backpack clutched to her chest, having extracted a promise of a return visit. The brother took one look at the scene — his daughter passed out on the local menace, the menace unbothered and unmoving so as not to wake her — and gave Kyu a look that was two-thirds exasperation and one-third something softer.

"What did you teach her," he said.

"Nothing," Kyu said serenely, "she didn't already have the aptitude for."

He left. Mai waved sleepily from his shoulder — and then, at the door, made a small deliberate gesture at Kyu: a little _feel for the pin_ mime with her fingers. Kyu returned it, grave, a secret handshake now.

The door shut. Will looked at zir.

"You're going to be the reason that child has a record."

"I'm going to be her favorite person at every family event for the next thirty years," Kyu corrected, stretching out into the warm spot Mai had left. "Cool uncle. Called it. She's going to request me." The corner of the mouth. "You liked it, by the way. Watching me all day. You did the sap face at the ice cream place. I clocked it."

"I did not—"

"You did. It's fine." Ze tugged him down onto the couch, into the space where the kid had been. "You're allowed. Just don't get ideas, neighbor."

Will, who was absolutely getting ideas, said nothing at all, and Kyu — who could read him like weather — felt the silence, and for once didn't tease it. Just pulled him in a little closer and let it sit there, unspoken, the way the biggest things between them always went.

---
# 
It clicked for Will in the middle of an ordinary thought, the way the real ones about zir always did.

He was doing the mental math he'd done a hundred times and never solved. Kyu worked three jobs. Kyu wore the same four shirts. Kyu drove nothing, splurged on nothing, and had once genuinely debated the merits of two brands of rice for a full minute in a supermarket aisle. The money went _somewhere._ Will had assumed, vaguely, savings, or something private he didn't ask about, because not-asking was the whole architecture of how you lived with this person.

But he'd just watched zir hand a six-year-old the biggest ice cream on the menu without a flicker, and buy a frog-backpacked child everything she pointed at, and something lined up.

"Wait," Will said. They were on the couch, Mai two hours gone. "The kid. Your siblings. Is that — is that where it goes?"

Kyu didn't look up from zir phone. "Where what goes."

"Your _money._ You work three jobs and live like a monk and I've spent months quietly wondering where it all — " He turned to face zir. "Your little sister. The youngest. You're paying for something."

A pause. Not a guilty one. Just the small recalibration of zir deciding whether this was worth the words.

"College," ze said. Flat. Like the weather. "She's smart. Smarter than me, actual test scores, not the useless kind. Parents can't cover it and she's not taking on forty years of debt for it, so." A shrug, one shoulder. "I've got three incomes and nothing I need. It's not a big deal."

"Kyu." Will was staring at zir. "That's — that's a _huge_ deal, that's—"

"It's really not." And ze meant it, that was the thing that undid him — no martyrdom in it, no performance of sacrifice, none of the weight a normal person would hang on the sentence. Ze said it the exact way ze said _had extra_ about the food ze cooked too much of on purpose, the exact way ze'd said _you'll catch cold, idiot_ to a stairwell without turning around. A fact of logistics. Money existed; it went where it was needed; ze didn't need it, so it went to the kid who did. "She works too. It's not all me. I just close the gap."

"How long."

"Since she started." A beat. "Since before that. Since she was little, kind of. It's just how it's—" ze stopped, and Will watched zir arrive at the edge of something ze didn't narrate, and step back from it. "It's not a thing. Oldest does it. That's the job."

And there it was — _that's the job_ — the same three words from the park, the same flat grammar the whole shameless menace of the third floor used to say the only things ze couldn't say any other way. Will sat with it. Sat with the shape of it: the frugality that wasn't frugality, the three jobs that weren't ambition, the entire monkish stripped-down life of the person he'd thought he'd figured out, quietly bent around a kid two cities away so she wouldn't start her life underwater. Months of _where does the money go,_ and the answer was that it went where zir did — outward, without comment, refusing to be named as anything but math.

He didn't make it a thing. He'd learned that much. Making it a thing was how you made ze go flat.

"You're a good person," he said anyway, quiet. He couldn't not.

"Don't." The reflex, fast. "I'm really not, I've done—"

"You put your sister through school on three jobs' worth of shirts you never buy." Will kept his voice even, didn't let zir wriggle off it. "You don't have to make it noble. I'm not saying noble. I'm saying I know you now. That's all. I just know you a little better than I did an hour ago."

Kyu didn't answer that. Looked at zir phone a beat too long, jaw working around something, the flat honesty holding but the edges of it doing something they didn't usually do.

Then, gruff, changing the subject the only way ze knew how: "She wants to meet you, by the way. The sister. Saw a photo. Said you looked—" a pause, the smug creeping back in like armor reassembling, grateful for the exit, "—'way too handsome for someone who can't cook.' Direct quote. Runs in the family, apparently."

"I'm going to love her," Will said.

"You're going to _fund_ her, at this rate, the way you're looking right now." Kyu leaned back into him, the moment closing, the soft thing smuggled safely back out of sight where ze could stand it. "Don't get sappy about the money. It's the least interesting thing about me."

"It's really not," Will said — but he let zir have the exit, hand finding zir hair, and left the rest of it unsaid, the way the biggest things between them always stayed.

___
# The monk
"For the record," Kyu said, some days later, apropos of a grievance ze had clearly been nursing, "I don't live like a monk."

Will looked up from his laptop. "Sorry?"

"You said it. On the couch. _Lives like a monk._" Ze made air quotes, which ze despised and only deployed as a weapon. "I've been thinking about it. It's inaccurate and I want it on the record."

"You own four shirts."

"I own the _correct_ four shirts." Ze held up a finger. "This is a lifestyle. It's called minimalism. It's aspirational. People make whole accounts about it."

Will set the laptop down, because this was clearly going to be a whole thing and it was going to be delightful. "Minimalism."

"I have exactly what I need and nothing I don't. That's intentional. That's _discipline._" Kyu was warming up, gesturing around the apartment with the confidence of someone constructing a rebrand in real time. "A monk gives things up because zir suffering for something. I give things up because everything else is _clutter_ and I have refined taste. Completely different energy."

"You debated two brands of rice for a full minute."

"I made an _informed decision._ Do you want me to be reckless with rice? Is that the man you want?"

"You brought home a chair someone left on the curb."

"That chair is _incredible_ and you sit in it every day." True. Will did. It was the best chair in the apartment and it had been free and ze reminded him of both facts constantly. "That's not being a monk, that's having an _eye._ I rescue quality furniture. I'm basically a curator."

"You reuse the little bags from the produce aisle."

"That's the _planet,_ Will. That's me caring about the environment. You're going to sit there and mock me for caring about the environment—"

"You once made a single rotisserie chicken last a genuinely alarming number of meals—"

"That's _resourcefulness._ That's a _skill._ People pay for classes to learn that." Ze was fully committed now, flat-faced and shameless, defending a life philosophy ze had invented approximately ninety seconds ago and now believed in completely. "I could teach a course. _Kyu's Guide to Not Being Wasteful Like a Certain CEO Who Throws Out Bread The Second It Goes Slightly Hard—_"

"That bread had _mold_—"

"It had _character._"

Will was laughing now, which he knew was a mistake, because laughing meant ze'd won, and ze knew it too, the corner of the mouth already going.

"So to summarize," Will said, "you're not frugal, you're a minimalist. You're not living like a monk, you're a curator with an eye and refined taste and a deep personal commitment to the environment."

"Finally. He gets it." Kyu leaned back, vindicated, monarch of four shirts and one curbside chair. "Put _that_ in the file. Correct the record."

And Will did not say the thing he was actually thinking — that he knew exactly where the money went, that the four shirts and the salvaged chair and the character-bread weren't minimalism or discipline or an eye, that they were a kid's tuition wearing a very convincing costume — because ze'd built the whole bit precisely so he wouldn't, because _minimalist_ was easier to carry around than the real answer, and letting zir have the costume was its own kind of knowing zir.

"Consider it corrected," he said instead. "My mistake. Minimalist."

"Thank you." Ze picked the laptop back up and put it in his hands, satisfied, order restored. "Now move over. You're in the curator's chair."

---
# The siblings

The video call was Mai's idea, apparently, and Mai's mother's phone, and it caught Kyu mid-sprawl on the couch entirely off guard.

"It's my brother," ze said, squinting at the screen. "Why is he—"

He answered. On-screen: the brother, plus a face Will hadn't seen — younger, sharp-eyed, unmistakably related. The college sister. And Mai's head bobbing in and out of frame at knee height.

"UNCLE KYU," Mai bellowed.

"Hey, criminal." Kyu's whole face did something it did for approximately three people. "You practicing?"

"DAD TOOK THE LOCK AWAY."

"Good instincts, your dad. Terrible man." Ze glanced at the sister. "You're up late. Don't you have a thing."

"Submitted it. Wanted to see the boyfriend." The sister leaned into frame, unabashed, scanning past Kyu. "Is he there? The handsome one who can't cook? Mai told _everyone._"

Will, ambushed, leaned in and waved. "Hi. Will."

The sister's eyebrows went up with open approval. Then, to Kyu, in the fond and merciless register of a younger sibling holding a lifetime of ammunition: "Wow. Okay. He seems _normal._ How'd you trick a normal one into it — does he know about you? Does he actually _know?_"

"He knows everything," Kyu said, flat.

"Does he know about the—"

"_He knows,_" Kyu repeated, with a note of warning that had zero effect on anyone who'd grown up with zir.

"The walls," Will supplied helpfully. "That's actually how we met. Thin walls."

There was a beat. Then the sister _howled,_ the brother put his face in his hand, and Mai said "what walls" and was ignored.

"Oh my _god,_" the sister wheezed. "That's how — of _course_ that's how. Of course the neighbor found out. Zir been like this our whole _lives,_ you know that, right? We shared a wall growing up too, I have _stories_—"

"Do not," Kyu said.

"There was a _phase_—"

"We're hanging up—"

"—where our mother had to institute a _rule_—"

"Okay, love you, bye—" and Kyu hung up on zir own sibling, flat-faced, while Will lost it beside zir on the couch.

The phone buzzed immediately. A text, from the sister, to both of them now: _he seems great. good luck living next to that, i did 18 years 💀 make him tell you about the summer camp incident._

"There's a summer camp incident," Will said, delighted beyond words.

"There is no summer camp incident."

"Your sister says there's a summer camp incident."

"My sister is a liar and I paid for her entire education, which she should remember." Kyu took the phone out of his hands and set it face-down, dignity fully mustered. "They don't get to team up with you. That's the rule. Mai's already yours, I lost Mai, I'm not losing the rest of them—"

"Too late," Will said serenely. "It's in the file."

"It's always in the file with you—"

"Big family file now. Growing fast." He was grinning. "You're the shameless older sibling, aren't you. You've been _exactly like this_ since you were a kid, and they all know, and they've just been waiting for someone to compare notes with."

Kyu looked at him. Considered denying it. Elected, for once, not to bother.

"...Mai really likes you," ze said instead, which wasn't a denial, and pulled him back down onto the couch, and let the summer camp incident live to be extracted another day.

---
# 
The summer camp incident took three weeks and a bribe to extract, and Will got it, in the end, not from Kyu but from the sister, over a second video call Kyu had made the tactical error of leaving the room during.

"Okay so." The sister settled in, gleeful, the look of a woman who had waited years for an audience that hadn't heard it. "Ze was — what, fifteen? Sixteen. Got shipped off to one of those free council summer camps, the kind they do for kids whose parents work through August. Two weeks. Cabins, lake, the whole thing."

"I'm already enjoying this."

"So you have to understand ze was _exactly_ like ze is now, just smaller and with worse hair. Zero shame. None. Ze'd been keeping half our building awake for a year at that point, our mother had given up—" she waved a hand, "—that's a different story. Point is, ze goes to camp, and ze looks at this place, and ze decides, on day one, that the camp is _poorly run._"

"Oh no."

"_Ze staged a coup._"

Will put his drink down.

"Not a — okay, not a _coup_ coup," the sister allowed. "But ze figured out within about a day that the camp store was overcharging kids for snacks, that the counselors were pocketing the difference, and instead of, like, telling an adult, ze—" she was starting to lose it, "—ze set up a _rival snack operation._ Undercut them. Ran it out of zir cabin. Bought in bulk on the one town trip, marked it up just enough to cover cost, sold to the entire camp cheaper than the official store. Within a week the real store was _dead._ No customers. Kids were coming from other cabins. Ze had a _supply chain._"

"That is the most—" Will was wiping his eyes. "That's _exactly_—"

"It gets better. The counselors figure out their scam's been broken by a _fifteen-year-old,_ right, so they try to shut ze down, and ze just — ze wasn't even _doing_ anything against the rules, that was the thing, ze'd read the whole camp handbook, ze knew there was no rule against sharing snacks, ze just kept saying ze was _sharing_ and technically the money was _donations_—" the sister was fully gone now, "—and they couldn't do anything, and ze knew it, and ze had that _face_ on, you know the face—"

"I know the face."

"—the whole time. Flat. Polite. Absolutely running a black market out of a bunk bed and daring them to prove it." She exhaled. "The counselors got fired. Not ze. _Them._ For the original scam ze exposed by accident. Ze went to a free camp for troubled kids and left having conducted a _labor investigation_ and cornered the snack market, and ze came home and wouldn't even tell the story, our mother heard it from another parent—"

"WHO is telling the camp story."

Kyu was in the doorway.

"Nobody," the sister said instantly. "Weather. We're discussing weather."

"You're discussing the camp." Kyu crossed to the couch, flat, betrayed, and reclaimed the phone from Will's hands with the grim dignity of someone whose legend had escaped containment. "It's wildly exaggerated. There was no coup."

"You had a supply chain," Will said.

"I had _reasonable snack prices_ and a _commitment to fairness_—"

"You got counselors _fired._"

"They were _stealing from children,_ Will, I was the _good guy_ in that story—" and even as ze said it Will watched the exact thing happen that always happened, the flat protest and underneath it something ze'd never name: that at fifteen, at a camp full of kids nobody was watching too closely, ze'd looked at a small unfairness and quietly, thoroughly, without being asked or thanked, _closed the gap_ — same as the rice, same as the chair, same as the tuition, same as the too-much food left at a stranger's door.

Will didn't say any of that. He'd learned.

"You're the good guy in a lot of stories," he said instead, mild, "for someone who insists ze isn't one."

"It was _snacks._" Kyu hung up on the still-cackling sister and dropped onto the couch. "Don't make it a thing. Fifteen-year-olds do dumb stuff."

"Fifteen-year-olds do dumb stuff. You ran a _humanitarian black market._"

"...Okay, that part's a little funny." The corner of the mouth, at last, grudging. "The bunk bed was a good distribution point. Bottom bunk. Easy access, hard to see in." A pause. "Don't tell Mai. She'll want to franchise."

---
# You're rich

"Why do you love me?"

He felt zir consider it. In the dark, drowsy, ze went for the easy one.

"You're rich."

Will laughed. "That's the whole thing? My money?"

"Mm. Saw the record crates. Thought, _retirement._" Ze was already pleased with zirself, sinking back toward sleep, case closed. "Very calculated. I'm a professional."

"Okay. Then why've you never touched a cent of it?"

Silence.

"...What?"

"My money. You're a gold-digger. So dig." Will was awake now, feeling it click. "You put your _sister_ through college. On three jobs. You fought me for six minutes over who paid for _tacos_ last week. You reuse produce bags. You have four shirts." He propped up on an elbow. "That's the worst gold-digging I've ever seen. You're _bad_ at it. You live like a broke curator two feet from an open wallet and you've never once—"

"Okay, hang on—"

"—reached for it. Not once. For someone here for the money you go to _incredible_ lengths to avoid the money."

A long pause. He could hear the gears.

"...Huh," Kyu said. Genuinely thrown. "Yeah. The money." Beat. "Forgot that was there."

"You _forgot my money._ Your one stated reason."

"Slipped my mind completely." Deadpan, digging the hole deeper with total commitment. "Been really busy not exploiting you. Full schedule."

"You're the reason. Say it's not the money."

"It's the money."

"Kyu."

"The money's _right there,_ Will, I'm getting to it—" ze was not going to say it, was going to ride the bit straight into the ground first, "—give me a decade, I'll warm up to the money, these things take—"

Will kissed zir, mostly to stop the bit. It half-worked.

"Fine," ze mumbled against his mouth, folding at last, flat and fast and graceless. "Not the money. You _ask_ me things. Nobody asks. You keep asking and staying. That's it. Ruin a man's whole cover, why don't you."

"Worst gold-digger alive."

"_Historically_ bad. They'll study me." Ze pulled the blanket over zir face. "Going to expense a boat tomorrow to fix my reputation."

"You will not."

"...No," Kyu admitted from under the blanket, defeated, honest, half-asleep. "Hate boats. Waste of money."

---
# 

It was a coffee machine.

Not a car, not a watch — a coffee machine, arriving on a Tuesday in a box the size of a small appliance because it was one, which Will unpacked onto the counter with the ease of a man replacing something that had broken.

Kyu watched from the couch. Watched it come out of the box. Watched the second box of accessories. Recognized it, somewhere in there, because ze'd pulled shots on this exact line three nights a week for years.

"Will." Flat. "That's the bar's machine."

"Old one died. This one does the milk thing you like."

"That's a _commercial_ unit. People don't put that in a _kitchen._" Ze got up, came over, looked at it the way you'd look at a horse someone had parked indoors. "For two people. In an apartment. How much was this."

"Normal amount," Will said, innocent, which was a lie, and they both knew it was a lie, and he let it sit there being a lie.

Kyu did the math. Ze'd priced this machine wholesale, for a _business._ Ze looked at it, then at Will, then at the apartment around them both — the mid-tier building, the sensible furniture, the wall you could hear a neighbor sneeze through — and arrived, unhurried, at a conclusion.

"You're rich."

"I'm the CEO of a company, Kyu."

"No, I knew _that,_ I mean you're—" ze gestured at the machine, "—_buy-the-café-a-new-espresso-line-for-fun_ rich. Actually rich. And you live—" the gesture widened, taking in the whole apartment, the thin wall, the free chair, the modest everything, "—_here._"

"...Yeah?"

"You could live _anywhere._" Ze said it with genuine, flat bafflement, turning slowly to take in the room like ze was seeing it for the first time and finding it deranged. "You have — what did you buy, tell me you didn't buy a _cheap two-bedroom next to a bartender_ on purpose. You picked this. A rich man looked at the whole city and chose the building with the drywall you can hear _everything_ through—"

"The walls are how we _met._"

"The walls are a _construction defect,_ Will. You're a millionaire. You could have a wall. A real one." Ze was fully into it now, entirely composed, prosecuting his life choices. "You make your own coffee. You burn your own pasta. You do your own _taxes,_ I've seen you do your own taxes, you have a _company,_ why are you—"

Will, watching zir stay this unbothered while turning the entire reveal into an interrogation of _him,_ decided to play his one card anyway.

"You know," he said, leaning on the counter, smug, "you told me you were a gold-digger. In it for the money." He nodded at the machine. "Well. There it is. All the money. On the counter. Start digging."

Kyu looked at the machine. Looked at him. Did not, in any measurable way, react.

"Yeah, apparently I'm terrible at that," ze said, flat as the counter, reaching past him to pull the first shot out of a machine ze knew better than he did. "Real blind spot. Never once occurred to me." Ze tasted it. Nodded, grudging, because it was good. "Not what I'm stuck on right now, though. Right now I'm stuck on the _drywall._"

"Can you let the wall go—"

"You're rich and you live like this _for fun._" Ze pulled a second shot, unbothered, handing Will his cup like ze'd always lived there. "That's the weird thing in this apartment. It's not the four shirts, neighbor. It's _you._"

---
# 

The thing nobody warned Will about being rich was that it would be completely useless to him at home.

He had money. He wanted, reasonably, to spend some of it on the person he lived with. This turned out to be one of the hardest things he had ever attempted, harder than the company, because the person he lived with had decided — unilaterally, without discussion, the way ze decided everything — that provisioning this household was _zir_ job, and had been quietly doing it for months before Will fully clocked the scale of it.

The groceries were all zir. Every bag. Will had tried, exactly once, to do a shop, and come home to find the fridge already full, Kyu unbothered on the couch, having simply gotten there first.

"I was going to do that," Will said.

"I already did it."

"I _wanted_ to—"

"Then you can want to next time and I'll have done it again." Ze turned a page. "Put the eggs away, you're letting them sit out."

The bills were only Will's because Will was _faster._ He'd learned to pay them the instant they arrived, ambush-style, because if he waited even a day ze'd have handled it — and ze'd handle it flat, no announcement, the transaction just silently _done,_ like the household was a thing ze ran and Will happened to live inside.

"I pay the electric," Will said once, brandishing his phone like evidence. "I got it. It's paid. I did that."

"Congratulations." Kyu did not look up. "You got to a bill first. Want a medal, or you want me to act surprised."

The joint account was the one that actually got him.

He'd brought it up like a normal adult in a serious relationship. _We live together, we should maybe—_ and Kyu had looked at him over dinner with genuine, flat incomprehension, like he'd suggested they get matching tattoos of the moon.

"That's a married-couple thing."

"It's a — people do it before—"

"It's a married-couple thing," ze repeated, closing it, and went back to eating, and that was apparently the entire discussion, filed and shut. Which left Will in the surreal position of a millionaire CEO who could not get his own boyfriend to _share a bank account with him_ because ze had drawn a line, and the line was earlier than Will's, and ze would not be moved off it.

So it kept happening. Will would reach for a check and find it already paid. He'd try to cover a dinner and Kyu's card would already be down, flat, no fight, no ceremony, just _handled_ before he got there. He'd buy something nice and get thanked exactly the way ze thanked no one — barely, and by having the favor quietly returned in triplicate before the week was out.

"I make more than you," Will said one night, exasperated, genuinely at the end of it. "I make — Kyu, I make _significantly_ more than you, let me _use_ some of it, this is insane, I'm _asking_ to pay for things—"

Kyu considered him with mild interest, the way you'd consider a man complaining about a problem that wasn't a problem.

"Okay," ze said. "So make more. Good for you." Ze went back to what ze was doing. "Doesn't change whose turn the groceries are."

"There's no _turn,_ you've made it _never_ my turn—"

"Because you're _bad_ at it. You forget the eggs. You let bread mold." A shrug. "I'm faster and I don't forget. It's efficient. You should be grateful, honestly, I'm saving you time." And there it was, flat and unbothered and immovable — the reveal that this was never pride, never _I won't take your help._ It was simpler and more impossible than that. Ze wasn't refusing to be provided for. It hadn't occurred to zir as a thing that happened. Providing was what _ze_ did. The idea of sitting still and letting someone do it _to_ zir didn't compute; there was no slot for it.

Will looked at zir — this unbothered, self-appointed quartermaster of a household ze didn't need a cent of his to run — and understood he was not going to win this the normal way. That the richest man in any room was going to have to _fight,_ trick, and out-maneuver his own partner for the simple right to buy zir a nice thing, because ze'd claimed the entire territory of caretaking and posted no vacancy.

"I'm opening the joint account anyway," he said. "One day. When you're not looking."

"That's fraud, neighbor." Kyu almost smiled. "And it's still a married thing."

"..._Fine._ Then—" and Will stopped, because he'd heard it, and Kyu had heard it, and the word _married_ sat in the room a beat too long, and for once it was Will who'd wandered somewhere he hadn't meant to go.

Kyu let it sit. Watched him color. Enjoyed it, unhurried.

"Buy the groceries when you can beat me to the store," ze said finally, merciful, letting him off the hook and taking the win in the same motion. "That's the deal. That's the only opening you get."

Will started setting alarms.

He still lost most weeks. But he got the eggs right, eventually, which Kyu never once acknowledged, and which was, Will understood by then, precisely how ze said _thank you._

---
# 
The friends had been tracking the situation for months, and the friends had a verdict.

It came out at drinks — Will three in and venting, because he'd tried to pay for the round and discovered Kyu had somehow, from across the city, not even present, already left a tab open in his name at his own regular bar. The man wasn't even _here._ He'd pre-empted Will remotely.

"I can't win," Will announced to the table. "I want you to understand the situation I'm in. I am _rich._ I am, by any measure, a wealthy man. And I cannot buy my own boyfriend a sandwich."

"Poor baby," Lena said, into her drink.

"I'm serious. I brought up a joint account like a _grown-up_ and got told it's 'a married-couple thing.' I tried to do the groceries and the fridge was already full. I pay the bills by _ambush._ I set _alarms_ to beat ze to the store—"

"You set alarms," Daniel repeated.

"To buy _groceries._ For a household where I make ten times what ze does. And I still lose. I lost the _electric bill_ this month, and when I won the _water_ bill ze acted like I'd done a cute little trick." Will put his head in his hands. "Do you understand how humbling this is. I run a company. Two hundred people do what I say. I go home and I'm not allowed to buy _paper towels._"

The table was, he noticed, not sympathetic. The table was delighted.

"So let me get this straight," Lena said, leaning in, savoring it. "Your problem — the thing you've dragged us out to complain about — is that your partner insists on taking care of you, refuses your money, quietly handles the entire household, and you _can't make ze stop._"

"When you say it like that—"

"There's no other way to say it, Will."

"He's the richest man I know," Daniel told the table, "and he's crying because his boyfriend keeps buying him things."

"Ze keeps buying me _dinner,_" Will corrected, miserably. "Ze fights me for the check. I have to be _fast._ I have to be _sneaky._ Do you know what it does to a man's soul to have to _trick_ his own partner into letting him pay for a nice hotel—"

"Did you get to pay for the hotel?"

"...No. Ze booked it while I was in the shower." He said it into his glass. "Ze doesn't even make hotel money. Ze just — got there first. Ze always gets there first."

Lena was openly wheezing now. "This is the best thing I've ever heard. You found the one person on earth who won't be gold-dug and you're _furious_ about it—"

"I just want to provide!" Will said, with the anguish of a man betrayed by his own instincts. "I have all this — I have so much money, and someone I love, and the two things won't _connect,_ ze won't let them connect—"

"Have you considered," Daniel offered, "that ze also just wants to provide, and you two are the same and you can't both be the caretaker, so now it's a war?"

Will opened his mouth. Closed it. Looked, for a moment, genuinely struck.

"...That's annoyingly insightful," he muttered. "I hate that. Yeah. That's the whole thing. We're the same idiot."

"So one of you has to lose," Lena said cheerfully. "And zir better at it than you. zir been providing-for-people since ze was fifteen running a snack cartel. You've been rich for like six years. Ze has _seniority._"

"I don't want to _lose,_ I want to buy ze a _coat,_ ze wears the same four shirts—"

"Buy ze a coat, then."

"I _tried._ Ze returned it and used the money on _groceries._" Will slumped. "For the _house._ For _both_ of us. Ze gold-dug me in _reverse._"

The table lost it completely. Somewhere in the wreckage Lena raised her glass and declared a toast — "to Will, the only man alive going broke from not being _allowed_ to spend money" — and the whole group drank to it, and Will drank too, because there was nothing else to do.

He got the bill, at least. He lunged for it the second it hit the table and got there first, for once, and felt a flush of pure triumph entirely disproportionate to paying for four people's drinks.

His phone buzzed as he signed. A text from Kyu: _heard you're out with the friends. tab's covered, don't fight the bartender, it's rude._

Will stared at it.

"...Ze got the bill," he said, hollow. "Ze wasn't even here and ze got the bill."

Lena patted his hand. "To Will," she said again, gently.

---
# The tickle
Will came home from those drinks with a plan, which was a generous word for it. It was less a plan than a decision that he was going to _win_ something today, and the money was a lost cause, so he'd take a different front entirely.

Kyu was on the couch. Reading. Unbothered. Monarch of a household ze'd paid for from three cities' worth of income while Will's CEO salary sat uselessly in a bank ze wouldn't share.

"You," Will said, "covered my tab. From across the city. While not present."

"You're welcome."

"I was going to pay for my friends. I'm _rich._"

"You keep saying that." Kyu turned a page. "Hasn't come up as useful yet."

And Will — who had lost the groceries, the bills, the hotel, the coat, the joint account, and every single check for the entire duration of the relationship — snapped, mildly, and crossed the room, and did the one thing left available to a man with no financial recourse.

He reached down and pinched zir cheek.

Kyu froze. Actually froze — the flat face registering genuine surprise, because _this,_ whatever this was, was not in the repertoire of things people did to zir. "...What are you doing."

"Throwing a tantrum," Will said, and pinched the other cheek, tilting zir face up. "You won't let me pay for anything. This is what's left. You've reduced me to this."

"That's — stop, that's _stupid_—" but there was already something under zir voice, a fray in the composure, because Will had just discovered — in that half-second freeze — that the most unbotherable person on three floors had a tell ze'd never once let show, and it was this.

He went for the ribs.

What happened next was, for Will, one of the great triumphs of his life. Because Kyu — flat, immovable, never-flustered Kyu, who fielded jealous men and CEOs and interrogating mothers without a flicker — was _catastrophically_ ticklish. The book went flying. The composure detonated. Ze made a sound Will had genuinely never heard, high and helpless and completely undignified, and folded in on zirself, and _laughed_ — real laughing, gasping, nothing performed in it, no armor anywhere, the whole flat menace of the third floor coming apart under Will's hands into a wheezing wreck on the couch cushions.

"WILL—" ze got out, between it, thrashing, "—I will _end_ you, I will—"

"You can't pay your way out of this one," Will said, delighted, merciless, pinning zir down and going for it. "No money involved. No checks to grab. Nothing to provide. Just you, losing, _completely_—"

"_Stop_—" ze was crying now, actual tears, kicking, hopeless, "—this is — this is _abuse,_ this is a _hostile_—"

"Hostile _home_ environment," Will corrected, gleeful, throwing zir own words back. "This is _our_ apartment and I have _rights._"

Kyu could not answer, because Kyu was too busy laughing to form words, reduced to a helpless, gasping heap by the one weapon in the world that ze had absolutely no counter for — no smug that survived it, no flat delivery, no wallet fast enough, nothing. Ze who had lost composure in exactly one room of the apartment ever now lost it comprehensively on the living room couch, and Will got to _watch,_ got to be the cause of it, got the great and total unbottling of the person who kept everything battened down.

He let up, eventually. Generous in victory. Kyu lay there in the aftermath, wrecked, breathless, hair a disaster, glaring up at him with zero heat because ze was still catching zir breath and could not make the glare land.

"...You're going to regret finding that out," ze wheezed.

"No I'm not." Will was radiant. "I'm never going to regret this. This is the best day of my life. You _pay every bill in the house_ and you're _this_ ticklish. Do you understand the power I have now. Do you understand what you've handed me."

"I hate you."

"You bought my drinks tonight from another city because you couldn't stand not to provide for me." Will flopped down beside zir, breathless himself, insufferably pleased. "You don't hate me. You've just got no move left. I found the one thing you can't out-provide, out-smug, or out-pay." He grinned at the ceiling. "Turns out it was armpits."

Kyu, still recovering, said nothing dignified, because there was nothing dignified to say. Ze just rolled over, wordless, and dropped zir wrecked head onto Will's chest — cornered, defeated, laughing still in little aftershocks — which was, Will had learned by now, exactly how ze conceded.

"...Don't tell the friends," ze muttered.

"Absolutely telling the friends."

"_Will._"

"It's already in the file."

---
# 
It became a _thing,_ which Will supposed was inevitable, because he had no impulse control where this was concerned and Kyu had no defense.

The discovery reorganized the entire balance of power. All this time Will had been trying to beat zir at zir own games — the money, the smug, the public exchanges — and losing most of them, because ze was better at those, ze'd been running them longer. And it turned out the answer was never to _out-adult_ zir. The answer was to go the other direction entirely. To get _childish._

He learned the whole moveset. The cheek pinch, which never stopped catching zir off guard because it simply wasn't a thing anyone had ever done to zir. The ribs, the reliable nuclear option. The specific spot just above the hip that reduced zir to instant treason. He learned that if he flopped his entire weight onto zir on the couch like a large dog, ze'd complain and not move him. He learned that ze — flat, unbotherable, thirty-something menace of the third floor — could be reduced to helpless wheezing laughter in under four seconds by a man being deliberately, strategically annoying.

"You're _insufferable,_" Kyu informed him, held down, mid-tantrum, Will having launched an assault purely because ze'd paid for lunch again.

"You made me like this. You wouldn't let me pay for lunch."

"So the — so the _mature_ response—"

"There's no mature response left. You took all of them." He got the hip. Ze shrieked, ze _shrieked,_ the sound still catastrophically undignified every time. "You closed off every adult avenue. Money, bills, checks, the account — all yours. This is the only territory I have. I _live_ here now."

And the maddening, wonderful thing — the thing Will figured out somewhere in the third week of it — was that ze _let_ him. Ze could have stopped it. Ze was strong, faster than him, could pin him one-handed and had, in other contexts, done exactly that. Ze never once actually escaped a tickle attack ze didn't want to. Which meant that every time the flat menace of the third floor came apart laughing under his hands, it was because ze'd _decided_ to let it happen — because being reduced to a childish, undignified, gasping wreck by Will was, apparently, one of the vanishingly few things ze'd allow done to zir.

It was the same grammar as everything else. The provision, the four shirts, the tab covered from across the city. Ze didn't have words for _I'll let you all the way in, I'll let you see me completely without armor._ But ze had this. Ze had lying still and letting Will be a menace and losing on purpose, laughing helplessly on a couch ze'd paid for, in an apartment ze ran, next to a wall too thin to hide any of it.

"You're doing the sap face," Kyu said, one evening, breathless in the aftermath, catching him at it.

"I'm not."

"You are. You get all — soft. Right after. Every time." Ze narrowed zir eyes, suspicious, wrecked, hair everywhere. "You _like_ this."

"I like that you let me," Will said, before he could stop himself, honest the way the late hours made him. "You don't let anyone do anything to you. You provide, you handle, you — you never just lie there and let something happen. Except this." He shrugged, caught out, not taking it back. "So yeah. I like it. It's the most you let go all week."

Kyu looked at him for a long moment. The smug didn't come. The deflection didn't come. Ze just — held the look, and then, wordless, tipped zir head back against the cushion and, deliberately, offered zir side to him again. An invitation. The one concession ze knew how to make.

Will didn't take it, not right away. He just leaned down and kissed the ridiculous, unguarded, laughed-out expression off zir face instead, and felt zir let _that_ happen too.

"...You're still telling the friends," ze muttered against his mouth, resigned.

"Told them Tuesday. Lena wants it on a cake."

"_Of course_ she does."

---
# The couch
Will's back had been at him for weeks — the desk, the hours, the particular toll of a job done sitting down — and one evening he made the mistake of groaning about it out loud, at which point Kyu, without being asked, told him to lie down on the floor and proceeded to fix it with zir hands in a way that made Will briefly reconsider his entire understanding of what a spine was for.

"You're _wrecked,_" Kyu said, thumbs working a knot near his shoulder blade that made him see color. "This is desk damage. This is what happens when your whole job is sitting still and buying espresso machines."

"That's not — _oh_ — that's not fair—"

"You're lazy."

"I run a _company._"

"You run it from a _chair._" Ze found another knot. Will made a noise he'd deny later. "You should come to the gym with me. I coach. I could take you on. Cheap rate, friends-and-lovers discount, get you moving before your back locks up entirely and I have to carry you everywhere. More than I already do."

And Will — floating, boneless, half-dissolved under zir hands — knew, with total clarity, even in that compromised state, that this was a catastrophically bad idea. He'd seen bar-Kyu. He'd seen how ze got when ze was _on,_ in command of a space, good at one of zir three things. He'd watched zir coach a six-year-old to pick a lock with the patience of a saint and the moral compass of a raccoon. Letting that person become his _personal trainer_ — handing zir a whistle and a professional excuse to boss him around and a room where ze held every advantage — was the kind of decision a wiser man would sleep on.

"Okay," Will said.

Because his back hurt, and because ze'd asked, sort of, and because — he could admit this only in the privacy of his own dissolving spine — some traitorous part of him actively _wanted_ to see it. Wanted to watch zir be good at the thing. Wanted the version of zir that came home from that gym especially unbothered, applied professionally, at him.

The thumbs paused. He felt, rather than saw, the smug arrive.

"...Yeah?" Kyu said, and there was something in it now, low and delighted and _predatory,_ the sound of a person who had just been handed a much better present than expected. "You'll come to the gym. Let me coach you."

"Don't." Will's eyes were still closed. "Don't do the voice. I already regret it."

"You should regret it." Ze pressed a thumb into the knot, merciless, pleased. "I'm going to be _so_ good at this. You have no idea. You think the desk visits were bad. You think losing the grocery war was bad." The massage continued, wonderful and ominous in equal measure. "I coach _early._ Six a.m. You're going to be there at six a.m., neighbor, in shorts, doing exactly what I say, and I'm going to have a _whistle,_ and there is going to be _nothing you can do about it,_ because you _asked._"

Will opened one eye. "I can still back out."

"You can't. You said okay. I heard it. Witnesses—" ze gestured vaguely at the empty apartment, "—the wall heard it." The thumbs dug in, victorious. "You just handed me a whole new front. A gym. A _professional_ setting where I'm the authority and you're the client who can't touch his own toes. Do you understand what you've done." A pause, savoring. "Six years I've waited for a domain like this. I mean, months. Whatever. Six a.m., neighbor."

"My back feels amazing," Will said weakly, "and I've made a terrible mistake."

"Both true." Kyu patted the fixed shoulder, the massage complete, the trap fully sprung. "Get some sleep. You're going to need it. Coach's orders."

---
# 
Six a.m. came, as threatened, and Will arrived at the gym in shorts to discover that Coach Kyu was a different creature entirely from every other version of zir he'd met.

Not louder. Not meaner. _Focused._ Ze had a clipboard ze clearly didn't need, a whistle ze absolutely didn't need, and the calm, total authority of someone standing in the one room on earth built entirely to zir specifications. The bar was where ze was _on._ This was where ze was _in charge,_ and there is a difference, and Will felt the difference in his stomach the second he walked in.

"You're four minutes early," Kyu said, not looking up from the clipboard. "Good. Warm-up first. We're going to find out exactly how bad it is."

It was bad. That was the first humiliation. Will was, by his own estimate, a reasonably fit man — he looked fine, he walked places, he'd carried his own record crates once, briefly, before straining something. Under Kyu's flat assessment he lasted about ninety seconds into the warm-up before his body filed a formal complaint.

"That's it?" Kyu observed, mild, watching him wheeze through the last of a set. "That's the CEO. Two hundred people answer to _this._"

"I have — people — for the physical stuff—"

"You have people. For touching your toes." Ze made a note on the clipboard. Will was fairly sure the clipboard was empty and existed purely as a prop of authority. "Okay. Get up. That was the _warm-up,_ neighbor. That was the part designed to be easy."

What followed was the most comprehensive dismantling of Will's dignity since the office-lobby incident, and this time there was no folder to hide behind. Kyu coached the way ze did everything — flatly, competently, without a single wasted word, and with a bottomless, serene enjoyment of Will's suffering that never once tipped into cruelty and never once let up either.

"Lower."

"I _am_ low—"

"You're hovering. That's not a squat, that's a _flinch._ Lower." A pause. "I've seen you get lower than this. In other contexts. I _know_ your range of motion, Will, don't lie to me in my own gym—"

"_Kyu—_" Will hissed, scarlet, because there were _other people_ in the gym, early-morning regulars who clearly knew zir, one of whom snorted.

"What. I'm coaching." Perfectly innocent, perfectly flat, in front of God and the 6 a.m. crowd. "Range of motion's range of motion. Lower."

It went on like that. Ze corrected his form with a hand at the small of his back that had no business being that distracting at six in the morning. Ze counted his reps in a bored voice that somehow made each one more mortifying than the last. When Will tried to sneak a rest ze materialized instantly — "did I say stop?" — with the uncanny timing of someone who'd spent months tracking his every attempt to get away with something. When he did something _right,_ he got a flat "better," which he was appalled to discover he'd started chasing like a dog after a treat.

And the worst part — the part that undid him — was that ze was _good._ Genuinely, professionally good. Under the merciless flat commentary there was real skill, real attention, the same total focus ze aimed at everything ze'd decided to keep an eye on. Ze caught the exact moment his knee wanted to buckle and adjusted him before it did. Ze read his body the way ze read his face — completely, faster than he could — and coached to it, and Will, dying on a mat, sweating through a shirt, absolutely humiliated, also understood he was in the hands of someone who knew exactly what ze was doing and had known the whole time.

"Water," Kyu allowed, finally, and Will collapsed gratefully onto a bench and drank like he'd crossed a desert.

Kyu sat down beside him, entirely un-winded, insufferable, watching him recover with open satisfaction.

"So," ze said.

"Don't."

"New front." Ze was radiant, in the flat way ze got radiant. "I have you at the _bar._ I have you at the _office,_ apparently, per your staff. I have you on the _couch,_ which we don't discuss in daylight. And now—" ze gestured at the wreckage of Will on the bench, "—I have you _here._ Six a.m. In shorts. Calling me _Coach._"

"I have _never_ called you Coach—"

"You will. By week three. I've seen it happen." Ze handed him a towel he hadn't asked for, the provision reflex running even here, even mid-gloat. "You picked the one hobby where I'm the boss of you and there's a _whistle._ You did that. Nobody made you."

"My back," Will said, into the towel, "does actually feel better. Which is the only reason you're getting away with this."

"Your back feels better because I'm _good_ at my job." Ze stood, stretched, offered him a hand up he was too proud to refuse and too destroyed to decline. "Same time Thursday. Wear the other shorts. These ones are a distraction and I'm trying to _work._"
