Will took the hand. Got hauled up. Stood there sweating in a gym at dawn, thoroughly beaten on yet another front, coached into the ground by the person he lived with, and — the traitorous truth of it — already knowing he'd be back Thursday.

"...Fine," he said. "Thursday."

"Good boy," said Kyu, purely to watch him malfunction, and strolled off to torment the next client, whistle swinging, monarch of one more kingdom.

---
# Kyu's sick
Kyu got sick the way ze did everything — without discussion, without acknowledgment, and with a total refusal to admit the thing was happening. Will noticed before ze did. Noticed the flat face was a degree too flat, the movements a beat too slow, and then noticed, at breakfast, that ze pushed the plate away, which ze had never once in recorded history done to food.

"You're sick," Will said.

"I'm fine."

"You didn't finish breakfast. You've never not finished breakfast. You eat like the food's about to be repossessed—"

"I'm _fine._" Ze stood up to prove it and swayed, caught the counter, and glared at the counter like it had moved. "...The floor's crooked."

"The floor is not crooked."

By noon there was no arguing it. Kyu had a fever, a real one, the kind that turned the unbotherable menace of the third floor into a congested, aching, miserable wreck — and Will discovered, with a joy he tried and failed to keep off his face, that the universe had finally handed him the one thing no counter existed for.

Ze couldn't provide from a sickbed.

That was the whole game, and Will understood it the second it began. Ze couldn't cook. Couldn't do the groceries. Couldn't beat him to a bill or cover a tab or coach a 6 a.m. session or fight him for a check. Every front ze'd held for the entire relationship collapsed at once, because ze could not get off the couch, and Will — for the first time — held _all_ of them. There was nothing ze could do about it. And ze was going to _hate_ it.

Ze hated it immediately.

"I'm going to make soup," ze announced, hoarse, attempting to stand.

"You are going to lie down." Will put a hand on zir shoulder and pushed, gently, and ze — appallingly — went, which was how Will knew exactly how sick ze was, because upright-Kyu could pin him one-handed. "I'll make the soup."

"You can't make soup. You burn _water._"

"I'll figure it out."

"You'll _poison_ us—"

"Then I'll poison us. Lie down."

What followed was the single most difficult nursing job Will had ever attempted, not because Kyu was fragile but because Kyu was the _worst patient in the history of illness._ Every time Will left the room ze tried to get up and do something. He'd come back from the kitchen to find zir attempting to fold laundry, swaying, feverish, gripping a towel like it was keeping zir upright.

"What are you _doing_—"

"Laundry needs—"

"The laundry does not need _anything,_ you have a _fever,_ put the towel down—"

"Someone has to run the—"

"_I_ am running the house." Will took the towel out of zir hands, steered zir back to the couch, and pointed. "For two days. That's it. That's the whole ask. You lie there and let me do it."

And there it was — the actual nerf, the thing under all of it. Because Kyu looked genuinely, deeply _distressed,_ and it wasn't the illness. It was the horizontal. It was being on the receiving end of the exact grammar ze'd run on everyone ze loved for years — the food appearing, the bills quietly handled, the care delivered without being asked — and discovering ze had no idea how to _receive_ it. There was no slot for it. Being taken care of didn't compute. Ze'd built a whole self out of being the one who provided, and Will had just, by pure viral luck, taken that away for forty-eight hours.

"Stop looking at me like that," Kyu rasped, buried under a blanket ze kept trying to escape.

"Like what."

"Like you _won._"

"I did win." Will set down soup — edible soup, a genuine miracle he intended to reference for years — on the table beside zir. "I won completely. You can't stop me. You can't beat me to anything. You can't provide. You're _stuck_ here, being cared for, and there's nothing you can do about it, and it's _killing_ you." He was incandescent. "This is the best two days of my life. You've been gold-digging me in reverse for a year and now you _physically cannot._"

"I hate this."

"I know."

"I hate this so _much._ I'm the one who—" ze gestured weakly at the apartment, the whole kingdom ze ran, "—I _handle_ things, I don't get _handled,_ this is _wrong,_ the natural order is—"

"Eat your soup."

"—the natural order is _inverted_ and I want it _noted_—"

"_Eat the soup,_ Kyu."

And Kyu, cornered, feverish, robbed of every counter ze had, looked at the soup, and looked at Will, and — with the expression of a monarch signing a surrender ze found personally offensive — picked up the spoon.

Ze ate the soup. It was, infuriatingly, fine. Will had followed a recipe. Ze couldn't even criticize it, which was its own small defeat, layered on the larger one.

Will spent two days winning. He did the groceries unopposed. He paid every bill without an ambush, just _paid_ them, luxuriously, unhurried, no race. He kept zir hydrated and medicated and horizontal, physically returning zir to the couch approximately every ninety minutes when the provider-reflex overrode the fever and ze tried to go run the household ze was too sick to run. He covered zir bar shift by calling in for zir. He canceled zir 6 a.m. clients. He held every single front, and ze _let_ him, because ze had no choice, and being given no choice was the only way this person was ever, ever going to sit still and be loved out loud with nothing to trade back.

On the second night, fever breaking, Kyu lay with zir head in Will's lap — not tickled into it, not argued into it, just _there,_ wrung out and quiet — and said, hoarse, into the dark, the closest ze'd come to the white flag:

"...You made okay soup."

"I made _great_ soup."

"Don't push it." A long pause. Then, smaller, grudging, the real thing smuggled out under the congestion: "...Thanks. For the — all of it. I don't—" ze stopped, the word _know how to say this_ audibly refusing to form, "—I'm bad at this part. The being-done-for part. So. Thanks."

Will didn't make it a thing. He'd learned.

"Anytime," he said, and meant it, and kept his hand in zir fever-damp hair, and let the menace of the third floor be, for one rare and hard-won stretch, entirely and only _his_ to take care of.

Ze was back to covering tabs from across the city within the week. But Will had the two days. Nobody could take the two days.

---
# Ronan
Ronan had not seen Kyu in years. Ronan did not, in fact, know Kyu lived in this city. Ronan was here for a conference, and he wandered into a bar near his hotel at eleven on a Tuesday because it was the closest one with the lights still on, and he was three steps in and loosening his tie when he looked up at the bartender and stopped dead.

"..._No,_" he said.

Kyu, mid-pour, glanced over. Registered him. Did not stop pouring.

"Ronan."

"You _bartend?_" Ronan's voice cracked up an octave, a man witnessing a law of physics break in front of him. "You — Kyu. _Kyu._ You had — you were on the _architecture team._ You had a _401k._ You presented to the _board_ once, I _watched_ you present to the board—"

"I quit."

"I _know_ you quit, everybody knew you quit, you quit in the most Kyu way possible — you just didn't come back from lunch one day and emailed HR from the _parking lot_ — but I assumed you went to a _competitor,_ not—" he gestured at the entire bar, the apron, the rag over zir shoulder, "—_this._ You're pulling _beers._"

"I'm pulling a beer for _you,_ if you sit down and stop yelling in my bar." Ze slid it across. "You're scaring the regulars."

Will, at his usual end stool, was watching this unfold with the slow-dawning delight of a man realizing he has stumbled onto a treasure he didn't know existed. A person from the _before._ A person who knew a version of Kyu that Will had only ever heard denied.

"I'm sorry," Will said, leaning in. "Hi. I'm Will. Did you say _board?_"

"Who's this," said Ronan.

"That's Will. He lives across the hall. We're together." Kyu said it flat, already anticipating the disaster. "Will, this is Ronan. We worked together a hundred years ago. Don't—"

But it was far too late, because Ronan had swiveled toward Will with the electric expression of a man who has just found the one person in the world who wants his information, and Will had swiveled back with the identical expression, and Kyu watched the two of them recognize each other as natural allies in real time and understood, with grim certainty, that ze had lost control of zir own bar.

"You're _together?_" Ronan said. "With _this?_ Does he — sorry, does he know you? Like, _know_ you—"

"He knows me."

"Does he know you once refactored the entire billing system over a _long weekend_ because a code review _annoyed_ you? And then wouldn't take credit because taking credit meant _meetings?_"

"I did _not_—"

"You absolutely did, it's _legend,_ they still—"

"You had a _desk job,_" Will breathed, staring at zir like ze'd grown a second head, delighted beyond measure. "You had a _lanyard._ You presented to a _board._ You've been telling me you 'freelance IT' like it's — you were an _architect,_ you were _good,_ you had a _401k_—"

"I freelance now _because_ I was good then," Kyu said, with the strained patience of someone being dismantled from two directions at once. "That's how it works. You're good, you do it for a while, you get _bored,_ you leave and do it on your own terms and pick up a couple other things you actually like. It's not a mystery. It's not a _tragedy,_ Ronan, stop looking at me like I died—"

"You had _stock options._"

"I still _have_ the stock options, they vested, I'm not an _idiot_—"

"_You still have the—_" Will started.

"Ze," Ronan told Will, ignoring zir entirely now, fully committed to his new best friend, "was the scariest person in that entire building and the _nicest,_ which was the confusing part, ze'd destroy you in a design review and then quietly fix your broken deploy at 2 a.m. and never mention it. Whole team ran on zir and half of them didn't know it. And then one day ze just — _left._ No drama. Emailed HR from the parking lot. We found out ze was gone when the standup was quiet."

"That is _exactly_ how ze does everything," Will said, thrilled, "that's — the _food,_ ze does that with the _food_—"

"THE FOOD. Ze did that with _snacks,_ ze had a whole drawer—"

"Okay," Kyu said loudly, "you two are not doing this."

They were absolutely doing this. Kyu was going to spend the next two hours pulling beers while zir CEO boyfriend and zir old coworker sat at the bar and assembled, from two completely different eras, a single unified profile of a person who had spent a great deal of energy making sure no one had the whole picture. The architecture years. The parking-lot exit. The bar. The three jobs. The niece, the sister, the four shirts. Ronan supplying the before, Will supplying the now, both of them lighting up every time a piece clicked into a pattern they both recognized.

"I'm getting Lena," Will said, reaching for his phone. "She needs to meet you. You have _primary sources._"

"Who's Lena—"

"You'll love her. She wants everything on a cake."

Kyu set two more beers down in front of the pair of them, resigned, monarch of a bar that had just been colonized by both halves of zir own timeline.

"For the record," ze said, to no one who was listening, "I liked it better when you two hadn't met."

Nobody was listening. Ronan was mid-sentence — "_ask_ ze about the parking lot, ask ze _why_—" — and Will was leaning in, and the wall behind the bar, for once, was carrying the most dangerous sound of all: two people who loved zir, comparing notes.

---
# 
"Here's the thing about zir that nobody warns you about," Ronan said, four beers deep, gesturing with the fifth. "zir good at _everything._ And it's not — it's not fair, is the thing, because ze doesn't even _try._"

"I _know,_" Will said, with feeling.

"No, you don't know, because you met the finished product. I watched it happen in _real time._" Ronan turned fully toward Will now, a man unburdening a years-old grievance to the only person who'd understand. "We hired ze as a _developer,_ right? Standard. Good coder, fine, whatever. Six months in zir redesigning the whole architecture and zir _right,_ zir always right, it's infuriating. Cool. Fine. zir a genius at code. That's zir _thing,_ I told myself. Everyone gets one thing."

"It was not zir one thing," Will guessed.

"It was _not_ zir one thing." Ronan slammed the beer down. "Company softball league. As a _joke,_ someone signs ze up. zir never played. Ze shows up, watches for one inning, and then zir just — _good_ at it. Immediately. Like ze downloaded it. We _won_ that year. Ze pitched." He pointed at Kyu, who was pointedly not listening from the other end of the bar and hearing every word. "Then it's _chess_ at lunch. Then someone finds out ze can _cook_ — ze brings in food one day and it ruins everyone's sad desk lunches forever. Then it comes out ze speaks like _four languages_—"

"Five," Will said.

"_FIVE?_"

"Ze switched to — someone at the bar last week, a tourist, ze just answered them in whatever it was, didn't even look up." Will was nodding, grave, a fellow sufferer. "Five languages. Ze mentioned it _once._ In passing. Like it was nothing."

"See, THAT—" Ronan jabbed a finger, "—that's the part. It's never a _flex._ If ze _bragged_ I could hate ze cleanly. But ze just quietly _is_ good at everything and finds out you're impressed and looks at you like _you're_ the weird one for making it a thing." He took a mournful sip. "IT. Softball. Chess. Cooking. Boxing, apparently, now? Ze _coaches?_"

"Ze coaches. Six a.m. zir terrifyingly good at it."

"Of _course_ ze is." Ronan slumped. "Bartending — look at this, look at zir make four drinks at once without looking, who taught ze that, nobody taught ze that, ze just _decided_ to be good at bartending too. This mother fucker," he announced to the bar at large, with the exhausted awe of a man who has made peace with a fundamental unfairness, "is good at _everything._"

"I heard that," Kyu said, sliding another beer down without being asked, provision reflex running even mid-slander.

"You were _supposed_ to hear it. It's a _compliment_ wrapped in a _complaint,_ it's how I say things—" Ronan caught himself, blinked. "God. That's how _you_ say things. Did I catch that from you? Twelve years ago? Is that yours?"

"Everything good about you is mine," Kyu said flatly. "Drink your beer."

Will was delighted. "So ze was like this the _whole_ time. Ze didn't — this isn't a thing ze became. zir always just been—"

"Effortlessly, casually, _infuriatingly_ competent at any task placed in front of zir, yes." Ronan turned back to Will, and there was something under the grievance now, warmer, the thing he'd never say directly. "And here's the part that actually got me, back then. All that — all that talent, ze could've done _anything_ with it. Climbed. Cashed in. Ze had the whole building's respect and half the exec track already mapped for zir if ze wanted it." He shrugged. "And ze looked at all of it and went _nah,_ and walked out to go pull beers and coach boxing and do whatever the hell ze feels like. Everyone thought ze was insane." A beat. "Took me years to realize ze might've been the only sane one in the building."

The bar was quiet for a second.

"...Don't," Kyu said, from the other end, not looking up.

"Don't what?"

"Don't get _nice_ about it. You had a whole good rant going." Ze wiped the counter. "Stick to calling me a motherfucker. It's less embarrassing for both of us."

"You're a motherfucker," Ronan said, agreeably, raising his glass.

"Thank you."

"Who's good at everything and quietly paid for half the office's coffee for two years and never said a word about it—"

"_Ronan._"

"—drink's on me," Ronan finished, and then, because he'd caught more than the speech patterns off ze twelve years ago, he did the exact thing Kyu did, the thing Will did, the thing everyone who loved ze eventually learned: he let the nice thing land and then covered it with something easy, and slid the money across the bar before ze could refuse it.

Kyu looked at the money. Looked at the two of them — the CEO and the old coworker, both grinning, both onto zir.

"I liked being a mystery," ze informed them.

"Too late," they said, at the same time, and then pointed at each other in delight, and Kyu poured zirself, for once, a drink.

---
# Jason
The phone rang the next morning and Kyu answered it one-handed, still in bed, and Will — half awake beside zir — felt the exact moment the call went wrong, because ze went very still.

"...How do you know that," Kyu said into the phone.

A pause. Will could hear a voice on the other end, tinny, entirely too pleased with itself.

"_Ronan_ told you? When did Ronan get your—" Kyu sat up. "You two don't even — _why_ do you and Ronan have each other's numbers, that's — that's not a thing that should exist, those are two separate parts of my life, they're not supposed to _touch_—"

The voice said something. Will watched zir face do something rare: alarm.

"No. _No._ Absolutely not." Kyu was fully upright now. "You are not coming. I'm forbidding it. Jason. Jason, I'm _forbidding_ this—"

"Is that your brother?" Will whispered, delighted.

Kyu put a hand over the phone. "Go back to sleep." Back to the call: "You have a _daughter_ to raise, don't you have — isn't it a _school_ day, aren't you _responsible_ now, that was the whole thing, you're _responsible_ now—"

The voice, clearly, was not deterred by being responsible.

"If you get on that train I will—" Kyu cast around for a threat and found nothing, because what was ze going to do, provide for him harder. "—I will tell Mai about the summer you—"

Whatever the summer was, it landed. There was a beat of negotiation. Then the voice said something smug enough that Will could hear the shape of it through the speaker, and Kyu's shoulders dropped in defeat.

"...Fine," ze said, with the tone of a monarch signing a surrender. "_Fine._ But you don't get to bring — is the sister coming too. Is this a — are you _convening,_ are the two of you _convening_ with Ronan, is that what's—" A pause. "You already have a group chat. You made a group chat. My brother, my sister, and my coworker have a _group chat._"

Will was laughing into the pillow now, helplessly.

"What's it _called,_" Kyu demanded. Then, hollow: "Don't tell me what it's called."

They told zir what it was called.

Kyu hung up. Sat there. Stared at the middle distance with the expression of a person watching several separate containment fields fail at once.

"They're all coming," ze reported, flatly, to the ceiling. "My brother. My sister. Ronan. They've formed a — there's a group chat. They named it. Your friend Lena is somehow already _in_ it, I don't know how, I don't want to know how." Ze turned to Will. "This is your fault. You started this. You handed Ronan your phone and said _you have primary sources._ I heard you. You _weaponized_ him."

"I did do that," Will admitted, unrepentant, thrilled. "Wait — everyone's coming _here?_ At once? Your whole family and Ronan and Lena, all in one—"

"In _this_ apartment. With the wall you can hear a sneeze through." Kyu lay back down and pulled the blanket over zir face, the surrender complete. "The CEO one and the coworker one and the two siblings and the six-year-old lock-picker and Lena-who-wants-everything-on-a-cake. Everyone, in one room, comparing notes, with _nothing_ between them and the neighbors but drywall."

"It's going to be incredible," Will said.

"It's going to be a _disaster._"

"Same thing. With you it's always been the same thing." He tugged the blanket back down off zir face and kissed the corner of zir scowl. "You started it, you know. You carried a stranger's record crates up three flights and now the stranger's got your whole family in a group chat. That's on you."

Kyu considered this. Could not, apparently, argue with it.

"...I _knew_ I should've let you strain your back," ze muttered, and let Will laugh, and did not — Will noticed — actually look all that unhappy about the invasion, under the theatrics. Cornered by everyone who loved zir, in a home too small and thin-walled to hide any of them.

There were, Will thought, worse ways to lose control of your own apartment.

---
# The invasion
They arrived within twenty minutes of each other, which meant there was no gentle escalation — just a doorbell that would not stop, and Kyu's apartment filling past its structural intent, and Kyu standing in the middle of it watching every separate compartment of zir life collide in one room with the expression of a general who has lost a war before lunch.

Ronan came first, because Ronan was staying at the hotel down the road and had nothing better to do and had, since Tuesday, developed a personal mission. He walked in, surveyed the apartment, and immediately said, "Four shirts. Ze told you about the four shirts? zir had four shirts since 2011, I recognize _two_ of these—"

Then Jason, with Mai, because there was no sitter and also because he would not have missed this for anything. He hugged Kyu, who allowed it with the stiffness of the betrayed, and then held zir by the shoulders and said, with the deep sincerity of a younger sibling who has waited his whole life for reinforcements, "Thank you for finding someone. I was worried. We were all worried." he told Will, "We thought ze'd just adopt a series of strangers and pay their rent forever—"

"_Jason._"

"—it's a compliment—"

Then the sister, who arrived last and loudest, took one look at the assembled room — the boyfriend, the coworker, the brother, the niece — and threw her arms wide like a woman coming home from war.

"THE COVEN IS ASSEMBLED," she announced.

"There's no coven," Kyu said.

"There's a group chat. A group chat is a pre-coven." She was already crossing to Will, hand out, delighted. "You're the handsome one who can't cook. I've heard _everything._ I'm the sister ze put through college and refuses to be thanked for—"

"_Nobody_ is—"

"—it's _fine,_ it's zir whole thing, you'll learn." She shook Will's hand warmly. "We're so glad you exist. Do you know ze names the money going to me something else in zir head so ze doesn't have to feel like zir being generous? Ze calls it 'the account.' Like it's a _utility._"

"I have HEARD that exact energy," Ronan called from the couch. "Coffee fund. Office coffee fund. Two years—"

"Oh, ze does the coffee thing to _everyone_—"

And that was it, the last containment field down, because now they were _cross-referencing._ Will watched it happen with the pure joy of a man who had spent months excavating this person one reluctant fact at a time and was now sitting in a room full of people who had the rest of the map. The sister had the childhood. Ronan had the corporate years. Jason had the middle stretch nobody else did. Will had the now. And Mai, six years old, had the single most damning testimony of all, which she delivered unprompted, from the floor, where she was building something out of Will's coasters:

"Uncle Kyu taught me to open a lock. And Dad got mad. And Uncle Kyu said don't tell Dad. But I told Dad." She looked up, grave. "Uncle Kyu says I'm the best one."

"The best one at _what,_" Jason said, with the exhaustion of a father who already knew.

"Everything ze teaches me that you don't like."

The room lost it. Kyu put a hand over zir own eyes.

It went for hours. It was, structurally, a roast, but it was the _loving_ kind, the kind that only happens when every person in a room has independently arrived at the same conclusion about someone and finally gets to say it out loud in front of witnesses — that ze was the best of them, that ze'd quietly carried pieces of all their lives, that ze had spent years making very sure nobody ever caught ze doing it, and that they had _all noticed anyway_ and just never had the chance to compare notes until now.

"The thing about zir," the sister said, wine in hand, warming to it, "is ze thinks zir _subtle._"

"Ze is not subtle," Ronan said.

"Ze is the _opposite_ of subtle. Ze just does everything with a straight face and assumes that counts." She turned to Will, delighted. "Has ze done the thing where ze fixes your entire life and then acts personally attacked if you say thank you? Like you've been _rude?_"

"Constantly," Will said. "I tried to thank ze for soup last week and ze told me not to push it."

"SOUP. Ze made you soup and got _defensive_—"

"I was sick," Kyu said, from the counter, where ze had retreated under the fully reasonable pretext of getting more drinks. "It's a normal thing to do for a sick person. You're all making it _weird._"

"_We're_ making it weird," Jason said. "Ze paid my rent for two years and would only communicate about it through a spreadsheet labeled 'utilities.'"

"It _was_ a utility—"

"You cannot _hear_ how that sounds—"

This was the discovery of the evening, and the room fell on it like wolves: that the one thing capable of dismantling the unbotherable menace of the third floor was not an insult, not a challenge, not a jealous man or a CEO or a fever. It was a _compliment._ Say something mean to zir and ze wouldn't blink. Say something _nice_ to zir, out loud, in front of people, and the flat face developed a hairline crack.

So they did it on purpose. They figured it out collectively, within about ninety seconds, and then they weaponized it.

"You're really good at bartending," Will offered, sweetly, testing it.

"Don't."

"No, genuinely, I watch you make four drinks at once and I feel things—"

"_Stop it._"

"Ze pitched a winning softball season having never played," Ronan told the room. "zir good at everything. It's genuinely remarkable. zir one of the most naturally gifted people I've ever—"

"Okay, that's _enough_—" Kyu's ears had gone visibly red, and the whole room _lit up,_ because there it was, the tell, the crack in the armor, the discovery that the person who told a tomato vendor about zir sex life without a flicker could be reduced to squirming mortification by _earnest praise._

"ZE'S BLUSHING—"

"I am _not_—"

"You put your sister through _college,_" Mai said, from the floor, not entirely understanding the game but wanting in, "and you're the _best._"

"Mai—"

"You ARE the best," the sister crowed, seizing it. "You're kind and you're talented and you take care of everyone and you have a heart of _gold_—"

"I will _leave,_" Kyu threatened, "this is _my_ apartment and _I_ will leave it—"

"—and you'd throw yourself in front of a bus for any person in this room—"

"—I'm getting my _keys_—"

But ze didn't get zir keys. That was the thing Will clocked, watching it — the old Kyu, the parking-lot Kyu, would have simply _left,_ would've walked out on a room that made ze feel this exposed. This Kyu stayed. Squirming, ears red, telling everyone to shut up, threatening an exit ze had no intention of making — but _staying._ Sitting in the middle of a room full of people who loved zir and refusing, for once, to flee from being told so, even if ze had to survive it with zir face on fire and a running commentary of _stop it, don't, this is abuse._

"You're doing so well," Will told zir, mock-gentle, hand on zir knee. "Look at you. You've received four compliments and you haven't run out of the building once. Growth."

"I _hate_ every single one of you—"

"That's a lie and it's _documented_ now," Ronan said. "Six witnesses."

"—and I'm never introducing any of you to each other ever again, this was a catastrophic error—"

"Too late," said Jason, Ronan, and the sister, in perfect unison, and then delighted themselves by having done it in unison, and Kyu put zir burning face in zir hands and made a sound of pure defeat into them while the entire room howled.

Ze surfaced eventually. Red, wrecked, thoroughly roasted, and — Will noted — still there. Still on the couch. Still in the middle of them.

"Are you done," ze said, muffled.

"No," said everyone.

"...Fine." And ze reached over, grabbed a throw pillow, and put it over zir own face, and lay back, and let them keep going — which was, for someone who could not survive a single sincere word without wanting to disappear, the bravest thing in the room. Not fleeing. Just lying there under a pillow while people ze loved told ze ze was good, and taking it, and staying.

"zir under the pillow," Mai reported.

"Ze does that," said the sister, fondly. "Means it's working. Keep going."

They kept going.

---
# 
The compliments eventually wore themselves out, the way even the best roasts do, and the room drifted into the loose warm wreckage of a good night — wine low, everyone sprawled, Mai asleep against Jason's side. And somewhere in the lull, the sister squinted at Kyu, who had at last removed the pillow from zir face, and said the thing that had apparently been bugging her for thirty years.

"Okay but here's what I've never gotten." She gestured with her glass. "You're the most _private_ person alive. We had to run a whole intelligence operation tonight just to compliment you. You won't take a thank-you, you launder your own generosity through _spreadsheets,_ you left a whole career without telling anyone why—"

"Where's this going—"

"—but you'll tell a _stranger at your bar_ your entire sex life without blinking." She spread her hands. "How. How are you _both._ How is the same person locked down like a vault about everything that matters and completely, catastrophically shameless about _that._"

The room, it turned out, had all wondered this. Will most of all — it was, after all, the first thing he'd ever learned about zir, through a wall, months before he learned anything else. Ronan nodded slowly, a man who'd sat with this paradox for twelve years. Even Jason leaned in.

"Yeah," Ronan said. "Actually. Yeah. You'd broadcast _that_ to the whole office and then refuse to admit you bought the coffee. What's the _logic_—"

Everyone looked at zir. Waited.

Kyu considered the question with the mild interest of someone being asked why the sky was a color.

"That stuff's not private," ze said, like it was obvious. "It's just sex. Everybody does it. Why would I be weird about it."

"But you're weird about _soup_—"

"Soup's different."

"_How is soup different from_—"

"It just is," Kyu said, flat, final, and reached for zir drink, and that was apparently the whole answer, filed and shut. No elaboration. No thread to pull. The one genuine mystery in the entire person — the reason ze could hand zir body to the conversation without a flicker but couldn't survive being thanked for a meal — sealed off behind three words and a shrug, as impenetrable as it had been the day Will first heard ze through the drywall.

The room sat with it.

"...That's it?" the sister said. "'It just is'?"

"That's it."

"Thirty years. That's the answer I get. 'It just is.'"

"You could've not asked," Kyu offered.

And Will, watching zir — watching the person he'd spent months excavating produce, on this one specific point, _nothing,_ the vault fully closed on the exact hinge everyone wanted opened — started, helplessly, to laugh. Because it was perfect. Because they'd cracked everything tonight, the childhood and the corporate years and the money and the compliments, assembled the whole map between five people — and here at the center of it was one small blank square none of them would ever fill, and ze wasn't hiding it, wasn't being coy, genuinely just _didn't think it was interesting._ The most exposed person any of them knew, still, somehow, holding the one card that made no sense to anyone but ze.

"What," Kyu said, catching Will laughing.

"Nothing." He shook his head, wiping his eyes. "You're just — after all of it. Everyone in this room's got your whole number. And you're _still_ a mystery. One square left. Right in the middle."

"It's not a mystery, it's _sex,_ you're all—"

"It's a little bit a mystery," Ronan said.

"It's _so_ much a mystery," the sister agreed.

"It's genuinely the most mysterious thing about you," Will said, delighted, "and it's the one thing you'll talk about freely, which makes it _worse_—"

"I'm surrounded by _idiots,_" Kyu announced to the ceiling, and drank zir wine, and did not explain, and — for once — didn't need to flee the room to keep a secret. Ze just kept it in plain sight, the way ze kept everything, and let them all stay baffled, which was somehow the most comfortable ze'd looked all night.

---
# 
It was Jason who broke the moment, and he broke it because a specific horror had been slowly assembling behind his eyes for the last several minutes, and it finally arrived.

"Wait." He sat up. Mai stirred against his side. "Wait. No."

"What," Kyu said.

"You're not—" Jason pointed, a father sensing a threat to the perimeter. "That. Whatever _that_ is. The — the _it just is,_ the completely-shameless-about-that thing. You're not teaching that to my _daughter._"

The room, which had been winding down, snapped instantly back to life.

"I'm not teaching a six-year-old about _sex,_ Jason, what is _wrong_ with you—"

"Not _now,_" Jason said, with the grim foresight of a man who had grown up alongside ze and knew exactly how these things went, "not now. But she's _six._ She's going to grow up. And you're the cool uncle, you've got her opening _padlocks,_ she already thinks you hung the moon, and one day she's going to be — I don't know, _fourteen_ — and she's going to come to _you_ about something instead of me, and you're going to answer her with a completely straight face and _zero shame_ and ruin my entire life—"

"That's _years_ away—"

"I'm PLANNING AHEAD. That's what responsible parents do, we plan _ahead_—"

"UNCLE KYU," Mai said, now fully awake and delighted to hear her name, "is going to teach me things?"

"NO," said Jason.

"Yes," said Kyu, purely to watch him suffer.

"_Kyu._"

"Not _that_ thing," Kyu clarified, flat, entirely too amused. "Other things. Useful things. How to pick a lock. How to make four drinks at once. How to tell when someone's lying to you. How to not care what a room thinks of you." Ze looked at Jason, and the corner of the mouth went up. "How to answer nosy questions with 'it just is' and refuse to elaborate. She's going to be _incredible._"

"She is going to be a _menace,_" Jason said, with despair.

"She's going to be _free,_" the sister corrected, thrilled. "zir going to raise the most unbotherable teenager in recorded history and there's nothing you can do about it, because you can't un-cool the cool uncle, Jason, that's not how it works, we _tried_ with our parents—"

"I know how it works, I was RAISED by the prototype—"

"And you turned out _fine,_" Kyu said.

"I turned out _paranoid,_ I check my daughter's backpack for lockpicks—"

"Which is a skill _I_ gave you the foresight to have. You're welcome."

Jason put his face in his hands. Mai, sensing an ally being formed and a father being defeated, climbed off the couch, padded across the room, and installed herself against Kyu's side with the serene confidence of someone choosing a side in a war.

"I want to learn the things," she announced.

"See," Kyu said, over her head, to her father, insufferable, victorious. "She's already made her choice. Democratic. Nothing I can do."

"You are a _bad uncle,_" Jason said, with feeling.

"I'm the _best_ uncle," Kyu corrected, and Mai nodded firmly against zir side in agreement, and Will watched Jason lose the argument he'd been losing since before he had a daughter, since he was a kid himself being handed a lockpick by the sibling who half-raised him — and understood that the cycle was simply going to continue, one generation down, and that there was, genuinely, nothing anyone could do about it.

"Don't teach her the 'it just is' thing," Jason pleaded, weakly, the last stand of a doomed man.

"Already did," Kyu said. "Watch. Mai. Why do you want to learn the things?"

Mai looked at her father, absolutely straight-faced, and said: "It just is."

The room came apart. Jason lay down on the floor in defeat. And Kyu, monarch of a household ze'd colonized and a family ze'd shaped and a niece ze was corrupting exactly on schedule, looked entirely, completely at home in the wreckage of the loudest, fullest, least mysterious night the thin walls had ever carried.

---
# 
It came up because Kyu was still, three weeks later, not over the drywall.

"I've been thinking about it," ze announced, apropos of nothing, over dinner. "You're a millionaire. You chose this apartment. On _purpose._ With money in the bank. I want to understand the illness."

"It's not an illness—"

"You have a company. You have _stock._ And you looked at the whole city and picked a two-bedroom where you can hear me _sneeze._" Ze pointed a fork at him. "Rich people don't do this. Rich people buy a wall. It's the first thing they buy. Why don't you have a wall, Will."

And Will, who had successfully avoided this line of questioning for the entire relationship, sighed the sigh of a man about to explain his whole childhood.

"Because of my parents."

Kyu set the fork down, immediately interested. "Go on."

"So my parents are — okay, you met them. My dad's very formal, my mother has opinions. What you didn't get, in one dinner, is that they are also — " Will searched for it, "— _aggressively_ normal. On purpose. Militantly. My dad made his money, like, first-generation, built the whole thing from nothing, and he has this deep, unshakeable terror that money makes people _soft_ and _useless._"

"I like him already."

"You would. You two are the same disease." Will took a breath. "So growing up — and we _had_ money, real money, by the time I was ten — my parents raised me and my sister like we didn't. Aggressively. On principle. We flew _economy._ My father has flown economy his entire life, he could buy the _plane,_ he sits in 34C and he _radiates_ virtue about it—"

Kyu was starting to smile.

"—we had chores. We had _allowances,_ tiny ones, that we had to _budget._ My mother made me get a summer job at _sixteen,_ a real one, at a garden center, and when I complained she said, and I quote, 'you'll thank me,' which is the most infuriating sentence in the English language—"

"You'll thank me," Kyu repeated, delighted. "Oh, that's _good._ I'm using that."

"Do not use that on me—"

"I'm using it on _everyone._ Continue."

"So the whole thing," Will said, "the whole ethos I got beaten into me from birth, is: _just because you can afford something doesn't mean you should have it._ You earn your comfort. You don't buy your way out of normal life, because normal life is where character comes from, or whatever — I have my father's voice in my _head,_ Kyu, permanently — and so when I moved here, for the job, and I was looking at apartments—"

"You couldn't do it."

"I could _not_ do it." Will threw up his hands. "I looked at the — I toured a penthouse. A real one. Glass everywhere, a view, a _concierge._ And I stood in it and I felt my father's disappointment reach across the entire country and land on me like a _weather system,_ and I heard 'you'll thank me,' and I could not sign the lease. Physically could not. I broke into a light sweat." He slumped. "So I got a nice-but-normal two-bedroom in a nice-but-normal building, and I told myself it was _grounding,_ and I have been quietly not-enjoying my own money ever since, out of pure inherited guilt."

There was a silence.

Then Kyu started laughing — really laughing, the rare undignified kind, the kind Will usually had to work for.

"So let me get this straight," ze said, wiping zir eyes. "The reason we met. The _entire reason._ The thin wall, the record crates, the toilet paper, all of it — is that a millionaire was too _guilty_ to buy a good apartment because his self-made dad flies economy out of _spite._"

"When you say it like that—"

"That's the _only_ way to say it, Will—"

"—it sounds insane, I'm aware—"

"You had _generational-wealth guilt_ and it landed you across the hall from _me._" Ze was gleeful now, delighted beyond measure. "Your dad's neurosis is the reason I know what you sound like at 2 a.m. Your dad _built_ this relationship. On a foundation of _economy-class shame._"

"Please stop enjoying this—"

"I want to _thank_ him. I want to shake his hand. I want to fly _economy_ with that man and _bond_—"

"You would. You'd get along horrifically." Will pointed at zir, grim. "You know what he said, when I told him I bought the _machine?_ The coffee machine? I mentioned it, casually, and there was a _silence,_ and then he said—" Will did the voice, flat and disappointed, "—'_must be nice._'"

Kyu gasped. "He _didn't._"

"'Must be nice.' Three words. I felt them in my _soul._ I almost returned it."

"_That's_ why you were weird about how much it cost—"

"I'm weird about how much _everything_ costs, I contain a permanent auditor who has my father's face—" Will was laughing now too, defeated. "So yes. That's the answer. That's why the millionaire lives like this. Guilt. Pure, inherited, economy-class guilt. My father raised me too well and now I can't enjoy anything and I met the love of my life because I felt too bad to buy a wall."

Kyu reached across the table, took his hand, and said, with immense warmth and total sincerity:

"Will. Sweetheart." A beat. "You'll thank him."

Will took his hand back. "I'm breaking up with you."

"You're not. I make the groceries." Ze was still grinning, radiant. "Wait till I tell your dad we met through a _construction defect_ you were too frugal to avoid. He's going to love me. I'm going to be his _favorite._ We're going to fly economy together and talk about _character_—"

"You are never meeting my father unsupervised—"

"Too late," Kyu said, serene. "It's in the file."

---
# 
"Master of none," Will said, "is how the phrase ends. _Jack of all trades, master of none._ There's supposed to be a _catch._ A cost. You give up depth for breadth. That's the _deal._"

"Sounds like a you problem," Kyu said, not looking up from the thing ze was fixing that ze had no business knowing how to fix.

"It's supposed to be everyone's problem! That's the _point_ of the saying! It's a comfort to normal people — you can be good at everything OR great at one thing, and most of us have to _choose_—" Will gestured at zir, aggrieved. "You didn't choose. You skipped the choosing. You're good at everything AND you're — you don't have the _master of none_ part, you're _master of several,_ that's not how the deal works—"

"Maybe the saying's just wrong."

"The saying is not _wrong,_ the saying is _load-bearing folk wisdom_—"

But here was the thing Will had figured out, and it was the only consolation available: the price _was_ paid. It just wasn't paid in skill. Jack of all trades, master of none — except this jack was master of most, and the cost had simply been rerouted. The tax on being good at everything wasn't mediocrity.

It was that ze was a _menace._

That was the balance. That was the universe restoring order. A person given that much effortless competence could not _also_ be given the ability to be normal about it — so instead ze got the smug, the shamelessness, the total refusal to let anyone win, the compulsion to be good at things purely to watch other people react, the inability to accept a compliment or a thank-you or a nice apartment or help while sick. The cosmic ledger had looked at all that talent and gone: _fine, but ze has to be like THIS about it._ And it was.

"You know what your 'master of none' is," Will said, arriving at it. "Your one deficiency. The thing you're catastrophically bad at."

"Enlighten me."

"_Being normal._ That's your blind spot. That's what you traded. Everyone else gives up a skill — you gave up the ability to just _quietly be good at something_ without turning it into a whole event." He pointed. "You can pick a lock, coach a gym, code an architecture, pull four drinks, speak five languages, and win a softball season you've never played — and you cannot, for the _life_ of you, receive a compliment, share a bank account, or let someone else buy the groceries. THAT'S your master-of-none. You're a menace. That's the price. The universe made you pay it in _personality._"

There was a pause.

Kyu considered this with the seriousness of someone genuinely weighing it.

"...Okay, but," ze said, "I'd rather be a menace than bad at things. So actually I still win."

"THAT—" Will threw his hands up. "That right there. That's the disease. A normal person would be _insulted._ You heard 'the cost of your gifts is a broken personality' and went 'good trade.'"

"It _is_ a good trade." Kyu finished the fix, whatever it was, and it worked, immediately, because of course it did. "You're just mad because your master-of-none is _pasta_ and you'd kill for mine." Ze set the thing down. "Wouldn't you. Be honest. Menace personality, good at everything. Versus: nice guy, can't boil water. If you could swap right now—"

"...I hate that I have to think about it."

"You don't have to think about it. You already know." Ze was insufferable, radiant, victorious. "You'd take the menace. Everyone would take the menace. Being a menace _rules,_ that's why it's the price for being good at everything — it's not even a _punishment,_ it's a _bonus_ they _pretend_ is a punishment so the rest of you don't riot."

Will opened his mouth. Closed it. Found, horribly, that he had nothing.

"Make dinner," Kyu added, sweetly, "since you're so worried about balance. Master your one trade. I'll wait." A beat, the mouth going up. "I'll get the fire extinguisher. For balance."

---
# 

A little bastard who was, catastrophically, _right,_ which was the worst part — because Will made dinner anyway, purely out of spite, and set off the smoke alarm in eleven minutes.

Kyu didn't gloat. Worse: ze got up, unhurried, killed the heat, opened the window, and fanned the alarm quiet with the same dish towel from the very first time, months ago — the whole rescue on muscle memory now, a routine they'd rehearsed into the ground.

"You turned the burner up," ze said. Not a question.

Will froze, guilty. He _had._ Ten minutes in, impatient, he'd cranked it and stepped back to admire the result. There was no way ze'd seen — ze'd been on the couch, facing away.

"How do you—"

"You always do that." Ze scraped the ruined pan, started over from the wreckage without being asked. "You get impatient, you crank the heat, you stand back like it's done. Every time. It's your one move."

"You weren't even _looking._"

"Didn't need to." And ze glanced back, just once, and read whatever was on his face — the spite, the wounded pride, the _she's about to be right again_ — and the corner of the mouth went up before he'd said a word. "It's all over you. You've got no poker face. I clocked the whole dinner the second you rolled your sleeves up with something to prove."

Will opened his mouth to deny it and found the denial already visible on his face, which proved zir point, which he could see zir watching happen.

"...Okay, that's two now," he muttered, sitting down, defeated. "Pasta _and_ whatever that was."

"Pour the wine." Twenty minutes and there'd be real food, made from his failure, by the person who'd called the failure to the minute. "Menace handles it. You watch."

---
# 
It clicked for Will, embarrassingly, in the middle of doing nothing — folding laundry, half-listening to Kyu talk about a bar regular, when the thought arrived fully formed and made him stop with a shirt in his hands.

"I spent _weeks,_" he said, "building a whole theory about you. Off the wall."

Kyu glanced over. "The wall."

"The noise. Early on, before — everything. I used to lie there and — I had _theories,_ Kyu. I had a whole intelligence file. Who you were seeing. How often. Whether it was the same person. I read the whole — I was doing _forensics_ through drywall." He set the shirt down. "The first night, there were two voices, so I figured, okay, zir got someone. Then the toilet paper night the place was empty and I nearly had a _breakdown_ recalibrating—"

"You did look pretty destroyed that night."

"Because my whole model _broke._ I'd built this — this narrative, this mysterious person with a secret complicated love life, and then it turned out half the broadcasts were just _you._ Alone. And I thought that meant I'd had it _wrong._" He looked at zir, and the thing finally landed, simple and complete. "But I didn't have it wrong. I had it _backwards._ There was never a story to decode. It's just — that's the _volume you come in._ Company, alone, doesn't matter. You feel things loud and you don't muffle it because muffling it would be, what—"

"Weird about sex," Kyu supplied, flat, unbothered, the way ze'd say the weather. "Which I'm not."

"Which you're _not._ And I kept trying to read it like a clue when it was never a clue, it was just—" he gestured, helpless, "—_you._ At full volume. The wall wasn't telling me _about_ you. It was just _you,_ leaking through, exactly as much as everything else about you leaks through, which is — apparently — a lot, all the time, whether you mean it to or not."

Kyu considered this, entirely at ease, no flicker of the embarrassment that a compliment would've triggered — because this wasn't praise, it was just fact, and facts about _this_ ze had never once minded.

"Yeah," ze agreed. "That's just how I'm built. Sensitive. Always been loud. Solo, not solo—" a shrug, "—same broadcast. It's not a performance, Will. I'm not doing it _at_ anyone. That's just the sound I make." The corner of the mouth. "You're the one who turned it into a _case file._ That's a you thing. Very you. Couldn't just hear a neighbor and let it be a neighbor, had to build a _dossier._"

"In my defense—"

"There's no defense. You did surveillance on me for a month and then moved in across a wall you _knew_ was thin. You did that on purpose eventually, admit it—"

"I did _not_ keep the earplugs in the drawer on purpose—"

"You absolutely did." Ze went back to the laundry, unbothered, a person who had never in zir life been mysterious and had simply been _misread_ by someone determined to find a puzzle. "The whole time you thought you were solving me. There was nothing to solve. You could've just _asked._" A beat. "Nobody ever asks. You did, eventually. That's the only reason you got the answer. Everyone else is still standing on the third floor with a dossier."

Will thought about that — the months of theories, the elaborate forensics, when the actual person had been right there the whole time, hiding nothing, waiting to be asked instead of decoded.

"...That's kind of the whole you, isn't it," he said, quieter. "Not hidden. Just never asked."

"Now you're getting it." Ze handed him a folded shirt, ending the moment the only way ze knew how — provision, deflection, done. "Took you long enough. Fold faster, you're slow at this too. Add it to your list."

---
# The ex
The bar, a Friday, and the woman two stools down knew Kyu — that much was obvious from the easy "hey, stranger" and the way Kyu poured her usual without asking. What took Will a second longer to clock, from the specific warmth of it, was the _category._

"This is Will," Kyu said. "Will, this is Nadia. We go back."

"Ah." Nadia looked at Will with frank, friendly appraisal, and then at Kyu, and grinned. "_You_ settled down. I didn't think I'd see the day. Good for you." To Will: "Congratulations. Genuinely. zir a catch and a menace, in that order."

"I've noticed both," Will said.

It was, Will realized, entirely pleasant. Nadia was funny, warm, clearly fond of Kyu with zero residual weirdness, and the whole exchange had the relaxed ease of two people who'd shared something once, enjoyed it, and moved on like adults. She finished her drink, hugged Kyu over the bar, told Will to keep ze humble, and left.

Will waited a beat. "So that was—"

"Yeah." Kyu wiped the counter, unbothered.

"You're not going to—"

"It was years ago. It was good. We're friends now." Ze shrugged. "That's how it usually goes. No drama. Why make it dramatic."

And here was the thing Will had braced for and didn't feel: he wasn't _jealous,_ exactly. He was doing _math._ Because over the following weeks it kept happening — a regular here, a nod there, the bar friends' casual references, the sheer unbothered _scale_ of a life ze'd lived openly and well and felt not one complicated thing about. Ze had a _history,_ diverse and cheerful and entirely un-hidden, and ze referenced it the way ze referenced everything, flat, no shame, no editing.

"You've been doing the face," Kyu observed one night. "The calculating one. Since Nadia."

"I'm not — okay, I'm doing a little math," Will admitted. "You have a very... _rich_ back catalogue."

"I'm sensitive, I'm open about it, and I'm easy to get along with. Yeah. I had a good time before you." Ze said it without a flicker. "That bother you?"

"No. That's the weird part. It _doesn't._" Will turned it over. "I think I just — I keep waiting to feel something about it and I don't, and _that_ feels like it needs explaining." He paused. "For the record, it's not like I showed up blank. I've got exes. I had a whole serious two-year thing in my twenties, I've got a college one I still get a Christmas text from, I'm a normal adult, I've _lived_—"

"I know."

"You know?"

"You told me about the two-year one months ago. You mention the Christmas text every December, you get all fond about it, it's cute." Ze poured him a drink. "I'm not weird about yours either. Why would I be. You had a life before the wall. So did I. Mine was just louder and there was more of it." The corner of the mouth. "Volume, remember. It's my whole thing."

Will laughed. "Okay. Fair."

"The difference," Kyu said, and here something shifted, just slightly, the flat delivery carrying something underneath it the way it did when ze was about to say a real thing badly, "is I never _stayed._ For any of them. It was good, it was easy, I liked all of it, and I never once thought _this one, I'll keep._ Didn't want to. Didn't see the point." Ze shrugged, looking at the counter instead of him. "Then some guilt-ridden millionaire moves in across a thin wall and does _surveillance_ on me for a month and asks me things nobody asks and I—" a stop, a jaw working around it, "—stayed. First time. On purpose."

The bar hummed around them.

"So the back catalogue's real," ze finished, recovering, smug rearming. "Big, even. Diverse, since you did the math. But you're the only one I ever bothered to keep, so I'd retire the calculator if I were you. The number stopped mattering the day I let you keep the coffee."

Will didn't say anything for a second, because his chest was doing the thing.

"...That's the nicest thing you've ever said to me," he managed, "and you buried it under 'back catalogue.'"

"That's how I say things." Kyu slid him the drink. "You knew that when you kept me. No takebacks."

---

# 
Will connected the last dot on a Sunday, at the bar, watching Kyu greet a man at the far end with the exact warmth ze'd shown Nadia three weeks earlier.

Same easy "hey, stranger." Same pour-the-usual-without-asking. Same unmistakable _category,_ which Will could now identify from across a room, because he'd developed a sense for it, the way you develop a sense for anything you've quietly been keeping a spreadsheet on.

The man — Theo, it turned out — was warm and funny and clearly fond of Kyu with zero weirdness, and stayed for one drink, and hugged ze over the bar on the way out, and told Will to keep ze humble, which was apparently the standard exit line issued to all of Will's predecessors.

Will waited until Theo was gone.

"So," he said. "Nadia. And Theo."

"Yeah." Kyu wiped the counter.

"Both of them are—"

"Exes, roughly. Ancient history. Both good." Ze restocked the limes, entirely at ease, not seeing where this was going because to zir there was nowhere for it to go.

"Right." Will did some final arithmetic. "So the back catalogue isn't just — extensive. It's also — you don't—" he gestured, vaguely, encompassing Nadia and Theo and whatever the full unsorted breadth of it implied. "You never filtered. By — anyone. At all."

Kyu looked at him, mild, genuinely not tracking the significance. "Filtered by what?"

"By — you know." Will felt, absurdly, like the one being weird about it, which was the correct instinct. "Gender. You've been with — it's just, whoever. Men, women, the whole—"

"Oh." Kyu blinked, like he'd pointed out that ze drank both coffee and tea. "Yeah. Sure. Whoever's interesting." Ze went back to the limes. "Why're you saying it like you cracked a code."

"I'm not — I'm just—" And Will realized, mid-sentence, that he had once again built a Discovery out of something that was, to Kyu, not information. He'd walked all the way up to a Moment, a big-reveal, help-me-understand-your-identity beat — and Kyu was standing on the other side of it holding a lime, waiting for him to get to a point, because from where ze stood there was no beat. There was nothing to reveal. There had never been anything to sort or announce or come out of.

"...You've genuinely never thought about it as a thing," Will said, slowly. "Have you."

"Thought about _what_ as a thing? Who I like?" Ze shrugged, the most natural motion in the world. "I like who I like. Some of them were men, some weren't, some were like me, whatever _me_ is. It's not a — Will, it's the same as the sex thing. Making it a category would be being weird about it. And I'm not—"

"—weird about it," Will finished, faintly. "Right."

"Right." Ze slid him a drink he hadn't ordered. "You keep doing this. You find some totally normal thing about me and you go all _forensic._ First the wall, now this. There's no case, neighbor. I'm not encrypted. I just don't sort stuff into the boxes you sort it into." The corner of the mouth. "You're the one with the boxes. Must be exhausting."

And Will — economy-class-guilt, takes-things-seriously, whole-serious-two-year-thing-in-his-twenties Will — sat with the fact that he had once again shown up to decode a mystery that was just a person being unhidden, and that the person didn't even understand it was supposed to be a mystery, which made it, somehow, the least mysterious thing about zir and the most.

"For the record," he said, "I've got a slightly narrower... range. Than you."

"I know. You told me. It's fine." Ze wasn't even looking up. "Doesn't need to match. You liked _me,_ not a category — you spent a month proving that through a wall before you even saw my face properly. You didn't know anything about me except the sound. Wasn't a type. Was just me." A beat, and then, dry, the closest ze'd come to making it tender: "Turned out fine. You kept me. I kept you. Nobody had to fill out a form."

"Nobody had to fill out a form," Will agreed, and drank his drink, and let the last dot connect itself quietly, into a picture that had, it turned out, never needed decoding — just asking, which he'd finally learned to do.

---
# 
Because Will had figured out, somewhere back around the flirting-regular incident, that jealousy was a bad look and a worse use of his time — and that he had, available to him, a far more satisfying response to a night of watching half the bar try their luck with the person he lived with.

He didn't say anything about it on the walk home. That was the discipline. He let Theo's replacement-of-the-week finish flirting, let Kyu handle it flat and easy the way ze always did, let the whole shift play out — and just filed it, quietly, and waited until the door was locked behind them.

"You were quiet tonight," Kyu observed, toeing off zir shoes. "No bodyguard routine. I'm almost disappointed—"

Will kissed zir mid-sentence, backed ze into the door, and the sentence didn't get finished.

Because this was the thing, the one clean advantage, the domain no amount of shameless or smug or unbotherable could touch: Will didn't get jealous anymore. Will got _even._ Every stranger who'd leaned across that bar tonight had gotten flat professional nothing from zir — the warm wall, the walked-nowhere. Will got to come home and dismantle the person underneath all that composure, and he was, by now, exceptionally good at it.

He took ze apart slow. He'd learned that patience was the whole weapon against someone this responsive — that ze was sensitive, hair-trigger, wide open, and that all his attentiveness had to do was find the reactions ze couldn't hide and _stay_ there, refusing to rush, refusing every push toward faster, until the running commentary ran dry and the flat menace of the third floor was reduced to gasping into his shoulder with a hand fisted in his shirt.

"You don't get jealous anymore," Kyu managed, wrecked, somewhere in it. "You just—"

"I just do this." He didn't speed up. "Better use of the feeling."

And then he stopped talking and made _thoroughly_ sure of it — took ze to pieces the way only he'd ever figured out how, merciless and precise and unhurried, until every sound the bar had heard fielded politely tonight came apart into something that had never once been for an audience. Ze was loud — ze was always loud, that was the entire origin of them — but this was the uncontrolled kind, past composure, past pride, past any thought of the wall or the floor or who might hear, just Will's name breaking apart in zir mouth over and over with nothing dressed over it.

He held ze right at the edge and kept ze there, attentive, ruthless, _enjoying_ it, until ze was shaking and swearing and begging in two of the five languages, and then he didn't let up, drove ze straight over it and through it and didn't stop until ze screamed — genuinely, helplessly, the sound tearing loose with zero control in it at all — and the third floor, one imagines, updated its records accordingly.

Afterward ze lay wrecked and boneless and unable, for a while, to form a complete sentence, which Will considered a personal triumph on par with anything he'd ever built at the company.

"...You're insufferable," Kyu finally rasped, eyes still shut, "about that."

"You're the one who was loud enough for the whole building." Will was radiant. "Ze at the bar got a nod and a comped drink. I got _that._" He kissed zir temple, smug beyond words. "I'm not jealous. Why would I be jealous. I know exactly what I go home with."

Kyu did not have a comeback. Kyu was, for once, entirely out of them — flat on zir back, catching zir breath, the one argument in the world ze couldn't win still ongoing and still, decisively, lost.

"...Do it again," ze said, after a minute, which was as close to _you win_ as ze was ever going to come.

Will did it again. The third floor did not file a complaint. The third floor had, frankly, come to expect this.

---
# 
They talked about moving, once. Seriously, even — Will floated it, a bigger place, thicker walls, the kind of upgrade a man with his money could sign for without his father's voice landing on him like weather, now that he'd more or less made peace with the money.

Kyu heard him out. Then said, flat: "The walls in a nicer place are thicker."

"That's — yeah, that's the _point_—"

"So the whole floor won't hear us."

"...That's also the point, Kyu, most people consider that a—"

"I'd miss it," ze said, and went back to zir book, and that was apparently that.

Because here was the thing neither of them had quite said out loud: the building had become a character. The thin walls that started the entire disaster had, over a year and change, turned the third floor into something closer to a very tolerant extended family — one that had heard every fight, every rescue, every burnt dinner, every loud night, and had simply _absorbed_ all of it into the texture of the place.

3B knew them. 3B had known them before they knew themselves — the old woman had winked at Will about "youthful energy" back when he was still pretending not to hear it, and she still left covered dishes at his door "in case, dear," and had recently upgraded to leaving _two,_ because she'd decided Kyu didn't eat enough, which was wrong, but the sentiment was sound. 2A had watched the whole arc and called them "the couple who found each other through the wall" to anyone who'd listen, which was mortifying and accurate. The downstairs couple had stopped making excuses about "too much food" and now just openly fed Will like a project.

And the noise — the endless, load-bearing noise — had become a thing the building was, against all reason, _fond_ of. Nobody complained. Nobody ever had. If anything the third floor treated the broadcast like weather they'd grown attached to: a little embarrassing, entirely reliable, and theirs.

"If we left," Will said slowly, coming around to it, "3B would be devastated."

"3B would _mourn._ zir decided we're zir grandchildren." Kyu turned a page. "And I'd have to find a new bar-walk home, and Mai knows this building, ze can pick the lobby lock now, that's _convenient_—"

"You taught your niece to break into our building."

"For _emergencies._ Focus." Ze set the book down. "Point is. Nice place, thick walls, no neighbors who know us, nobody who watched the whole thing happen. And for what. So strangers can't hear us." A shrug. "I'd rather have the family that came free with the drywall. Even if the drywall's a construction defect."

Will looked at zir — the person who'd carried his record crates up three flights before either of them knew what they were starting, in the building that had heard everything since — and understood he was never going to convince ze to leave, and that he didn't actually want to.

"We're staying," he said.

"We're staying." Kyu picked the book back up. "Buy the building, if you're so rich. Frost your own glass, leave everyone else's thin. Fix 3B's radiator, it clanks." A beat, the corner of the mouth. "Make yourself useful with all that guilt money."

And Will — who could, in fact, buy the building, and was already, quietly, deciding to — didn't say anything. He just filed it, the way ze filed things, and let the thin walls carry the ordinary sound of two people staying exactly where they'd been found.

The third floor, had it been asked, would have been relieved. But nobody asked. They just stayed, and the building kept them, and the wall kept talking, and everyone on the third floor — every last one of them — would have missed it if it ever stopped.

---
# The neighbor's party
The invitation came through 3B, which meant it was less an invitation than a summons.

"She said seven," Will reported, holding the little handwritten note that had been slid under their door. "She said bring nothing. She underlined _nothing._ Twice."

"That means bring wine," Kyu said, already finding the good bottle. "Underlining _nothing_ is how ze says _don't come empty-handed but let me pretend._ I know zir dialect."

Will did not know why they were going. Will suspected Kyu did — there was a particular flatness to how ze'd accepted, a _knowing_ something, the look of a person aware of a joke about to arrive. But Kyu wouldn't say, and asking Kyu to explain something ze'd decided to let unfold was a losing move, so Will put on a decent shirt and followed ze down the hall to 3B's door at seven, wine in hand.

It was not a dinner for four. It was the whole floor.

3B's apartment — bigger than theirs, thicker-walled, the woman had _seniority_ — was packed. 2A was there. The downstairs couple. A man from the fourth floor Will had never met but who greeted Kyu like an old friend. Someone had made an alarming amount of food. It had the warm, slightly conspiratorial air of a gathering that had happened many times before and had, for reasons Will was about to discover, finally decided to include the guests of honor.

"THERE they are," 3B announced, delighted, pressing drinks into their hands before the door had closed. "Sit, sit. We've been dying to have you properly. You two never come to things."

"We're private people," Kyu said, deadpan, and the entire room laughed, and Will understood, with a slow and total dawning horror, exactly what kind of evening this was going to be.

Because the third floor had a favorite topic. And the third floor had, at long last, gotten the subjects of that topic into a room.

"I have to ask," said the fourth-floor man, about ten minutes in, with the air of a scholar approaching a long-studied text, "is it _scheduled?_ Because I could set a watch. Tuesdays and Thursdays, and then Sundays are a wild card—"

"_Klaus,_" said 3B, scandalized, thrilled.

"What! We've all wondered. We've _discussed_ it—"

"We have a _whole_ thread," the downstairs woman admitted, gleeful. "In the building chat. It's called—" she caught her husband's eye, "—well. We're not going to say what it's called."

"You have a building chat," Will said, faintly.

"You're the most-discussed item on it," 2A said, patting his hand kindly. "By a wide margin. Above the parking dispute. Above the boiler."

And there it was: the reveal that the noise, the endless load-bearing broadcast, had not merely been _tolerated_ by the third floor. It had been _followed._ Analyzed. Made into a shared civic pastime, a thing the whole building had a fond and detailed opinion about, complete with a chat and a schedule theory and, apparently, a running commentary spanning the entire year-plus since Will moved in.

Kyu, of course, was unbothered. Kyu was _thriving,_ the one person in the room more comfortable with the topic than the people raising it.

"It's not scheduled," ze offered, flat, taking a sip of wine while Will slowly turned to stone beside zir. "That's just when we're both home."

The room _erupted._

"I KNEW it wasn't scheduled—"

"Pay up, Klaus, I said it was availability-based—"

"There's _money_ on this?" Will said, horrified.

"There's a small pool," 3B admitted, not sorry at all. "It's very good-natured. We're all very _fond,_ dear, that's the thing you have to understand—" she reached over and patted his cheek, "—we've watched the whole thing. From the beginning. The complaining, then the not-complaining, then—" she gestured, delicately, at the general concept of them, "—_this._ You're our favorite. You're like a show. We were all _rooting_ for you."

"You could hear us _rooting for us,_" Will said, weakly.

"We could hear _everything,_ dear. That's rather the point." She refilled his wine with the mercy of a woman who has decided to be gentle about it. "Kyu knew. Kyu's known for ages we all knew. Didn't you."

"Whole floor's known since the first week," Kyu confirmed, entirely at ease, monarch even here, in someone else's apartment, being discussed to zir face. "Will's the only one who thought it was a secret. He does that. Builds a whole private thing in his head that everyone can already see." Ze looked at Will, the corner of the mouth going up. "That's why I brought you. Time you found out you've been the building's favorite program for a year."

Will looked around the room — the fond faces, the wine, the betting pool, the whole tolerant conspiratorial family that had assembled out of thin drywall — and put his face briefly in his hands, and then, because there was genuinely nothing else to do, started to laugh.

"...Availability-based," he confirmed, to the room, defeated. "zir right. It's not scheduled."

The floor cheered. Money changed hands. 3B looked deeply satisfied. And Kyu leaned over, under the noise, and said into his ear with immense and insufferable warmth: "See? Told you we couldn't move. You'd break their hearts. They've got _bets_ on us."

They stayed till midnight. It was, everyone agreed, the best floor gathering in years — and on the walk back down the hall to their own thin-walled apartment, both of them a little drunk and thoroughly roasted by an entire building, Will took ze hand and accepted, fully and finally, that privacy had never once been on the menu, and that the whole warm ridiculous floor would, genuinely, have missed them if they'd ever thought to leave.

---
# 
The floor's fascination, Will came to understand over the course of that evening, wasn't really about the noise anymore. They'd made their peace with the noise a year ago. What genuinely captivated them — what kept the building chat alive, what Klaus wanted a _scholarly_ answer to — was Kyu. Specifically, how _casual_ ze was about all of it.

Because ze sat there in 3B's living room, being asked semi-directly about zir sex life by five neighbors and a betting pool, and answered every question with the flat, mild good humor of a person discussing zir commute. No blush. No deflection. No _performance_ of shamelessness even — that would've implied there was something to overcome. Ze just… answered. Availability-based. Sundays a wild card because of zir shifts. Fielded a follow-up about the walls with a shrug. And the room hung on every word, not because it was scandalous, but because none of them had ever _seen_ someone be that unbothered, up close, in captivity.

"How are you like this," the fourth-floor man, Klaus, finally asked, with open awe. "I would _die._ If my whole floor — I'd have to move continents. And you're just—" he gestured, helpless, "—_sitting there._ Having a nice wine."

"It's a nice wine," Kyu agreed.

"But how do you not—"

"There's nothing to be weird about, so I'm not weird about it." Ze said it the way ze said everything true, flat and final, and the whole room _marveled,_ because it was so simple and so completely beyond any of them. "You all heard a thing through a wall. Okay. Everybody does the thing. Now you know I do it too. That's not information. That's just Tuesday."

3B looked like she might frame the sentence.

And then, one by one, the fascination pivoted — the way it always eventually did, the way Will had watched it happen in every room ze walked into — from Kyu to _Will._ Because if Kyu was the marvel, Will was the _miracle:_ the ordinary, mortal, capable-of-embarrassment man who had chosen to _live_ with the marvel, across the thinnest wall in the building, on purpose, for over a year.

"_You,_" 2A said to Will, with a new and pitying respect, "are a _saint._"

"I'm not—"

"You are. You live with _that._" She gestured at Kyu, fondly. "The rest of us just _hear_ it. You have to be in the _room._ You have to sit across from someone who tells a floor gathering zir 'not weird about it' and just — _cope._ Every day. Klaus is right, we'd all crumble. You _thrive._"

"He does not thrive," Kyu said. "He turns colors. He's turning a color right now."

"I'm _fine_—"

"He's the color of the wine," Klaus confirmed, delighted. "See, THIS is the fascinating part. zir immovable and _he's_ still bashful after a _year,_ how does that _work,_ how do you two even—"

"Balance," Kyu said serenely. "I don't get embarrassed, he does enough for both of us. It's efficient." Ze patted Will's scarlet cheek in front of the entire floor. "Somebody has to carry the shame in the relationship. He volunteered. Isn't he great?"

The room agreed, warmly, that he was great — and Will sat there being simultaneously admired as a saint and roasted as a blusher, the designated shame-bearer for a household run by the most unbotherable person any of them had ever met, and understood that this, too, was simply his role now, floor-wide, on the record, in the chat.

"To Will," 3B said, raising her glass, "who lives with it."

"To Will," the floor echoed, with genuine affection and real admiration, "who lives with it."

Will raised his own glass, defeated, scarlet, and beloved. "I do," he admitted, "live with it."

Beside him, Kyu — the culprit, the marvel, casual as anything about the whole business — looked entirely, insufferably pleased, and drank to him too.

---
# 
"Hehe," said Kyu, actually out loud, apparently having decided that watching Will slowly perish in front of the whole third floor was the single greatest entertainment the building had produced all year.

"I'm dying," Will informed the room. "For the record. In case anyone's keeping minutes. This is where I died."

"He's dramatic," Kyu told 3B, fondly, patting his knee. "He does this."

"I am _appropriately_ reactive to being the subject of a _neighborhood betting pool_—"

"Hehe," Kyu said again, delighted, sipping the nice wine, watching him go redder.

And that was the whole dynamic, laid bare for the entire floor to witness and catalogue in the building chat: one of them incandescently, insufferably pleased, and the other one dying — the marvel and the mortal, the culprit and the coper, the one who'd never been embarrassed a day in zir life and the man who'd volunteered to carry the embarrassment for both of them and was, at this exact moment, cashing out.

"You're _enjoying_ this," Will accused.

"I'm enjoying this _so much._" No shame, none, radiant with it. "Best night out we've had. Whole floor loves us, there's _money_ on us, and you're the color of a stop sign. Where else am I getting this."

"I would like to leave now. While I still have some dignity."

"You have no dignity. You lost it in the office lobby months ago. In my shirt." Kyu stood, though, taking pity, hauling him up by the hand — provision even here, even mid-torment. "Come on. Say goodnight to the floor. They'll be crushed."

Will said goodnight to the floor. The floor was, in fact, crushed, and made him promise to come to the next one, and 3B pressed a covered dish into his hands "for the walk home," which was ten feet, and Klaus shook his hand with the solemn respect of one man acknowledging another's endurance.

Out in the hall, door closed behind them, Will exhaled the exhale of a survivor.

"I hate that you loved that."

"I _loved_ that." Kyu was still grinning, walking ze fingers along the small of his back toward their own thin-walled door. "Hehe."

"Stop saying hehe—"

"Hehe," said Kyu, one more time, purely to finish him, and unlocked the door, and pulled zir dying, blushing, thoroughly beloved boyfriend inside — where, the third floor would note with fond and professional interest, the programming resumed shortly after, right on schedule, availability-based.

---
# 
It was late, and Kyu was asleep, and Will — who had gotten up for water — came back to find the most unbotherable person on the third floor doing the one thing ze would never consent to being seen doing: existing completely off-guard.

Except even that ze couldn't do normally.

Because Kyu, dead asleep, face slack, wrecked black hair everywhere, the grey-white strands catching the streetlight — was _smug._ Somehow. Even unconscious. There was a specific set to zir mouth, the corner just barely tipped, the exact expression ze wore right after winning something, and ze was wearing it _in zir sleep,_ aimed at nobody, apropos of nothing, like ze was out cold and still beating someone at something in there.

Will stood at the foot of the bed and looked at it for a while.

It was, he decided, the single most Kyu thing he had ever witnessed. He'd chased the "was ze ever normal" question for weeks — dug for a before, a baseline, a version of this person that predated the menace — and here was the answer, delivered by zir own sleeping face. There was no normal Kyu. There was no off-switch. Ze went to _sleep_ and the smug just kept running on its own, a background process, factory-installed, no reason and no origin, all the way down.

It was also unbearably cute, which he would never say to zir face and could barely think without cringing at himself.
