So he did the only reasonable thing. He got his phone. Very quietly. And he took a picture.

The first one had flash by accident — he fumbled it — and he froze, heart pounding, but Kyu only made a small displeased sound, turned zir face further into the pillow, and _smug-frowned,_ which was even better, and Will nearly died trying not to laugh. He turned the flash off. He took several more. The sleeping smug. The smug-frown. One where zir hand had migrated across the mattress to where Will should've been, reaching, unconscious, and Will's chest did the thing so hard he had to sit down on the edge of the bed.

He was reviewing his haul, deeply pleased with himself, when a voice came from the pillow, thick with sleep and entirely without opening its eyes:

"...You're taking pictures of me."

Will froze. "No."

"I heard the flash. Twice." One eye cracked open, unimpressed, and even _that_ was smug. "You're photographing me in my sleep like a _weirdo._"

"You were—" Will was caught, phone in hand, no defense. "You do the face. Even asleep. You're _smug_ while _unconscious,_ Kyu, it's — I had to document it, it's a public health matter—"

"Delete them."

"Absolutely not. This is evidence. This is the answer to the whole question." He was grinning, holding the phone out of reach on reflex. "You've never been normal. Not for one second. You _sleep_ smug. There's no before. I've been looking for a normal version of you and it turns out even your _unconscious mind_ is winning an argument—"

"Give me the phone—"

"You reached for me, too," Will added, before he could stop himself, quieter. "In your sleep. Your hand went looking for me. That one's my favorite."

Kyu stopped. The grab for the phone paused. And in the dark Will watched the rare thing happen — the smug, awake now, faltering, because _that_ wasn't a fact ze could be casual about the way ze was casual about everything else. Being caught reaching. Unconscious, undefended, wanting him even asleep.

"...That's not—" ze started.

"It's in the file now," Will said gently. "The good file. You did it on your own. No witnesses but me. Can't take it back."

Kyu looked at him for a long moment. Then, defeated, ze flopped back down onto the pillow, pulled Will down with zir by the wrist, and settled him into exactly the spot zir sleeping hand had been reaching for.

"...Keep the reaching one," ze conceded, into his hair, grudging, half-asleep already. "Delete the smug ones. I look stupid."

"You look _smug._"

"Same thing on me, apparently." A pause. The smug, audibly, creeping back. "...Was I winning? In the picture."

"You were absolutely winning."

"Good." And ze was out again inside a minute, face smug, hand now curled around the one thing it had been looking for — and Will lay there in the dark, wide awake, holding a phone full of evidence that the creature was insufferable all the way to the bone, and could not, for the life of him, stop smiling about it.

---
# Catnip
Will found out by accident, the way he found out everything real about zir — sideways, unannounced, catching a behavior ze'd never once explain.

It was the shirt thing, at first. His shirts kept migrating. He'd go looking for one and find it gone, and then find it _on Kyu,_ who wore it with the flat entitlement of someone who'd decided the geography of _yours_ and _mine_ was negotiable, and who, when questioned, said only "it was there" — which explained nothing. Will had assumed it was a size thing, or a comfort thing, or just more of the casual boundary-dissolving that came with living together.

Then he caught zir doing it.

He came home early. Quiet. And found Kyu on the couch, half-asleep, wearing one of Will's shirts — and, in a moment ze plainly believed was private, turning zir face into the collar and just… breathing it in. Slow. Unguarded. The flat face gone soft, the whole unbotherable menace of the third floor with its nose in Will's laundry like it was the best thing in the apartment.

Will stood in the doorway and made the mistake of making a sound.

Kyu's head came up. The soft thing vanished behind the flat one so fast it was almost impressive.

"You're home early."

"You were smelling my shirt."

"I was _not_—"

"You had your whole face in it. You were _inhaling._" Will was delighted, crossing the room, a man who'd caught the uncatchable. "Kyu. Were you _sniffing my laundry._"

"It's a comfortable shirt—"

"You have a _drawer_ of comfortable shirts, they're all technically mine now anyway, you don't smell _those_—" He sat down beside zir, grinning. "You like how I _smell._"

And here was the tell: Kyu, who could discuss zir sex life with a betting pool of neighbors without a flicker, who was immovable about anything that actually mattered — went, faintly, pink. Because this wasn't sex, which was Tuesday. This was _tender,_ which was the one register ze had no armor for, and getting caught being tender was worse than any confession.

"...It's not strong," ze muttered, defensive, aggrieved, cornered into honesty. "You don't wear anything. No cologne, no — you just smell like _you._ Warm. It's—" ze stopped, hating this, "—it's _nice,_ okay. It's a nice smell. Don't make it weird."

"You're the one with your face in my shirt—"

"It's like—" and ze gestured, helpless, reaching for it, "—you know how a cat gets, with the — the plant, the—"

"Catnip." Will was going to remember this for the rest of his life. "You're telling me my _smell_ is—"

"I regret saying it. I regret all of it. I'm getting up—"

But Will got an arm around zir before ze could, and pulled ze in, and Kyu — betrayed, mortified, thoroughly outed — resisted for exactly two seconds before giving up entirely and putting zir face in the crook of his neck, right where the smell apparently was, and going boneless with a small defeated exhale that Will felt all the way down.

"...This is humiliating," ze informed his collarbone.

"It's the cutest thing you've ever done."

"It's a _chemical_ thing. It's not romantic. It's basically pheromones, it's biology, don't—"

"You steal my shirts to smell me when I'm not home." Will was radiant, one hand coming up into zir hair, holding ze there. "You're a menace and a marvel and good at everything and you get _catnip-brained_ over how I smell. That's the best thing I know about you. That's going straight in the file."

"There's no file—"

"There's a whole file. This is the front page now." He pressed a kiss to the top of zir head. "You want the shirt, just take the shirt. I'll stop washing them so much."

Kyu went still. Then, muffled, grudging, giving up the last of it: "...Don't switch soaps. The one you use. Keep using it."

"I'll keep using it."

"And don't tell Ronan."

"I'm telling Ronan immediately."

"_Will—_"

But ze didn't move, didn't pull away, just stayed exactly there with zir face against the warm nothing-smell of him — catnip-still, cornered, content — while Will held ze and grinned at the ceiling and added it to the long, growing, insufferable list of ways the creature had, without ever once meaning to, given zirself completely away.

---
# The bar's event

Kyu did not volunteer. That was the first thing ze wanted on the record, loudly and repeatedly, in the days leading up to it.

The bar was doing an event — some anniversary thing, live music, a stage set up in the back, the regulars invited, a whole production. And when the question of _performances_ came up, Kyu's coworkers had, with the swift coordinated cruelty of people who genuinely liked ze, voted zir onto the bill. Unanimously. Before ze could leave the room.

"I didn't agree to this."

"You were outvoted," said the other bartender, serene. "It's democratic. We took a vote."

"I wasn't _present_ for the vote—"

"That's why you lost. Should've been present." A pause. "Everyone wants to see it. You know you can. You've never once done it _for_ us. Two years. Time to pay rent, Kyu."

Because — and this was the thing Will only fully assembled in the days that followed, from Ronan and the coworkers and Kyu's own furious non-denials — Kyu could, of course, do this too. Naturally. Effortlessly. Ze was musical the way ze was everything, had apparently played some instrument since forever, sang in that low unbothered register, and had spent two years pointedly _not_ doing it at work, deflecting every request, keeping this one thing back — which only made the entire staff more determined to finally, _finally_ see it.

"You _sing,_" Will said, at home, delighted beyond reason. "You've been holding out on me. You sing and you never—"

"It's not a _thing._"

"Ronan says you used to do open mic nights at the office bar—"

"_Ronan_ needs to lose my number—"

"—and that you were _good,_ and that you stopped because people started _coming to watch,_ which is—" Will was gleeful, connecting it, "—that's the _compliment_ allergy, isn't it. You stopped singing in public because people liked it and told you so and you couldn't _stand_ it—"

Kyu's silence confirmed the entire diagnosis.

"So you'll broadcast your sex life to a whole floor," Will marveled, "you'll tell 3B's dinner party it's availability-based, you'll do _anything_ shameless — but you won't get on a stage and sing, because someone might clap and mean it."

"Different thing," Kyu muttered, which was as close to _yes, exactly_ as ze'd come.

The night of, Will got a seat up front, because he was not missing this for anything, and neither was anyone else — the coworkers, the regulars, Ronan (who had flown back into town _specifically,_ claiming a conference that did not exist), Lena (somehow), the whole warm ecosystem that had accreted around zir. The room was packed. The pressure was enormous. Kyu looked, for the first time in living memory, genuinely _cornered_ — not by a challenge, ze could handle challenges, but by the prospect of being _seen_ doing something and having people be sincere about it.

And then ze got up on the stage, flat-faced, deeply put-upon, and picked up the instrument, and the room went quiet.

And ze was, of course, extraordinary.

Not showy. Ze didn't perform _at_ anyone — that would've been the shameless register, the one ze had unlimited access to. This was the other thing, the guarded thing, low and unhurried and _good_ in the way that made the whole packed bar stop talking, stop drinking, just listen. Five languages in that voice somewhere, the same total competence ze brought to everything, except here it came with something ze couldn't flatten out — because you cannot sing that well and stay armored, the singing _is_ the armor coming off, and everyone in the room knew it, and Kyu knew they knew it, and did it anyway, cornered into vulnerability by a democratic vote ze never agreed to.

Will forgot to breathe for most of it.

When it ended there was a beat of silence — and then the bar _erupted,_ the coworkers loudest of all, a room full of people who'd waited two years roaring for the person who never gave them this — and Kyu, on stage, exposed, applauded to the rafters and _meaning_ it aimed at zir from every direction, did the thing ze physically could not help: went red, ducked zir head, and got off that stage as fast as dignity allowed, fleeing the sincerity like it burned.

Ze came straight to Will's table and sat down hard and put zir face in zir hands.

"Never again," ze said, muffled.

"That was—" Will's voice wasn't working right. "Kyu. That was—"

"Don't."

"I'm not going to say it if it's going to make you leave—"

"It's going to make me leave. Everyone's already—" ze gestured, agonized, at the whole cheering room still buzzing about it, "—they're _saying things._ Nice things. To my _face._ This was a _trap,_ the vote was a _trap_—"

Will pried one of zir hands down, gently, and held it, and did not say _you were incredible,_ because ze couldn't take it, and instead said the only thing that would land:

"You didn't run. Until the end. You stayed up there the whole song." Quiet, just for zir, under the noise. "You knew they'd clap and mean it and you did it anyway. That's the bravest I've ever seen you."

Kyu peeked at him over zir other hand.

"...It was Ronan's idea, the vote," ze muttered. "I know it was. He's dead."

"He flew in for it. From a fake conference." Will grinned. "He's not dead, he's your best friend and he wanted to see it, same as all of them. Same as me." He squeezed zir hand. "You gave the whole room something you never give anyone. And now you have to sit here and let them be happy about it. That's the price. Menace tax."

Kyu groaned into the table.

But ze stayed. Red-faced, mortified, thoroughly seen — ze stayed, and let the coworkers come by and say the nice things, and survived every one of them with Will's hand in zir and zir face on fire, which was, from the person who'd rather do anything than be sincerely admired, the entire white flag.

"...Was I good," ze asked Will later, small, on the walk home, the way ze always eventually asked.

"You were the best thing I've ever heard."

"...Okay." A pause. The smug trying, weakly, to reassemble, and not quite managing it. "Don't tell them I asked."

"Never," Will lied, warmly, already composing the text to Ronan, and walked zir home under the thin-walled sky, insufferable creature and all.

---
# The spike
It was a guy named Marcus, and he'd been circling for a while before Will understood what he was watching.

Marcus was a regular-adjacent — knew the bar, knew the coworkers, one of those people who's around enough to feel entitled to things. And he'd clocked, the way people occasionally did, that Kyu was generous. That ze covered tabs, fixed problems, gave without keeping score. Most people received that gratefully. Marcus saw a _resource._

It started small — a drink on the house he asked for like it was owed, a favor, then another. Kyu handled the early ones flat and easy, the way ze handled everything, and Will, watching from his stool, felt something prickle before he could name it. Because there's a difference between someone _receiving_ Kyu's provision and someone _pulling_ on it, and Marcus was pulling.

Then the ask got bigger. Marcus leaned on the bar one night, familiar, and floated it: he was in a spot, needed to borrow some money, and Kyu was good for it, right, ze was always helping people out, what was a little more — and he said it with a smile that had _pressure_ in it, the smile of a man who'd decided the generosity was a weakness he could stand on.

"You help everybody," Marcus said, easy, entitled. "Come on. You're not gonna help _me?_ After all this time? Kinda cold, honestly."

And Will watched it happen — watched the thing he'd never seen before.

The warmth didn't heat up into anger. That would've been a normal person. The warmth just… went _out._ Switched off, clean, like a room going dark. The flat face ze wore all the time was suddenly a different flat — not the unbothered kind, not the deadpan. Something colder underneath it, older, the face of someone who had spent a life reading exactly this move and had never once fallen for it.

"No," Kyu said.

Just that. No heat, no lecture. Ze set down the glass ze was polishing and looked at Marcus with an attention that made the man shift on his stool.

"I help who I _decide_ to help," ze went on, level, quiet, and somehow that quiet was worse than any raised voice. "That's mine. I pick. You don't get to reframe it as something I owe you because you noticed I'm generous with other people. That's not how it works. That was never how it works." A beat. "You've been testing the edges of this for a month. Small stuff, then bigger. Seeing how far the free ride goes. I clocked it the first week. I let it run because it was cheap and I was curious how far you'd take it." The corner of the mouth, but nothing warm in it. "This far, apparently. Borrowing money. Bold."

"I didn't—" Marcus started, the smile cracking. "Come on, it's not like that—"

"It's exactly like that. You mistook _kind_ for _soft._" Ze leaned in, unhurried, and dropped zir voice to something Will felt in his spine from three stools away. "Those aren't the same thing. People make that mistake with me sometimes. It goes badly for them, and then it doesn't happen again." Ze straightened. "You're done here. Not banned — I'm not going to make a scene, that's beneath both of us. But the tab's closed, the favors are over, and you and I are strangers from now on. Finish your drink. Don't ask me for another."

And that was it. No shouting. No drama. Marcus sat there with the entire architecture of his plan collapsed, realizing far too late that he'd spent a month probing someone who'd seen the whole thing from move one and simply let him hang himself. He finished his drink in silence and left, and Kyu went back to polishing the glass like nothing had happened, warmth already flowing back into zir the second the threat cleared the door — 3B's grandchild again, Mai's cool uncle again, the floor's beloved menace again, the switch flipped back as clean as it had flipped off.

Will was staring.

"...What," Kyu said, catching it.

"I've never seen that version of you. The whole temperature went—" He didn't have the word. "I didn't know you had it."

"Course I have it." Ze restocked the limes, calm. "You think I got this far being _nice?_ Nice is a choice. It's not a door." A shrug. "People who think the giving means they can take find out. Then I go back to pleasant. No point staying mad."

"That was terrifying." A beat. "It was also kind of magnificent."

Kyu glanced over, and the warm smug came back, the one that was for him. "Yeah? You into that?"

"I'm — a little into that, apparently. I'm processing."

"Noted." Ze slid him a drink he hadn't ordered, the switch fully flipped back, 3B's grandkid again, the floor's menace again. "Drink up. Stop looking at me like that. We're in public."

He kept looking anyway.

---
# 
The bar after close was its own kind of quiet — chairs up, lights low, the front locked, just the two of them and the hum of the coolers. Kyu was cashing out the register. Will was supposed to be helping and was instead watching ze do the Marcus thing over again in his memory, the temperature flip, the flat cold competence of it, and apparently it showed, because Kyu didn't even look up.

"You're doing the face."

"I'm not."

"You've been doing it since Marcus. You went weird and quiet and you keep _looking._" Ze closed the drawer, turned around, leaned back against the bar, and there it was — ze'd clocked exactly what was wrong with him and had decided, in the way ze decided everything, to do something about it. "We talked about this. The looking. In public."

"We're not in public. It's closed."

"Front's glass, neighbor. Street's right there." Ze tilted zir head, the smug arriving slow. "Anyone walks by, they see straight in." A beat, entirely unbothered, testing the edge just to watch him. "Doesn't bother me. I'm not the one who dies of embarrassment getting complimented in a lobby." The corner of the mouth. "You, though. This'd finish you."

"You're doing this on purpose."

"I'm doing _nothing._ I'm cashing out." But ze wasn't moving, and the look ze was giving him had a dare folded into it, and Will — who had spent a year getting bolder, who now won the one room, who had watched zir switch that warmth off like a blade and then _aim_ it back at him warm — felt the last of his economy-class restraint lose a very short argument with the rest of him.

He crossed the space. Backed ze into the bar. kissed zir hard, and Kyu made a low pleased sound and kissed him back just as hard, hands already in his shirt, and they went down behind the counter half-tangled, out of the sightline of the glass, onto the mats where ze pulled shots all night — and there was a giddy, illicit charge to it that had nothing to do with the apartment, nothing to do with the safe locked door, everything to do with the street ten feet away and the glass and the possibility.

And here was the problem, the delicious problem, the whole reason it worked: Kyu was _loud._ Kyu was always loud — it was the origin of them, it was the thing the entire third floor had a betting pool about — and Kyu was congenitally, gleefully incapable of being quiet about this, and they were _behind a bar,_ on a _street,_ with a _glass front._

"You have to be quiet," Will breathed, against zir throat, already knowing it was hopeless.

"I don't do quiet." Ze said it like a fact about the weather, no intention of trying. "You know I don't do quiet. That's your—"

Will put his hand over zir mouth.

And felt zir go _still_ — a hitch of surprise, the flat menace of the third floor caught off-guard by the one thing that actually solved the problem — and then felt zir, against his palm, _grin._ Because ze found it funny. Because being muffled, cornered, physically kept quiet behind zir own bar while Will did exactly what he was good at was, apparently, a delight — a new game, a fun one, ze the sensitive hair-trigger disaster and Will the only one keeping them from getting caught, his hand the sole thing between zir and the whole street knowing.

"Shh," Will said, low, half-laughing, half-losing his mind. "Someone's going to walk by. You have to — Kyu, I _swear_—"

Ze couldn't answer. That was the point. Ze made a sound _into_ his hand instead, muffled, helpless, zir eyes bright with the mischief of it over the top of his palm, thoroughly enjoying the fact that Will had found the one method that worked and was now _committed to it,_ hand clamped over the mouth of a person who broadcast to three floors for a living, keeping ze quiet by main force while the coolers hummed and the street stayed, mercifully, empty.

He was attentive as ever, and merciless, and _fast_ about it this time out of pure necessity — no drawing it out, not here, not with the glass — and Kyu came apart under his hand exactly the way ze came apart everywhere, except now every sound of it got caught in his palm, muffled to nothing, the loudest person in the building reduced to shaking, biting-back, silent-except-for-Will's-hand wreckage, and finding the whole thing _hilarious_ even as it destroyed zir, laughing and gasping into his hand in equal measure.

When it was over zir lay there on the bar mats, wrecked and breathless and grinning like an absolute menace, and Will finally lifted his hand, cautious.

"...You okay?"

"That," Kyu said, hoarse, delighted, "was the most fun I've had in months. You _panicked._ The whole time. You had your hand on my mouth like a — you were _terrified_ someone would see—"

"Because they _would_ have—"

"Nobody saw. Front's angled wrong for the mats, I've known that for two years." Ze was insufferable, flushed, thoroughly pleased. "You were never in danger. I just let you think you were, because you get all—" ze gestured, at his heaving, mortified, still-half-panicked state, "—_that._ It's cute. The muffling thing's new. I liked the muffling thing. We're keeping the muffling thing."

"You _knew_ we couldn't be seen and you let me—"

"You needed the danger. You're the shame-bearer, remember. You can't just _enjoy_ it, you need stakes." Ze pulled him down by the collar, warm, catnip-close, entirely un-fazed by having just been thoroughly taken apart behind zir own workplace. "Relax. Nobody caught us. Nobody was ever going to. And now you know you've got a hand that shuts me up, which is more than the whole third floor's ever managed." The corner of the mouth. "Lock up. Take me home. Do it again where I'm _allowed_ to be loud."

Will locked up. His hands were still shaking, mostly from adrenaline, a little from the rest of it. Kyu strolled out into the empty street entirely composed, the picture of innocence, and Will followed the loudest, most shameless, most insufferable person on the third floor home through the dark — thoroughly rattled, thoroughly gone, and already, God help him, thinking about the muffling thing.

---
# 
They did not, it turned out, make it home in any dignified sense.

The bar was maybe fifteen minutes from the apartment on foot. It should have been a simple walk. It was not, because Will was still lit up from the adrenaline and the _rest_ of it, and Kyu was strolling beside him being deliberately, insufferably innocent — hands in pockets, face serene, radiating the specific calm of someone who had just gotten exactly what ze wanted and knew precisely what ze was doing to the man beside zir.

"You keep speeding up," Kyu observed, pleasantly, four blocks in. "Something on your mind, neighbor?"

"Shut up."

"I'm just _walking._ It's a nice night." Ze was not walking any faster. Ze was, if anything, slowing down, purely to watch Will vibrate. "You're practically jogging. Where's the fire."

"You know exactly where the fire is—"

"I do," Kyu agreed, warm, unhurried, catnip-close as ze bumped zir shoulder into his. "I lit it. Behind the bar. With my—"

Will kissed zir against a lamppost, because he couldn't get another block without it. Kyu made a low, delighted sound and kissed him back, and for a second the fifteen-minute walk threatened to become a whole other public incident right there on the sidewalk — until Will pulled back, breathing hard, and physically took zir hand and started _hauling._

"_Home,_" he said. "Now. Where you're allowed to be loud and I don't have to—"

"You liked the hand thing."

"I _loved_ the hand thing, which is exactly why we're going _home_ where I can — Kyu, _walk faster,_ I am _begging_—"

They speed-walked. Then, somewhere around the halfway mark, dignity fully abandoned, they outright _hurried_ — Kyu laughing now, actually laughing, letting Will drag ze by the hand through the dark streets like they were being chased, the most composed person on the third floor undone into breathless giggling by how absolutely _gone_ Will was, how the guilt-ridden CEO who died of a compliment in a lobby was now towing zir home at a near-run because he could not survive another fifteen minutes of restraint.

They hit the building's front door. Will fumbled the lock. Kyu, unhelpful, pressed up against his back and said something into his ear that made him drop the keys entirely.

"You're not _helping_—"

"I'm helping a _lot._" Ze picked the keys up — and, because ze could, because Mai had learned it from zir, had the lobby door open in about four seconds flat without them. "There. Faster. You're welcome."

The elevator was too slow. They took the stairs. Three flights, at speed, Kyu keeping easy pace while Will's lungs filed a complaint, both of them arriving at the third-floor landing flushed and breathless and laughing and entirely undone — and Will got their door open on the second try, and got ze inside, and got the door _shut,_ and the second it clicked closed the whole careful restraint of the last fifteen minutes came off at once.

"_Finally,_" Kyu breathed, already pulling him in, "no glass, no street, no hand over my—"

"Be as loud as you want," Will said, against zir mouth, walking ze backward toward the bedroom, done waiting, done restraining, done being the shame-bearer for one whole night. "Whole floor's home. Give them a show."

"_Now_ you're getting it—"

The door swung shut on the rest.

And the third floor — 3B, Klaus, 2A, the downstairs couple, the whole fond conspiratorial ecosystem — stirred, one by one, at the familiar sound of the programming resuming, later than usual and louder than usual, and rolled over, and smiled into their pillows, and did not, any of them, file a single complaint.

Availability-based, the building chat would note in the morning. Unscheduled. A strong showing.

---
# The old IT time (backstory)

The architecture pod ran along the east windows, and by 10 a.m. the light came in hard enough that Kyu had zir screen tilted against the glare and a coffee going cold at zir elbow, three tickets deep and entirely elsewhere.

The floor around zir was conducting its usual quiet theater, which ze did not perceive.

At the platform pod, Marcus stood up. It was 10:07. Marcus's coffee was, by any objective measure, still full. Marcus picked up the full coffee anyway and began the long, casual, architecturally-unnecessary walk toward the kitchen, which ran — as it happened, as it always happened — directly past the east windows.

"Morning," Marcus said, slowing.

"Mm," said Kyu, not looking up. Ze was refactoring something and the something was wrong and ze had opinions about why.

Marcus lingered a beat past the natural length of a _morning,_ found nothing to attach to it, and continued to the kitchen to pour out a full coffee and make a fresh one he didn't want. Two desks over, someone watched this happen and made a small note on a shared document they would deny existed.

Ronan, beside zir, did not look up from his own screen. "That's the fourth time this week he's done the coffee walk."

"Who," said Kyu.

"Marcus. Platform." Ronan tipped his head. "He times it to your water breaks. There's a — never mind. You want the merge or not, it's broken again."

"It's broken because Devlin keeps force-pushing to the shared branch like an _animal_—" Kyu pulled the merge up, and zir whole attention went into it, and the floor's theater continued around zir unwatched. A junior from the design side found a reason to ask architecture a question that could have been an email. A senior engineer named Present detoured past on his way to a meeting in the opposite direction. None of it registered. Ze was in the branch.

"Devlin," Kyu called across the pod, not unkindly, "come here a second."

Devlin came. Devlin was twenty-four and had been force-pushing to the shared branch like an animal, and Devlin arrived at Kyu's desk already slightly pink, because being summoned by _ze_ did that to people, and Kyu — who did not know this, who had never once known this — turned in zir chair and looked up at him with the full flat weight of zir attention and both mismatched eyes and said:

"You keep clobbering the shared branch. Here—" ze pulled the history up, patient, "—look. This is your push. This is what it ate. See it?"

Devlin did not, immediately, see it. Devlin was experiencing the specific short-circuit of being looked at directly by the most striking person in the building while being gently corrected, and his brain had allocated most of its resources elsewhere, and the git history was not currently among his priorities.

"...Yeah," Devlin said. "No. Yeah."

"You don't see it."

"I—" Devlin's ears were fully red now. "Can you — show me again?"

Kyu showed him again, slower, with the total unhurried patience ze brought to anything ze'd decided to be good at, entirely unaware that the slowness was making it _worse,_ that Devlin would remember this two-minute git tutorial for a year, that across the pod two people had stopped typing to watch Devlin quietly disintegrate.

"—so branch off first, then push. Don't force it. Yeah?"

"Yeah," Devlin managed. "Thanks. Sorry. Thank you."

"S'fine." And Kyu had already turned back to zir screen, done, moving on, the interaction closed and filed, while Devlin walked back to his desk on unsteady legs and Ronan, beside zir, exhaled the long breath of a man who had watched this exact thing happen approximately two hundred times.

"You destroyed that kid," Ronan said, mildly.

"I _helped_ that kid. He's force-pushing to shared. Somebody had to—"

"He's going to be thinking about that git lesson at three in the morning, Kyu."

"Then he'll remember not to force-push. Good. That's the outcome I wanted." Ze reached for the cold coffee, drank it without noticing it was cold, and went back into the branch, genuinely, completely, magnificently unaware that ze had just done the thing ze did every single day — walked through the middle of a floor full of people quietly losing their minds over zir and come out the other side thinking about git.

At noon someone brought donuts. There were exactly enough. Kyu took none, because Kyu had already, at some point that morning, quietly refilled the communal coffee supply that had been running low for a week, and said nothing about it, and would deny it if asked. Nobody had seen ze do it. The coffee was simply, one day, not a problem anymore, the way problems around zir tended to quietly resolve and get attributed to no one.

"The coffee's full again," someone said, mystified, in the kitchen.

"Weird," said Ronan, who knew exactly who, and had learned long ago that saying so was a good way to get kicked under a desk.

And Kyu worked through lunch, and fixed two more things ze wasn't asked to fix, and left at six on the dot without a word to anyone — and the floor exhaled, and reset, and began quietly bracing itself for the identical devastation of tomorrow, which would arrive, as ze always did, entirely by accident, thinking about nothing but the merge.

---
# 
It was 1 a.m. and the deploy was on fire, and Ronan was the only other person stupid enough to still be in the building.

The office at that hour was a different place — half the lights on a timer, the floor gone quiet and blue-lit, just Ronan at his desk swearing at a failed release and, three desks down, the one other glow: Kyu, who had not gone home, because Kyu did not appear to operate on a schedule that included going home.

"You're still here," Ronan said, mostly to the room.

"So are you." Ze didn't look up. "Yours is the thing that keeps paging, right? The platform release?"

"It's — yeah. It's dying. I've been at it two hours, I can't find—"

"It's not your code." Kyu was already rolling zir chair over, uninvited, which nobody did, which Ronan was too tired to be weird about. "It's the migration. It ran halfway and stalled and now the schema's in a state nothing expects. Your code's fine. It's downstream of a mess somebody left." Ze pulled his screen toward zir without asking. "Move. You've been staring at it too long, you can't see it anymore."

And Ronan — exhausted, defeated, past the point of pride — moved. And watched zir find in about four minutes the thing he'd been drowning in for two hours, not with any flourish, just the flat total competence ze brought to everything, narrating it in the bored voice of someone reading a menu.

"There. That's your stall. Roll the migration back, re-run it clean, your release goes out. You didn't break anything. Devlin did, probably. It's always Devlin."

"...It's always Devlin," Ronan agreed, weakly, staring at the fix.

Ze rolled back to zir own desk. That should have been it. Colleague helps colleague, late night, war story. Except Ronan, watching ze go back to whatever ze'd been doing at 1 a.m. for no deadline he was aware of, said the thing that cracked it open — not a rehearsed thing, just tired honesty:

"Why are you always still here? It's one in the morning. You don't have a — is there a deadline? Nobody's making you stay."

Kyu paused. Considered the question like it was mildly novel.

"Nothing to go home to that's better than this," ze said, flat, no self-pity in it, just fact. "It's quiet. I get more done. Nobody does the—" ze gestured, vaguely, at the empty floor, "—the thing everyone does during the day. The weird walking-past thing. At night it's just the work."

And Ronan — who had, unlike the rest of the floor, actually been paying attention to the _person_ — heard what ze didn't say. That ze knew. Not about the crushes, ze'd never clock those. But ze knew the floor kept ze at a distance, treated ze as an event, never quite talked to zir straight. Ze'd read a building full of people too rattled to be normal at zir as a building full of people who found ze _odd,_ and had quietly decided nighttime was easier because at night there was nobody to not-talk-to zir.

Ronan sat with that for a second.

"That's grim, man," he said, but warm, casual, the way you'd rib a friend, not the careful way everyone else spoke to zir. "You're telling me you stay till 1 a.m. because it's less awkward than lunch."

"Lunch is very awkward," Kyu confirmed, dead serious.

"Lunch is awkward because you spend the whole time talking about _database migrations,_" Ronan said, before he could think about it. "You know that, right? People ask you to lunch and you give them a — a _seminar._ That's why it's weird. Not — it's not _them._ It's the migrations."

There was a pause. Kyu looked at him — really looked, the flat face doing something Ronan hadn't seen it do, a kind of surprised recalibration.

"...Is that what that is," ze said slowly. "I thought they just stopped asking."

"They stopped asking because you _databased_ at them—"

"Nobody _told_ me—"

"I'm telling you _now,_" Ronan said, and laughed, and it was — that was the moment, though neither of them marked it — it was the first time anyone had been _casual_ enough with zir to just tell ze the truth about zirself instead of tiptoeing around the phenomenon of zir. Not intimidated. Not crushing. Just a tired coworker at 1 a.m. going _hey, you're doing the thing, here's why it's not landing._

Kyu absorbed it. Nodded, slowly, filing new information about a social world ze'd apparently misread for months.

"...Huh," ze said. "So I should talk about a different thing. At lunch."

"You should talk about _literally_ any other thing. Or nothing. Nothing's also fine. You don't have to fill it with schema."

"Noted." A beat. Then, flat, testing something: "Are _you_ going to keep talking to me, or is this a one-time 1-a.m. thing."

And Ronan, who had no idea he was being quietly interviewed for the position of _zir only friend,_ shrugged and said, "I mean, you just fixed my release in four minutes and saved me till dawn, so. Yeah. I'll keep talking to you. Somebody has to tell you about the migration thing."

"Okay," said Kyu, and went back to zir screen — but something in the set of zir shoulders had changed, some small tension gone out of it, the tension of a person who'd just, unexpectedly, at 1 a.m., over a broken deploy, been treated like a colleague instead of a leopard.

They stayed till three. Ronan's release went out clean. And when he finally packed up to leave, wrecked, he found — without comment, without acknowledgment, appearing at his elbow the way these things did around zir — a fresh coffee ze'd made him at some point while he wasn't looking, too strong, exactly what he needed for the drive home.

"You didn't have to—" Ronan started.

"Had extra," said Kyu, which was a lie, ze'd made it on purpose, and they both let it be a lie, and that — Ronan would understand much later, years later, at a bar in a different city — was the moment ze had quietly, silently, in the only grammar ze had, decided to keep him.

---
# 
By spring they were, improbably, _friends_ — the kind that got lunch on purpose, and Kyu had stopped databasing at people (mostly; Devlin still occasionally triggered a relapse), and Ronan had settled comfortably into his role as the one human on the floor with normal-person access to the phenomenon.

Which meant Ronan got the version nobody else did. Not the leopard. The actual, unfiltered, off-duty person — and the actual off-duty person, it turned out, was a _menace._

The floor's Kyu was flat, competent, quietly devastating, entirely work-focused. Lunch Kyu, once ze relaxed, was something else: dry as sand, quick, and — this was the discovery that reorganized Ronan's understanding — _completely without shame._ About anything. It came out in pieces over weeks of shared tables, and every piece startled him a little more.

"You're in a good mood," Ronan observed one Monday, ze visibly, unusually loose.

"Had a good weekend."

"Yeah? Do anything fun?"

"Mm." Kyu ate a fry. "Met someone Friday. Didn't leave the apartment till Sunday." Said it exactly the way ze'd report a merge going through — flat, factual, zero self-consciousness, while Ronan inhaled his drink. "You?"

"I—" Ronan coughed. "You just — that's — you just _said_ that."

"Said what?"

"That! The — the weekend thing! Just, out loud, at _lunch_—"

Kyu looked at him, genuinely puzzled. "You asked if I did anything fun. I did. That was the fun." A pause, the first flicker of the smug Ronan would come to know so well. "Why're you red? It's just sex, Ronan. Everybody does it. You do it. Probably badly, going by the face you're making right now—"

"I do _not_—" Ronan was fully choking now. "You can't just — okay, note to self, do not ask ze about weekends—"

"You can ask me about weekends. I'll tell you. That's the _point_ of friends, isn't it, you tell them things—" ze stole one of his fries, unbothered, "—you're the one making it weird. I thought you were the normal one. You told me _I_ was the weird one about lunch. This is a glass-houses situation—"

And that was the thing that genuinely broke Ronan's brain, over the following weeks: the _inversion_ of it. On the floor, Kyu was the one who couldn't read a social cue, who databased at people, who Ronan had to gently coach through normal human interaction. But get ze onto _this_ subject and suddenly _Ronan_ was the flustered, blushing, can't-handle-it one, and Kyu was serene, frank, and openly enjoying his discomfort. The student had a subject the teacher couldn't touch.

"How are you like this," Ronan demanded, another lunch, ze having just answered a completely innocent question with a completely non-innocent honesty. "You can't tell when someone's flirting with you across the _actual office_ — Devlin's been in love with you for a year, you have no idea — but you'll tell _me,_ over a _sandwich,_ about—"

"That's different." Kyu shrugged. "I don't notice flirting because I'm not looking for it. I'm at work. I'm working. But if you _ask_ me a direct question, I answer it. I don't do the—" ze waved a hand, "—the _dance._ The thing where everybody's embarrassed about a normal thing and pretends. I find that exhausting. Just say the thing." A beat. "You'll get used to it. Or you won't. Either way you're stuck with me now, you fixed my lunch problem, I keep people who fix my problems."

"You _keep_ people—"

"Yeah. You're kept. Sorry. Should've told you." Ze ate another of his fries, entirely at peace, the smug fully arrived. "You're mine now. It's a whole thing. Comes with the frank lunch conversations. No refunds."

And Ronan — red, flustered, thoroughly out of his depth on a subject he'd have sworn he was more worldly about than a git-obsessed architecture nerd who worked till 3 a.m. — looked at this person who was somehow simultaneously the most oblivious and the most shameless individual he had ever met, and understood he'd made a terrible and permanent friend.

"...Fine," he said. "But I'm setting rules. No weekend stories before I've finished eating."

"No promises." Kyu took the last fry. "You asked. That's on you. You keep _asking._"

"I'm never asking again—"

"You will. You're curious. It's your whole thing." And ze was right, of course — ze was always right — because Ronan, God help him, _would_ keep asking, and would spend years being scandalized by the answers, and would not, for anything, have traded the one friend on that floor who told ze the truth and got the truth right back, flat and shameless and entirely without a filter, over a sandwich, forever.

---
# 
It was Marcus who saw, which was almost too perfect, given the coffee-walks.

He'd gone out that Friday to a place across town — good bar, loud, the kind with a line and a bartender who knew regulars — dragged along by friends who had nothing to do with work, a completely separate compartment of his life. He was two drinks in and not thinking about the office at all when he glanced toward the bar and his entire brain rebooted.

Because that was Kyu.

Except it _wasn't_ — not the Kyu he knew. The Kyu Marcus knew was flat, buttoned, heads-down at the east windows, devastating precisely _because_ ze was so contained. This person was leaning on the bar with a drink and an easy grin, loose in a way Marcus had genuinely never witnessed, mid-conversation with someone who was very obviously flirting and getting _somewhere,_ and — this was the part that broke him — ze was flirting _back._ Openly. Fluently. With a low unbothered confidence that the office had never once seen a single frame of, because the office got the monk and the town got _this._

Marcus stared for a full, unwise minute.

He watched zir say something that made the other person laugh and lean in. He watched zir not move away. He watched the whole easy shameless _aliveness_ of a person he'd spent a year building a spreadsheet around from thirty feet and a coffee-walk's remove, and understood, with the vertigo of a man whose model of reality has developed a fault line, that he had never actually met ze at all. He'd met the work version. The town got the real one. Or — no — they were _both_ real, that was worse, that was so much worse—

He should have looked away. He did not look away in time.

Because Kyu, mid-sentence, felt the stare the way ze felt everything, and glanced over, and clocked him — Marcus, platform team, coffee-walk Marcus, gaping from across a bar ze'd assumed no one from work would ever be in — and did not, in any way, react the way Marcus's entire nervous system was reacting.

Ze just lifted zir drink a half-inch. A nod. _Hey._ Unbothered. And went back to zir conversation.

Marcus fled to the bathroom and had a small crisis against a sink.

---

Monday arrived the way disasters do — inevitably, and too early.

Marcus came in with the haunted eyes of a man who had Seen Something and spent all weekend failing to un-see it. He got his coffee. He did not do the coffee-walk. He sat at his desk in the platform pod and stared at the east windows, where Kyu was already in, flat and contained and monkish and _exactly the same,_ three tickets deep, radiating none of the loose shameless heat of Friday night, as if the person at the bar had been a fever dream.

But it hadn't been a dream. And Marcus knew it hadn't. And _ze knew he knew,_ because ze'd lifted zir drink at him.

The whole floor felt something was off within an hour, the way the floor always sensed disturbances in the Kyu-field. Marcus was _behaving strangely._ Marcus, who timed his hydration to zir water breaks, was now pointedly _not_ looking at the east windows, which was somehow more conspicuous than the looking had ever been.

Ronan, who missed nothing, drifted over to Marcus's desk. "You okay? You look like you saw a ghost."

"I saw—" Marcus lowered his voice to a hiss. "I saw _ze._ Friday. Out. At Lumen, the bar on the west side. Ze was — Ronan, ze was _completely_ different. Ze was — there was someone, and ze was — I have never seen ze like — the _office_ ze and the _bar_ ze are not the same _person_—"

Ronan, who had known this for months, who had been getting frank weekend reports over stolen fries since spring, kept his face admirably still. "Oh yeah?"

"Ze was _flirting._ Ronan. _Flirting._ Good at it. _Really_ good at it. And then ze _saw_ me, and ze just — _nodded,_ like it was nothing, like I hadn't just watched—" Marcus put his face in his hands. "I can't look at zir. I have to quit. I have to change teams. I have to change _cities._"

"You could also just," Ronan offered, enjoying this more than was kind, "act normal."

"There's no _acting normal_ after — you don't understand, the person at that bar and the person at those windows—"

"Same person," said a flat voice.

They both froze. Kyu had materialized at the edge of the pod — refilling the communal coffee, as it happened, which nobody would see ze do and which would later be mysteriously full — and had, with zir usual gift for arriving mid-sentence, caught the entire thing.

Marcus made a sound like a stepped-on toy.

"You saw me at Lumen," Kyu said to him, entirely calm, setting the coffee down. "Friday. Yeah. I go there." A shrug. "I'm not two people, Marcus. That's just what I'm like when I'm not at work. This is what I'm like _at_ work. Both real. You look like you're having a stroke about it."

"You were — there was a — you were with—"

"Yeah, probably. It was Friday." Said with the flat unbothered frankness that had scandalized Ronan for months and was now doing catastrophic damage to Marcus in real time. "That's a me thing. It's not a work thing. That's why you've never seen it _here._ Two different — you don't shit where you eat, you know?" A beat, and then, mild, the first curl of the smug: "Why're you so rattled? It's just me having a life. You've all been so _weird_ about me for a year, I figured you'd be relieved to find out I'm a normal person off the clock. Loud, but normal."

"_A YEAR?_" Marcus said, seizing on entirely the wrong part. "You _know_ about—"

"I know you time your coffee to my water breaks, yeah." Ze picked the coffee pot back up. "Everybody does something. There's apparently a spreadsheet. Ronan told me." Ze glanced at Ronan, who was now the one turning pale. "I don't care about any of it. Do what you want. But I'm the same person at the bar as I am here, I'm just off the clock, so you can stop looking at me like I grew a second head." The smug landed fully now, flat and devastating. "Or don't. Have your crisis. I've got tickets."

And ze went back to the east windows, coffee replenished, having in ninety seconds confirmed every rumor the floor had ever half-believed, revealed ze'd known about the spreadsheet the whole time, and left Marcus a smoking crater at his desk — while the rest of the floor, who had absolutely been eavesdropping, quietly and collectively lost their minds, because the myth now had a _witness_ and a _confirmation_ and the legend of Kyu had just ascended to a whole new tier.

By lunch the entire building knew Marcus had seen the _other_ Kyu at Lumen. By Wednesday, three separate people had developed sudden plans to go to Lumen. By Friday, the spreadsheet — Ronan would later swear — had grown a second tab.

And Kyu, monk at the windows, menace at the bar, entirely the same person in both and entirely unbothered by the collision of zir two worlds, worked through it all thinking about nothing but the merge, and went out that weekend exactly as loud and shameless as ever, because someone from work having seen ze changed, as far as ze was concerned, absolutely nothing.

"You started a _war,_" Ronan told ze at lunch, delighted, horrified. "There's a _reconnaissance mission_ to Lumen forming. Devlin's going."

"Devlin's going to Lumen." Kyu stole a fry, unmoved. "He'll have a nice time. It's a good bar." A pause. "He should relax about the force-pushing, though. Even off the clock. Tell him."

"You're not going to—"

"I'm going to keep going to my bar and living my life," ze said serenely, taking another of Ronan's fries, "and they're going to keep being weird about it, and none of it's going to change anything, because it never does. Watch. Two weeks, it's old news, and I'm back to being the person who fixed the migration." Ze ate the fry. "That's the only part that ever sticks anyway."

Ze was right. Ze was always right. But it took closer to a month, and the spreadsheet never lost the second tab.

---
# The reconnaissance
It was billed, in the group chat the leadership did not know existed, as _scouting._ In reality it was four people from three teams who had, over the course of a fortnight, talked themselves into going to Lumen on a Friday to see the other Kyu with their own eyes — because Marcus's testimony had been vivid, and secondhand legend was no longer enough, and Devlin had said, fatally, "how bad could it be."

Very bad. That was the answer. That was always going to be the answer.

They arrived at nine and told themselves they were being casual, which they were not, because four coworkers clustered near the door of a bar scanning the room like meerkats is not casual, and the bartender clocked them as tourists inside a minute. They ordered drinks they held too tightly. They waited.

And then, around nine-thirty, ze arrived — and the whole table stopped breathing at once.

Because this was it. The confirmed article. Off-duty Kyu, walking into zir own regular spot loose and easy, greeted by name by the bartender, handed a drink ze hadn't ordered, folding into the room with a warmth and shameless _aliveness_ that architecture-pod Kyu had never once displayed. It was, Marcus had been right, like watching a different animal wearing the same face. Devlin made a small noise.

They had a plan. The plan was to observe from a safe distance and leave undetected, mission accomplished, legend confirmed.

The plan lasted four minutes, because Kyu felt the stare — the _collective_ stare, four separate work-stares aimed from one table, an unmistakable disturbance — glanced over, and identified them instantly. Devlin. Two from platform. And Priya from design, who had genuinely thought they'd be inconspicuous.

Ze did not look surprised. Ze looked _delighted,_ in the flat, terrible way ze got delighted.

And ze came over.

"You found my bar," Kyu said, arriving at their table, drink in hand, entirely at ease, while four adults experienced simultaneous system failure. "All of you. Together. On a Friday." Ze pulled a chair from the next table, spun it, sat. "This a work thing? Is this a _reconnaissance_ thing?" A pause, reading their faces, the smug blooming. "Oh my god. It's a reconnaissance thing. You _scouted_ me."

"We were just — out," Devlin tried, voice cracking on _out._

"You were _just out._ At the specific bar Marcus told the whole floor I go to." Ze sipped zir drink, savoring it. "You planned this. There was probably a chat. Was there a chat?"

The silence confirmed there was a chat.

"There was a chat." Kyu leaned back, monarch of the situation, having flipped the entire reconnaissance in under a minute so that _they_ were the ones being observed. "Okay. Well. You're here. This is me off the clock. Take your notes." Ze spread zir hands, offering zirself up for inspection, which was so much worse than being caught staring, because now they _had_ to look and had nowhere to hide. "This is it. I drink. I'm loud. I flirt, I'm good at it, I know the bartender, I'll probably leave with someone. Nothing you didn't already put in the spreadsheet. Anything you want to actually _ask,_ or are we just going to sit here while you have four simultaneous crises?"

Nobody could speak. Priya was staring into her drink like it held an exit.

And then — because ze was ze, because the frank shameless off-clock version had zero mercy and infinite ease — Kyu did the thing that finished them: ze started being _nice._ Genuinely. Flagged the bartender, bought the whole table a round they hadn't asked for, and settled in like a host, because four coworkers had come all this way and it would be rude to let them flounder.

"Relax," ze told them, as the drinks landed. "You came all the way out here. Have a good time. I'm not going to be weird about it — you're the ones being weird, I'm just at my bar." Ze pointed at Devlin. "You. Stop force-pushing to shared. Even here. I can tell you're going to do it Monday, it's on your face."

"I— how do you—"

"It's always you." Ze stood, drink in hand, the audience apparently concluded. "Enjoy Lumen. It's a good bar. Tell the rest of the floor I said the spreadsheet's flattering but the water-break tab's an invasion of privacy and I want it deleted." A beat, the smug at full power. "You won't delete it. That's fine. Just so you know I know."

And ze drifted back into the crowd, back to zir night, leaving four coworkers at a table with a round of drinks they hadn't ordered and the crushing realization that the reconnaissance had been a _catastrophic_ failure — that they'd been made in four minutes, dismantled with total ease, bought a round _by the target,_ and sent home with a message for the floor. They had gone to observe the legend and the legend had _observed them back,_ hosted them, and won.

Monday, the report they brought back to the group chat did not demystify anything. It could not. Because the account was: _we got caught, ze came over, ze knew about the chat and the spreadsheet, ze bought us drinks, ze told Devlin to stop force-pushing, and then ze left with someone and we all went home._ Which only made it _worse._ Which only fed the legend. Which grew a third tab.

"How'd the recon go," Ronan asked Devlin, who had the thousand-yard stare of a man returned from a failed war.

"Ze bought us drinks," Devlin said hollowly. "Ze knew we were coming. Ze _hosted_ us. And then ze told me to stop force-pushing." He looked at Ronan. "How does ze _know_ about the force-pushing—"

"Ze always knows about the force-pushing," Ronan said, not without sympathy, and patted him on the shoulder, and went to get coffee from the pot that would, mysteriously, already be full.

---
# 
The reconnaissance disaster had a consequence nobody anticipated, which was that it made one person on the floor _brave_ instead of scared — and that was so much more dangerous.

Her name was Priya, from design, and she'd been at the Lumen table, and unlike the others she had not come back demystified or defeated. She'd come back _emboldened._ Because she'd watched Kyu handle four ambushing coworkers with total ease and buy them drinks and leave with someone, and instead of concluding _unattainable,_ she'd concluded: _zir just a person. A frank, direct person who hates the dance. Everyone else keeps losing because they orbit and simper and ask about tickets. Maybe the move is to just — not do that._

Priya decided to shoot her shot. Properly. Directly. The way you would with a normal human being.

The floor did not know this was coming. The floor only knew that on a Wednesday, Priya walked over to the east windows — no coffee prop, no ticket pretext, just walked over — and the ambient hum of the pod dropped by half as people sensed, with the floor's finely-tuned instrument, that _something was happening._

"Hey," Priya said. "Got a second?"

"Depends." Kyu didn't look up. "Is it about the design handoff, because that's not on me, that's—"

"It's not about the handoff." Priya took a breath, and did the brave thing, the direct thing, the thing the whole floor had spent a year being too scared to do. "I think you're great and I'd like to take you to dinner. A date. Not a work thing. Just — you, me, dinner, if you're interested."

The pod went _dead_ silent. Somewhere, a keyboard stopped mid-word. Ronan, three desks down, slowly set his coffee down to watch, because this was history, this was someone actually _saying the thing_ out loud, and he would not miss it for anything.

Kyu looked up. Actually looked at her — and here was the thing that separated ze from anyone else in the building: ze did not get flustered, did not deflect, did not do the awkward dance of pretending to misunderstand. Ze registered the ask cleanly, gave it the flat total attention ze gave everything, and answered honestly, because ze didn't know how to do anything else.

"No," ze said. "But respect for asking straight. Genuinely. Nobody here does that."

Priya, to her enormous credit, held her ground. "Can I ask why? No hard feelings, I just — you seem like someone who'd rather be told."

"I am." Kyu leaned back, and the answer came flat and clean, no cushioning, which was somehow the kindest thing ze could have done. "Two reasons. One, I don't do work. Ever. Whatever I've got going on off the clock, none of it comes in this building. That's a hard rule, it's not about you, I'd say no to anyone from here. Keeps things clean." A beat. "Two — and this is the actual reason — you don't want me. You want the thing everyone here's decided I am. The bar version, the mystery, the Lumen story. That's not a person, that's a _rumor._ You've talked to me directly for about ninety seconds, ever. You're asking out a legend the floor built. I'm not zir. I'm a guy who works till 3 a.m. and thinks about database migrations and steals Ronan's fries." Ze shrugged. "Take yourself to dinner. Save the effort."

Priya absorbed this. Nodded slowly. "...That's fair. That's really fair, actually."

"I know. That's why I said it." And then, because ze wasn't cruel, because the frankness cut both ways — "For what it's worth, that took guts, and guts is rarer than you think around here. Whoever you _do_ end up asking out, lead with exactly that. The straight version. It works on people who aren't allergic to their own workplace." The corner of the mouth, small, not unkind. "Now go, before the whole floor combusts. They've been holding their breath for a full minute. It's unhealthy."

Priya laughed — actually laughed, the tension broken — said "thanks, Kyu," and walked back to design with her head up, rejected but weirdly _fine,_ because ze had done the impossible thing: turned someone down so honestly and so respectfully that she came away liking ze _more._

The floor, of course, detonated the second she sat down. Whispers everywhere. _Did she just — did that just happen — what did ze SAY—_

Ronan wheeled his chair over to the windows.

"You just rejected someone," he said, "and I think you made her _feel good_ about it. How. That's not — rejection isn't supposed to leave people _cheerful,_ Kyu—"

"I told her the truth. Truth's kinder than the dance." Ze was already back in a ticket. "Everybody thinks the nice thing is to soften it. It's not. The nice thing is to be _clear,_ fast, so nobody's left decoding it for a week. She asked straight, I answered straight, we both walk away knowing exactly where we stand. That's respect." A pause. "The soft let-down is for cowards. Makes it about _your_ comfort, dressing it up so _you_ feel nice while the other person goes home confused. I'd rather just say the thing."

Ronan stared at zir. "You realize that's, like, emotionally _evolved._ That's more evolved than most people manage. And you say it like it's a git command."

"It's not complicated." Kyu stole a fry off the plate Ronan had brought over. "People just make it complicated because they're scared. I'm not scared. That's the whole trick." Another fry. "There's no trick. Stop looking at me like there's a trick."

And the floor, watching Priya return upright and unbroken from an interaction that should by all rights have destroyed her, added a new and confounding entry to the ever-growing legend: that ze could reject you so cleanly, so honestly, so _respectfully,_ that being turned down by ze felt less like a loss and more like being handed a gift you hadn't asked for.

Which of course made everyone want ze _more._ Because obviously it did. Nothing ze did ever made anyone want ze less. That was the whole curse of the thing.

"Devlin's going to try now," Ronan predicted grimly. "Priya survived. He's going to think he can too."

"Devlin should stop force-pushing to shared and worry about _that,_" Kyu said, and stole the last fry, and went back to work.

---
# 
Ronan had, over months of friendship, tried many strategies to defend his fries. Ordering a second portion (ze ate both). Moving the plate (ze had long arms). Direct appeal to decency (ze had none, re: fries). He had, by the time of the office party, simply accepted fry taxation as a cost of the relationship — which is a useful thing to establish, because the office party was where the floor learned that _everyone_ eventually accepts a cost, in exchange for zir.

The party was a company thing — end of quarter, a rented space, an open bar, and the fatal ingredient: a _loosened dress code_ and _alcohol,_ in a _sanctioned setting,_ which meant work-Kyu and off-clock-Kyu were about to occupy the same room on company property for the first time in floor history. The monk wall, lowered a couple of inches, indoors, where the whole building could see.

The floor had been quietly dreading and craving this for weeks.

Ze arrived — and there was an audible shift in the room, the collective inhale of a department that had only ever seen the buttoned version now getting ze in _party_ configuration: not the full Lumen animal, ze wasn't stupid, ze knew it was still work-adjacent — but _looser._ Dressed with intent. A drink in hand. The flat face carrying, for once, an easy edge of amusement instead of pure containment. It was maybe thirty percent of the off-clock version, and thirty percent was enough to put the entire party on notice.

"zir _drinking,_" Marcus hissed to Ronan, scandalized, thrilled, keeping a respectful surveillance distance.

"Ze drinks. zir a bartender on the weekends, Marcus, ze drinks _professionally_—"

"zir _smiling._"

"That's the same amount ze always—" Ronan squinted. "Okay, that is slightly more than usual. Two drinks in. Interesting."

Because the thing about zir off the clock — even a work-safe thirty percent of it — was the _frankness_ came online, and the party learned this in real time and in public, one horrified delightful exchange at a time. A VP made polite party small talk, asked what ze did for fun, and got a flat honest answer that made him choke on his wine. A junior asked, emboldened by the open bar, one of the questions people only ever asked in whispers, and got it answered _directly,_ out loud, at volume, ze having no setting for pretending a normal thing was scandalous. The party rippled with the aftershocks of zir simply _being ze_ in a room that had only ever seen the redacted version.

And then there was the moment. The moment the floor would talk about for the rest of the company's existence.

Someone — drunk, brave, doomed — decided the party was the place to finally _flirt_ with zir. Openly. In front of everyone. Worked up the nerve at the open bar and leaned in with a line, the whole room subtly orienting to watch, because a public shot at zir was blood in the water.

And Kyu — loose, amused, two drinks in and off the strict work-clock enough to actually _engage_ instead of monk-deflecting — did not shut it down cold. Ze _volleyed._ Flat, dry, devastating, entirely unbothered, taking the flirt and returning it with a frankness that left the poor brave soul red to the ears and the surrounding party _dying,_ because they were watching the bar-Kyu they'd only heard rumors of operate live on company property. Ze didn't take the bait — the hard work/life rule held even loosened — but ze _played,_ just enough, just once, and it was the single most tension-filled ninety seconds the floor had ever collectively witnessed.

Ronan watched with his face in his hands. "Oh, they're going to be _insufferable_ about this Monday—"

The flirt ended with zir patting the brave soul on the shoulder — "cute, but I don't do work, sorry, drink water" — and drifting off, leaving a smoking crater and a party that had just been fed for a year.

But the _actual_ drama of the night — the thing Ronan would treasure — came late, when the party had thinned and loosened, and ze ended up in a corner with Ronan and a handful of the brave-enough, the ones who'd graduated from orbiting to actually _talking,_ and the conversation got real the way parties get real at the end. And someone asked ze, emboldened by the hour and the open bar, the question the whole floor had wondered for two years:

"Why'd you even take this job? You could — you're clearly — you don't seem like a _cubicle_ person. Why are you _here?_"

And Kyu, loose enough to answer honestly, considered it.

"Money was good. Work was interesting for a while." A shrug. "Honestly? It's fine. I don't hate it. But I'm not going to be here forever. This isn't the—" ze gestured, vaguely, at the whole apparatus of the company, "—the _thing._ It's a thing I'm doing. When it stops being interesting I'll go do something else." Ze sipped zir drink. "I'm not built for the ladder. The ladder's for people who need the ladder to mean something. I don't need it to mean anything. I just do the work while it's good and then I leave."

The corner of the group went quiet, because that was — for a floor full of people climbing, striving, defining themselves by the rungs — a genuinely alien and slightly enviable thing to hear. Ronan, who knew ze best, heard the prophecy in it and said nothing. (He would remember this conversation, later, standing at a quiet standup, realizing ze had told them all _exactly_ what ze was going to do and none of them had believed ze meant it.)

"That's kind of the dream, though," the junior said, wistful. "To just — not need it to mean anything."

"It's not a dream. It's just not being scared." Ze finished the drink. "Anyway. Someone stop me before I get philosophical, this is a _work party,_ it's embarrassing." Ze stood, and the corner of the mouth came up, the amusement back. "Ronan. You've had four. You're doing the thing where you get sentimental. I'm putting you in a cab before you tell everyone you love them."

"I _don't_ get—"

"You told Marcus he was 'a good egg' twenty minutes ago. I heard it." Ze hauled him up by the arm, the provision reflex running even here, even now, getting zir drunk friend home the way ze got everyone everything. "Come on. Cab. Say goodnight."

And that was how the party ended: with the legend of the floor, having flirted a brave soul into a crater, answered the VP too honestly, confirmed every rumor at thirty percent capacity, and quietly foretold zir own departure to a corner of people too tipsy to hear the warning — pouring zir best friend into a cab because he'd had four drinks and gone soft, unbothered, monkish again already, the party's chaos rolling off ze like everything did.

Monday, the floor was a _wreck._ The flirt story alone sustained the group chat for two weeks. The spreadsheet, Ronan swore, grew a _fourth_ tab.

And ze came in, flat, contained, three tickets deep by ten, thinking about the merge, entirely unaware that ze'd just given the building enough material to last until ze left — which, though nobody believed it yet, ze'd told them at the party ze was going to do.

---
# The new hire who didn't know.

His name was Sam, he started on a Monday, and he had the singular advantage of total ignorance. He hadn't heard the legend. He hadn't seen the spreadsheet. He didn't know about Lumen or the party or the reconnaissance disaster. He knew only that he'd been assigned a desk in the architecture pod next to a tall coworker with mismatched eyes who was very good at git, and he treated ze accordingly — like a coworker.

The floor watched, appalled, as Sam did the unthinkable: he was _normal_ at zir. He asked ze a direct question about the codebase and didn't go pink. He offered to grab ze a coffee and didn't rehearse it. On day three he said, "you want to get lunch? I don't know anyone yet," with the casual ease of a man who had no idea he was asking out the most orbited person in the building.

"Sure," said Kyu.

The pod nearly fainted.

And here was the thing — because Sam didn't know ze was supposed to be a phenomenon, he just _talked_ to zir, and ze, starved as ever of people who did that, talked back. It was Ronan all over again. By week two they were friends. By week three Sam had learned about the fry-stealing (his fries, now, also taxed) and had been fully absorbed into the small circle of people who got the actual person instead of the rumor.

"How'd you _do_ that," Marcus demanded of Sam, weeks in, genuinely tortured. "You just — you're _friends_ with zir. Casually. How—"

"With Kyu?" Sam blinked. "zir nice. Bit of a menace about lunch orders. Why's everyone so weird about zir?"

The floor did not have an answer that didn't make _them_ sound insane. Sam never did fully understand what the big deal was, which — Ronan noted — was precisely why ze liked him.

---

**The code review massacre.**

It happened because a senior engineer named Present — the one who used to detour past architecture on his way to opposite-direction meetings — pushed a design ze had _opinions_ about, and made the tactical error of defending it in a review with zir in the room.

The floor gathered, sensing violence.

It was not loud. That was what made it terrifying. Kyu did not raise zir voice, did not gloat, did not make it personal. Ze simply, methodically, flatly, walked through every flaw in Present's design in the bored tone of someone reading a grocery list — the race condition here, the scaling wall there, the assumption on line 400 that would eat the whole system under load — and offered, unprompted, the correct version, which ze had apparently already sketched, because of course ze had.

Present tried to defend line 400.

Kyu let him. Let him talk all the way to the end of the defense, patient, and then said, "yeah, that's the part that breaks," and showed him exactly why, and there was nothing left to say, because ze was right, ze was completely right, ze was right in the specific devastating way that left no room to be gracefully wrong.

"...I'll refactor it," Present said, to the table.

"I already did." Ze shared the branch. "It's there. Use it or don't. But that—" a nod at his original, "—will page someone at 3 a.m. within a month. Your call." And ze went back to zir own screen, the massacre concluded, no victory lap, which was so much worse than a victory lap.

"You _demolished_ him," Ronan whispered, delighted, later.

"I fixed his design. He can be mad or he can not get paged at 3 a.m. That's the trade." Ze stole a fry. "I'd rather someone tell me I'm wrong fast than let me ship a thing that fails. That's a favor. Present will figure that out eventually." A beat. "Or he won't and he'll keep resenting me, which is also fine. Not my problem how he feels about being correct-adjacent."

Present, for the record, used the branch. It did not page anyone at 3 a.m. He never thanked ze, and resented ze slightly forever, and both of them were completely at peace with this arrangement.

---

**The thing with the coffee, finally explained.**

For two years the communal coffee had mysteriously refilled itself. The floor had theories. A generous facilities person. A rotating secret angel. Nobody had ever caught the culprit, because ze did it early, or late, or in the gaps, the provision reflex running silent the way it ran everywhere.

Sam caught ze. New, unbriefed, in early one morning, and there was Kyu at the coffee station, quietly restocking the supply from a bag ze'd apparently _bought,_ with zir own money, because the office stuff was bad and ze'd decided everyone deserved better and had said nothing about it for two years.

"Oh — you do the coffee?" Sam said, guileless. "That's you? Everyone thinks it's magic. There's a whole—"

"Don't," Kyu said, flat, caught. "It's not a thing. The office stuff is bad. I fixed it. That's all."

"You've been buying the whole floor's coffee for—"

"_It's not a thing, Sam._"

And Sam, being Sam, being the one person too normal to make it weird, just shrugged and said "cool, thanks, it's good coffee," and let it go, and did not tell the floor, because ze asked him not to and he saw no reason to make a fuss.

Which is the only reason the floor never found out. To this day, in that building, some of them still think it was magic.

---

**And the thing under all of it.**

Because here's what the legend never captured, what the spreadsheet couldn't tab, what Marcus and the reconnaissance and the party stories all missed: the floor had spent two years orbiting ze as a _phenomenon_ — the looks, the mystery, the Lumen animal, the untouchable competence — and the entire time, quietly, underneath, ze had been the person buying their coffee. Fixing their deploys at 3 a.m. Refactoring their messes and refusing credit. Telling Devlin to stop force-pushing not to humiliate him but so he'd stop breaking the branch. Getting Ronan into cabs. Absorbing Sam.

They watched the leopard. They never noticed the person in the filing cabinet had been taking care of them the whole time, silently, in the only grammar ze had.

Ronan noticed. Sam noticed, eventually. A handful of others.

The rest just kept the spreadsheet, and orbited, and never once understood that the most-crushed-on person in the building was, underneath the whole devastating surface, simply — and only — _kind,_ in the specific way that never announces itself and never takes the thank-you and never, ever explains.

And then one Tuesday ze didn't come back from lunch, and emailed HR from the parking lot, and was gone — and the coffee ran out within a week, and stayed out, and nobody ever figured out why the magic had stopped.

That's when some of them finally understood. Too late, the way people always understand ze. Standing at a quiet standup around an empty desk, realizing the weather had a name the whole time, and they'd spent two years watching the wrong thing.

---
# 
It happened because Ronan invited himself, which was the only way anything happened with Ronan.

"You go out Fridays," he said, at lunch, mid-fry-theft-defense. "To your bar. The famous one. Marcus's crime scene."

"Lumen. Yeah."

"Take me."

Kyu paused, a fry halfway to zir mouth — Ronan's fry — and looked at him with something ze didn't usually deploy at work: consideration. Because this was a real ask. The work/life wall was load-bearing; ze'd told Priya, told the whole floor, _I don't do work._ And here was work, asking to come to the other side.

"You're from the office," ze said, flatly. A test, not a no.

"I'm from the office at the office. I'm asking as—" Ronan shrugged, casual, the same casualness that had cracked ze open at 1 a.m. months ago. "I dunno. As your friend. If that's a thing that's allowed. You've never actually let me hang out with you when we're not both being paid to be in a building. Kind of noticed that. Kind of wondered if it was on purpose."

And that landed, because it _was_ on purpose, and ze hadn't quite examined why, and Ronan had — as usual — just said the true thing out loud where ze had to look at it.

"...Friday," Kyu said, after a beat. "Nine. Don't be weird about it."

"I'm never weird."

"You get sentimental after four drinks and tell strangers they're good eggs."

"That's not weird, that's _warm_—"

Friday, nine, Ronan showed up at Lumen — and got the version of zir the office would have committed crimes to see. Because the second they crossed the threshold, the monk was gone. Off-clock Kyu: loose, loud, greeted by name by the bartender, handed a drink ze hadn't ordered, sliding into the room like ze owned a piece of it. Ronan had seen Marcus's face describing this. Seeing it himself was different — the _ease_ of it, the total absence of the work-wall, a whole warm shameless person the office only ever got thirty percent of.

"Okay," Ronan admitted, taking his own drink. "Yeah. This is — you're _different_ here."

"I'm the _same._ I'm just not on the clock." Ze clinked zir glass against his. "This is the setting where I don't have to be a monk about everything. You wanted to see it. Here it is." A pause, and then, dry: "Try not to fall in love with me. Everyone from the office does. It's a whole thing."

"I've been immune for months. Fry-theft killed the mystique."

"Good. You're useless to me as an admirer, you're only good as a friend—"

And it was a _good night_ — genuinely, the first purely-social, nobody's-getting-paid, no-tickets-involved time they'd ever spent, and it turned out the friendship worked even better off the clock, because off the clock ze wasn't rationing zirself. Ronan got the stories. Got to watch ze be magnetic at the bar and completely unbothered about it. Got to see, when a stranger flirted and ze volleyed it back flat and shameless, exactly what Marcus had stammered about — and got to be the friend _next_ to it instead of the poor soul receiving it, which was a much better seat.

And then, late, a few drinks in, Ronan did the thing he did — got a little sentimental, a little honest, the four-drink warmth coming on.
