"You know you never let anyone do this," he said. "The — the off-clock thing. You keep everyone at the office at the office. Priya, Marcus, the whole floor. But you let me come to your _bar._" He gestured, a bit loose. "That's — I dunno. That means something, right? You picked me. Out of the whole floor."

Kyu looked at him. The bar was loud around them, but the flat honesty came through anyway, quieter than the room.

"You were the only one who ever talked to me like a person," ze said. Simple. No deflection, because ze didn't do the dance even here — _especially_ not here. "Everyone else saw a — whatever they saw. You saw the guy at the next desk who wouldn't shut up about the shared branch. Told me to stop databasing at people at lunch. Told me the truth about myself when nobody else would." A shrug, but there was weight under it. "That's rare. I keep the rare ones. You've been kept for months, I told you that." A beat, the corner of the mouth. "This is just me finally letting you _know_ you're kept. Off the clock. Where it counts."

Ronan, four drinks in, got visibly a little emotional about this.

"Don't," Kyu warned.

"I'm not—"

"You're doing the good-egg face. I can see it coming—"

"You're my best friend," Ronan said, undeterred, warm and sincere and slightly slurred, "and I'm _glad_ I sat next to you, and I'm glad you fix my deploys, and—"

"Okay, that's four drinks, you're cut off." But ze was almost smiling, the real one, and ze flagged the bartender for water instead of another round — the provision reflex, always, getting zir sentimental friend home safe — and let Ronan be sappy at zir for exactly as long as ze could stand it, which was longer than ze'd let anyone else, because it was Ronan.

They closed the place down. Ze poured Ronan into a cab at the end, exactly like the office party, except this time it wasn't a work obligation — it was just a friend making sure a friend got home.

"Text me when you're in," Kyu said, through the cab window.

"You _care,_" Ronan said, delighted, drunk.

"I care whether you make it home. Different thing." But it wasn't a different thing, and they both knew it, and the cab pulled away with Ronan grinning and ze standing on the curb outside zir bar having, for the first time in the entire run of the friendship, let someone from the sealed side of zir life all the way across the wall — on purpose, because he'd earned it, because he was the rare one, because ze kept the rare ones and this was ze finally saying so.

Years later, a different city, a different bar — Ronan would find ze again, changed almost past recognition, and the first thing that would still be true, the thing that survived everything, was this: he was still the one who got to sit _next_ to the person instead of orbiting the phenomenon. He'd earned that seat at a desk over a broken deploy, and he never once gave it up.

---
# Where the legend started
Ze quit the way ze did everything: without ceremony, without warning, on zir own terms and nobody else's.

It wasn't a crisis. That was what people always got wrong about it. There was no blowup, no burnout, no dramatic final straw. The work had simply, quietly, stopped being interesting — ze'd solved the interesting problems, automated the rest, and one Tuesday ze looked at the ticket queue and felt the specific flatness of a thing that had run its course. So ze went to lunch, didn't come back, emailed HR from the parking lot, and that was that. Ze'd told the whole party months earlier exactly this would happen. Nobody had believed ze meant it.

Ronan believed it, eventually, standing at a quiet standup around an empty desk. But ze called him that same week — because ze kept the rare ones — so it wasn't a loss so much as a change of venue. The friendship survived the building. It always would.

And then ze did the thing ze'd been quietly planning: ze left the city entirely.

There was no grand reason. A new place, a fresh start, somewhere ze had no legend and no spreadsheet and no floor full of people orbiting — just a city where ze could be nobody in particular and do whatever the hell ze felt like. Ze had the freelance IT lined up, remote, on zir own hours. Ze picked up bartending because ze liked bars and ze was good at it and it filled the evenings. Ze started coaching because someone asked and it turned out ze was good at that too. Three jobs, none of them a career, all of them chosen — the life ze'd described at the party, the not-needing-it-to-mean-anything life, finally assembled on purpose.

Ze found the apartment because it was cheap and unremarkable and nobody would look at zir twice, which was exactly the point. A mid-tier building. A sensible unit. Third floor.

Nobody warned ze about the walls. Nobody warned the building about zir, either.

Because here was the collision waiting to happen: ze was, and had always been, _sensitive_ — loud, responsive, congenitally incapable of muffling anything, and completely without shame about any of it, because being weird about a normal thing was exhausting and ze didn't do exhausting. In every previous apartment this had been fine. Thick walls, or detached, or neighbors far enough away. Ze had lived zir loud, open, unbothered private life for years and it had never once been a public event.

The third floor of this building had walls you could hear a sneeze through.

Ze found this out the first night, in the way ze found out everything — by simply _being ze_ and letting the consequences arrive whenever they arrived. And the consequences arrived immediately, through drywall, to every unit within range: the new neighbor was, it turned out, _audible._ Extremely. On a schedule the whole floor could nearly set a watch by.

And ze, being ze, felt no particular way about it. The walls were thin. Okay. That was a fact about the building, like the door that stuck in summer. It was not a fact ze intended to change zir life around, because muffling zirself to spare strangers a sound everyone made would be — well. Weird about it. And ze wasn't.

So the broadcast began. Night one. And the third floor, over the following weeks, went through its stages — the shock, the theories, the who-is-that, and then, slowly, the strange dawning fondness, because the loud new neighbor was also, they discovered, the one who fixed the lobby light nobody had reported, and left too much food at doors, and carried old Mrs. from 3B's groceries up without being asked, and shoveled the walk before anyone else was awake. The provision reflex, running silent, in a brand-new building full of strangers ze'd decided, quietly, to take care of.

Loud through the wall. Kind through the door. Same person. The building learned to hold both, the way everyone eventually did.

And it settled into the equilibrium that had been running for over a year by the time a man with a car full of record crates pulled up outside, straining his back on the first box, about to meet the neighbor who would carry the rest up three flights without being asked — with no idea, yet, that the thin wall between their apartments was about to start telling him the first true thing he'd ever learn about zir.

---
# Present time

"I'm not a monk," Kyu said, apropos of nothing, over breakfast. "Monks give things up. I don't give up anything I like." A beat. "I like your dick."

Will inhaled his coffee.

"CAN YOU—" he got out, between coughs, scarlet, "—can you be any _more_ subtle about it—"

"I could be less subtle. I was being generous." Ze ate a piece of toast, entirely serene, while Will died across the table. "You want less subtle? Because I have more—"

"NO. I want — Kyu, it's _nine in the morning,_ the _window's open,_ 3B is _right there_ watering her—"

"3B knows. 3B has a betting pool. 3B would be _thrilled_ to hear I'm domestically satisfied, it's good for the odds—"

"_Please_—"

"See, this is the thing." Ze pointed the toast at him, warming to the philosophy of it now, unbothered as ever while Will wished for the sweet release of the floor swallowing him. "Everyone calls me a monk because I don't want money or status or the ladder or a nicer apartment. And they're right, I don't want any of that. Genuinely couldn't care less." A sip of coffee. "But monk implies _no desires._ And I have desires. They're just — focused. Efficient. I want good coffee, I want to be left alone at lunch, and I want—" the corner of the mouth, deadly, "—you. Specifically. Regularly. Loudly. That's not monkhood, Will, that's just knowing exactly what I want and having _excellent_ taste—"

"You said that to my _neighbor_ through an _open window_—"

"_Our_ neighbor. And I said it to _you._ Ze just happened to be within earshot, which, again, thin walls, we've been over this, it's the building's fault—"

Will put his face in his hands. From the window, faintly, came the sound of 3B's watering can pausing, and then a delighted, elderly "_good_ for you, dears," followed by the can resuming, and Will made a sound like a dying kettle.

"See?" Kyu said, radiant, vindicated. "Thrilled. Told you."

"I am going to _combust._"

"You say that a lot and you never do. It's an empty threat at this point." Ze got up, kissed the top of his mortified head on the way past, unbothered and unashamed and entirely at peace with having announced zir domestic satisfaction to the third floor before ten a.m. "I'm not a monk. Monks are _quiet._" The corner of the mouth, one last time, at the door. "Whatever else you can call me, neighbor — you cannot call me quiet."

And Will, scarlet at his own breakfast table, beloved by a shameless creature and an eavesdropping floor alike, could not, in fact, call ze quiet, because it was the single most accurate thing anyone had ever said about zir.

---
# 
The building chat, over the months, had evolved from a betting pool into something closer to a support group with sports commentary, and lately it had developed a new and fervent cause.

They were rooting for Will.

Not in the earlier way — the _aw, the neighbor found love_ way. This was different. This was _tactical._ Because the third floor, connoisseurs of the broadcast that they were, had begun to notice a _pattern_ in the programming, and the pattern was that sometimes — not always, but unmistakably, thrillingly _sometimes_ — the usual dynamic _inverted._

They knew ze. They'd known ze for over a year. They knew the smug in ze the way you know a neighbor's footsteps — it came through in everything, the flat unbotherable ease, the way ze ran the floor and the building and every interaction. Nothing rattled ze. Nothing ever had.

Except, apparently, on certain nights, _something did_ — and they could _hear it._

> **3B (Margarethe):** it happened again last night. around eleven. you know the one i mean. **Klaus (4F):** I heard it. I HEARD it. that was NOT the usual. **Klaus:** that was ze LOSING. that was ze not in charge for once **2A (Bettina):** the boy did something. our Will did something. **downstairs (Tomás):** you can hear the smug just. go. it breaks. and then it's just— **Tomás:** anyway. good for him. **Klaus:** GOOD FOR HIM. do you understand what he's accomplished? that person has never been flustered in the entire time we've known ze. ze bought my radiator part and wouldn't take thanks. ze is UNSHAKEABLE. **Klaus:** and Will found the one place ze shakes. **Margarethe:** a gifted young man. **Bettina:** god sent, honestly.

Because that was the thing they'd assembled, collectively, from acoustic evidence alone: that Will — ordinary, blushing, can't-cook Will, the shame-bearer, the one they all _pitied_ for having to live with the creature — had somehow, against all odds, found the single override to the smugness. The one context where the unbotherable menace of the third floor was, unmistakably, _bothered._ And the whole floor could tell, because the whole floor could hear the exact moment the composure came apart, and it was the most satisfying sound in the building's long history of overhearing things.

They had, frankly, come to _admire_ him for it.

> **Klaus:** i saw him in the lobby this morning. i shook his hand. **Bettina:** you DIDN'T **Klaus:** i did. i said "you're doing important work, young man." he went completely red. didn't know what i meant. **Tomás:** KLAUS **Klaus:** he'll figure it out. or he won't. either way. respect.

Will found out about the cheering the way he found out about everything mortifying: too late, and in public.

It was 3B — Margarethe — who let it slip, at the mailboxes, patting his arm with the fondness of a proud relative. "We're all so _pleased_ for you, dear," she said warmly. "The whole floor. We think you're doing _wonderfully._"

"...Doing wonderfully at—" Will, sensing danger, "—at what, exactly?"

"Oh, you _know,_ dear." She winked. She winked the exact wink from a year ago, the _youthful energy_ wink, except now it was aimed at _him,_ approvingly. "You've got that one _sorted,_ haven't you. We can tell. We can always tell." She patted his arm one more time. "God sent you, we think. Nobody's ever managed it. You should be _proud._"

And Will stood at the mailboxes, holding his mail, slowly turning the color of an emergency, as the full horror assembled: that the building had a chat, and the chat had _noticed,_ and the chat had determined — from _sound_ — that he had cracked the one thing about zir that had never been cracked, and that the entire third floor was, collectively, _rooting for him to keep doing it._

He told Kyu that night, mostly to suffer aloud.

"The building thinks I'm — Margarethe called me _god sent._ They have a whole — Kyu, they can _tell._ When you — they can _hear_ it. They know I'm the only one who ever—" He put his face in his hands. "They're _cheering._ For me. Because of the — the _sounds._"

Kyu absorbed this. And then — of course — instead of being embarrassed, ze looked _delighted,_ and also, faintly, _impressed._

"Huh." Ze considered it. "So the whole floor knows you're the one person who gets me to—" the corner of the mouth, insufferable, "—lose the plot. And they _respect_ you for it."

"It's _humiliating_—"

"It's the opposite of humiliating. It's the most respected you've ever been in this building." Ze was thoroughly enjoying this. "You spent a year being the guy they fed like a stray. Now you're the guy who _cracked the code._ You got a _promotion,_ neighbor. Klaus _shook your hand._" Ze leaned in, radiant, shameless as the day they met. "You should be proud. I'm a little proud. That's a hard thing to do and you're the only one who's ever managed it, and now it's _public record,_ and honestly—"

"Do not—"

"—we should give them something to cheer about," ze finished, delighted, already pulling him toward the bedroom, "since they've come all this way to root for you. Rude to disappoint the fans."

And the third floor, that night, updated its records with satisfaction — the god-sent man was, once again, doing important work — and not one door filed a complaint, because you don't complain about a _sport_ you're invested in.

Klaus, they say, kept a tally. Will has never asked to see it. Some legends are better left un-audited.

---
# 
Will pinched zir cheek.

He did it mid-sentence — right as ze was saying "rude to disappoint the fans" with that insufferable radiant confidence — reached up and got the cheek, firm, tilting that smug face sideways, and had the deep satisfaction of watching the whole sentence derail.

"—the _hell_—" Kyu froze, the one attack ze had never developed a defense against. "Did you just—"

"You were being smug." Will got the other cheek, both hands now, squishing that unbearable face like a stress toy. "The whole floor thing. The 'I'm a little proud' thing. The _fans_ thing. You were _so_ smug about being smug. So." He squished. "Cheek pinch. It's the only language you understand."

"That's not—" ze tried, muffled, face squished, dignity evaporating, "—that's a cheap—"

"It's not cheap, it's _strategic._" He was thriving, a man who had learned the one exploit and used it without mercy. "You can out-talk me. You can out-smug me. You can announce our sex life to Margarethe through an open window and get _cheered_ for it. But you cannot—" he squished harder, "—do _anything_ about this. Look at you. The building's legend. Squished."

Kyu, cheeks in Will's hands, tried to summon a comeback and could not, because it's genuinely difficult to be devastating with your face compressed into a shape, and Will _knew_ this, and that was the whole point.

"The fans," Will continued, insufferable now himself, "would be _devastated_ to see this. Their god-sent hero, and here's the actual dynamic — I just have to grab your face and the whole menace of the third floor goes _offline._ Klaus's tally doesn't account for the cheek thing. I could tell them. I could tell the _chat._"

"You wouldn't—" muffled, betrayed.

"I would. 'Update: zir catastrophically vulnerable to having zir cheeks pinched, here's photographic—'" He was reaching for his phone with one hand, still squishing with the other, and _that_ was what finally broke ze — because the threat of the chat, the fans, the record —

Kyu made a sound of pure indignation and retaliated the only way available, which was to go for Will's ribs, and Will shrieked and let go and the whole thing collapsed into a tangle on the couch, two grown adults, one of them the building's legend, wrestling like children while the third floor — one imagines — cocked an ear at the unfamiliar sound of _laughing_ and thumping and no discernible smug at all.

"Truce," Will gasped, eventually, pinned, breathless. "Truce, truce—"

"You started it. You and your _cheek thing._"

"You started it a _year_ ago carrying my record crates—"

"_You_ started it doing surveillance through a wall—"

They lay there, tangled, wrecked, neither winning, which was its own kind of winning.

"...The fans," Kyu said finally, into Will's shoulder, hoarse, the smug creeping back one last time, "are going to be so confused about tonight. No broadcast. Just thumping."

"Let them wonder." Will kissed the top of zir squished-recovering head. "Not everything's for the chat."

"Everything's a _little_ for the chat—"

"_Kyu._"

"Fine. Not everything." A pause. Then, muffled, grudging, warm: "...Do the cheek thing again. I'll allow it. Once."

Will did the cheek thing again. Ze allowed it. The chat got nothing that night, and wondered, and never found out — and that, too, was a kind of privacy, the only kind the two of them ever kept: the quiet ones, on purpose, just for them.

---
# The lap
Will was on a deadline. This meant, by the immutable law of the household, that Kyu had decided the most urgent thing in the world was to be exactly where Will was trying to work.

Ze didn't announce it. Cats don't announce. Ze simply appeared at the side of Will's desk, assessed the situation — a man, focused, typing, clearly in need of interruption — and, with the total entitlement of a creature that has never once considered whether it's wanted, climbed directly into his lap and sat down.

"Kyu."

"Mm." Ze settled in, getting comfortable, back against Will's chest, entirely blocking his view of the screen.

"I'm _working._"

"So work." Ze reached over and picked up Will's coffee — Will's coffee — and drank it. "Don't let me stop you."

"You're _on_ me. You're _on the keyboard._ I can't see the—" Will tried to lean around zir and failed, because ze was a large, warm, immovable obstacle who had decided this was the spot now. "You have a whole apartment. Two apartments, technically, you never gave up yours. You have a couch. You have _your own desk._"

"This one's warmer." Ze wriggled once, aggravatingly, finding the ideal position, which happened to be the one most catastrophic to Will's productivity. "You're comfortable. You radiate heat. It's efficient."

"It is not _efficient,_ I have a—"

"Deadline. You said. You keep saying it." Kyu tipped zir head back onto Will's shoulder, eyes closing, the picture of a creature that has claimed a sunbeam and will not be moved. "Deadlines are a construct. This is real. I'm real. The warmth is real. Pick reality."

And Will — arms trapped, coffee stolen, screen fully eclipsed by the most self-satisfied person on the third floor — did the math he did every time, and lost, the way he lost every time. Because he could physically move ze. Ze was letting him hold the fiction that he couldn't, the same way ze let him do the cheek thing, the same way a cat lets you believe you have any say at all. But moving ze would mean _ending_ this, and this was — despite the deadline, despite the coffee theft, despite everything — genuinely nice. The weight of zir. The warmth. The complete, unbothered trust of a creature that had decided his lap was the correct place to be and had no doubt whatsoever about its welcome.

"...Ten minutes," Will said, defeated, resting his chin on the top of zir head, giving up entirely. "Then I actually have to—"

"Sure." Ze was already half-asleep, warm and heavy and content, having gotten exactly what ze came for, which was never really the coffee. "Ten minutes."

It was not ten minutes.

The deadline waited. It was used to waiting by now. And Will worked around zir eventually, one-handed, chin on zir hair, the other arm looped around a purring, dozing, entirely-victorious creature who had — as ze did with everything — simply decided how the afternoon was going to go, and been right.

The lap was, in fact, warmer.

---
# 
Will typed the last line, read it twice, and hit save.

"Done," he said, exhaling — the specific exhale of a man released from a deadline, tension going out of his shoulders. "Okay. Finally. That's—"

"Done," Kyu agreed, from his lap, where ze had been the entire time. Warm. Patient. Not asleep, it turned out. Ze'd never been asleep. Ze'd just been _waiting,_ the way a creature with all the time in the world waits, and now that the file was saved ze tipped zir head back against his shoulder and Will felt, rather than saw, the smug arrive.

"You waited," he said slowly.

"I had time." Ze shifted in his lap, deliberate now, none of the sleepy weight from before — that had been the patience, and the patience was over. "You needed to finish. You get all — twitchy — if you don't. Can't enjoy anything till the work's done. Economy-class guilt." Ze turned, in his lap, so ze was facing him, arms looping around his neck, the warmth of before turning into something with intent behind it. "So I let you finish."

"That's almost considerate—"

"It was _entirely_ considerate. I'm a considerate person." The corner of the mouth. "But it's done now. You saved it. I watched you save it." Ze leaned in, close, and Will's hands had somehow found zir waist without being told to. "So there's no deadline anymore. No 'ten minutes.' Nothing to hide behind." A pause, deadly, warm. "And I've been sitting on you for an hour being _very_ patient, neighbor. I'd like that noted."

"You stole my coffee. That's not patient—"

"I'm collecting now. The coffee was interest." And ze kissed him, unhurried, the whole hour of waiting folded into it — and Will, deadline dead, work saved, no guilt left to run interference and no reason on earth to be decent about it anymore, stopped pretending the chair was where either of them wanted to be.

The laptop got closed. Gently — ze reached over and did it, because even now, even mid, the provision reflex ran, and a dropped laptop would be a problem. Then ze pulled him up out of the chair by the collar, and the afternoon went exactly where it had been going since the moment ze first climbed into his lap and decided to wait.

The deadline had waited an hour. Ze'd waited the same hour. Only one of them had been rewarded for it, and it wasn't the deadline.

---
# The toy
It happened because Will was being helpful, which was, as usual, his first mistake.

Kyu was out at a coaching session. Will was tidying — he did that, the domestic reflex, the one household front ze _let_ him hold — and in the course of tidying he went to move a box down from the top of the bedroom closet. A shoebox-adjacent thing, unlabeled, heavier than expected. He got a hand under it, misjudged the weight, and the box tipped, and its entire contents came down onto the bedroom floor in a single comprehensive cascade.

Will looked at what was now spread across the rug.

Will sat down on the edge of the bed.

Because he _knew._ He'd known since day one. The wall had told him, the toilet-paper night had confirmed it, ze referenced it with the flat neutrality of discussing groceries — the fact of the collection was not new information. He was not scandalized. He was way, way past scandalized.

What he was, sitting there, was — for the first time in the entire relationship — actually _confronted with the inventory._ Knowing a thing abstractly and seeing the full range of it fanned out across your bedroom floor in the afternoon light are, it turns out, different experiences, and Will was having the second one.

He picked one up. He didn't fully mean to. He just — there was a _variety,_ is the thing, a genuine _breadth,_ and some part of his brain that had spent months being a competent adult was now going _huh_ at a volume he couldn't suppress. He turned it over. He put it down. He picked up a different one and had a more involved _huh._ He was not judging. He was _studying,_ the way you'd study a well-organized toolbox that belonged to someone clearly more accomplished at their craft than you'd fully appreciated.

"This is a _range,_" he said, out loud, to the empty apartment. "This is a whole — this is very thorough. Why is this one—" he squinted, "—I don't even know what this one—"

The door opened.

"I'm back, the kid actually landed a—" Kyu came around the corner into the bedroom and stopped, and took in the scene: the tipped box, the contents fully deployed across the rug, and Will sitting on the edge of the bed holding one of them and frowning at it with the intense scholarly focus of a man doing genuine research.

There was a silence.

"...You dropped my box," Kyu observed.

"I was _tidying,_ it fell, I—" Will realized what he was holding and did not put it down fast enough. "I wasn't — I know about these. I've always known. I'm not—"

"You're _examining_ them." The corner of the mouth was already going. "You've got the face. The forensic face. The one you did about the wall, and the sexuality, and the — Will, are you _studying_ my sex toys on the bedroom floor like a _ticket queue?_"

"I've never _seen_ them all at once!" Will said, with the wounded dignity of a man caught mid-research. "I knew _conceptually._ This is — this is different, this is the _full collection,_ there's a — there's genuinely a lot of _thought_ here, this is more organized than my _actual_ work files—"

"It's more organized because I care about it more than you care about your work files." Kyu crossed the room, entirely unbothered, and crouched to start collecting them back up — and then, catching Will's face, decided instead to make it so much worse. "You want the tour?"

"_No._"

"You _clearly_ want the tour. You're holding the — okay, you don't even know what that one is, I can tell." Ze plucked it out of his hand, held it up, and explained it, flatly, in the exact tone ze'd use to explain a git merge, and Will made a sound and put his face in his hands. "See? Educational. You've lived with me over a year and you're _still_ learning things. That's a healthy relationship."

"I was just going to _put the box back_—"

"And instead you sat down and did a _deep dive._" Ze was thoroughly enjoying this, gathering the collection back into the box with the brisk competence of a person entirely at peace with owning it. "It's fine. It's a good collection. I've refined it over years. I'm not going to be weird about you seeing it, because I'm not weird about anything, which you _know,_ which is why I don't understand the—" ze gestured at his scarlet face, "—the whole _this._"

"The _this_ is that it fell on me with no warning and there was a _lot_ of it—"

"There's a _reasonable_ amount of it." Ze set the last one in the box. "For someone with my — appetite. It's proportional. Anything less would be under-equipped." Ze stood, box under one arm, and looked down at Will with the full smug now, radiant. "You knew day one. You've heard the results through a wall for a _year._ And _this_ is the thing that finally makes you sit down." Ze leaned in. "You're adorable. You want the tour or not, offer expires when I put the box back up."

"...Put the box back up."

"Coward." Ze put the box back up — on a shelf Will could reach, he noted, which felt pointed — and left him sitting on the edge of the bed, thoroughly educated, mildly changed, and married in all but paperwork to the most thorough, unbothered, well-equipped creature on the third floor.

"For the record," Will called after ze, "your organizational system _is_ impressive."

"I _know_ it is," came the reply from the kitchen. "Better than your spreadsheets. Everything I care about is."

---
# The marrige
The word had been floating around the edges of things for a while — Will had walked into it himself once, that "*fine, then—_married_" that hung in the air a beat too long back during the joint-account war. Neither of them had picked it up. It just sat there, in the room, a thing they both heard and let pass.

Because functionally, it was already true, and they both knew it. Ze had never given up zir own apartment across the hall, on paper — but zir stuff had migrated into Will's by degrees until the second unit was a formality, a storage annex, a place the record crates _weren't._ They shared a bed, a kitchen, a coffee machine ze wouldn't let him touch, a niece, a whole family, a building's worth of neighbors who'd decided they were a unit years ago. They fought over who paid the bills. They'd met each other's parents. The floor had a _betting pool_ on their sex life. There was, structurally, nothing left of marriage they hadn't already done except the document.

Will brought it up once. Carefully. Two-week-face carefully.

"So we're basically—"

"Don't," Kyu said, not looking up.

"I'm just _observing_—"

"You're doing the two-week face and you've decided something and you're building a runway." Ze turned a page. "The answer's the same as the joint account. That's a married-couple thing. And we're not—" a pause, "—we haven't done the _thing._"

"We've done every single thing _except_ the thing. You won't share a bank account because it's 'a married thing,' but we're _already_ married in every way that isn't a form—"

"Then we don't need the form."

"That's not — Kyu, that's _backwards,_ if we're already doing all of it then the form is just—"

"The form is a _big deal,_" Kyu said, and there it was, the flat thing carrying something underneath it, the way it did when ze was about to say a real thing badly. "That's the one that's — everything else I can do sideways. Provide, stay, keep you, all of it, without ever having to — stand up and _announce_ it. The form makes you stand up. In front of people. And _say_ it. On purpose. Out loud." A beat. "You know how I am about saying things out loud."

And Will did know. It was the whole shape of zir — the provision that never gets named, the care smuggled under jokes, the _I know_ instead of _I love you too,_ the compliment allergy, the fleeing a room of praise. Marriage was the one thing you couldn't do in the silent grammar. Marriage made you _declare._ And declaration was the single register ze had no armor for.

"...Okay," Will said, gently, letting it go. "We don't need the form."

"We don't need the form," ze agreed, relieved.

And they didn't, for a long time. They just kept being married in all but paperwork — the bills, the bed, the family, the floor — and let the document stay unsigned, because forcing ze up in front of people to say the thing out loud would've cost ze more than the paper was worth, and Will had learned, long ago, that you don't make ze declare. You let ze say it the way ze says everything.

Which is why it mattered, later — much later, and this part Will didn't push, ze got there on zir own — when ze came home one evening, flat as anything, set something down on the counter, and said, not looking at him, in the most offhand voice ze owned:

"Did the paperwork. It's a good tax situation, apparently. Efficient." A pause. "You have to sign the other half. It's on the counter."

Will looked at the papers. Looked at zir — who was already very busy putting groceries away, back turned, ears going faintly red, having done the one thing ze couldn't do out loud by doing it the only way ze could: as _logistics._ As a tax matter. As a thing that appeared on the counter without ceremony, the way food appeared at doors, the way bills got quietly handled, the way ze had said every real thing ze'd ever said.

Will didn't make it a thing. He'd learned.

"Efficient," he agreed, picking up the pen, and signed the other half, and let ze keep pretending it was about the taxes — which was, from zir, the loudest _I love you_ the paperwork would ever hold.

The floor found out within a week. The betting pool paid out enormously. Margarethe cried. Klaus updated the tally to _married._ And ze never once said the word out loud, and never had to, because ze'd already said it the only way ze knew how — on the counter, as a good tax situation, no ceremony, no declaration, just done.

---
# 

Will's parents were the easy call, because Will's family ran on the normal machinery — a phone call, his mother crying the good kind of tears, his father saying "well" in the tone that meant approval, and a lunch invitation Will could schedule and survive. His mother had, of course, slipped ze her number months ago and threatened to team up; the marriage just made the alliance official. Easy. Done in an afternoon.

zir side was the one that mattered, and ze put it off for exactly as long as ze could, which Will clocked immediately.

"You told my parents," Will said. "You haven't told yours."

"My parents don't need telling." Flat. True. "They provided. They weren't _around._ We're on fine terms, they'll hear eventually, they'll send something nice. It's not—" ze shrugged, "—they're not the ones."

"The siblings are the ones."

"The siblings are the ones." And there it was again — the thing marriage kept dragging into the open, the _declaring._ Because telling Jason and the sister wasn't a formality. They weren't people ze was on fine distant terms with. They were the ones ze'd raised, half-raised, looked after through the whole divorced-parents childhood while the money came in and the parents didn't. Telling them meant saying a real thing to the people who'd know exactly how big it was.

Ze did it, in the end, the way ze did everything hard — sideways, and by accident on purpose.

It was a video call, ostensibly about Mai's birthday. The sister and Jason both on, Mai bobbing in frame. And ze let the logistics of the party run for a while, and then, in the exact offhand voice ze'd used at the counter, dropped it in like it was nothing:

"Also I got married. So."

The call _detonated._

"You— WHAT—" the sister, instantly, at volume. "You got — to _Will?_ When? WHEN did — was there a — you didn't _tell_ us, you didn't—"

"Telling you now."

"That's not — Kyu, that's a _wedding,_ you don't just—"

"There wasn't a wedding. We did paperwork. Good tax situation." Ze was already retreating into the logistics, the shield going up. "It's not a big—"

"It IS a big deal, it's the _biggest_ deal, you have NEVER—" The sister was near tears now, the real kind. "You never stayed with _anyone,_ you never — and you got _married_ and you told us like it was a _dentist appointment_—"

And Jason, who'd been quiet — Jason, who ze'd looked after most, who knew ze best of anyone on that screen — cut through it, and his voice had something in it the sister's didn't.

"Hey." Everyone quieted. Jason looked at zir through the camera, steady. "You okay? Genuinely. Not the tax thing. You — married someone. That's — you don't do that. You take care of everyone and you never let anyone—" he stopped, found it, "—you never let anyone take care of _you._ And you married a guy who fights you to buy the groceries." A beat. "Did you actually let someone in? For real?"

The call went quiet. Ze looked, for once, like ze didn't have the sideways exit — because Jason had named the exact thing, the thing ze couldn't logistics zir way out of, asked plainly by the one person who'd grown up watching ze provide for everyone and receive from no one.

"...Yeah," ze said finally. Quiet. Flat, but real under it. "Yeah. I did."

The sister burst fully into tears. Mai asked what was happening. And Jason just nodded, slow, the older-brother-raised-by-the-sibling nodding at the sibling who'd finally, after a whole life of taking care of everyone else, let somebody take care of zir back.

"Good," Jason said. "About time. He seems like he can handle you." A beat, and then the warmth cracked into the family register, the teasing, the mercy of it. "Does he know what he signed up for? Did you disclose the—"

"He's known everything since day one. Thin walls."

"—oh my god, _still_ with the thin walls—"

"He signed anyway," ze said, and there was the smug, finally, back on solid ground, the moment survived. "Fully informed. No refunds."

"We're coming over," the sister announced, wiping her face, already organizing. "All of us. To celebrate. You don't get out of this one, you didn't get a wedding so you're getting a _dinner,_ I'm not negotiating—"

"You're going to make it a whole—"

"A WHOLE THING, yes, obviously—"

And ze let them. Grumbling, flat, pretending to hate it — but ze let the sister plan the dinner and let Jason bring Mai and let the family make a thing out of the thing ze'd tried to deliver like a tax update, because they were the ones, and the ones got to make it a thing, and that was the price of having told them.

Mai, in the background, not fully understanding but wanting in: "Is Uncle Will my uncle now too?"

"Yeah," ze said. "He's your uncle now."

"Can he teach me things?"

"He can't cook," ze said, "so no. But he's rich, so be nice to him."

"KYU," Will said, from off-camera, and the whole call laughed, and it was, everyone agreed later, the best non-wedding anyone had ever thrown.

---
# The dinner

The dinner was at Will's — their — apartment, which meant it was too small, which the sister had been warned about and ignored.

She arrived first, with wine and a bag she wouldn't explain. Jason and Mai came twenty minutes later, Mai already talking. Ronan showed up because Ronan was family now by attrition, and Lena because Lena had a sixth sense for these, and within an hour the apartment held more people than it was built for and the wall was doing its usual work of ensuring the whole floor knew there was a party.

"It's _small,_" the sister said, delighted, handing Kyu the wine. "You married a millionaire and you live in a _shoebox._ Why."

"Thin walls," said Will, from the kitchen, and got a laugh he didn't understand the full size of.

Kyu was doing the thing ze did at gatherings — ostensibly hosting, actually just cooking, food appearing without anyone seeing ze start. The sister followed ze into the kitchen with the specific persistence of someone who'd waited weeks for a private minute.

"Okay, but seriously." Lower now, under the noise. "Married. You. I keep saying it to see if it sounds real. It doesn't." She leaned on the counter. "I'm not going to make you do a moment. I know you hate the moment. I just—" she stopped, uncharacteristically. "I'm happy. That's all. I'm really happy about it."

"I know." Kyu didn't look up from the pan.

"That's it? _I know?_"

"What do you want me to say."

"Nothing. That's the whole point, you never say anything—" the sister huffed, and then, giving up on the moment because ze wasn't going to give her one, went for the other thing: "Fine. Then I'm giving my speech at dinner instead, and you can't stop me, and it's going to be _devastating,_ and you're going to sit there and take it."

"Please don't—"

"Too late. It's written. I have notes."

There was, in fact, a speech.

She stood up halfway through dinner, wine in hand, over zir flat "oh no," and did not deliver the tearful thing everyone braced for. She'd learned, apparently — she went the other way, and roasted ze instead, because that ze could survive.

"I just want to say," she started, "that when we were kids, this one—" she pointed at zir, "—used to do our laundry, and cook, and forge our mom's signature on permission slips, and once threatened a teacher who was being unfair to Jason, at _twelve,_ and never once let any of us thank ze for a single thing. Ze'd just get mad. 'It's not a thing.' Everything was 'not a thing.'" She raised the glass. "So the fact that ze found somebody who fights ze to buy the _groceries_ — who ze _lets_ fight ze — that's the miracle. Not the marriage. The marriage is whatever. The miracle is Will figured out how to take care of zir and ze didn't run him off." A beat. "So to Will. Bravest man I've ever met. Genuinely no idea what he's in for."

"He knows exactly what he's in for," Jason said. "He's known since day one."

"THIN WALLS," half the table said at once, and lost it.

Will stood, because it seemed required, and was not a natural at speeches and didn't try to be.

"I don't have notes," he said. "I was going to say something about the wall but you've all — the wall's covered." He looked at zir, sitting there flat and braced for sincerity. "I'll just say — I know ze doesn't like this part. So I'll keep it short." He raised his glass. "I got really lucky with my apartment. That's it. That's the whole speech. Thanks for the terrible building."

Which was exactly calibrated — funny enough that ze didn't have to flee, true underneath — and ze gave him a look across the table that was half _thank you for not making me survive a real one_ and half something warmer that ze'd never name.

Mai, who had been waiting, stood on her chair. "I have a speech."

"Oh no," said Jason.

"Uncle Will is my uncle now," Mai announced, to the room, with total gravity. "He's rich but he can't cook. And Uncle Kyu is going to teach me things. And Dad said I can't franchise the lock thing but Uncle Kyu said we'll see." She sat back down, business concluded. "That's my speech."

"We are NOT—" Jason started.

"We'll see," Kyu said, over him, and Mai beamed, and Jason put his face in his hands, and the whole table came apart, and that was the closest anyone got to a toast that made ze uncomfortable — deflected, survived, turned into the kid.

Later, when it thinned out — Lena gone, Ronan poured into a cab, Mai asleep on the couch against Jason's side, just the sister lingering at the door — she hugged ze, which ze allowed, stiffly.

"You didn't make me do a moment," she said into zir shoulder. "I'm proud of me for that."

"You did a whole speech."

"A _roast._ Different." She pulled back. "I'm still happy. I'm not going to stop being happy about it just because you won't say anything back."

"Good," Kyu said. Which was, from zir, the entire thing.

The sister grinned, got it, took the win, and left. And ze closed the door on the wreckage of the best non-wedding anyone had thrown, and Will came up beside zir holding two glasses of the last wine, and neither of them said anything, because the day had already said all of it, in every grammar but the one ze'd never use.

---
# The ring

Will did not do the knee. He'd thought about it — for about four seconds — and then pictured zir face, the flat _what are you doing down there,_ and understood that a proposal, a real one, a _moment,_ would send ze straight through the wall and possibly the building. Ze had already married him via the counter, as a tax situation. The theatrics were not the way in.

So he did it wrong on purpose, which was the only way to do it right.

They were on the couch. Ordinary evening. Ze was doing something on zir phone, half-sprawled against him, and Will had been carrying the small box in his pocket for two days waiting for a moment that wasn't a Moment, and this was as un-momentous as it got, so he just — took it out, and set it on zir stomach, and went back to watching the TV.

Kyu looked down at the box sitting on zir sternum. Looked at Will, who was pointedly not looking back.

"...What's this."

"Ring. You should have one." He kept his eyes on the screen, matching zir own grammar back at zir, flat, offhand, a thing set down without ceremony. "We did the paperwork already. This is just — the visual version. It's efficient. Stops people at the bar."

A pause. He felt ze go still against him.

"You're doing my thing," Kyu said. "You're doing the — the _set it down like it's nothing_ thing. That's mine. You stole my move."

"Yeah, well. It's a good move. Works on you." He risked a glance. "Open it or don't. No pressure. It's just there now. Like the toilet paper. Full circle."

Ze opened it. And Will watched the flat face do the thing it did when something got past the armor — the small crack, the ears going faintly red — because he'd handed ze the one version of this ze could actually receive: no knee, no speech, no _will you,_ no forcing ze to say a word back. Just an object, set down, framed as logistics, meaning everything underneath exactly the way ze meant everything.

It was a plain band. He'd thought about that too — nothing flashy, nothing that announced itself, because ze wore four shirts and drove nothing and would've hated a thing that _performed._ Just a good, simple, durable ring, the kind you could box in and bartend in and forget you were wearing until you spun it.

"It's not — you didn't have to—" ze started, the reflex, the deflection.

"I know I didn't have to. I wanted to. Different thing." He took the box back gently, took the ring out, and held it — not making a production, just holding it, offering. "You don't have to say anything. I know saying things isn't — I'm not asking for a speech. I just want you to have it. That's the whole ask. Give me the hand."

And ze — who could not declare, who fled sincerity, who'd married him as a tax matter specifically to avoid this exact register — gave him the hand. Wordless. Which was zir entire vocabulary for the biggest things, and Will knew it, and took the hand, and put the ring on it, and didn't make _that_ a moment either, just did it, quiet, done.

Ze looked at zir own hand for a second. Turned it. The flat face working around something.

"...It's practical," ze said finally, hoarse, reaching for the exit ze always reached for. "The band. No stone to catch on anything. You thought about the — I could wear this at work. You picked one I could actually—"

"I thought about it. Yeah."

"That's—" ze stopped. Started again. Couldn't quite get to _thank you,_ which ze didn't do, or _I love it,_ which ze couldn't say, and landed instead where ze always landed, the realest thing in the flimsiest wrapping: "...I'm keeping it on. Even at the bar. I'll figure out the wrap thing. I'm keeping it on."

Which, from zir — _I'm keeping it on_ — was the entire yes. The whole vow. Said the only way ze had.

Will didn't make it a thing. He put the empty box on the coffee table, and pulled ze back against him, and went back to the TV, and let ze sit there turning the ring on zir finger, quietly, over and over, for the rest of the night — the smug entirely gone, replaced by something ze'd never name and didn't have to, because he already knew.

The floor found out when ze showed up at the bar with a ring and cut zir flirting-deflection time by eighty percent, and was, for the record, insufferably smug about the _efficiency._ But that was later. That night it was just the two of them, and a plain band ze wouldn't take off, and the one thing ze couldn't say, said anyway, in the only grammar that was ever going to hold it.

---
# The husband

Will discovered that he was, now, a husband — and never fully recovered from the discovery.

It started reasonably. A form asked his marital status and he ticked _married_ and felt something warm and stupid about it. That would've been fine if he'd left it there. He did not leave it there.

"I'm a married man now," he informed a coworker who had not asked.

"Can't, I've got a husband to get home to," he told a client, glowing, about a meeting that ran late.

"As a _husband,_" he began a sentence to the barista, for no reason, apropos of a coffee order.

Kyu clocked the pattern inside a week.

"You're doing a thing," ze said over dinner. "You keep announcing you're a husband. To strangers. The barista doesn't need your marital status with the oat milk."

"I'm just _being_ one," Will said, radiant, unrepentant. "I'm a husband. It's accurate. You married me. I'm allowed to enjoy the job title."

"You're _savoring_ it. You get the whole—" ze gestured at his face, "—_look._ Every time. All pleased with yourself. 'As a husband.' Nobody talks like that."

"I talk like that. I earned it." He said it directly at zir, on purpose. "I get to be somebody's husband. Your husband, specifically. That's — do you understand what a good deal that is for me? I'd put it on a business card."

"Do not put it on a—"

"_Will Reeves. Husband._ Company logo underneath, small—"

And there it was, the crack, the ears going faintly red — because it wasn't Will being embarrassing in public that got ze. It was that every gleeful "I'm a husband" was a tiny declaration, sideways, indirect: _I chose this person and I'm delighted enough to tell the mailman._ He wasn't saying it _at_ ze. He was walking around the world visibly thrilled to be married to zir, and ze had to _witness_ it, which was so much worse than being told, because there was no armor for watching someone you love be that openly happy about having you.

"You can't even hear it," Will realized, leaning in, delighted. "It's not even _about_ you and it still gets you. I just say I'm happy to be married and you go pink."

"It's a _lot,_" ze muttered, aggrieved, caught. "You say it like it — everyone else says 'married' like a fact. You say it like you won a prize. In front of the _barista._"

"I did win a prize. That's exactly what it is." He was thriving, having found the one loophole where he got to speak his own language and ze couldn't shut it down — because objecting to your spouse being happy to be your spouse is impossible, and ze knew it. "I learned to do everything your way. The counter, the not-making-it-a-thing, the ring-as-workflow. I speak fluent quiet. But _this_—" he grinned, "—me being obnoxiously glad I'm your husband? That's mine. You can't take it. We're legally—"

"I regret the tax situation," ze announced to the ceiling.

"You don't. It was efficient. Made me a husband and everything." He patted zir hand, victorious. "Eat your dinner. My spouse can't cook, but ze packed this, so."

"_Will._"

"Happily married man, can't hear you—"

Ze put zir face in zir hands, red to the ears, thoroughly gotten — and didn't try very hard to make him stop, which was, from zir, the whole quiet yes under the mortification.

---
# # The grievance

The leadership meeting ended at four, and Will did not leave the conference room, which was the signal — known to all senior staff — that the CEO had a _personal item_ on the agenda.

Daniel, who had survived years of these, sat back down. Lena, who enjoyed them, poured herself the last of the coffee. Priya from design, newer to the inner circle, made the mistake of asking if everything was okay.

"I want to workshop something," Will said, in the exact tone he used for quarterly strategy. "A rejection I received. I need outside perspective, because I've reviewed the facts repeatedly and I believe the ruling was wrong."

"Board thing?" Priya asked.

"Marriage thing," said Daniel and Lena, in unison, without looking up.

"I proposed—" Will laid his hands flat on the table, a man presenting evidence, "—that I take Kyu's surname. William _Valentine._ I made a complete case. Romantic precedent. The matched-set principle. I had _momentum._ And ze—" a pause, the wound still fresh, "—_left the apartment._ Mid-pitch. Got zir keys and physically left the premises. I got a ruling from the hallway. The ruling was 'no, and you're not allowed to bring it to the wedding anniversary either, it's not agenda-eligible.'"

Silence. Priya looked at Daniel. Daniel's face communicated _do not engage._ She engaged.

"So... you keep your name? That's the whole—" She stopped. Recalibrated. "I'm sorry, most people would — that's not a _rejection_, that's just—"

"It is the _withholding of a name I have earned through marriage,_" Will said, with tremendous dignity. "Ze gets to be Valentine. Every day. The bit, the holiday, the whole saga — and I'm expected to stand _adjacent_ to the name, like a — like a _contractor._ I asked for shared branding and I was denied shared branding. From my own _spouse._"

"You're a CEO," Priya said faintly. "Your name is on the _lease._ Of this _building._"

"The building isn't _romantic,_ Priya."

Lena, who had been waiting, leaned toward Priya and delivered the context quietly, like a museum guide: "Ze said — and we've confirmed this through channels — 'the name is earned, he can have the husband, the surname stays with the original holder.' There's also a notepad ze confiscated. Signature practice. _W. Valentine_, three styles. It's considered evidence in the ongoing case."

"The notepad was _private_—"

"Ze kept it, though," Daniel said, not looking up from his phone. "Tell her the part where ze kept it."

Will paused. The grievance flickered, briefly, into something else — the thing it actually was underneath, which everyone at the table could see and he could not.

"...Ze kept it," he admitted. "It's in a drawer. Ze thinks I don't know which one."

Priya looked around the table at the assembled leadership of a nine-figure company, gathered at four p.m. on a Thursday to hear their CEO litigate being told he couldn't have a _cuter last name_, his own name notwithstanding, and arrived at the question she would later describe to the design team as the moment she understood everything about this company:

"And you're... sulking about the _rejection?_ When ze kept the — sir, ze _kept the signatures_ —" she gestured, helpless, at the entire situation, "—what _is_ this marriage."

"Compounding," said Daniel and Lena, in unison, and closed their laptops, because the meeting, as ever, had resolved itself.

Will sat back, somewhat restored, already redrafting the pitch for next year — and the following morning, on his desk, with no note and no acknowledgment available for purchase, sat the notepad. Returned. Open to a fresh page, on which, in a flat, familiar hand, someone had written a single line and left it there like a ruling:

_Some Reeves. — K. Valentine_

He framed it. It's in his office. He tells people it's an internal memo, and it is.

---
# New bed

Will looked at the apartment one Sunday with fresh eyes and arrived at a conclusion.

"We need new furniture."

Kyu did not look up from zir book. "We have furniture."

"We have furniture that came from a curb, an estate sale, and my college years." Will surveyed it. "The couch is fine. The chair — the free chair, which I've accepted — is fine, actually good, I've made my peace. But the _bed,_ Kyu. We have to talk about the bed."

"What's wrong with the bed."

"It's a bed you found. It's older than our relationship. It makes a noise." He was building his case with the methodical energy of a man who had clearly been thinking about this. "And — this is the thing — I have money. Real money. And we spend a _significant_ amount of time in that bed. Arguably it's the single most-used object in this household. And it _creaks_ and it's _small_ and half the mattress has a—"

"So buy a bed." Ze turned a page. "You've been building up to it for ten minutes like I'm going to fight you. Just buy a bed."

Will paused, thrown. "...You're not going to fight me on this."

"Why would I fight you on a bed." Ze finally looked up, flat. "You want to spend your guilt money on the one piece of furniture we actually both use, constantly, that's currently _loud_ and _too small?_ That's not extravagance, that's maintenance. That's the most _sensible_ thing you've ever wanted to buy." A beat. "The espresso machine was insane. A commercial unit for two people. This is a bed. Beds are load-bearing. Literally." Ze went back to the book. "Get a good one. Get the expensive one, even. I don't care about most things but I'll notice a bed."

Will stood there, genuinely disarmed, because he'd prepared for the grocery-war version of this — the fight, the ambush, the "we have a bed, it's fine, stop being rich at me." And instead ze had, with total consistency, evaluated it on the one axis ze evaluated everything: _is it actually useful._ A commercial espresso machine for two people was absurd. A good bed for two people who lived a very physical life was, to zir, simply correct engineering.

"So this whole time," Will said slowly, "the reason you fought me on everything wasn't that you hate me spending money. It's that you thought the _specific things_ were dumb."

"Obviously." Ze didn't look up. "I don't care about money. I care about _waste._ The machine was waste. The bed's not. You want to spend, spend on things that earn it. A bed earns it every single night." The corner of the mouth. "Twice on Sundays."

"_Kyu._"

"Availability-based." Ze turned another page. "Get one that doesn't creak, while you're at it. Give the floor a break. Klaus deserves it."

So Will bought a bed. A very good one — the kind of purchase that would've triggered his father's _must be nice_ voice, except this time he had a defense, and the defense was that ze had _approved_ it, on the grounds of sound engineering, which somehow made the money guilt evaporate entirely. Ze fought him on tacos and lost him no sleep buying a serious bed. He was still working out the logic of zir and probably always would be.

It arrived. It was excellent. It did not creak. It was large enough that ze could sprawl fully and still not reach the edges, which ze tested immediately, starfished across the middle of it with the satisfaction of a cat claiming new territory.

"Okay," ze admitted, from the center of the new bed. "Yeah. This was a good call. This earns it." Ze patted the enormous empty space beside zir, an invitation and a verdict at once. "Come here. We're testing it properly. For the floor's sake — new bed, they'll want a status report."

"You said give the floor a break—"

"That was before the bed was this good." Ze was entirely unbothered, entirely shameless, entirely at home. "Get in. Husband. We're doing quality assurance."

Will got in. The bed passed inspection, comprehensively, and the floor — one imagines — noted the new acoustics with professional interest and no complaint at all.

---
# The joint account
It happened casually, the way the biggest concessions from zir always did — no ceremony, dropped into a Tuesday like it was nothing.

"Fine," ze said, apropos of a bill Will had just ambushed. "The joint account. Set it up. It's stupid doing it separately now anyway, we're married, it's a tax—"

"It's efficient," Will finished, delighted, already reaching for his laptop before ze could reconsider. "You're doing it because it's efficient."

"I'm doing it because you lunge for every check like a raccoon and it's undignified and I've decided to make the raccoon-ing our shared problem instead of my problem." Ze shrugged. "Set it up. Both incomes in. Whatever."

And that was the phrase — _both incomes in_ — that led, an hour later, to Will sitting very still in front of the account setup, looking at zir numbers for the first time.

Because he'd never seen them. That was the thing. In the whole relationship, ze had guarded the money the way ze guarded everything — not out of secrecy, just because it never came up, because ze fought to _not_ spend and refused to be provided for and lived like a person with four shirts. Will had built a whole picture: modest income, three modest jobs, the surplus quietly bled off to the sister. He'd assumed ze got by. He'd assumed his money vastly outweighed zir, which was half of why the grocery war had felt so absurd to him.

The numbers said otherwise.

"Kyu."

"Mm."

"Your — the freelance IT income." Will was staring. "This is—"

"Yeah."

"This is a _lot._ This is — the contracts pay _this?_" He scrolled. "And the bar, and the coaching, on top — Kyu, this is — you make—" He did the math and the math surprised him. Not CEO money. He still out-earned ze, comfortably. But nowhere near the gap he'd assumed. Ze made _serious_ money, the kind that came from being genuinely, rarely good at a high-value thing and charging accordingly, and then simply... not spending any of it. "You've had this the whole time. This whole time you've been fighting me over _tacos_ and you had—"

"I told you the freelance was good." Ze wasn't even looking up. "I was a developer. A good one. I quit the salary, not the skill. People pay a lot for someone who can actually fix the thing, and I only take the contracts I want, so I charge what I want." A shrug. "It adds up. I don't spend it. Where would it go."

"The _sister,_" Will said, connecting it live. "And — and where's the _rest,_ because this is more than the sister's tuition, this is—"

"Saved. Invested, some. The stock options from the old job vested, I told Ronan, I'm not an idiot." Ze finally glanced over at his face and found it deeply entertaining. "Why do you look like that. Why do you look like you found out I've been _lying._ I never said I was poor. You _decided_ I was poor. You looked at the four shirts and the free chair and built a whole story."

"You _live_ like—"

"I live like a minimalist, remember. That's not the same as _can't afford things._" And there it was, the actual correction, delivered flat: not deprivation, never deprivation. Choice. Ze had the money. Ze had _always_ had the money. Ze wore four shirts because ze wanted four shirts, drove nothing because ze wanted nothing, took the curbside chair because the chair was genuinely excellent and free — not because the alternative was out of reach, but because the alternative was _pointless._ "I could out-furnish you if I cared about furniture. I don't care about furniture. That was never a budget thing. That was a _me_ thing."

Will sat with the full recalibration of it. Every assumption reorganizing at once. The grocery war hadn't been a broke person's pride, or a proud person refusing charity — it had been two people with money, one of whom simply had no interest in spending it and had claimed the provisioning as zir job. Ze wasn't letting Will pay because ze needed it. Ze wasn't refusing because ze couldn't reciprocate. Ze refused because ze _wanted_ to be the one who provided, and ze could afford the position, and had been affording it, invisibly, the entire time.

"You could've just — _had_ a nice apartment," Will said, faintly. "This whole time. You picked the thin walls too."

"For the records, this is a nice apartment." A pause, the corner of the mouth. "Best decision I ever made, incidentally. Cheap apartment, thin wall, some guilt-ridden millionaire moves in across the hall and does surveillance on me. Wouldn't have happened in a top building. Top buildings have _walls._" Ze went back to the laptop, closing the discussion. "Put both incomes in. Stop looking at my numbers like they betrayed you. And for the record—" the flat smug, landing, "—you married someone who out-invests you _and_ refuses to spend it _and_ fights you for the grocery bill. You didn't marry a project, neighbor. You married someone who could've bought their own espresso machine and chose not to because it was stupid." A beat. "The bed, though. The bed was correct. Keep making calls like the bed."

Will set up the joint account. Both incomes in. And spent the rest of the evening quietly restructuring his entire understanding of the person he'd married — who had never once been the one who _needed,_ who had chosen every modest thing on purpose, who had the means to want anything and simply, serenely, wanted almost nothing at all.

Except, apparently, him. And the bed. And, for the record, better coffee — which ze paid half of, now, from an account that turned out to be a great deal healthier than Will had ever guessed.

---
# Freelancer
Will had known, abstractly, that Kyu did IT freelance. He'd seen the income now, in the joint account, and been suitably humbled by it. What he had never actually _seen_ was ze doing the work — because ze did it on zir own hours, quietly, in the gaps, the same way ze did everything, and it had never once occurred to Will to watch.

He watched by accident. Came home early, and there was ze at the desk in the corner — not the couch, an actual desk, which Will realized he'd sort of forgotten ze had — with three monitors he definitely hadn't bought running walls of something Will couldn't read, and ze was _in_ it. Fully. Not the flat unbotherable surface ze wore everywhere else. Locked in, fingers moving fast, that total focus ze usually reserved for reading a room aimed instead at a screen full of code, muttering something under zir breath in what might have been one of the five languages.

Will stopped in the doorway and just looked, because he'd never seen this version.

Because here was the thing: he'd married the bartender, the coach, the shameless creature, the buddha of the third floor. He'd heard about the developer — Ronan's stories, the parking-lot exit, the refactor-out-of-spite legend. But hearing about it and watching it were different, and watching it, Will finally understood what Ronan had meant. Ze was _good._ Frighteningly good. The competence he'd seen in every other domain — the four drinks at once, the six-year-old's lock, the boxing form — was here too, except concentrated, aimed at something genuinely hard, and ze moved through it with the same unbothered ease ze brought to pouring a beer, which made the hard thing look like nothing, which was somehow the most impressive part.

"You're staring," Kyu said, not turning around, still typing.

"I've never seen you work. The real work. The—" Will gestured at the monitors. "I forgot you had a whole — you're just _good_ at this. Ronan said, but I—"

"I told you I was good. That's why the freelance pays." Ze finished a thought, hit something, and a wall of red on one monitor turned green, which even Will could tell was the point. "This is a contract. Somebody's system's been broken for three weeks, four other people couldn't fix it, they're paying me stupid money to make it stop. It'll take me the afternoon." Ze leaned back, finally, and looked at him. "That's the whole business. I only take the ones nobody else can do. Then I charge for the fact that nobody else could do it. It's not complicated."

"That's — Kyu, that's not a _normal_ freelance operation, that's—" Will came closer, looking at the screen he couldn't read. "You're the person they call when everyone else has failed. That's a specific, rare, extremely valuable—"

"It's a person who's good at a thing and doesn't want a boss." Ze shrugged, deflecting the size of it as always. "I did the salary version. Hated the meetings, hated the ladder, hated people deciding what I worked on. So now I just — take the interesting broken things, fix them, get paid, and go bartend, which is more fun anyway." A beat. "The bar's the job I _like._ This is the job that's _easy._ People always assume it's the other way around because this one pays more. It's not. This is just — this is nothing. This is a Tuesday afternoon." Ze turned back to the screen. "The bar's where I get to actually talk to people. This is where I get to be left alone. Both good. Different good."

And Will stood there recalibrating _again_ — because he'd assumed, like everyone, that the high-paying IT was the "real" career and the bartending was the whimsical side thing, and ze had just flatly informed him it was the reverse. Ze did the lucrative genius work because it was _easy_ and let ze not have a boss. Ze poured beers because ze genuinely liked it. The market valued them in exactly the opposite order to how ze did, and ze did not care even slightly, because ze had never once let the market tell ze what anything was worth.

"You're doing the face," Kyu said, typing again. "The recalibrating face. You do it every few months. You find out one more thing and you have to redo the whole model."

"I keep thinking I've got you figured out—"

"You never will. That's not an insult, it's just — I'm not hiding anything, there's just a lot, and you only find it when you happen to walk in on it." Green spread across another monitor. "Like this. You didn't know I had three monitors. They've been here the whole time. You just never came home early." Ze glanced back, the corner of the mouth. "Go make dinner. Badly. I'll be done in an hour and I'll come fix whatever you burn. This is the easy job, remember. You're the hard one."

Will went to make dinner. He burned it, mildly. Ze came out an hour later — contract finished, stupid money earned, entirely unbothered — and fixed it, and never mentioned the work again, because to zir it had been nothing, a Tuesday, the easy thing.

Will thought about it for days.

---
# 
Will now knew that Kyu at work — the real work, the locked-in concentrating version — was a problem. Specifically, a problem for him.

He hadn't expected it. But he'd seen ze in that focused state now, three monitors, fingers moving, that total attention aimed at something hard, and some traitor part of his brain had filed it under _unbearably attractive_ and would not let it go. It was the concentration itself. The furrow. The way ze went completely still and present and unbothered, muttering in some other language, wholly absorbed — a version of zir that wasn't performing anything for anyone, just _being good at a thing,_ and Will found it did something to him that he was frankly embarrassed about.

Which meant, of course, that he had to do something about it.

Because for the entire relationship, ze had been the one with the distraction advantage. Ze sat on his desk. Ze climbed into his lap mid-deadline. Ze came home from the gym especially unclothed while he had spreadsheets. Ze had spent years dismantling his productivity for sport, and Will had never once had a way to return fire — until now, until he'd discovered that ze, too, could be at a desk, concentrating, _interruptible._

Turnabout.

He started small. Wandered over while ze was mid-contract, leaned on the desk, casual. "How's it going."

"Don't." Ze didn't look up. "You've got a tone. I know that tone. That's your revenge tone."

"I don't have a—"

"You've been looking at me weird since you saw me work. You think it's _hot._" Flat, still typing, not remotely thrown. "You do. You've got a whole thing about the concentrating. I can feel you having it from across the room."

"...Okay, that's — you can't just _say_ that—"

"You did surveillance on me through a wall, Will, I can read you doing it across a _living room._" A wall of code turned green. "And now you want to do to me what I do to you. The desk thing. The distraction thing. Payback." Ze finally glanced over, and there was the smug, unbothered, several steps ahead as always. "Go ahead. Try it. I'd like to see it."

Which was, of course, a challenge, and Will was not strong enough to decline it.

So he tried. He did zir own moves back — leaned in close, dropped his voice, put a hand where a hand would be distracting, did the whole patient attentive thing that worked so well in _other_ rooms. And discovered, immediately, the flaw in his plan.

Ze didn't break.

Because concentrating-Kyu, it turned out, was a fortress. The same total focus that made ze good at the work made ze _immune_ to the distraction — ze registered every single thing Will did, acknowledged it, was even a little amused by it, and did not stop typing. The green kept spreading across the monitors. Will escalated. Ze noted the escalation, dryly, out loud — "that's a good one, that would've worked if I weren't three functions from done" — and kept working.

"This isn't _fair,_" Will said, thwarted, a man discovering his enemy had no exploitable weakness in this particular arena. "When you do this to me I _fold_ in like ninety seconds—"

"Because you don't actually want to work. You want an excuse to stop. I _like_ this part." Ze hit something; another monitor went green. "I don't want to be distracted, so I'm not. That's the whole difference. You're distractible because you're looking for the door. I don't have a door open." Two more lines. "Also — and this is the thing — I _invented_ this move. You're doing my move at me. I have complete immunity to my own move. That's just how immunity works."

"So there's _nothing_—"

"Finish the contract, then I'm all yours, and I'll be _very_ distractible, I promise." Ze said it flat, focused, not even a courtesy glance now. "Ten minutes. Wait ten minutes and you get the version of me that has nothing better to do. Try to distract me _now_ and you get nothing, because the work wins until it's done. Those are the rules. I made the rules. I'm very good at the rules."

Will slumped against the desk, defeated on yet another front, a man who had found the one arena where his own best weapon simply bounced off. And he waited — sulking, thwarted, watching the monitors go green one by one — because ze had, as usual, correctly identified that the only move available to him was patience, which was zir move too, which meant ze had beaten him with his own follow-up to zir original attack.

The contract finished. Green, all the way across. Ze leaned back, cracked zir neck, and turned in the chair with the full, unhurried, now-entirely-available attention of a person who had finished the work and kept zir word.

"Okay," ze said, the smug complete. "Now I'm distractible. You waited so nicely. Go on, husband. You had a whole plan. Let's see it."

Will had, in fact, forgotten most of the plan in the ten minutes of sulking, which ze found hilarious, and which cost him the upper hand entirely — but he improvised, and ze had promised to be distractible, and ze was, gloriously, a creature of zir word.

The floor got a status report. It was, they agreed, worth the ten-minute delay.

---
# 
Will, it must be said, was a _good_ sulker.

Not dramatic. He didn't announce it. He just went quietly, magnificently sullen — the specific sulk of a man who had been told to wait ten minutes and was going to make those ten minutes _known_ — and Kyu, working, could feel the whole weather system of it building against zir back.

"You're radiating," ze said, not turning around. "Stop radiating. I can feel you being sad about the ten minutes."

"I'm not sad. I'm _fine._" He was draped over the back of the couch now, chin on his arms, watching ze work with the wounded patience of a dog left outside a shop. "Take your time. Do your important little contract. I'll just be here. Waiting. Having had a whole plan."

"You forgot the plan."

"I _had_ a plan. Present tense doesn't matter." A pause. Then, unable to help himself: "This is what you do to me, you know. This exact — you make me wait, you make it look easy, and I just have to _sit_ here—"

"Now you know." Green spread across a monitor. Ze was thoroughly enjoying the sulk, Will could tell, which only made it worse. "It's character-building. You're building character. Two more functions."

"You've said two more functions _twice._"

"And both times it was true. This is a third, separate two-more-functions." Ze glanced back, just once, caught the full sullen slump of him, and the smug bloomed. "God, you're _pathetic_ about this. Look at you. You're pouting. The CEO. Draped over a couch pouting because ze wouldn't stop working to pay attention to you." Ze turned back, delighted. "This is the best thing I've seen all week. I might slow down. Might add a function. Just to watch."

"You _wouldn't_—"

"I would. I'd bill the extra time, too. Efficient." Typing, radiant. "Keep sulking. It's motivating. I work faster when I know there's a sad husband on the couch who's going to be _so_ relieved when I turn around."

And that was the thing — the sulk didn't work as leverage, because ze _fed_ on it, converted his impatience directly into fuel and smugness, so that the more Will pouted the more delighted ze got and the less inclined ze was to hurry. It was a perfectly closed loop designed to torture him, and ze ran it with total serenity, and Will lay there and _took_ it, because there was no other option, because ze held every card in this particular room.

"I hate that you like the sulking," he informed the ceiling.

"I _love_ the sulking. It's my favorite thing you do besides the other thing." Green, green, one amber warning left. "Almost done. For real this time. And then—" ze let it hang, cruel, "—you'll have to remember your plan. Which you forgot. So really the sulk cost you the plan, which means the sulk cost you the whole revenge, which means I win this _twice._ Once for the contract, once for the plan." The last warning went green. "Done."

---
#
Kyu found Will still draped over the couch after the contract, sulky, and decided — because ze kept zir word, and because a sad husband was, on reflection, zir own doing — that he'd earned a reward and not another minute of waiting.

"Alright." Ze crossed the room, took his chin, tipped his face up out of the sulk. "You waited. You forgot your whole plan doing it. That's tragic, and it's a little my fault, so." Ze pulled him up off the couch by the front of his shirt, unhurried, entirely in charge. "Making it up to you. Come here."

"You said I'd have to remember the plan—"

"Change of plans. My plan now. Better than yours anyway, yours was improvised, mine's _considered._" Ze walked him backward toward the bedroom — the new bed, the good one, the one that didn't creak — and there was none of the making-him-wait left in ze now, just the shift into the register ze only ran with him, the smug melting down into something warmer and more focused. "You've been very patient. Patience gets rewarded in this house. That's the rule."

"You made the rules—"

"I make excellent rules." Ze got the door with a foot, got him onto the bed, and set about actually making it up to him — and this time ze wasn't the receiving end, this time ze was the one paying attention, turning all that formidable focus, the same total absorption Will had found so unbearably attractive at the desk, _onto him_ instead of a screen.

And that undid Will faster than anything, because he'd spent the whole afternoon wanting exactly this — ze concentrating, locked in, that furrow, that stillness — and now it was aimed entirely at him, deliberate and unhurried and thorough, ze bringing to _this_ the same frightening competence ze brought to the work, the drinks, the everything. Ze read him the way ze read code. Found every reaction, used it, and did not rush, because ze had decided the reward was going to be _comprehensive_ and ze was, as ever, a creature of zir word.

"This is—" Will got out, already coming apart, "—this is what you were doing at the desk, the _focus,_ you're doing it at _me_—"
