"You said you liked it." Low, unbothered, entirely absorbed in the task. "The concentrating. So." Ze didn't look up from what ze was doing. "Concentrating. On you. All of it. That's the reward. Try to keep up."

He did not keep up. That was the point. Ze took him apart with the patient, merciless, total attention ze'd denied him for the ten minutes of the contract, and gave him everything at once now, and Will — who'd sulked, who'd lost, who'd forgotten his plan — got the far better end of the deal in the end, because the reward for waiting was ze deciding to be _thorough,_ and ze was catastrophically good at thorough.

The bed, mercifully, did not creak. The floor noted the new acoustics with interest. And Will, wrecked and thoroughly compensated afterward, sprawled boneless while Kyu looked insufferably pleased with a job well done.

"See," ze said, warm, smug, victorious even in generosity. "Worth the ten minutes."

Will could not form the words to agree. Which was, ze decided, agreement enough.

---
# The bath
The bath was Will's idea, which meant it was, structurally, a mistake — because Will pictured something soft and domestic, and Kyu was constitutionally incapable of letting anything be simply soft when it could also be a whole ordeal about logistics.

"We should take a bath," Will said. "Together. It's a thing married couples do. It's supposed to be nice."

"Our tub's small."

"It's a fine tub."

"It's a _fine tub for one average person,_ and I'm not average-sized, and neither of us is small, and you want to put both of us in it." Ze considered the geometry with the flat pragmatism of an engineer reviewing a doomed spec. "This is going to be a whole negotiation. Someone's back is going to be against the taps. It's going to be _me,_ isn't it. You've already decided it's me."

"You run warmer. You against the taps makes thermodynamic sense—"

"You've _thought_ about this. You've done the _thermodynamics._" Ze was almost impressed. "Fine. But when my spine's against the faucet don't expect romance. Expect complaints."

They got in. It was, as predicted, a negotiation — a tangle of too-many-limbs and one adjustment and then another, Will apologizing, ze issuing dry logistical corrections, water going over the side, before they finally settled: Will against the back, ze against him, the tub full and warm and just barely large enough if nobody moved suddenly.

And then it _was_ nice, which surprised ze, and ze didn't say so, which didn't surprise Will.

Ze went quiet — the real quiet, the head-in-lap register, the one ze only ran with the doors locked. Settled back against Will's chest, the warm water and the warm person behind it, and some of the perpetual low readiness went out of zir the way it only ever did here. Will had a hand moving idle through the wet strands of zir hair, the grey-white ones darker now, plastered, and he could feel ze slowly, grudgingly, admitting to comfort.

"...Okay," ze conceded, eyes shut. "This is fine. The tub's still too small. But this is fine."

"High praise."

"Don't push it. My back's against the taps. I'm being generous under difficult conditions."

Will laughed, low, and kept his hand in zir hair, and let the quiet sit. It was one of the rare ones — no smug, no performance, no floor to broadcast to, just the two of them and the warm water and ze fully, wordlessly at ease against him, armor off, the whole magnetic unbotherable creature reduced to something soft and heavy and content in a tub that was, genuinely, too small.

"You're comfortable," Will said, quiet, a little amazed, the way he always was when he caught ze like this.

"Water's warm. You're warm. It's efficient." A pause, and then, smaller, the truth smuggled under the deflection as ever: "...And it's just us. No wall. No floor listening. Nobody to be anything for." Ze pressed back against him, a fraction, the wordless _this, yes, stay._ "It's quiet. I like the quiet ones. Don't tell anyone."

"Never."

"The floor thinks I'm loud all the time. Let them keep thinking it." Eyes still shut, sinking further into the warmth and him. "This one's not for them. This one's just yours."

And Will held ze in the too-small tub, water cooling slowly, neither of them moving to fix it, and understood he'd been handed one of the quiet ones again — the version of zir nobody else got, the one that had no audience and needed none, the one that only ever surfaced here, with the doors locked and the water warm and no reason left in the world to be anything but held.

The floor heard nothing that evening. There was nothing to hear. And that silence, Will was learning, was its own kind of the loudest thing ze could say.

---
# The campaign
It became a quiet campaign, and Will ran it the way he ran everything — patiently, in small doses, never all at once.

Because he'd figured out, over the married months, that the way to get ze comfortable with romance wasn't to overwhelm ze with it. It was to make it _ordinary._ Low-dose, frequent, so small each time that ze couldn't quite object, until the accumulation of them wore a groove and the whole category stopped triggering the flee reflex. He wasn't trying to make ze _like_ being romanced. He was trying to make it _familiar_ — background weather instead of an ambush.

So he did the little things, on rotation, always just under the threshold.

Coffee, brought to zir at the desk without being asked — using zir own grammar back, no comment, just set down. Ze noticed. Ze always noticed. Said nothing, drank it, and Will counted it a win.

A hand at the small of zir back, brief, passing through the kitchen. Nothing dramatic. Just contact, delivered and withdrawn before ze could decide to be weird about it. The first few times ze went faintly stiff. By the twentieth, ze leaned into it without noticing ze had.

A flower, occasionally — not a bouquet, not a production, just one, in a glass on the counter, appearing the way zir food appeared at doors. Ze called them unnecessary every single time. Every single time ze also left them there, and Will noted that ze left them there, and neither of them mentioned that ze left them there.

"You're doing a thing," Kyu said, eventually, catching the pattern the way ze caught everything. "A campaign. Small stuff. Constant. You're — you're _acclimating_ me. Like I'm a nervous animal."

"You are a nervous animal. About this specific thing." Will didn't deny it, because denying it would insult zir ability to read a room. "You can take anything except being loved out loud. So I'm doing it quiet. In pieces. So you get used to it."

"That's—" Ze stopped, because it was, annoyingly, both true and considerate, and ze had no counter to considerate. "That's manipulative."

"It's _patient._ Different thing." He handed ze the coffee, on cue. "I'm not tricking you into anything. I'm just — I love you, and I'm going to keep doing it, and I know you can't take much at once, so I do a little at a time and let you get familiar. That's it. That's the whole scheme." A beat. "You can tell me to stop."

Ze didn't tell him to stop.

That was the thing. Ze grumbled, ze called it a campaign, ze called the flowers unnecessary — but ze never once told him to stop, and slowly, over months, the doses started to land differently. The stiffening stopped. The flee reflex quieted. Ze started, without seeming to decide to, _expecting_ the coffee, the hand, the small nothing gestures — and one day Will forgot the flower for a couple weeks and ze said, flat, not looking up, "no flower lately," which was, from zir, roughly a symphony.

"You _missed_ the flower," Will said, delighted.

"I noticed the _absence_ of a variable. Different thing." But ze was pink, caught, and Will got a flower that afternoon and ze left it on the counter and called it unnecessary and the whole familiar loop ran again, except now the loop was _comfortable,_ now it was just how the house worked, now romance was background weather instead of a threat — which had been the entire point.

He never escalated past what ze could hold. That was the discipline. He kept it small forever, on purpose, because the goal was never to make ze endure grand gestures. The goal was to make the small ones _ordinary_ — to build a life where being gently, quietly, constantly loved was just the ambient condition ze lived in, familiar enough that ze stopped bracing against it.

And it worked, slowly, the way patient things work. Ze never became someone who wanted a bouquet and a speech. But ze became someone who left the flowers on the counter, and leaned into the hand without stiffening, and noticed when the coffee didn't come — someone acclimated, at last, to being loved, one dose at a time, by a man with infinite patience and a very long game.

"For the record," ze said one evening, apropos of the flower Will had just set down, "these are still unnecessary."

"I know."

"...Keep buying them anyway." And ze went back to zir book, having said the truest thing ze had in zir whole vocabulary — _keep going, I've gotten used to it, I'd notice if you stopped_ — in the only grammar ze would ever use for it.

---
# 
Will's campaign had a spoken front, too, run on the exact same principle: small doses, low threshold, said quiet so ze couldn't flee it.

Because the flowers and the coffee were the easy part — objects, no words, safely inside zir grammar. The words were harder. Ze could take a hand at the small of the back long before ze could take _I love you_ to the face. So Will rationed the verbal the same way, careful, watching the flinch, backing off the instant ze reached for the exit.

He kept it low and offhand. Not the grand register — that would've sent ze through the wall. Just small true things, dropped sideways, easy enough to survive.

"Glad you're home," as ze came in, not looking up from what he was doing, so ze didn't have to hold his gaze while it landed.

"Missed you," into the top of zir head, brief, gone before ze could stiffen.

"Love you," at the end of a phone call, fast and casual, hanging up before ze had to answer — which meant ze never had to produce the words back, which was the whole mercy of the delivery.

The first few times ze went quiet, or changed the subject, or said "don't" flat and reflexive. Will didn't push. He'd just let it sit, unbothered, and try again another day at the same small size. Never bigger. Never all at once. He was acclimating a nervous animal to the sound of a word, and you don't do that by shouting it.

"You're doing the verbal version now," Kyu observed, catching it, because ze caught everything. "The little — the drive-by ones. You say it and then you leave so I don't have to say anything back."

"Yeah."

"Because you know I can't—"

"Because I know it's hard for you to hear, and harder to say, and I don't need you to say it back." He kept it flat, easy, zir own register. "I just want you to get used to hearing it. That's all. You don't owe me a return."

And that — _you don't owe me a return_ — was what took the teeth out of it. Because the reason declaration terrified ze was that declaration usually _demanded_ something: a matching declaration, a moment, a standing-up-and-saying-it. Will kept removing the demand. He said the thing and asked for nothing, over and over, small, until the word stopped being a trap that required a response and became just — a sound ze lived near. Familiar. Weather.

Slowly, the flinch went out of it. Ze stopped saying "don't." Started, instead, just letting the small ones land — a quiet acceptance, no return required, which was itself a kind of receiving. And then, eventually, ze started answering, in zir own way, in the grammar ze had: not the words, never quite the words, but the nearest thing ze could reach.

"Love you," Will said one night, offhand, the drive-by version, already turning to leave the room.

"...I know," ze said. And then — rare, quiet, costing ze something, said to his back so ze didn't have to hold his eyes for it — "You too. Obviously."

Will didn't stop, didn't turn, didn't make it a thing. He just let it land where it fell and kept walking, and filed it — the whole scale of it — because _you too, obviously_ from zir, said out loud, unprompted, was the verbal equivalent of the counter, the ring, the flowers left where they sat. The word smuggled out at last, in the only wrapping ze could manage.

He never made ze say more than that. Never needed to. He kept saying his, small and constant and asking nothing, and ze kept, more and more often, letting the _you too_ slip out to his retreating back — and that was the whole spoken language of them, built one undemanded sentence at a time, patient as everything Will ever did: a man saying _I love you_ into a room and a creature learning, slowly, that it was safe to say it back.

---

# The glasses

Kyu noticed the glasses on an ordinary evening, and was, for a moment, genuinely thrown, which almost never happened.

Will had come home late, gone straight to the corner desk to finish something, and hadn't bothered to switch to contacts — and there he was, in the lamplight, glasses on, frowning at a spreadsheet with the small tired focus of a man at the end of a long day. Kyu, passing through, stopped.

"You wear glasses."

Will glanced up. "For screens. At the end of the day, when my eyes are tired. I usually do contacts, or I take them off before you're—" He touched the frames, self-conscious. "Why? Do they look—"

"No." Ze was still looking. "You've had these the whole time."

"I've had these for years. You've just never caught me in them. I'm vain about it, apparently." He went a little pink. "They're a — I don't love them. What."

And here was the reversal Kyu was quietly enjoying, because for the entire marriage _Will_ had been the one caught off guard by things — the income, the freelance, the endless recalibration of zir. This was ze finding a new variable in _him,_ a small one, a domestic one, and having to redo a piece of the model. The composed successful husband, at the desk, in glasses, tired — a version ze hadn't had.

"I like them," ze said. Flat. Which from zir was significant, because ze did not hand out that kind of thing lightly.

Will blinked. "You — really?"

"You do the concentrating thing. You told me you liked it when I concentrate. Turns out—" ze tilted zir head, unhurried, taking him in, "—it goes both ways. You at the desk, tired, glasses, frowning at your little spreadsheet. It's a good look. Domestic. I didn't have it before." A beat, and then, because ze could never hand over a compliment without immediately complicating it: "You've been _hiding_ it. Taking them off before I get home. Vain. That's the part I'm mad about. You've been depriving me of the glasses on purpose."

"I wasn't _depriving_ you, I just—" Will was fully pink now, caught. "I don't like how I look in them—"

"You look _good_ in them. That's the whole problem I'm having." Ze crossed to the desk, took the frames off his face gently, looked at them, put them back on him with the specific care ze used for things ze wasn't going to admit ze cared about — settling them straight, unhurried. "There. Keep them on when I'm home. That's the new rule. I made it. I make excellent rules."

"You can't just—"

"I can. You've seen me concentrate a hundred times and it's my whole appeal, apparently. I've been robbed of the glasses for _years._ We're correcting that." Ze straightened, victorious, the smug fully arrived. "Turnabout, husband. You find me hot when I work. I find you hot in the glasses. Now neither of us gets anything done in the evenings. That's marriage. That's what we signed up for."

"That's your fault, you just made a rule about it—"

"Best rule I've made. Finish your spreadsheet." Ze was already drifting toward the couch, thoroughly pleased with the discovery, having gotten one new variable of him to catalog after a marriage of zir being the one catalogued. "Slowly, though. I'm going to watch. For research."

And Will finished his spreadsheet, glasses on, ze watching from the couch with open, insufferable appreciation — the tables turned one more small degree, ze delighting for once in finding something new in _him,_ and him, pink and caught and secretly pleased, leaving the glasses on for the rest of the night.

---
# 
Kyu would never admit it out loud — that was a given — but the evidence was piling up, and Will had learned to read the evidence.

Ze enjoyed the married life. More than ze'd expected to. Possibly more than ze was comfortable knowing about zirself.

The signs were all in zir grammar, never in words. Ze kept the ring on. Ze left the flowers on the counter and complained about them and would've noticed instantly if they stopped. Ze had, without ever discussing it, quietly stopped spending nights at zir own apartment across the hall — the one ze technically still held, the storage annex, the formality — because this was home now and that one was just a room with boxes in it. Ze woke up before Will sometimes and, instead of getting up, stayed, reading, letting Will sleep against zir. Ze had a whole domestic rhythm now that ze'd have found unthinkable years ago, and ze ran it like it was nothing, like it had always been the shape of zir life.

"You're happy," Will said one morning, watching ze make coffee in the too-good machine, barefoot, unhurried, entirely at home. He said it carefully, low-dose, the way he said everything that might make ze flee. "You know that? You're — this is the happiest I've seen you. Since I've known you."

"Don't."

"I'm not making it a thing. I'm just noticing." He accepted the coffee ze handed him without being asked. "You like this. The married thing. More than you thought you would."

Ze was quiet for a second, which with zir was as good as a confession.

"...It's fine," ze said finally, which was zir word for _good,_ which was zir word for _more than good._ Ze leaned back against the counter, coffee in hand, and looked at the small nice apartment with the thin walls and the free chair and the flowers ze called unnecessary. "I didn't think I'd — I never stayed with anyone. You know that. Everyone knows that, it's my whole — the sister makes speeches about it." A shrug, offhand, the truth smuggled out sideways as always. "I figured I'd keep living the way I lived. Loud, alone, fine with it. I _was_ fine with it. I wasn't waiting for anything."

"And?"

"And then some guilt-ridden millionaire did surveillance on me through a wall." The corner of the mouth. "And now I've got a husband and a niece who picks locks and an entire building with a betting pool and a coffee machine that's frankly too good, and I—" ze stopped, at the edge of the thing, and did the ze move, stepped back from the declaration and landed on the truth underneath it anyway: "I didn't know it was better. This. I thought I had everything sorted. Turns out I was missing a thing I didn't know was a thing." A beat. "That's annoying. I don't like being wrong about myself."

"You're not wrong. You just didn't have the data."

"That's your line. That's the line you use about _me._" Ze pointed the coffee at him, caught him borrowing zir own grammar. "Stop being right at me in the morning. It's obnoxious." But ze wasn't moving away, and ze was pink, and ze had just said — in the most defended, deflected way available — that being married to him was the best thing that had happened to a person who'd genuinely believed they already had everything.

Will didn't push. He'd learned. He just drank the coffee ze'd made him and let it sit.

"For the record," Kyu added, after a moment, quieter, to the middle distance rather than to him, "I'm glad the walls were thin. That's — put it in the file. I don't say it twice." Ze pushed off the counter, done with sincerity for the morning, the smug reassembling. "Now stop looking at me like that. We're not doing feelings before nine. Drink your coffee. Husband."

"Husband," Will agreed, delighted, and let ze off the hook, and filed it — the whole enormous quiet size of it — where ze'd never have to look at it again.

Ze enjoyed the married life more than ze thought ze would. Ze would never say it plainly. But ze'd said _I'm glad the walls were thin,_ once, to the middle distance, and refused to repeat it — and from zir, that was a vow all over again.

---
# Their parents
The dinner was at Will's parents' house, which Kyu had survived once before, back when they were just dating and the linen and the multiple forks had been a battlefield. This time was different, because now it was official — married, filed, done — and Will's parents were, by paperwork and by their own enthusiastic insistence, zir parents too now.

Ze found this genuinely stranger than the wedding.

Because zir own parents were a distant, provided-for, fine-terms arrangement — money came, presence didn't, ze raised the siblings, that was the shape of it. Ze didn't have a working model for _parents who wanted to be involved._ And Will's mother wanted very much to be involved. Had wanted it since the first dinner, when ze'd told her they met because ze was loud and she'd laughed the real laugh. Now the marriage had given her a mandate, and she deployed it fully.

"There ze is," Will's mother said, at the door, and hugged ze — actually hugged ze, warm, uncomplicated, before ze could set up any defenses. "My other child. You know you're stuck with us now. Legally."

"...That's what the paperwork said," Kyu managed, hugged, thrown.

Will's father shook zir hand at the door and held it the beat too long, the closest that formal man came to warmth, and said "welcome to the family" in the tone he used for things he meant, and Will watched zir do the small recalibration ze did when sincerity got past the armor.

Dinner was the same trial as before and ze handled it the same way — flat, honest, immovable, answering the father's questions without a sales pitch, which the father had come to genuinely respect. But there was a new element ze hadn't braced for, which was that Will's mother had decided the marriage made ze _hers,_ and treated ze accordingly: fussed over whether ze was eating enough (ze was, ze ate like the food might be repossessed, this delighted her), asked after the sister and Mai by name because Will had told her about them, and generally folded ze into a family structure ze had no experience being _inside_ rather than _running._

"You take care of everyone," Will's mother observed, over dessert, having pieced it together the way mothers do. "Will told me. Your sister, your brother, that little niece. The whole — you look after all of them." She patted zir hand. "So who looks after _you?_"

Kyu, for once, did not have a flat answer ready. The question landed somewhere ze wasn't defended.

"...Will does," ze said finally, quiet, and it came out more honest than ze meant it to, because the woman had asked plainly and ze didn't know how to lie to a plain question. "He's — annoyingly persistent about it. Fights me to pay for things. Won't let me not be—" ze gestured, vaguely, at the whole campaign, the coffee and the flowers and the bills, "—it's a whole thing. He's very stubborn."

"Good." Will's mother looked deeply satisfied, and shot her son a look of pure approval. "Somebody had to. You'd have run yourself into the ground taking care of everyone else forever." She squeezed zir hand once more and let it go. "That's what he's for. That's the job. Let him do it."

And Kyu sat with that — a mother, a real involved present one, telling ze plainly that being taken care of was _allowed,_ was even the point — which was a thing zir own distant parents had never once said, and which ze had spent a whole life not believing, and which was now being handed to zir across a table with linen and too many forks by a woman who had decided ze was hers.

Will, beside zir, said nothing, because he'd learned. He just found zir hand under the table and held it, and let his mother do the work he'd been doing quietly for years — telling ze, out loud, plainly, that ze got to be looked after too.

In the car afterward, ze was quiet a long time.

"Your mother," ze said eventually, to the windshield. "She just — says things. Out loud. The real ones. Straight at you." A pause. "How do you _survive_ that. Growing up with someone who just — says it."

"You get used to it." Will glanced over. "It's how normal families work, apparently. Nobody warned me it was rare until I met yours."

"...It's a lot." Ze looked out the window. And then, smaller, the truth ze'd never say to his mother's face but could just barely say to a windshield in the dark: "It's nice, though. The — being somebody's. I didn't have that one. The parent version." A beat. "Don't tell her I said that. She'll make it a thing. She'll hug me again."

"She's going to hug you again regardless. You're hers now. That's permanent."

"...Yeah." And ze didn't argue it, didn't deflect it, just watched the road go by — a person who'd raised a family and belonged to no one, now folded, at last, into the strange warm ordinary machinery of a family that simply _said_ things and meant them and had decided, paperwork and all, to keep ze.

The floor got no broadcast that night. They came home late and quiet and ze went to sleep held, which was its own kind of the loudest thing — a wild thing, tamed by patience, learning late and slowly that being someone's was not a trap but a place to rest.

---
# 
Will's office had, over the course of the marriage, become a place Kyu visited — not often, but enough that the staff had a whole file on it, and enough that Will now had a specific low-grade dread whenever reception called up to say he had a visitor.

Today, though, it was reversed. Today Will was just _at work,_ an ordinary day, and the thing making the office interesting was Will himself.

Because the staff had noticed things. They noticed everything; that was established. And lately what they'd noticed was that the boss was — different. Softer around the edges. Happier in a settled, unremarkable way. He wore the ring, obviously, but it was more than the ring. He said "husband" too much (the staff had a tally; Dana ran it). He left on time more often. He'd stopped being quite so knotted about small things. Marriage, the group chat had concluded, agreed with him.

"You're in a good mood," his assistant observed, bringing in the afternoon coffee.

"Am I?"

"You've been in a good mood for like a year. It's honestly a little disorienting. You used to be scarier." She set the cup down. "Your husband called, by the way. Said don't pick up dinner, zir cooking, and — quote — 'tell him not to fight me on the groceries again, I already went.'"

Will smiled at the spreadsheet, helplessly, the way he did.

"See. _That._" His assistant pointed. "That face. You do that face at your emails now. It's very strange to work for someone who does that face."

"I'll try to be more menacing."

"Please don't. We've all gotten used to the peace." She left, and Will went back to work — and this was the quiet difference the office had clocked and couldn't quite name: the man used to run on drive alone, the economy-class-guilt engine, the need to earn his comfort, never quite letting himself have anything. Now he ran a company the same as ever, worked as hard, was as good at it — but there was a floor under him now that hadn't been there before. He went home to something. The work wasn't the whole thing anymore. It was just the work, and then he got to go home to the loud creature who'd already done the groceries.

His phone buzzed. A text from Kyu: _what time. i'm timing the food. don't be late, it's the good version._

_6:30,_ Will sent back. _Don't let me be late, come drag me out if I'm not moving._

_i will absolutely come drag you out. i'll do it in front of your whole staff. try me._

Will considered this. Considered the group chat. Considered the tally. Considered the reconnaissance-mission energy of his entire workforce, who would _lose their minds_ if ze showed up to physically extract him from his own office.

_I'll be on time,_ he sent.

_coward,_ came the reply, _🙂_

And Will put the phone down and finished his work and left at 6:15 — early, on time, to go home to dinner ze had cooked and would refuse to be thanked for — and his staff watched the scariest boss they'd ever had leave cheerfully before dinnertime, and updated the group chat with satisfaction, because whatever ze was, whoever the loud mysterious husband was, ze had clearly done the one thing none of them had managed in years of working for the man: gotten him to go _home._

---
# The belly

Will noticed the weight the way you notice these things — a shirt sitting differently, a belt on a familiar notch that wasn't quite the familiar notch anymore. Over thirty now, married a while, fed extremely well by a creature who cooked constantly and would not let him fail to eat. It added up. He looked in the mirror, turned sideways, and found he was not especially bothered.

"I've gained weight," he reported, mildly, to the bedroom at large.

"I know," said Kyu, from the bed, not looking up. "I did that. On purpose. Sort of."

"You _did_ it?"

"I cook. You eat. And then you sit at a desk all day. It's just math." Ze shrugged. "You look fine. Better, honestly. You were a little too — CEO-gaunt when we met. Stressed-thin. This is a _fed_ look. It suits you." A pause, and then, flat and shameless as ever: "I like the belly. For the record. It's good. Keep it."

"You _like_—"

"I like it. I'm allowed to like it. You're my husband, I get an opinion on the — yes. The belly's good. Don't lose the belly." Ze said it with total conviction, the way ze said everything, zero self-consciousness, while Will went slightly pink at being complimented on his gut with such flat sincerity. "But—"

"There's a but."

"There's a but." Ze set down what ze was reading and looked at him properly, and the flat affection sharpened into something more coach-shaped. "You've slacked on the gym. Again. Third time this year you've drifted off. And I like the belly, but I don't like you being _unfit,_ those are different things — one's how you look, which is great, one's your actual body working, which is _slipping._ You get winded on the stairs now. You didn't used to."

"The stairs are—"

"Three flights. You built up to them fine when I was coaching you and then you stopped coming and now you're winded again." Ze pointed. "That's not about the weight. Keep the weight. That's about you not moving. I'm not going to watch you go soft in the _bad_ way — the way where your back seizes and your heart works too hard — just because I fed you well and you took it as license to never exercise again."

Will considered this. It was, annoyingly, both an unqualified compliment and a firm order, delivered in the same breath, which was very ze. _I like your body, now go maintain it._

"So you like the belly," he clarified, "but I have to come back to the gym."

"Yes." Ze was already reaching for zir phone, clearly about to put him on a schedule he had not agreed to. "The belly can stay. It's a good belly, I stand by the belly. But you're coming back to six a.m. because I'd rather have a husband who's fed _and_ fit than a husband who's fed and gets winded carrying groceries up the stairs I _watched_ you struggle with yesterday." A beat, the smug arriving. "Also I like coaching you. You're the worst client I have and it's the most fun I have all week. Watching the CEO do burpees. Keeps me humble about you."

"You do burpees _at_ me for fun—"

"I do burpees at you because you _need_ them and it's _funny,_ which is the ideal overlap." Ze was texting now, locking in a time he hadn't approved. "Six a.m. Thursday. Wear the shorts. Bring the belly, it's welcome, but it's doing burpees this time." Ze glanced up, warm under the flat. "I'm not trying to change how you look. You look good. I just want you _around_ a long time. That's the whole thing. Fed and fit. Both. I get both."

And Will, complimented on his gut and conscripted into a 6 a.m. session in the same thirty seconds, understood he had once again lost an argument he hadn't known he was having — and that ze demanding he go to the gym _specifically so he'd be around a long time_ was, underneath the coaching bark, one of the more direct things ze'd ever said about wanting him to stay.

"Fine," he said. "Thursday. But you can't complain about the belly at the gym after telling me to keep it."

"I would never. The belly and I are at peace." Ze set the phone down, business concluded. "It's the rest of you that's doing burpees. Now come here. You can start the fitness campaign Thursday. Tonight the belly and I have plans."

---
# The bad ex
The bad one was named Dominic, and Will knew, the second the man clocked ze across the bar, that this wasn't going to be like the others.

There was none of the warm easy recognition. No "Kyu?" in a delighted rising tone, no fond stunned "you got _married?_" Dominic went still, and then his face did something complicated and cold, and he came over anyway, which was worse than if he'd left.

"Huh," he said. "So you're still around."

"Dominic." Kyu's voice went flat in a different register than usual — not the unbothered flat, the _careful_ flat, the one Will had never heard before. Ze set down zir drink. "Been a while."

"It has." His eyes went to the ring, and something in his jaw tightened. "_Married._ Wow. Great. Good for you." It was not good-for-you. It was the exact opposite, delivered with a smile that had teeth in it. "So it turns out you _could_ keep someone. Just not — huh. Just not me."

And Will, watching, understood immediately that he was seeing the version of zir history that didn't end clean — the one break in the amicable field, and he understood it wasn't going to be about anyone doing something monstrous, because the air was thick with something more painful than that: an old wound that had never gotten a villain to blame.

"That's not—" Kyu started, and stopped, and Will watched zir reach for the flat exit and not quite find it, because this one got under. "I told you. At the time. I was clear about what I—"

"You were _clear._" Dominic laughed, short, ugly. "God, you love that. You were _clear._ You said it like it was a favor. 'I'm not going to keep you, I'm not built for it, don't get attached.'" He was bitter, years bitter, the kind that had calcified. "And I thought — I don't know what I thought. That you didn't mean it. That if I just — that I could be the one who—"

"I know what you thought." Ze said it quietly. "I know. That was the problem. I said the thing and you decided I was wrong about myself. You spent the whole time trying to — _fix_ it. Like I was a wall you could get through if you pushed hard enough." Ze wasn't cruel about it, but ze wasn't soft either, because ze didn't do soft with the truth. "I wasn't lying to you, Dom. I was the most honest I've ever been with anyone. You just didn't want the honest version. You wanted the version where you were the exception."

"And now there's a _ring,_" Dominic said, and there it was, the actual wound, laid bare. "So somebody _was_ the exception. Just not me. So it was never that you couldn't. It was that you wouldn't. With me."

And the bar went quiet around them, and Will sat very still, because this was the thing — this was the part that had no villain, the part that hurt precisely _because_ nobody had done anything clearly wrong. Ze had been honest. Dominic had refused to believe the honesty. Ze left exactly as ze'd warned. And now, years later, the existence of a ring made it look, to Dominic, like proof that ze _could_ have stayed and simply chose not to — when the truth was messier and kinder and worse: ze hadn't been able to, then, with anyone, and the difference wasn't that Will was better, it was that Will never once tried to _make_ ze be someone ze wasn't.

Kyu was quiet for a long moment. And then ze said the truest thing ze had, flat and low, and it wasn't a defense.

"You're right that I wouldn't. You're wrong about why." Ze met his eyes. "It wasn't that you weren't good enough. You were — you were good, Dom. It's that you never stopped _pushing._ The whole time. Trying to change my mind, prove me wrong about myself, be the one who cracked me. And I can't — I couldn't stay with someone who treated me like a problem to solve. Who needed me to want more than I wanted so they'd feel chosen." A beat. "The person I married never did that. Not once. He heard 'I don't keep anyone' and he just — didn't push. Stayed decent. Asked for nothing. And so I got to _choose_ it, instead of being cornered into it." Quiet. "That's the difference. It was never that you weren't enough. It's that you needed me to be someone I'm not, and he never did."

Dominic stood there. The bitterness didn't leave his face, exactly — years don't dissolve in one bar — but something in it shifted, some old story he'd told himself getting complicated by a version he hadn't considered.

"...That's a hell of a thing to hear," he said finally.

"I know. I'm sorry it took this long for me to say it right." And ze meant it — not an apology for leaving, ze'd never apologize for that, but an apology for the years he'd spent thinking it was about his worth when it was about his _approach._ "You'll find someone who doesn't have to be pushed. You won't have to prove anything to them. And it'll be better than trying to break down someone who told you at the door ze was leaving."

Dominic looked at ze a moment longer. Then at Will — one flat, assessing look, the look of a man measuring the person who got the thing he didn't — and then he just nodded, once, and left, taking his old bitterness with him, maybe slightly lighter for having finally heard the real reason, maybe not. Some wounds don't close in a bar. But the story he'd carried had at least been corrected.

The table was quiet after.

"That was the bad one," Will said, gently. Not a question.

"That was the bad one." Ze picked zir drink back up, and zir hand wasn't quite as steady as usual, because that one had cost something. "Everyone else I left clean because they believed me. He didn't believe me, so it — it broke wrong. He spent years thinking it was because he wasn't enough." A breath. "It was never that. It was that he couldn't hear no. Couldn't let me be what I said I was. Kept trying to earn a different version of me."

Will reached across the table and took zir hand — the ringed one — and didn't say anything for a second, because he was sitting with the size of what he'd just learned: that the reason he'd succeeded was never that he was more lovable than Dominic, or luckier. It was that he'd done the one thing that worked and the one thing that felt, at the time, like doing nothing. He hadn't pushed. He'd heard _I don't keep anyone_ and made no attempt to change it. He'd just been patient and decent and let ze arrive at staying on zir own.

"I never tried to fix you," he said, slowly, understanding it fully for the first time.

"No. You didn't." Ze looked at their hands. "That's the whole reason you're the one wearing the ring and he's not. Not because you loved me more. Because you loved me _as I was,_ and never once needed me to be talked into wanting you. You just — waited. And let me pick it." A pause, and then, quieter, the realest thing: "Do you know how rare that is? Everyone wants to be the exception. You never tried to be. You just made it _safe_ enough that I turned out to be the exception on my own." Ze looked up. "That's why. That's the whole why. In case you ever wondered."

Will had wondered, actually. He'd never quite understood why him and not the field of cheerful exes and one bitter one. Now he knew. It wasn't that he'd won ze. It was that he was the only one who never tried to — and that, with this specific wild and honest and unkeepable creature, was the one thing that had ever worked.

"For the record," he said, "I'd do it again. The nothing. The waiting. All of it."

"I know you would. That's why I keep you." Ze squeezed his hand, the wobble gone now, the flat certainty back. "Come on. Let's go home. I've had enough of the past for one night." A beat, the corner of the mouth returning at last. "And you're not allowed to get sappy about being the exception. I can see it building. Don't."

"Wouldn't dream of it."

"You're already dreaming of it. It's all over your face." Ze stood, pulling him up. "Home. Now. Before you say something and I have to survive it in public."

They went home. Will did not get sappy, out loud — he'd learned. But he held the truth of it the rest of the night, quiet and enormous: that being loved by ze had never been about being enough. It had been about being the one person who never asked ze to be anything other than exactly what ze was.

---

# Will's ex

Will had exactly one ex worth mentioning, and it came up because Kyu, having recently been ambushed by zir own history, decided turnabout was fair.

"So you've met my whole catalogue," ze said, over dinner. "Nadia, Theo, Cara, Dominic. The good and the bad. You know all of mine." Ze pointed a fork at him. "You've never once mentioned yours. That's suspicious. You had a whole serious thing in your twenties, you mentioned it once, years ago, and then never again. What happened there."

"Nothing dramatic. That's the honest answer." Will set down his own fork. "Her name was Elise. Two years, my late twenties. It was — good, actually. Serious. We were the couple everyone assumed would get married."

"And?"

"And she was ambitious, and I was ambitious, and we were both climbing, and it turned out we were climbing in different directions." He shrugged. "She got a role in another city. Genuinely good opportunity, the kind you don't turn down. And I was building the company here, couldn't leave, wouldn't have asked her to stay. So we — didn't fight, didn't cheat, nothing broke. We just looked at it honestly and admitted neither of us was going to give up our thing for the other. So we ended it. Sad, mutual, adult. We still send a Christmas text."

Kyu considered this. "That's it? Nobody was a villain?"

"Nobody was a villain. That's why it's not a good story." He half-smiled. "It's the boring version of a breakup. Two people who liked each other fine and wanted different lives more than they wanted each other. No bitterness. Just — geography and ambition." A pause. "Honestly it taught me something. She left, and it hurt, but it didn't _wreck_ me, and that told me the thing wasn't what I'd thought it was. We were compatible on paper and comfortable in person and there was no — I didn't miss her the way you're supposed to miss the love of your life. I missed the _idea_ more than the person."

"Huh." Kyu was quiet a second, doing something with the information. "So your worst breakup is 'we were both too busy and parted like grown-ups.'"

"My worst breakup is a scheduling conflict, essentially, yeah." Will laughed. "I'm aware it's not competitive with Dominic. I don't have a Dominic. I have an Elise, who I'm on perfectly nice terms with, and a couple of shorter things before her that were even less eventful. I'm boring. I told you. I'm the normal one."

And here Kyu did the thing ze rarely did, which was arrive somewhere thoughtful.

"That's not boring," ze said. "That's the whole reason it worked, actually. Think about it." Ze leaned back. "You dated someone you were compatible-on-paper with, comfortable with, and you _felt the difference_ when it turned out to be idea-love instead of the real thing. You learned what the comfortable-and-fine version feels like. So when you got the actual thing—" ze gestured, vaguely, at the two of them, "—you _knew._ You had the control group. You knew the difference between 'nice on paper' and whatever this is." A beat. "Elise did you a favor. She's the reason you recognized me. You'd already learned what it _isn't._"

Will looked at zir, a little struck. "That's — huh. Yeah. Actually."

"I'm right. I'm always right." Ze went back to eating. "You had the pleasant, sensible, geographically-doomed one in your twenties so you'd know, later, that pleasant and sensible isn't the same as _right._ And then you moved into a building with thin walls and heard the least sensible thing in the world through a wall and went, oh, _that's_ different." The corner of the mouth. "Elise gave you a baseline. I broke the baseline. That's the whole arc."

"You're comparing yourself favorably to my very nice ex-girlfriend—"

"I'm comparing myself _accurately._ She was nice. I'm the love of your life. Those are different tiers and you know it, you did the surveillance, you married _me._" Ze was thoroughly pleased with this analysis. "Send her the Christmas text. Tell her I said thanks. She trained you to recognize the real thing. I'd shake her hand." A beat. "Also she left for a _job._ You'd never leave me for a job. You leave _work_ early now. She'd be amazed. Character growth."

"She would genuinely be amazed. I was a nightmare about work back then—"

"And now you go home at 6:15 because dinner's ready." Ze pointed the fork again, victorious. "See? I fixed you. Elise couldn't fix you, the company couldn't fix you, thirty-plus years of guilt couldn't fix you. Thin walls fixed you. You're welcome." Ze went back to dinner. "Boring ex. Sensible arc. Great taste, eventually. No notes."

And Will, who had indeed once been the couple-everyone-assumed-would-marry with a perfectly nice woman and felt, honestly, only the loss of an idea when it ended — understood ze was right, as usual: that the boring breakup was the thing that taught him the difference, so that years later, through a wall, he'd recognize the real thing when it started broadcasting at him at midnight. Elise gave him the baseline. Ze broke it. That was the whole arc.

---
# Valentine saga

**Year One.**

They weren't anything yet. It was the casual stretch — the dinners that ended in the bedroom, the official position that nothing was happening — and Will, who by now knew zir name was _Valentine,_ also knew that doing a single thing about the actual day would send ze straight through the wall and possibly out of his life.

So he did nothing. Heroically. He bought no flowers. He made no reservation. He did not so much as say the words "Valentine's Day" out loud, because he understood, correctly, that ze would treat any acknowledgment as an ambush and bolt.

It nearly killed him.

Because he was, by nature, a man who wanted to _mark_ things, and here was the most markable day of the year, and here was the person he was falling for, whose literal name was the holiday, and he had to sit on his hands and pretend February 14th was an ordinary Thursday. He made dinner (badly; ze fixed it). They ate. Neither said the date. And Will went to bed having exercised more restraint than the founding of the company had ever required, and lay there thinking _I know your name, I know what today is, and I said nothing,_ and considered it, privately, one of the hardest things he'd ever done.

Kyu, for the record, knew exactly what day it was and exactly what he wasn't doing, and appreciated it more than ze would ever say. Ze just made a mental note that the man could, in fact, hold his tongue when it counted — which was, though neither of them clocked it yet, the first evidence of the thing that would eventually win ze over.

---

**Year Two.**

They lived together now. It was real. And Will, emboldened by a year of relationship and no longer able to fully suppress his nature, made the tactical error of _testing the water._

Nothing huge. He told himself it was tasteful. A small bunch of flowers, a nice bottle, and — this was the fatal part — a reservation. A restaurant. With a candle. He'd booked it a week out, quietly proud, picturing something warm.

Kyu came home, saw the flowers, saw the _outfit Will was clearly dressed for,_ connected the dots, and stopped in the doorway like ze'd hit a wall.

"No."

"It's just dinner—"

"It's a _candle._ You booked a candle. I can tell from your face there's a candle involved." Ze was already taking zir coat back off. "We're not — Will. It's my _name._ Do you understand what happens at a restaurant on Valentine's Day when my card comes back and it says _Valentine?_ The waiter _comments._ People at other tables — I'm not sitting under a candle being the Valentine who's out for Valentine's while a stranger goes 'aw.' I'll die. I'll actually die."

"I didn't think about the card—"

"You never think about the card." Ze hung the coat back up, the evening cancelled by executive order. "We're staying in. And we're establishing a rule, right now, so we don't do this every year: no restaurant, no candle, no flowers _for the date_ — you can do flowers whenever, just not _because_ it's today, because 'because it's today' turns them into a _statement_ and I can't — no statements. That's the rule. Aggressively normal. Order food. Don't say the date."

And that was how the treaty was written — not warmly, in friction, ze issuing terms while Will stood there in his nice outfit holding doomed flowers. He'd meant well. He'd meant _so_ well. And he'd learned, the hard way, that meaning well in his own language was exactly the problem.

They ordered food. It was fine. But it stung, a little, both ways — Will deflated, ze aware ze'd deflated him, neither quite able to fix it — and that was the low point, the year the day went wrong, the year Will realized loving ze on this specific date was going to take actual study.

---

**Year Three.**

Married now. And Will had spent the intervening year doing what Will did: learning.

He held to every clause of the treaty. No restaurant. No candle. No flowers-because-of-the-date. They stayed in, ordered food, and Will made no move to acknowledge February 14th at all — right up until the food arrived, when he handed ze a small flat thing across the table.

Ze took it with the deep suspicion of someone braced for a card full of the declaring ze couldn't survive.

It was not a card. It was a receipt, and a small parcel, and the receipt said it was a professional lock-picking set — the good brand, the training tier, the one Mai had been coveting.

"You got me a _lock-picking set._"

"For you and Mai. So you can corrupt her properly. Jason will hate it, which is a feature." Will shrugged, casual, watching zir face. "That's it. That's the whole day. It's not romantic. There's no candle. It's the least Valentine's-Day gift I could find, on purpose, because I know you, and I'd rather get my aggressively-normal spouse something _you'd_ actually want than something the date says I'm supposed to." A beat. "Happy nothing. It's just a Tuesday. Eat your food."

And ze looked at the receipt, and looked at him — at the man who'd sat on his hands the first year and blown it the second and then gone away and _studied,_ and come back with a gift that had nothing to do with romance and everything to do with zir — and something got past the armor, because he'd done the impossible thing: he'd honored the treaty to the letter and still, somehow, made the day mean something, by translating it entirely into zir language.

"...This is cheating," ze said, flat, hoarse. "You made the un-romantic thing romantic. You used my own — that's not fair, you can't take the day I hate and win it by _not doing the day._"

"It's not cheating. It's three years of practice." Will was radiant, and mercifully didn't push further. "Now let's not acknowledge the date, like the treaty says. Eat."

Ze ate. Ze did not acknowledge the date out loud. But the lock-picking set went on the counter where the flowers weren't allowed to go, and stayed there, and Will noticed, and neither of them said anything about it — which was, in the Valentine house, the whole holiday: no roses, no candle, no speech, just a man who'd taken three years to learn how to say it in the one language ze could stand to hear it in.

The name, of course, still haunted ze. Someone at the bar had already made the joke twice that week. But at least now, at home, on the actual day, the person who shared zir whole life had figured out how to let ze be a Valentine without ever once making ze act like one.

---
# 
For once, Kyu had nothing.

That was the remarkable part, the part Will privately treasured. The whole marriage ran on zir having the last word — the smug always reassembling, the deflection always landing, ze always finding the angle that turned the moment back into something ze could win. But the lock-picking set had done something the flowers and the candles never could: it left ze with no move.

Because ze couldn't be _smug_ about it. Smug needs a foothold — someone to be one step ahead of, some fluster to enjoy, some angle to work. But Will had handed ze a gift that understood ze so completely, in zir own exact grammar, refusing every romantic register ze'd have deflected, that there was nothing to be ahead of. He hadn't tried to romance ze so ze could bat it away. He'd just _known_ ze, precisely, and acted on it, and you can't smug your way out of being known.

"You're being quiet," Will observed, delighted, over the food. "You've got nothing. I can see you looking for the thing to say and there's no thing."

"There's a thing—"

"There's not. You'd have said it. You always say it immediately." He was insufferable, savoring the rare victory. "You can't tease me for being romantic, because I wasn't. You can't call it a statement, because it's a lock-picking set. You can't say I don't get you, because I _clearly_ get you. You've got nowhere to stand." He leaned back. "I finally did a thing you can't out-smug. Took three years. Worth it."

Kyu opened zir mouth. Closed it. Genuinely searched, the way ze searched for the branch's bug, and came up with nothing that would land — because every exit ze normally used had been quietly removed by the exact shape of the gift.

"...I hate that you did this," ze said finally, which was not smug, which was just true.

"You don't."

"I hate that I _can't_ be annoying about it. Which is worse." Ze pushed food around the plate, cornered, no armor that fit. "You took the one day I have completely handled — I have a whole _system_ for hating this day — and you found the one gap in it. That's — you studied me. You did _reconnaissance._ On my own holiday." A pause, and the closest ze came to the white flag: "It's a good gift. The lock set. It's — yeah. It's a really good gift. Don't make me say more than that."

"I won't." He wouldn't. He'd learned. "That's plenty. That's the whole thing."

And that was the win — not that Will had romanced ze, but that he'd found the one register where ze couldn't perform, couldn't deflect, couldn't reach for the smug, and had to just sit there, quietly, in the uncomfortable warmth of being understood completely, with no way to make a joke of it.

"For the record," ze added, after a moment, recovering the barest edge, "next year I'm getting you something so good you can't be smug either. It's on. You started a war."

"You've been trying to out-gift me for three years—"

"This year I _mean_ it. I've been letting you win the day. That's over now." But it wasn't quite the usual smug — it was warmer, a little defeated under it, the sound of someone who'd been thoroughly gotten and was pretending the rematch would go differently. "...Thank you. For the set. And for the — for knowing. That's the actual thank-you. You don't get another one, so."

Will didn't push. He just filed it — _that's the actual thank-you_ from zir, said plainly, no smug anywhere in it — and let ze eat, and let the rare quiet victory sit exactly where it had landed: on the counter, next to a lock-picking set, in a house where the least romantic person alive had finally been out-Valentined by a man patient enough to do it in the only language ze could stand.

---
# 
Kyu thought the war was over.

Valentine's Day had come and gone. Will had won it with the lock-picking set, ze had grudgingly conceded, ze had threatened a rematch "next year" — and then a month of peace had settled in, February safely behind them, and ze had relaxed. The holiday was annual. Ze had eleven months before ze had to think about it again.

Ze had forgotten that Will studied things.

March 14th, Will came home with a small box and set it on the counter — no ceremony, zir own grammar, just there — and Kyu looked at it with the instant suspicion of a creature who thought it was out of season.

"What's that."

"White Day."

"...What."

"White Day. March fourteenth. It's a return-gift day — a month after Valentine's, you give something back to the person who gave to you." Will was entirely casual, insufferably prepared. "I looked it up. It's a real thing. Which means the holiday you hate isn't actually _once_ a year. It comes in a _pair._ There's a whole second one, and I'm observing it, because I'm a thorough man and you married me."

Kyu stared at the box. Then at Will. Then at the box.

"You found a _second Valentine's Day._"

"I found the _return round._ You threatened to out-gift me next February. I've decided not to wait a year to lose. So." He nudged the box toward zir. "Round two. A month early. You didn't even get to plan because you didn't know the day existed. That's called strategy."

"That's called _ambush_—"

"Same thing. You taught me that." He was thriving. "Open it. It's White Day. There are rules. I read the rules."

Ze opened it, aggrieved, cornered by a holiday ze hadn't known to brace for — and it was, of course, another perfectly-ze gift, nothing romantic, something ze'd actually want, chosen with the same reconnaissance-level understanding as the lock set. Because Will had learned the _pattern,_ not just the day. The move worked in March exactly as well as February.

"You—" ze started, and stopped, because there was no smug available _again,_ the same trap sprung a second time, a month early, when ze thought ze was safe. "You did it _twice._ You found a whole other day to do it and you did it _again._ I can't — Will, I don't even have a _system_ for March, I only built defenses for February, you went _around_ the—"

"I went around the defenses. Yeah." He was radiant. "You fortified February. You left March completely open. Classic mistake." He sat down, victorious. "And now you know: there's no safe month. Any day could be a Valentine's-adjacent day if a man does enough research. You married someone who will find every single one of them and observe it, in your language, forever. That's the deal. There's no off-season."

Kyu put zir face in zir hands.

"How many are there," ze said, muffled, dread creeping in. "How many of these — days. Are there. That you're going to find."

"Oh, a _lot._ There's Valentine's, White Day, there's regional ones, there's the commercial spinoffs — I haven't even started on the—"

"_Stop._"

"—I'm going to observe all of them. Small. In your language. No candles. Just — a steady, year-round campaign of getting my aggressively-normal spouse things you'd actually want, on dates you didn't know were dangerous, forever." He patted zir hand. "Happy White Day. You can't be smug about it. Again. That's two for two."

And ze — surnamed Valentine, married to a man who had just weaponized an _entire second holiday_ against zir carefully-built defenses — accepted, with dawning horror and something warmer underneath it, that there was no calendar safe from this. That ze had married the god of patience, and the god of patience had discovered there was more than one day a year to be quietly, undeflectably loving, and intended to use all of them.

"...Next year," ze said, into zir hands, defeated, "I'm building defenses for the _whole calendar._"

"You won't find them all. I've got a head start." Will kissed the top of zir head, insufferable, triumphant. "Give up now. Save yourself the year. You're going to lose every holiday, on every date, in every month, forever, to a man who reads." A beat. "Happy White Day, Valentine. There's no off-season."

Ze did not have a comeback. There was, genuinely, no off-season. And ze left the gift on the counter next to the lock set, where the not-flowers went, and Will noticed, and neither of them said anything — which was, twice now, the whole holiday.

---
# 

It had crept up on zir, and Kyu did not like things that crept up on zir.

For years, ze had run every dynamic in zir life. That was simply the fact of zir — ze read the room faster than anyone, held the last word, kept the smug fully stocked because ze was always one step ahead. The bar, the office, the whole building, even Will, mostly — ze set the terms. Ze won the exchanges. Ze was, structurally, uncatchable.

And lately, ze kept losing.

Not badly. Not in a way anyone else would notice. But ze noticed, because ze noticed everything. Will had cracked Valentine's Day, then found a _second_ one and cracked that. Will had learned the cheek thing, the tickle thing, the exploit ze had no defense for. Will had figured out how to gift ze in zir own language so ze couldn't deflect. Will had, over years of patient study, quietly assembled a whole toolkit of moves that _worked_ — and the cumulative effect was that ze, the uncatchable creature, kept finding zirself without a comeback.

Ze could not be smug about _anything_ anymore, and it was starting to genuinely unsettle ze.

"You're brooding," Will observed, from across the room. "You've got the — the recalibrating face. But pointed at yourself. What's wrong."

"Nothing's wrong."

"You've said 'nothing' about four times today and you keep looking at me like I did something. Did I do something?"

"You—" Ze stopped, because articulating it meant admitting it, and admitting it was itself another loss. "You keep _winning._ That's the problem. You don't even — you're not gloating, that's the worst part, if you gloated I could work with it. You just quietly keep — the Valentine's thing, the White Day thing, the cheek thing, the _knowing_ me thing. I used to be able to — I always had a move. And lately I keep reaching for the move and it's not _there._" Ze looked genuinely thrown, which almost never happened. "I don't know when it happened. You used to be — I ran this. I ran _us._ And somewhere in the last couple years you got — good at me. And now I can't get the upper hand and I don't — I don't have a framework for not having the upper hand."

Will, to his credit, did not gloat. That would have been the wrong move, and he'd learned the right ones.

"You didn't lose control," he said, gently, crossing the room. "That's not what happened."

"It feels like—"

"You let someone _in._ That's what happened." He sat beside zir. "The reason I can crack Valentine's and do the cheek thing and know exactly what gift you want is that you _let_ me learn you. For years. You gave me the access. Nobody else ever got it — the exes, the whole office, the building, they all bounced off, because you never let them close enough to figure you out. You let me close." He shrugged. "So yeah. I know your moves now. I can counter you now. But only because you stopped guarding against me. That's not losing control. That's the thing you were never able to do with anyone else. You made yourself catchable. On purpose. For me."

Kyu sat with that.

"...So the reason I can't be smug," ze said slowly, "is that I trusted you enough to let you learn how to beat me."

"Basically, yeah."

"That's—" Ze exhaled, and something in the unsettled thing eased, because reframed like that it wasn't a loss at all — it was the single most vulnerable thing ze'd ever done, disguised as a losing streak. "That's _deeply_ annoying. You've turned my own — you've made 'you keep beating me' into a _love_ thing. I came in here upset about losing and you've made me feel — I was going to be _mad_ and now I feel—"

"Loved?"

"_Don't._" But there was no heat in it. "You did it again. Right now. I can't even be upset about not being able to be smug without you — you're doing the thing. You made _this_ one of your moves too. The reassurance. That's a move now. You've weaponized _comfort._"

"I have a lot of moves now." Will was warm, unhurried, not pushing. "You gave them all to me. Every single one. You handed me the whole toolkit by letting me close enough to build it." He took zir hand. "You didn't lose control, Kyu. You just finally trust someone enough to lose _to._ That's not the same as losing. That's the opposite of losing. That's the safest you've ever been."

And ze — who had come into the conversation genuinely rattled by zir own defeated streak — sat there holding Will's hand and understood ze'd been reframed out of zir own bad mood, again, by a man who had learned that the way to handle zir rare vulnerability was not to seize on it but to gently show ze it was a strength.

"...I still don't like not being smug," ze muttered.

"You don't have to like it. You just have to know why it's happening." He squeezed zir hand. "And for what it's worth — you can still out-smug the whole rest of the world. The bar, the building, strangers, everyone. It's just me. I'm the only one who gets to win. That's not you losing your edge. That's just the one person you let past it." A beat, and then, because he'd learned ze needed the exit: "Also you're going to be insufferable again by tomorrow. This is temporary. You'll find new moves. You always do. I'm just going to have to keep studying."

"...Yeah." The smug crept back, faintly, reassured. "I _will_ find new moves. This isn't over. You've gotten comfortable. Comfortable is when I strike."

"I look forward to it."

"You should be _afraid._"

"I'm a little afraid," Will lied, warmly, and let ze have that — the small returning edge, the sense of a rematch, the dignity restored — because he'd learned that the last, best move in the whole toolkit was knowing exactly when to let ze win one back.

---
# 
For all zir grumbling about lost ground and no smug and rematches to come, the day ended the way every day ended now, which told the truth ze'd never say out loud.

Kyu came to bed still muttering about it — "comfortable is when I strike," a whole speech about new moves and Will getting complacent — and Will listened, and made the appropriate afraid noises, and turned off the light. And then, in the dark, with all the threats spent and no audience left to perform them for, ze did the thing ze always did.

Ze burrowed in.

No announcement. No acknowledgment that ze was doing it. Ze just crossed the small distance in the big good bed and fit zirself against Will — head against his shoulder, an arm thrown across him, a leg hooked over, the whole long unbotherable menace of the third floor folding down into something warm and heavy and entirely without armor. The way ze did every night. The way ze'd done since before either of them called it anything.

Will got an arm around zir, easy, familiar, and said nothing, because saying something would make ze aware ze was doing it, and ze doing it _unaware_ was the whole point.

"You're smug," ze mumbled into his shoulder, not opening zir eyes. "I can feel you being smug. Stop."

"I'm not smug."

"You're thinking about how I lose all the arguments and then come cuddle you like a—" ze didn't finish, already sinking, "—it's undermining. My whole position. You know that."

"I know." He kept his hand moving through zir hair, the grey-white strands. "I won't tell anyone. The floor already knows anyway."

"The floor thinks I'm loud and dangerous." A yawn, ze pressing closer. "They don't know I do _this._ This one's not for them."

"I know. This one's just mine."

"...Yeah." And that was it — the whole day's argument about control and smugness and losing, dissolved into zir pressing closer in the dark, wordless, the leg hooked tighter, the _this, yes, stay_ said in the only grammar ze had. Because ze could lose every exchange, threaten every rematch, mourn the smug all ze wanted — and then night came, and ze rucked up against Will and slept, and that was the answer under all the noise. The uncatchable creature, caught, choosing to be caught, every single night, on purpose, and never once saying so.

"Night, Valentine," Will said, quiet, into the top of zir head.

"...Don't call me that." Already half-asleep. No heat in it. "'S a curse."

"Night."

"Night." A beat, smaller, the last thing before sleep, smuggled out where it was safe: "...Glad the walls were thin."

Ze only ever said it in the dark. But ze said it, most nights now — the whole enormous thing, folded into six words, said to a man who'd learned to hear it — and then ze was out, heavy and warm and held, and the floor, for once, heard nothing at all.

There was nothing to hear. There was just the two of them, and the good bed that didn't creak, and a wild thing finally, fully, asleep in the one place it had ever let itself rest.

---
# The con artist

"Will." Ze appeared in the doorway with the specific flatness that meant a bit was loading. "I have a confession. I'm just a simple bartender."

"Oh no."

"A humble worker. I pour drinks. I wouldn't know the first thing about _cars,_ or how to talk to a big scary man about a _transmission._" A tiny, devastating sniff. "If only there were someone. Someone strong. Someone wealthy. Someone who'd take pity on a poor little bartender—"

"You rebuilt a motorcycle engine last summer. On a livestream."

"That was a braver me. I'm delicate now." Ze wilted, a full-body collapse of helplessness from a person who could deadlift him. "Won't my big handsome CEO husband save me from the transmission?"

Will looked at zir. Ze looked back, dead-eyed, tragic, not selling it in the slightest and not needing to.

He got his keys.

"The sniff was too much," he said.

"The sniff was _perfect._"

"I'll take the car."

"My hero." Flat as a board, hand still pressed to zir chest. "However will I repay you. I'm but a poor bartender."

"Figure something out." He was already at the door.

"I'll pour you a drink. On the house. It's all I have." Ze went back to zir book, con concluded, victorious. "Drive safe. Think of me. Suffering here. Delicate."

Will took the car in. When he got back ze did the sniff again — "you _returned,_ I feared the worst" — and he told ze the sniff was still too much, and ze said the sniff was art, and at no point did either of them say a single true thing about any of it.

That was the whole marriage, really. Neither of them ever broke character. They didn't have to. They both already knew.

---
# Honeymoon

Will had prepared a speech. A good one, a week in the building — because a honeymoon was exactly the kind of Whole Thing ze'd shut down on principle, and he'd braced for the negotiation, the flat no, the treaty-writing.

He led soft, testing the ground. "So. We never did a wedding. I've been thinking—"

"We should go somewhere."

Will stopped. "...What?"

"A trip. Let's take one." Flat, already reaching for zir phone. "You've got the speech face on. Don't. I already want to go."

"I had a whole—"

"I know. You build a runway." Ze was scrolling now. "I've worked three jobs for years with no real break. We skipped the wedding. And I've never gone anywhere just to _go_ — always for work, or to leave somewhere. Never for fun. With someone." A shrug. "So. Where do you want to go."

Will sat there with a fully loaded, useless speech.

"You're supposed to fight me on this."

"Why would I fight you on a _vacation._" Ze glanced up, the corner of the mouth going. "Save the arguments for something I resist. This isn't a candle. It's a good time. I'm in favor of good times." Back to the phone. "Somewhere warm. Somewhere I can be nobody — no work, no bar, no floor, no family calling. Just us and nothing to do." A beat. "Book somewhere with good coffee. And don't tell Klaus or he'll want an itinerary."

And Will booked it — the speech unused, the battle that never came — because for once he'd braced for a fight over a thing ze simply wanted too.

---
# 
Somewhere warm, somewhere quiet, somewhere ze could be nobody — that had been the brief, and Will had delivered: a small coastal place, off the usual track, the kind with good coffee and no one who'd ever heard of the third floor.

The flight over, ze was ze — which is to say, ze knew the airport better than the staff, answered the customs officer in whatever language got them through fastest, and moved through the whole thing with the flat competence that made Will feel, as always, like the tourist he technically was. But something shifted once they landed and the last logistics fell away, and there was genuinely nothing to do, and nobody who knew ze, and no shift to cover or contract to finish or family to answer.

Ze got _quiet._ The good quiet. The kind that had nothing to prove.

They did almost nothing, which turned out to be the entire point. They slept in — ze, who never slept in, who ran on zir own hours and had for years, actually slept, because there was no reason not to. They ate slowly. Ze found the good coffee within an hour of arriving (of course ze did) and made it a ritual, the two of them on a small balcony in the mornings, the sea doing its thing, ze unbothered and barefoot and — Will noticed, and didn't say — more relaxed than he'd ever seen ze.

Because here was what the trip revealed, that home never quite could: at home, even happy, even married, ze was always _on_ a little. There was always a shift, a contract, a niece to corrupt, a floor to be the loud legend of, a whole ecosystem ze quietly ran. Ze never fully powered down because there was always something ze was tending. Here there was nothing. Nobody expected anything of zir. And the armor Will had spent years learning to see past just — came off, entirely, because there was no one to keep it on for.

"You're different here," Will said, on the second evening, the two of them watching the light go down over the water.

"I'm on vacation."

"No, it's — you're _off._ Fully off. I've never seen you all the way off." He wasn't making it a thing, just noticing, out loud, the way he'd learned to. "At home you're always running something in the background. The bar, the family, the — you're always a little bit working, even when you're not. Here you're just—" he searched for it, "—here. It's nice. You look like you can breathe."

"I can, actually." Ze said it simply, no deflection, because ze was too relaxed to bother reaching for one. "That's the thing about being nobody somewhere. Nobody needs me to be anything. No one's going to page me, or ask me to fix something, or need — I take care of a lot of people, back home. It's fine, I like it, but it doesn't stop. Here it stops." Ze leaned back. "I forgot what off felt like. It's been years. Might've been never."

And Will sat with that — the quiet enormity of it, that ze had spent a whole life being the one who provided, who fixed, who carried, and had genuinely never had a stretch where nothing needed carrying — and understood the trip was doing something no gift ever had. Not romancing ze. _Resting_ ze. Giving the wild thing a place with no demands, where it could put everything down and simply be, for a week, nobody's provider but its own.

They did small things. A market ze haggled through in a language Will didn't recognize, coming away with too much fruit and pleased about it. A boat, once, ze at ease on the water. A lot of nothing — reading, the balcony, the slow meals, ze sprawled across Will in the afternoons with no reason to get up. And at night the room had thick walls, for once, which ze noted with genuine delight — "no floor, no Klaus, no tally, we could be as loud as we want and _no one would know_" — and they tested that thoroughly, and no one did.

---
# 
The beach was the point, and it took Will until the third day to get ze into the water, because ze had opinions about it.

"It's _right there,_" Will said, on the first morning, gesturing at it. "The ocean. We came to the coast. Let's go in."

"In a bit." Ze was on the balcony with the good coffee, unhurried, in full vacation-off mode. "The sea's not going anywhere. That's the thing about the sea."

So they eased into it — the market, the boat, the slow mornings — and then on the third day ze finally, unceremoniously, walked down to the water and in, and Will discovered the thing he probably should have predicted: ze was, of course, a strong swimmer. Effortlessly. Because ze was good at everything, and swimming was just one more thing ze'd apparently absorbed at some point and never mentioned, and there ze went, out past where Will was comfortable, moving through the water with the same unbothered competence ze brought to a cocktail shaker or a git merge.

"Of course you're good at this too," Will called, wading in to his waist, indignant. "Of course. Why did I think there'd be one thing—"

"There's no one thing. You know there's no one thing." Ze floated on zir back, entirely at ease, the sun and the salt and the whole relaxed sprawl of zir. "Come out here. Stop standing in the shallows like a tourist."

"I _am_ a tourist."

"You're _my_ tourist. Come out." And ze doubled back, because ze wasn't going to let Will flounder — the provision reflex ran even here, in the ocean — and got Will out past the break, one hand steadying him when a swell came through, patient, the same way ze'd coached him through the rooftops of the gym once, except warmer, no whistle.

And it was good. It was the whole point, finally — the two of them out past the noise of the shore, the water holding them, ze relaxed in a way the land never quite managed, Will out of his depth in every literal sense and perfectly safe because ze was right there. They floated. Ze splashed him, once, deadpan, and denied it. Will retaliated and lost, because you cannot win a water fight against someone who swims like that. Ze dunked him, laughed the real laugh when he came up sputtering, and Will decided losing was fine, losing was the whole vacation, losing to zir in the ocean with the sun going was possibly the best he'd felt in years.

"See," Kyu said, later, the two of them drifting, catching their breath. "This is why the sea's the point. Not the sitting-near-it. The _in_ it." Ze floated, eyes closed, salt-wet and unarmored and entirely content. "I could stay out here. No floor. No walls. Just water. Nobody can page me in the ocean."

"We can stay as long as you want."

"I know we can. You're rich and I'm on vacation. Best combination there is." Ze cracked one eye at him, the corner of the mouth going. "Don't drown, though. I'd have to save you, and then you'd be smug about being carried, and I'm too relaxed to survive you being smug right now."

"I'd be a little smug."

"That's why I'm keeping an eye on you." And ze did — kept an eye on zir out-of-depth husband the whole time, casually, the way ze kept an eye on everything ze'd decided to look after, while the two of them floated in the warm water with nowhere to be and nobody who needed them, exactly the nobody-somewhere ze'd asked for, finally, actually, in the sea.

They swam every day after that. It was, they agreed, the point.

---
# 
They had, of course, brought the vacation energy into the bedroom, because it would not have been them otherwise.

That first thing ze had noticed on arrival — the thick walls — hadn't been an idle observation. It had been ze filing away a _possibility._ Because for the entire length of the relationship, the one constant constraint on the two of them, the thing woven into their whole origin, was the wall. The thin one. The one that meant every night came with an audience, a floor, a betting pool, Klaus and his tally.
