They'd made peace with it — ze was shameless, Will had learned to be past caring — but it was always _there,_ the third-floor awareness, the sense of being overheard.

Here there was none of that. Solid walls. No floor below. No neighbors who knew their names. For the first time in their entire history together, they were genuinely, completely _alone_ with it — and ze intended to make full use of the novelty.

"No wall," ze had said, the first night, with the specific gleam of a creature presented with new terrain. "Nobody listening. Nobody keeping a tally. Do you understand what that means?"

"That we can be normal about it for once?"

"That we can be the _opposite_ of normal about it, because there's no one to overhear the results." The corner of the mouth. "The wall's been holding us back this whole time and we didn't even notice. We've been _considerate._ We've been keeping something back for the floor's sake." Ze pulled him in. "There's no floor here. Nothing's off the table."

And so — being who they were, being ze specifically — the week was, among the swimming and the slow mornings and the good coffee, an enthusiastic exploration of everything the thin wall at home had always made them, on some level, mindful of. Ze came in with ideas. Ze always had ideas; ze was thorough, and shameless, and the full toy collection back home was evidence of a person who _thought_ about this. Away from the wall, unobserved, ze got to actually run the ideas without a single thought spared for who might hear, and Will — who had spent years being the shame-bearer of the relationship, dying at every broadcast — discovered that without an audience even _he_ loosened up, because the mortification had always been about being overheard, and there was no one to overhear.

They tried things. That's as specific as it needs to get — the door stays where it always stays. But it's fair to say ze approached the week's private hours the way ze approached everything ze'd decided to be good at: with total unbothered enthusiasm, complete absence of shame, and a clear intent to take full advantage of solid walls while they had them.

"This is _liberating,_" ze declared, at one point, wrecked and delighted, thick walls having earned their keep. "We should honeymoon more often just for this. No wall. No consideration required. Peak conditions."

"You're never considerate at _home,_" Will pointed out, breathless. "The whole floor—"

"I'm _slightly_ considerate. There's a ceiling on it at home, some baseline where even I go 'the neighbors have to sleep.'" Ze was insufferable, sprawled, pleased. "Here there's no ceiling. You're getting the _uncapped_ version. Lucky you." A beat. "Enjoy it. Back home the wall's thin and I go back to my regular, tragically _restrained_ self."

"That is not a restrained self, the whole building has a _betting pool_—"

"That's the _restrained_ version, Will. That's me holding back." The smug was total now, radiant. "This week's the real thing. No wall. No mercy. Try to keep up."

Will did not entirely keep up. That was, as ever, the point. And the thick walls kept every bit of it, and no one heard a thing, and no one kept a tally — which ze found, after years of the thin wall, almost novel enough to be its own kind of thrill.

They went home eventually, back to the thin wall and the floor and Klaus and the whole overhearing ecosystem — and ze went right back to the "restrained" version, which the third floor still could not keep up with. But ze had gotten the uncapped week. And every so often after, when Will suggested another trip, ze would agree with suspicious speed and say only "thick walls," flat, by way of reason — and Will knew exactly what ze meant, and booked it.

---
# # The new configuration

The trip ended. The wall did not get thicker. Everything else, the third floor would later agree, came back different.

It took Kyu four days to notice, which was three and a half days longer than it took the floor. Ze was at the counter on a Tuesday, cutting vegetables, in the middle of an ordinary evening — and Will came up behind zir, no preamble, hands settling at zir hips, mouth finding the back of zir neck with the unhurried confidence of a man who had done this exact thing many times, which he hadn't. That was the point. That had always been zir move.

Kyu did not stop cutting.

"You're initiating."

"I'm aware."

"On a Tuesday. Unprovoked. In my kitchen." Ze set the knife down, which was the concession, and turned, which was the surrender, and looked at him with open scientific interest. "You brought it home. The vacation thing. You weren't supposed to fit that in the luggage."

"Complain," Will said, against zir jaw, "any time."

Ze did not complain.

What ze did instead — because ze was zirself, and could not encounter a changed variable without isolating it — was run an experiment. Ze stopped initiating. Entirely. Cold. For a week ze made no moves at all, offered nothing, sprawled on the couch in maximum provocation and lifted not one finger, to establish whether the new behavior was a honeymoon echo that would decay on contact with the thin wall or an actual, load-bearing change in the man.

The data came in nightly. It did not decay.

"Interesting," ze said, on day six, flat on zir back, somewhat out of breath.

"You've been testing me." Will had figured it out around day three and let it run, which was its own new development. "You went full passive for a week to see if I'd keep—"

"Peer review," Kyu said. "Someone had to check the results were reproducible." A pause, staring at the ceiling, and then, with the reluctant gravity of a researcher confirming a rival's findings: "They're reproducible."

The floor, meanwhile, was in crisis.

Klaus's tally — years of meticulous, shameless record-keeping — had been built on a stable model: one instigator, one respondent, timing you could set a watch by. The new data broke the model. The chat lit up. Theories were floated. Someone proposed the honeymoon had "recalibrated the whole system" and was told to keep it scientific. The pool had to be restructured twice.

"Your pattern's gone irregular," Klaus informed them at the mailboxes, aggrieved, like they'd changed a bus schedule without notice. "There's no predicting it anymore. Wednesday? Nobody had Wednesday. Wednesday was never in play."

"Good," said Kyu, collecting the mail, radiant with something ze didn't bother to hide.

But the bar was where Will planted the flag.

He'd learned, by now, that zir moves bounced off him at a disadvantage and off zir not at all — that ze held immunity to zir own arsenal and always would. So he didn't use zir arsenal. He used his. He came in near close on a slow night, took his usual stool, waited for the lull, and when ze drifted down the bar to him he leaned in and said something — quiet, precise, fully worded, composed in advance the way he composed everything, in his register, sincere and absolutely filthy in equal measure and delivered like a term sheet.

Kyu was mid-pour.

The pour went a centimeter long.

Ze stopped it. Corrected it. Set the bottle down and looked at the glass, then at him, with the expression of a person who had just been scored on for the first time in a documented career.

"That," ze said, "was prepared."

"Extensively."

"You _drafted_ that. You've got a deck." The corner of zir mouth was fighting zir and losing. "You sat there with your crossword building a _pitch._"

"Slide two's better."

Kyu looked at him for a long moment — the flat face doing the small recalibration, the one he'd spent two years learning to read, that had never once meant nothing — and then wiped the bar down in front of him with terrible calm and said, "Shift ends at eleven. Hold slide two till we're home. If you burn it here I have to work another ninety minutes knowing the rest of the deck exists, and that's a hostile work environment."

"Now you know how the office felt."

"Now I know how the office felt," ze agreed, without a shred of repentance, and went to close out the far end of the bar — faster, he noticed, than the far end of the bar strictly required.

They took the short way home. First time ever. Ze set the pace.

Later — much later, the wall having fully briefed the floor, Wednesday now decisively in play — ze lay sprawled across him in the dark and delivered the verdict in the only language ze issued verdicts in.

"Keep the vacation thing," ze said. "That's the note. That's the whole review."

"Noted."

"And finish the deck." A yawn, zir face already against his shoulder, the smug audible even half-asleep. "I want the full presentation. Take your time on it. I'm a tough room."

---
# # The repayment

The problem announced itself the way all of Kyu's problems did: as an imbalance in a ledger.

Will had scored. Cleanly, in public, at zir own bar, with prepared material — and ze had reviewed the tape several times since, at zir leisure, with professional respect. But respect wasn't the issue. The issue was that the score now stood uneven, and ze had never in zir life let a debt sit. Groceries got out-bought. Tabs got pre-covered. Favors got returned in triplicate before the week was out. That was the system. That was the whole system.

Which meant the pitch had to be repaid. And repaid — this was the part that ruined zir week — _in the currency it was made in._

Because ze knew zir own arsenal wouldn't settle it. The desk-sitting, the doorway lean, the coming home from the gym insufficiently dressed — that was zir money, and paying a debt in your own currency at a favorable exchange rate was cheating, and ze did not cheat on ledgers. Will had come into zir arena and used _his_ register: worded. Composed in advance. Sincere and filthy at once, delivered like a term sheet. To return it properly, ze would have to do the same. Ze would have to _prepare remarks._

Ze got as far as opening a note on zir phone. Ze typed one line. Ze looked at it. Ze deleted it with the face of someone disposing of evidence.

"You're doing something," Will observed, that evening, from the couch.

"I'm not doing anything."

"You've been staring at your phone and grimacing. You never grimace. You're _composing._" He sat up, delighted, scenting it. "Oh my god. You're drafting a counter-deck."

"I'm checking the weather."

"You're checking the weather with the face of a man writing a _best man speech_—"

"The weather is _complicated,_" ze said, with dignity, and left the room.

The problem, ze concluded over the following days, was structural. Zir entire operating grammar ran on deniability — the smug carried the freight precisely because it never pronounced anything. Will's move had worked because he'd said the thing _out loud, in words, meaning it._ Sincerity was a load-bearing component. You couldn't strip it out and have the device still fire. Which meant repaying this debt required the one material ze had spent thirty-odd years not stocking.

Ze considered defaulting. For about four minutes, at the sink, ze genuinely considered letting Will keep the point.

Then ze finished the dishes, dried zir hands, and started preparing, because ze had never once defaulted and wasn't going to be broken by _this_, of all things, by a man who lunged at checks like a raccoon.

The delivery came on a Thursday. Ze picked the venue with care — his office had been done, her bar had been done, so ze chose the one arena with no precedent: an entirely ordinary breakfast. Will was reading something on his phone, coffee half-gone, defenseless, mid-toast.

"Hey," Kyu said.

"Mm."

"Look at me a second."

He looked up. And Will knew instantly — knew from the set of zir shoulders, which was wrong, formal, _braced,_ the posture of someone standing at a podium only ze could see — that something enormous was about to be attempted.

Ze held his eyes. Took a breath. And then said it — the prepared thing, the composed thing, full sentences, _worded,_ in his register and no protective wrapping anywhere on it: precise about what he did to zir, specific in a way that proved long observation, filthy in the middle of it and unbearably sincere at both ends, every clause paid for at full price. It was not long. It had clearly been _edited_ — cut down and down to the minimum survivable length, like someone shortening a walk over coals. Ze got all the way through it. Zir ears were scarlet by the end. Ze did not look away once.

Silence. Toast suspended.

"There," Kyu said, hoarse, straightening the cutlery for no reason. "Debt's settled. We're even. Never make me do that again."

Will put the toast down. Put the phone down. Stood up with terrible calm.

"You wrote that."

"I paid an _invoice_—"

"You drafted. You _edited._ You practiced — you practiced in the _bathroom,_ that's what the fan was, you ran the fan so I couldn't—" He was coming around the table now, and ze was backing up exactly one step, which was as far as zir pride would retreat. "You composed _sincere prepared remarks_, out loud, at breakfast, with your ears going—"

"The ears are unrelated—"

"The ears are _load-bearing._" He caught zir at the counter, close, radiant, entirely without mercy. "You know what the worst part is for you? It worked. It's the best thing anyone's ever said to me and I got it over _toast_ and I'm going to remember it word for word, forever, and you can't take it back. It's in the file. It's the _whole_ file now."

Kyu looked at him — cornered, scarlet, holding the flat face together through visible effort — and arrived at the only exit available.

"Slide two," ze said. "Kitchen counts as home. Burn it."

He burned it. The floor, at 9 a.m. on a Thursday, revised the model again — nobody had _morning,_ morning had never once been in play — and somewhere across the hall Klaus, without looking up from his coffee, added a column to the tally.

The ledger, for the record, did not stay settled. Will retaliated within the week, sincerity for sincerity, and ze retaliated back, and it became the one debt in the household that both of them serviced constantly and neither ever intended to close — the only account where Kyu, keeper of every balance, cook of every meal, payer of every bill, had quietly decided that _even_ was the wrong number to land on, and that owing him one, forever, was the correct cost of doing business.

---
# # The legend

Will had braced for it.

Not consciously — he'd have denied it under oath — but somewhere in the weeks before the honeymoon, a part of his brain had quietly convened a committee. Because he'd done the math. He had married the most shameless person on the continent. A person who narrated zir own sex life to strangers at a bar, unprompted, as small talk. A person whose reputation had a _floor_ attached to it, whose exploits had been load-bearing gossip for an entire building before Will had ever learned zir name. And ze had said it, at the airport, with the grin: _you're getting the uncapped version, lucky you._

So Will braced. Wondered, in the taxi, what the cap had been _holding._ Prepared himself, a modern and open-minded man, for the legend at full spec.

The uncapped version turned out to be the regular version, but happier.

That was it. That was the whole reveal. More relaxed, more unhurried, more sprawling, considerably louder now that there was no floor to brief — but structurally, _identical._ No apparatus. No revelations. Nothing Will could point at and say _there it is, the thing the cap was for._ Just Kyu, enthusiastic and straightforward and entirely at home, enjoying zirself the way ze enjoyed a good meal: with total appetite and zero garnish.

Will lay there on night three, staring at the ceiling, doing the audit.

"You're thinking," Kyu observed, from somewhere against his shoulder. "Loudly. What."

"Nothing."

"You've got the face. The spreadsheet face. Out with it."

"...That's it?" Will said, before diplomacy could stop him. "I mean — not _it_ it, it was — obviously it was — but that's the _legend?_ The whole third floor? Years of tally marks? The reputation that _preceded_ you into my life? This is the uncapped—"

Kyu propped up on one elbow and looked at him with genuine, unoffended curiosity, like he'd asked why water was wet.

"Good is good," ze said. "Why would I complicate a thing that works." A beat. "I don't garnish soup either."

"But you're — you're the most shameless person _alive_—"

"Shameless isn't the same as complicated." Ze said it patiently, a professional correcting a common industry misconception. "You mixed those up. Everyone mixes those up. The wall stuff was never about the _content_, Will, the content is meat and potatoes, always has been. I'm just not embarrassed about liking meat and potatoes _at volume._" Ze lay back down, case closed. "Freaks are ashamed people. Shame's the engine. I don't have the engine. What you married is a person with a healthy appetite and excellent fundamentals."

"Fundamentals," Will repeated, faintly.

"Strong fundamentals," Kyu confirmed, already half asleep. "Ask the floor."

And that would have been the end of it — a funny story, a theory collapsing, business as usual for a man whose theories about this person had a documented mortality rate — except that Kyu, who read everything, had of course not actually closed the case. Ze had merely filed it. And somewhere in the filing, ze had noticed the thing Will hadn't said.

Ze brought it up four days later, at breakfast, with the terrible casualness ze reserved for ambushes.

"So the interesting part," ze said, buttering toast, "isn't that I'm normal. It's that you're disappointed."

Will's coffee stopped halfway.

"I'm not _disappointed_—"

"Recalibrating, then." Ze took a bite, watching him with open scientific pleasure. "You braced for weeks. You did _reading_, I saw the tabs before you closed them, very studious. You came in ready for the legend and got soup, and some percentage of you — small, but I clocked it — had been _hoping._" Ze pointed the toast at him. "Which means the committee that did all that preparing had _ideas_, husband. Preferences, even. A little private catalogue you've been holding since god knows when, waiting for me to open the topic so you'd never have to."

"That is—" Will started, and then made the fatal error of pausing to choose his words, and Kyu watched him do it with the expression of a poker player watching an opponent physically hide his cards.

"There it is," ze said, delighted. "_There's_ the freak. Right on schedule. Shame's the engine — I told you, I don't have one, but _you_, you're _built_ on one, you've got a whole audience in your head and a permanent auditor and shame coming out of your ears, you're the most repressed man I've ever billed groceries to. Of _course_ it's you. It was always going to be you." Ze leaned back, radiant, insufferable, wronged party fully vindicated. "The floor had it backwards for years. The wall too. _I'm_ the meat and potatoes. You're the garnish, Will. You've been the garnish this entire time."

Will put his face in his hands.

"I want to hear the catalogue," Kyu said, gleeful, merciless, pulling zir chair closer with a screech. "Take your time. Use your slide voice. I have literally nowhere to be — no, don't do the face, this is a _safe space_, I run a _judgment-free establishment_—"

"You are the least judgment-free—"

"—strong fundamentals, willing to learn, excellent reviews," ze went on, over him, counting zir qualifications off on zir fingers, "and _deeply_ motivated, because — and I want to be honest with you here, since we're sharing — watching you go red like this is doing more for me than anything in your catalogue possibly could."

Which turned out, in the end, to be the actual settlement between them. Will, under duress, over the course of several deeply mortifying weeks, produced the catalogue — in installments, in his slide voice, dying visibly, while Kyu listened with the grave attention of a contractor reviewing blueprints and asked clarifying questions engineered solely to watch him combust. Some items ze approved on the grounds of sound engineering. Some ze vetoed as _overdesigned_ — "that's not a preference, that's a logistics problem, who's doing the _laundry_ after." One or two ze filed away without comment, which was somehow the most alarming outcome of all.

And the floor, whose model had survived the honeymoon, the Wednesday crisis, and the morning incident, never did figure out what to do with the newest data — because the noise profile hadn't changed at all, and yet Klaus swore, with a statistician's instinct, that something in the _pattern_ had. He was right. He just had the direction wrong, and the two people who knew were never, ever going to file the correction.

"Told you," was all Kyu said, months later, apropos of nothing, at volume, through the wall. "Garnish."

---
# # The catalogue, annotated

For the record — and Klaus's model, had it ever gotten access, would have confirmed this — the catalogue turned out to be less depraved than _thematic._ Every item, once ze laid them out on the mental table, pointed the same direction, and Kyu clocked the direction by item two.

"These are all the same kink," ze said, wondering. "You've written the same slide nine times. Every one of these is just — you, not in charge, being told what to do, by me." Ze looked up. "Will. You run a company. You have _one_ fantasy and it's _delegation._"

"That is a reductive—"

"You want to be _managed._ God. Of course you do. You're managed nowhere. Okay." Ze cracked zir knuckles, a contractor accepting a very easy job. "This is barely even a catalogue, this is a _staffing request._"

So: the annotated results.

**Approved on the grounds of sound engineering:** the bossing around, obviously, which required Kyu to change almost nothing about zir existing management style except venue. Also the blindfold, which ze endorsed as "sensory optimization, basically ergonomics" — and which backfired magnificently, because it turned out that removing Will's sight just meant his entire experience was Kyu's _voice_, unhurried, narrating, smug at close range in the dark, and the floor filed that night under _unprecedented_ without ever learning why.

**Vetoed as overdesigned:** the elaborate scenario ones. "That's not a preference, that's community theater." Roleplay died in pre-production, not because Kyu was embarrassed — ze was constitutionally incapable — but because ze _would not act._ "I'm not calling you that. You're Will. I know you. The premise collapses immediately." Will argued the point exactly once, got a flat "you want me to pretend to be a stranger who finds you attractive, when the documented reality is _better than that,_" and lost the argument by blushing at it.

**Approved with amendments:** the restraints, which Kyu assessed the way ze assessed all hardware — checked the load rating, vetoed Will's first purchase as "decorative garbage," and sourced proper equipment zirself, which was somehow the single most mortifying part of the entire process for Will and the most ordinary Tuesday imaginable for zir. The amendment: it worked in one direction only. They tried it the other way exactly once, experimentally, and discovered that tying Kyu up disabled precisely nothing that mattered — the mouth kept working, the running commentary kept running, the smug actually _intensified_ under conditions of theoretical helplessness — and Will untied zir after twenty minutes on the grounds that he was losing an argument to a person in restraints and it was destroying him. "Told you," Kyu said, rubbing zir wrists, victorious. "You can't tie up the problem. The problem is load-bearing."

**Approved instantly, no notes:** the praise. Which Will had buried at the bottom of the deck, deepest shame last, and which turned out to be the one item Kyu received without a single joke — just a long look, and then a quiet, thoughtful "yeah, okay," because ze had already noticed, months ago, what _you're doing so well_ did to him when ze'd only been kidding. Ze stopped kidding. The results were, per zir report, "conclusive."

**And the two filed without comment:** one of Will's, which ze never mentioned again and Will was too afraid to ask about, and — this was the part nobody had bet on — one of _zirs._ Because somewhere in week three of Will's mortified installments, Kyu had gone quiet, and then said, casually, terribly casually, buttering toast, "I'll add one." Just one. Ze delivered it in a single flat sentence, no preamble, ears staying suspiciously neutral, and Will — who had spent weeks combusting — sat very still and realized he was holding the only entry in the entire catalogue that had _cost_ something to say.

He never told anyone what it was. The floor never got it. Klaus's tally has a column with no header to this day.

It's in the file.

---
# # The gray

The strands had been there since before Will knew zir name.

He'd seen them the first week — you couldn't not, up close, silver-white threads scattered through the black, more at the temple, catching light when ze turned. And he'd said nothing, that whole first year, for the same reason everyone said nothing: some instinct that filed them under _don't_, next to the soup and the blank square. The floor never mentioned them. The bar regulars never mentioned them. An entire ecosystem of people who discussed this person's sex life at the mailboxes had collectively, wordlessly agreed the hair was off the menu.

So it took Will — new-era Will, the one who'd learned that the _don't_ pile was where all the good material lived — until a slow Sunday morning to finally do it. Kyu was against him, half awake, head on his chest, and the light was doing the thing to zir hair it always did, and Will reached up and caught one silver strand between two fingers, gently, like picking up a wire he wasn't sure was live.

"Been meaning to ask about these for two years."

He felt zir come slightly more awake. Not tense. Recalibrating.

"The gray."

"The gray."

A pause, in which Will braced for the deflection — _genetic thing_, the eye answer, topic closed. Instead ze said, into his chest, entirely unbothered:

"Had them since my twenties. They just showed up. No story." A beat. "Why, you counting?"

"There's more than there were."

"That's called _time,_ Will. It happens to everyone who isn't preserved by guilt like you." Ze tilted zir head back to look at him, reading his face the way ze read everything, fast and total — and then narrowed zir eyes, because ze'd found something. "Wait. What's the face. Why is this a _thing._ You've been sitting on this for two _years_ — did you think it was—" and Will watched zir arrive at it, watched the genuine surprise land, "—you thought there was a _story._ You thought it was going to be sad. You've been protecting a _hair color._"

"Everyone protects it! The whole building! Nobody's ever once mentioned it, there's clearly an understanding—"

"There's no understanding, you idiots invented an understanding." Ze was fully awake now and openly delighted. "Two years. A betting pool with _columns_ and nobody could ask about _hair._ What did you all decide it was? Be honest. Tragedy? Some kind of—" ze waved a hand, "—event?"

"...The theories varied," Will admitted.

"The theories _varied._" Ze put zir face back down on his chest and laughed — the real one, undignified, the whole body in it. "It's _melanin_, Will. It's the one thing about me that has no file. You people walked past the actual mysteries daily and built a shrine around _pigment._"

"In our defense, everything else about you turned out to have a file—"

"Fair," ze conceded, muffled, still shaking.

Will went back to it, the one strand, turning it in the light. And then, because he was new-era Will and the door was open and doors around here closed fast:

"I like them. That's the actual confession. That was the whole agenda for this meeting." He said it plainly, in his register, no bit available to hide in. "The gray. I noticed it before I knew your name — you were carrying record crates and the light hit it and I remember thinking, unhelpfully, at a stranger who'd just insulted my taste in music, _oh no._ So. Two years of minutes, filed. I like them. I hope there are more."

Silence. The specific silence.

Then, flat, into his chest, ears going: "You _would_ romanticize entropy."

"I married it."

"Unbelievable." But ze didn't move, and didn't deflect further, and after a while — payment, delivered at cost, in the smallest possible denomination — ze said, quietly, "The bar light's better for it. If you're going to stare, stare there."

Which was, Will understood by now, an invitation.

He took it. He became, in the following months, completely insufferable about it — a man openly watching his own husband under bar light like the floor watched the wall, until Kyu threatened to dye it just to end him, and did not dye it, and would not have dyed it under torture. Klaus asked once what he was always looking at. Will said "the fundamentals" and left it there.

And the gray kept coming, unhurried, the way time does when nothing is wrong — and if ze started angling the bar's one good lamp slightly differently on the nights Will took his stool, well. There was no file on that either.

---
# # The freezer incident

The mirror days had a signature. Kyu had it catalogued within a month: Will lingering half a beat too long at the hallway mirror in the morning, the small tilt of the head, the fingertip that came up near his eye and pretended to be fixing his hair. He never said anything. The auditor did the talking internally, and Will just paid the fines in silence and left for work slightly quieter than usual.

Kyu said nothing either. Ze had run the numbers on saying something and the numbers were bad — a compliment would bounce off that auditor like everything bounced off that auditor, and an observation would confirm the whole premise. So ze took the third route, the house route, the only route ze'd ever trusted.

Will came home on a Thursday — a bad mirror day, the worst in a while, a video call in unflattering light having done what video calls in unflattering light do — and opened the freezer for no reason, the way people open freezers when the day has been long, and found it.

A pint. Good brand. His flavor, the specific one, the one he'd mentioned exactly once, months ago, in passing, at a shop that didn't carry it.

He stood there with the freezer open, feeling the cold on his face, doing the math. Kyu didn't eat this flavor. Kyu barely ate dessert. Kyu was, at this moment, on the couch, reading, radiating a neutrality so perfect it was practically a signature.

"Kyu."

"Mm."

"There's ice cream in the freezer."

"Freezer's where it goes."

"It's my flavor. The one from the shop that doesn't—" Will turned, holding the pint like evidence. "You went somewhere for this."

"Had errands." Ze turned a page. "It was on the way."

"Nothing is on the way to that shop. That shop is on the way to _nothing._ I've looked it up, it's geographically hostile—"

"New route," Kyu said, serene, eyes on the book. "Very efficient. You wouldn't know it."

And Will — standing in the kitchen light with a pint going soft in his hand — did the second calculation, the slower one. Because the timing wasn't random. Nothing about this person was ever random. Ze had clocked the mirror. Of course ze had clocked the mirror; ze clocked everything, ze'd probably graphed it. Which meant this was — the pint was —

"Is this," he said carefully, "a comment on my face."

Kyu looked up for the first time. Gave him the flattest look in the building's recorded history.

"It's dessert, Will. Not everything is about the settling."

A silence, in which the word landed.

"The _settling._"

"Structural term." Back to the book. "Buildings do it. Perfectly normal. Foundation's fine."

"You've _noticed._ You've noticed and you've _named_ it, there's a _term_, how long has there been a—"

"Eat your ice cream."

"You can't just deploy a load-bearing metaphor and then—"

"Spoon's in the drawer," Kyu said, with the finality of a closed ticket, and that was visibly all the conversation that would ever be available on the subject.

So Will got the spoon. Sat on the other end of the couch with his pint and his settling and his geographically hostile ice cream, and ate it, and somewhere around the third spoonful discovered he felt — not younger, nothing so cheap — _accounted for._ Surveyed, assessed, and cleared. A building someone had looked at closely, recently, with expert eyes, and decided was sound.

"The foundation's fine?" he said, to the room, not quite looking over.

"Foundation's excellent," Kyu said, to the book, not quite looking over either. "Wouldn't have bought the building otherwise."

Will ate his ice cream. The auditor, for once, had nothing to add — provision had been made in a currency it couldn't dispute, and the invoice, as always, read _had extra._ And if the pint kept reappearing after that, restocked without comment on a schedule that mapped suspiciously well onto the mirror days, well. New route. Very efficient. He wouldn't know it.

---
Different medicine, different day. The ice cream was for the mood problem — Thursday needed sugar, not skincare. But the eye cream is absolutely still in the pipeline, because Kyu diagnosed two separate issues that evening and ze doesn't treat two problems with one purchase.

The eye cream arrives later, and quieter. No scene, no freezer moment — that's the difference in the delivery design. Ice cream _wants_ to be found the same day; eye cream wants to never be discussed at all. So it just materializes on Will's shelf one morning between his existing things, unlabeled, good brand, correct formula for exactly the region under audit — and Will, mid-routine, toothbrush in his mouth, stops and looks at it. Knows immediately. Knows there is no version of asking about it that ends well, because "is this for my wrinkles" forces Kyu to either confirm a comment on his face or lie, and zir already demonstrated the official position is _it's dessert, Will._

So he uses it. Says nothing. And the jar refills itself on the same phantom schedule as the pint — a supply chain with no invoices, no acknowledgment slot, no thank-you accepted. The only receipt Will ever gets is that months later, fishing shamelessly, he angles his face at the lamp and says "the settling looks slower lately, don't you think," and Kyu, without looking up: "Maintenance works when you do it. Foundation's fine." Which is as close to _I bought it, you're welcome, you look good_ as that grammar will ever print.

---
# The compounding

Will noticed it the way you notice a plant has grown — not in the moment, only against the record.

Because there was a record. Of course there was a record; he was Will. Somewhere in his head lived a file with a start date, and the start date said: _you loved this person completely two years ago._ He remembered the certainty of it. Standing in that bar with a toilet paper roll, thinking there was no more room, that the thing was full, that a man could not possibly hold more of this than he currently held.

The file said otherwise now. The file said the two-years-ago number, which had felt like a ceiling, had been quietly revised upward so many times that the original figure looked like a deposit. And the revisions never came from the big things — not the wedding, not the honeymoon, not the breakfast that lived word-for-word in permanent storage. The revisions came from Tuesdays. From watching ze debate two brands of rice with total seriousness. From the sound of the lock at 1 a.m. and the specific weight of zir footsteps deciding whether he was asleep. From the way ze said "husband" like a job title ze'd hired him into and was, on the whole, satisfied with the performance. Every ordinary day arrived, added some small unasked-for amount, and closed. Compounding. The one investment his father would've approved of, growing at an interest rate no bank would believe.

He said it out loud exactly once — because he was Will, and things that stayed unworded made him itch.

"It keeps going up," he said, apropos of nothing, on the balcony. "That's the strange part. I keep thinking I've hit the number and then it's Tuesday and there's more. It shouldn't work that way. Everything else in my life plateaus. You're the only thing that compounds."

Kyu didn't look over. Took a slow breath of the evening, considered it, and said: "Sounds inefficient. All that overhead. Recalculating daily."

"It does itself. No overhead. Best-run thing I own."

"You don't own it."

"Co-signed, then."

A pause. The corner of the mouth. "Better."

And that was the whole exchange, and Will didn't push for more, because the remarkable thing — the thing that would've been unthinkable two years ago — was that he didn't need to. He _knew_. Not hoped, not inferred from favorable evidence like a man building one of his doomed theories. Knew, with the flat certainty of a fact that had been demonstrated past all argument, that it ran both directions.

Because ze had told him. Never in the sentence — the sentence had been issued exactly once, at cost, over toast, and both of them understood it was a lifetime license, not a subscription. But in everything else, daily, at volume. It was in the freezer on the bad mirror days. It was on his shelf, unlabeled, refilling itself. It was in the bar lamp, angled. It was in the fact that ze — a person who had once rearranged zir entire life around the assumption that nobody was coming — now cooked for two without checking, planned around his schedule without being asked, reached for his side of the bed in zir sleep and, finding him, settled. It was in "the foundation's fine" and "keep the vacation thing" and "you're it for me" translated into forty dialects of provision, published daily, on every channel except the verbal one.

Once, he would have needed the words. That was the old Will, the one who theorized from surface data and got everything wrong. The new one had learned to read the actual ledger — and the ledger was unambiguous. Ze loved him. It was documented. It was _load-bearing._ Six witnesses, a betting pool, a floor of testimony, and a jar of eye cream that never ran out.

"You're doing the face," Kyu said, from the other chair, eyes closed. "The compounding face. I can hear it."

"Just reviewing the accounts."

"Mm." A long silence, evening settling over the street, and then — quietly, to the sky, in the smallest denomination, which he had learned was the largest one: "Numbers look good from here too."

Will didn't say anything. Didn't reach for it, didn't convert it, didn't thank zir. Just let it land, the way ze'd taught him — filed it, kept his eyes on the street, and sat there next to his whole portfolio while the light went down.

It kept going up. Nobody audited it. It was the one account in the house that both of them had agreed, without ever discussing it, to let run forever unbalanced — each of them privately convinced they owed the other, both of them wrong, both of them right, the interest compounding quietly through every ordinary Tuesday for the rest of their lives.

___
# The policy

Will found the policy in the fine print, the way he found everything — three days before the trip, mid-itinerary-review, at eleven p.m.

"Kyu."

"Mm."

"The onsen. The traditional one you booked, the famous one, the one you were weirdly efficient about—" He turned the tablet around. "There's a policy."

"There's always a policy."

"*No clothing.* None. It's — it's mandatory, it's not a suggestion, there's a whole paragraph, they cite *tradition* and *hygiene* and there's a diagram—"

"Yeah." Ze didn't look up from packing. "That's how they work. Swimsuits contaminate the water. You rinse, you go in. It's the correct system."

"You *knew.*"

"It was in the listing." A pause, and then, with the mild interest of someone finally locating the noise: "Why is your voice doing that."

His voice was doing that, it turned out, for seventy-two consecutive hours. Will read reviews. Will researched etiquette with the thoroughness of a man preparing for a hostile audit. Will asked, twice, casually, whether there were private booking options, and was informed both times that there were, and that they had not been booked, because the open-air pool was the entire point, it faced the mountains, you didn't fly this far to sit in a *tub*.

"It's strangers," Will said, on the plane, resuming a conversation from six hours earlier as though no time had passed. "A pool of strangers. And me. With no—"

"They're not looking at you."

"You don't know that—"

"I do, actually. Nobody's looking at anybody. That's the whole social contract of the place. Everyone's naked so nobody's naked." Ze turned a page. "It's the same as the wall, Will. Total exposure, zero audience. You'd think you of all people would recognize the architecture."

He did not find this comforting. He found this, somehow, worse.

The changing room was where the divergence became structural. Kyu undressed the way ze did everything — unhurried, unbothered, folding each of the four shirts' traveling representative with the efficiency of a person to whom this was logistics — and was done, towel in hand, small towel, *ceremonial* towel, before Will had committed to removing his watch.

"You're negotiating with a sock," Kyu observed.

"I'm *acclimating*—"

"You're stalling in a locker room. There's a mountain out there. Sock off. Let's go."

And then — the pool. Steam, dusk, the mountains doing exactly what the listing promised, four or five strangers scattered through the water at respectful distances, all of them, as forecast, monumentally uninterested in anything but their own dissolving spines. Kyu walked out into it like ze'd been born there. Rinsed, waded in, settled against the stone rim with the little towel folded on zir head — on zir *head*, correctly, ze'd done the reading years ago apparently or possibly never needed to — and exhaled a breath that seemed to leave zir two centimeters shorter.

Will made the journey from the door to the water in the posture of a man crossing a stage he had not agreed to be on. Nobody looked. He was certain everyone looked. Both were true in the only way that mattered. He got in fast, sat down faster, and submerged himself to the chin with the profound relief of the recently amnestied.

Silence. Steam. Mountains.

"See," Kyu said, eyes closed, head back, sixty seconds later. "Nothing happened."

"Something could have happened."

"Nothing was ever going to happen. You did a perp walk past five people whose eyes were closed." The corner of the mouth. "One of them is *asleep*, Will."

And here was the thing Will slowly discovered, over the following forty minutes, as the water did its work and the auditor gradually lost signal in the steam: ze was right about the architecture. Nobody looked. Nobody cared. The shame had nowhere to attach — no audience, no seats, no tally. He felt it happen almost mechanically, the same discovery as the honeymoon: remove the witnesses and even Will loosened, vertebra by vertebra, until he was just a man in hot water next to his husband in front of a mountain, and the whole seventy-two-hour crisis revealed itself as a tax he'd prepaid on an event that never occurred.

"Okay," he admitted, to the dusk. "Fine. It's the correct system."

"Mm."

"You could've just *told* me nobody looks."

"I did tell you. Twice. You don't take testimony." Ze cracked one eye at him, the gray at zir temple gone damp and dark, steam rising off zir shoulders, entirely at home in the one venue on earth built on zir personal operating principles. "You needed the evidence. Now you have it. It's in the file."

A long soak of a pause. The mountain went purple. Somewhere across the pool, the sleeping man snored gently, sealing his testimony.

"Although," Kyu added, unhurried, to the sky, in the tone of a person filing a completely neutral observation, "for the record — *I* looked."

Will went under to the nose.

"Foundation's fine," ze said, serene, and closed the eye again, and the mountain, professionally discreet, pretended not to have heard it.

___

# **The citrus**

The mandarins arrived at dusk on the second evening, and Will thought, at first, that he was hallucinating from the heat.

But no — an attendant with a wooden basin, moving along the pool's edge, tipping them in by the dozen: small bright mandarins, bobbing out across the dark water, riding the steam currents, bumping gently off stones and shoulders and drifting on. Seasonal thing, the sign by the door had said, in three languages Will hadn't read closely. The fruit scents the water. Good for the skin, good for the circulation, good — evidently — for the soul, because the sleeping man woke up, regarded the orange flotilla with deep approval, and went back to sleep.

The whole pool went faintly, impossibly fragrant. Citrus and steam and mountain dusk.

"Okay," Will conceded, watching a mandarin navigate past his knee like a small unhurried boat. "This place is showing off now."

"Mm." Kyu was in zir usual position — head back against the stone, eyes shut, towel folded on zir head, arms out along the rim, the posture of a person who had achieved a state most religions only advertise. A mandarin drifted into zir shoulder, rested there a moment as if greeting a colleague, and moved on. Ze didn't stir. Ze had, in the way of ze, simply incorporated the fruit into the situation without comment, the way ze incorporated everything.

And Will — warm, loose, three days from his auditor, watching steam curl off the water and small orange moons orbit his entirely serene husband — felt the idea arrive with the terrible clarity that ideas have in hot water.

The towel. The little towel. Folded on zir head. Flat on top. Like a plinth.

He moved slowly. That was the discipline of it — no wake, no ripple, a man with a company's worth of patience deploying all of it at once. He selected his mandarin with care: round, bright, structurally sound. He drifted, by degrees, into range. Kyu's breathing didn't change. The mountain held its breath.

And Will, with the gentle precision of a man defusing something, placed the mandarin on top of the folded towel on top of his husband's head. It sat perfectly.

Kyu did not move. Did not open zir eyes. The mandarin rode the towel like it had been installed at the factory — and for one long, sacred, steam-wrapped moment, the composition was complete: the flat unbotherable face, the ceremonial towel, the small bright fruit crowning the whole arrangement, mountains going purple behind it. Will pressed both hands over his mouth and shook, silently, with his whole body.

"I can hear you vibrating," Kyu said, without opening zir eyes.

"I'm not doing anything."

"There's weight distribution on my head that wasn't there before, Will."

"That's the minerals."

A pause. Ze reached up — unhurried, one hand — and touched the towel. Found the mandarin. Held it there a moment, reading it by touch like a safecracker. And then, instead of removing it, ze lowered the hand back into the water, resettled zir shoulders against the stone, and said, with the gravity of an official ruling:

"Balanced. Good instincts. It stays."

"It *stays?*"

"It's not hurting anyone. It's seasonal." A beat. "I'm decorative now. Festive, even. This is what peak relaxation looks like, take notes."

So it stayed. Kyu sat there in the dark water, eyes closed, steam rising, a mandarin enthroned on zir head with full institutional approval, and continued zir soak with a dignity so total, so unimpeachable, that the composition circled all the way past ridiculous into something like majesty. The other bathers, professionally discreet to the end, absolutely noticed and absolutely said nothing — though one woman across the pool quietly placed a mandarin on her own towel in what could only be read as tribute, and the tradition, for all Will knew, began that night.

He looked at his husband — flat-faced, fruit-crowned, radiating a serenity no shame could reach and never had — and felt the number revise itself upward again, unasked, the way it did on Tuesdays.

"What," Kyu said, sensing it.

"Nothing. Compounding."

"Do it quieter. The fruit needs stillness." And ze tipped zir head back a fraction more — carefully, keeping the crown seated — and gave the mountain the corner of the mouth, and let him look.

___
# The second mandarin

There was a moment — steam, dusk, the crown seated, the mountain holding still for it — where Will looked at zir and the thought arrived whole, without drafting, in no slide voice at all: *I'm keeping this person forever.* Not deciding it. Noticing it. The decision had been made years ago by someone in him with better instincts than his committees, and tonight was just the memo reaching the top floor. This exact creature — flat-faced, fruit-bearing, serene beyond the reach of any embarrassment ever manufactured — was his, on paper, in perpetuity, and the sheer luck of it went through him like the heat did.

A normal man would have said something. Will had been a normal man once. Instead he reached, with great tenderness, for a second mandarin.

The approach was slower this time, because the stakes were higher — there was an existing structure to respect. He drifted in. Selected for size: slightly smaller than the first, correct for stacking, he'd done the engineering without noticing he was doing it. And with the reverence of a man adding a floor to a cathedral, he placed it on top of the first.

It held. A tower now. Two mandarins, one towel, one husband, the whole assembly rising out of the steam like a small shrine to something the religion hadn't named yet.

Kyu was silent for a long moment. The tower was silent. The mountain was silent.

"There's development on the site," ze said at last, eyes still closed. "Unpermitted."

"It's an expansion. The first one was doing so well."

"You're stacking fruit on your husband."

"I am," Will agreed, from a place of complete peace, "stacking fruit on my husband."

Ze reached up again — the safecracker hand — and read the situation by touch. Two units. Vertical. Load path acceptable. The hand came back down.

"Structure's sound," ze ruled. "It stays. But this is the height limit. Third one and the whole district gets rezoned, and I rezone *you* into the cold pool." A pause, and then, because ze could read weight distribution but also everything else, always: "You're doing the face again. Under the giggling. The other face."

"Which face."

"The forever face." Ze said it flatly, to the sky, a surveyor reporting a finding. "You put it on right before the second mandarin. Whole thing was a ceremony. You think I can't feel a ceremony land on my head."

Will didn't deny it, because there was no denying it, and because he'd learned by now that around this person, the truth got you further than the cover story anyway.

"I was thinking," he said, "that I'm keeping you. That's the finding. Full report."

Silence. Steam. The tower stood.

Kyu didn't do anything for a while. Didn't deflect, didn't invoice it, didn't reach for the bit — just sat there under two mandarins with the ears going, faintly, visibly, the only part of the flat face that had never once learned to lie. And then ze shifted along the stone rim, careful, keeping the crown level — an engineering feat, a whole negotiation with physics — and closed the distance, and settled against his side in the hot water, shoulder to shoulder, the tower miraculously surviving the voyage.

"Keeping's mutual," ze said, to the mountain, in the smallest denomination. "For the record. It's in the file."

And they sat like that while the water went dark and the citrus drifted and the crown held — one man, one husband, two mandarins, the count going up on everything worth counting — until the attendant came through at closing and found them, and bowed slightly, and said nothing at all, because some compositions you don't disturb, you just dim the lights around them and let the mountain keep watch.
___
# The trip report

The office asked, because the office always asked. Will came back from a week away with hot-spring skin and a suspiciously settled air, and Lena cornered him at the coffee machine with the standard debrief: how was it, was it worth the flight, were the springs as good as the reviews said.

And Will opened his mouth to answer and discovered the file was corrupted.

The springs. Were the springs good. He consulted memory and memory returned — steam, yes, a mountain, some purple in it, water that was presumably hot because springs were hot. Had it helped his back? His back felt fine. Was that the minerals? He genuinely could not say. He'd read seventy pages of reviews before the trip and could not now recall a single claim from any of them.

"They were good," he said, with the confidence of a man citing a source. "Kyu said they were good."

"Kyu said."

"Kyu rated them very highly. Correct system, sound engineering." He nodded, secondhand, a tourist reporting a country through an interpreter. "The circulation thing is real, apparently. Ze had data."

"Okay, but what do *you* remember—"

And that was the question that broke it open, because what Will remembered — what his memory had kept, with perfect fidelity, in high resolution, at the expense of apparently everything else including an entire week of luxury bathing — was the tower.

Two mandarins. One towel. His husband beneath the whole arrangement with zir eyes closed and the mountain going dark behind zir, ruling *structure's sound, it stays* with the gravity of a planning commission. Will could recall the exact wobble of the second mandarin settling. The ears going. *Keeping's mutual.* The attendant's bow. He had, somewhere in his head, a complete architectural record of a fruit installation and its permitting process, and almost nothing else — the seventy-two-hour nudity crisis included, which he knew had happened, factually, the way you know about a war from before you were born. He'd been *worried* about something. The record showed worry. The content of the worry had been overwritten, wholesale, by citrus.

"There was fruit," he told Lena, helplessly, glowing. "In the water. Seasonal. I built a tower."

"You built a—"

"On Kyu. It's — there was a towel involved, it's structural, you had to—" He gave up and pulled out his phone, because of course there was a photo, one photo, taken at great personal risk in the changing-room doorway on the last night with the crown still seated, and it was now, without contest, the most valuable object he owned.

Lena looked at it for a long time.

"You flew eleven hours," she said, "to a legendary hot spring, and your review is 'my spouse said it was good and I stacked oranges on zir head.'"

"Mandarins," Will corrected, pocketing the phone, entirely at peace. "And yes. Five stars. Would not remember again."

Which was, when he thought about it later — and he did, at the mirror, where the settling was proceeding on schedule and bothering him not at all that morning — exactly how the accounting worked now, and had worked for a while. The auditor kept minutes on everything: costs, risks, exposure, the whole liability ledger of being a person in public. And then some Tuesday quality of zir came along and the minutes just — didn't survive. The worry got prepaid, the event occurred, and the only line item memory retained was the part where ze let the ridiculous thing stay. Ze'd have a term for it, probably. Selective retention. Storage optimization. *You don't take testimony, Will, you keep evidence.*

He kept the evidence. It was in the file — the only entry from the entire trip, and the whole review, and enough.

---
# The last invoice

The graduation was a family operation, because everything was now.

Will drove — the sister had banned Kyu from driving on the grounds that ze parked "like the building owed zir money" — and Jason navigated badly from the passenger seat while his wife, who had learned years ago to bring her own directions, corrected him from the back with one year's worth of seniority and no mercy. Mai sat between her mother and Kyu in a dress she had already negotiated two concessions on, holding the card she'd made. It said _CONGRATURATION_ in six colors. Nobody had corrected it. Nobody was going to.

They took up most of a row in the hot auditorium. Will in the good shirt, steaming-creases sharp; Jason filming vertically despite his wife's instructions; Mai standing on her seat at every wrong moment; and Kyu at the aisle end, flat-faced through two hours of names, watching the last of the kids ze'd raised — half-raised, looked after, funded, whichever word the year required — walk across a stage and become, officially, a person who no longer needed anything.

Jason whooped when her name was called. His wife whooped louder. Mai screamed "THAT'S MY AUNT" with the full authority of six years old, and Will clapped like a man at his own daughter's graduation, which drew a look from Kyu that he ignored completely.

Kyu didn't make a sound. But ze stood up. Nobody mentioned it.

The sister found them afterward in the crowd, still in the gown, and worked the row — hugged Jason, hugged his wife, held Mai upside down briefly by request, accepted Will's entirely oversized flowers ("they had extra," he said, straight-faced, and the whole family groaned at once because the joke belonged to the house now). And then she got to Kyu, at the end, and didn't do the moment — she'd learned, after the wedding, what the moments cost ze. She did the other thing instead. She held out the diploma.

"Hold it," she said. "You paid for it. You should get to hold it."

Kyu held it. Looked at it a while, the weight of it, the name on it. Handed it back.

"Heavy," ze said. Which was the whole speech.

"Yeah," the sister said, taking it back, eyes bright and voice steady, because she'd learned that too. "It's the good paper."

"CAN I HOLD IT," said Mai, who had not learned anything and never would, and the moment broke the way this family's moments always did — sideways, into noise, everyone talking at once, Jason saying absolutely not, she'll eat it, his wife saying she won't _eat_ it, Jason, she's six — and Kyu stepped back half a pace and watched all of it with the flat face on, which Will, holding the flowers, could read fluently by now: the day filing itself somewhere permanent.

---

The trouble started the next month, when the transfer went out on schedule and came back three days later. Full amount. Memo line: _I have a job now._

Kyu sent it again. It came back. _I HAVE A JOB. A GOOD ONE. STOP._

Ze sent it a third time, at which point the sister escalated through channels — meaning she called Will, copied Jason, and by that evening the whole family knew, because this family had no other setting.

"zir still sending it," she said. "I've returned it twice. Tell your husband the account is _closed._ I graduated. That was the finish line. zir been paying for my life since I was in primary school, Will — it's done now. That's supposed to be the good part. Tell ze the good part happened."

Jason's contribution, relayed separately: "Ze did the same thing to me when I got my first real paycheck. Took four months and a standoff. Good luck."

Will raised it that evening, carefully, with a Kyu who was chopping vegetables at slightly higher precision than the vegetables required.

"The transfer's automatic," ze said. "Standing order. Takes effort to cancel."

"It takes four taps. Jason says last time it took four months."

"Jason exaggerates."

"Kyu."

Ze put the knife down. Stood at the counter a moment, hands flat on the board, and Will watched zir arrive at the actual thing the way ze did — sideways, at zir own speed, in inventory form.

"That's the oldest thing I do," ze said, finally, to the cutting board. "That transfer. It's older than the bar. Older than the work city. I've sent that money from _pay phones._ Every fifteenth, whatever else was happening, wherever I was — that went out. It always went out." A pause. "I don't know what the date's for, now. The fifteenth. It's just going to be a date."

And Will — who had learned which silences were rooms and which were doors, and knew this one was a door, barely open, once — didn't fix it, didn't reframe it, didn't say _it's a good thing,_ because ze knew it was a good thing; that had never been the problem. He came around the counter and stood next to zir and said the one accurate sentence available:

"You finished. That's what the date is now. The date you finished."

Kyu was quiet a moment. Then picked the knife back up.

"...Fine," ze said, which meant good, which meant more than good. "I'll cancel it tomorrow."

---

Ze cancelled it. And on the fifteenth of the next month, at the exact hour the transfer used to run, there was a knock — and the hallway was full.

The sister, in front, holding grocery bags from the expensive store. Jason behind her carrying a crate of drinks under one arm and his daughter under the other. His wife with a cake box and the expression of a woman who had been assured this was normal for this family and was reserving judgment. They pushed past Kyu into the kitchen in a column, already unpacking, already arguing about counter space.

"What is this," Kyu said.

"Dinner. Fifteenth of the month. New system." The sister was unpacking with aggressive efficiency, chin at full elevation. "I cook. Jason brings drinks. Mai supervises. It happens every month whether you like it or not. I made the system. Don't fight the system — it has your name on it."

"You can't cook."

"She has a recipe and a salary," said Jason, setting Mai on a chair she immediately stood on. "We've all been briefed. Sit down. You know how this works — you _invented_ how this works."

Kyu stood in zir own kitchen, out-provisioned in zir own house by twenty years of zir own grammar coming home in a column through the front door, and looked at Will — who was already at the table, radiating delight, no help at all.

"Sit down, husband," Will said. "Ze made the rules. Someone made newer rules."

Ze sat. The dinner was mediocre and nobody said so — the garlic burned, the rice caught, Mai's supervision consisted of eating components before assembly — and Kyu ate two full plates of it anyway, fast, like it might be repossessed, which in this house had always been the sincerest review available. Jason told the paycheck-standoff story; his wife heard the pay-phone detail for the first time and looked at Kyu for a long second and said nothing, just refilled zir glass without asking, and Kyu let her, which the sister clocked, and filed, and did not make a moment of.

They left loud, in a column, the way they'd come — leftovers distributed, September's menu already announced, feedback not accepted. Mai's card ended up on the fridge. Nobody discussed it. It was simply on the fridge now, six colors, _CONGRATURATION,_ load-bearing.

Kyu stood at the door a moment after it closed, holding a container of burnt rice ze had not asked for and would absolutely eat.

"She had extra," Will said from the couch, gently, in the house language.

"Mm," said Kyu — and put the container in the fridge, front and center, where the important things went, and marked, without writing anything down, the new meaning of the fifteenth.

---
# The board

It started small, the way these things always started with Will: a chess app, downloaded on a whim, a few games during lunch. Within three weeks it had colonized every gap in his day.

Kyu clocked it before Will did, because Kyu clocked everything. The tells were domestic and specific — Will at the counter at midnight, phone propped against the coffee machine, muttering about a knight fork. Will skipping the actual dinner ze'd made to "finish this one game," which became four. Will waking at four a.m. to check if his opponent had moved. The auditor that used to run receipts on his spending had found a new target and redirected all its processing power into rating his own performance, move by move, obsessively, against strangers on the internet who didn't know or care that he ran a company.

Ze let it run for a while. Ze'd learned, by then, which fires needed water and which needed to be watched. This one crossed a line on a Tuesday when Will, mid-meal, lost his fork to the wrong side of the board and didn't notice for eleven minutes.

Kyu picked up the phone.

"Hey—"

"You haven't eaten." Ze set it face-down, out of reach, with the flat finality ze used for load-bearing decisions. "Three nights of this. You're at 2 percent battery on sleep and full charge on this app. That's not a hobby anymore, that's a leak."

"I'm _fine,_ I'm just close to sixteen hundred, if I can just get past this plateau—"

"You said fourteen hundred was the plateau. Then it was fifteen. There's no top of this, Will. It's not a ladder, it's a treadmill with a number on it." Ze crouched slightly, level with him, the way ze did with real fear, not performance. "I've watched you build companies. I've watched you run rooms of forty people. You don't need a phone telling you you're good at something. You already know you're good at things. This is just letting a stranger's rating replace knowing it."

Will didn't say anything for a moment. The apartment was quiet enough to hear the coffee machine cycle.

"It's the only thing that has a _number,_" he said finally, quieter than he meant to. "Everything else — the company, us — there's no scoreboard. I don't know if I'm — I just wanted one thing where I could see if I was winning."

Kyu was silent a moment, taking that in the way ze took in anything real.

"You're not losing anything that needs a number to prove it," ze said. "But if you need one that badly, I'll give you a better game than the phone will." Ze straightened, took his hand, pulled him up from the counter. "Physical board. My terms. I'm terrible and I'll insult you the entire time, which is worth more than sixteen hundred rating points from a stranger who doesn't know your name."

They played that night, badly, on a board Ronan had built years ago for reasons nobody remembered. Kyu was, in fact, terrible — no patience for the long game, sacrificing pieces out of what looked like spite — and won exactly once, by what Will suspected was accident, and gloated about it for a week regardless. But he stopped checking the app at four a.m. The board came out most weeks after that, badly played, thoroughly enjoyed, no rating attached to either of them, and it turned out that was the part he'd actually been missing — not the number, just someone in the room, watching him think.

---
# The sword

It came out at the community fair, of all places — the one the building attended annually as a bloc, because Margarethe ran the preserves table and attendance was not optional.

There was a demonstration booth. Historical fencing club, padded jackets, two rows of loaner swords, a cheerful instructor letting people whack at each other for charity. Will did it, obviously — he was a good sport and worse, an enthusiast — and got politely dismantled by a teenager while the building cheered against him.

Then the instructor, scanning the crowd, pointed at Kyu. "You. Big fellow. You've got the stance for it."

"Ze doesn't—" Will started.

Kyu handed him zir drink and stepped over the rope.

What followed took eleven seconds and the fair talked about it for a year. Ze picked up the loaner sword, weighed it once, said something quiet to the instructor — who grinned and set his feet properly — and then the friendly charity booth briefly hosted an actual bout: two exchanges, a bind, a step Will's eyes didn't fully track, and the instructor's sword on the grass. The instructor was delighted. The teenager stared. Somewhere behind Will, Klaus said "_ohne Scheiß_" with deep reverence.

Kyu handed the sword back, retrieved zir drink from Will's frozen hand, and returned to the preserves table, where Margarethe, unsurprised by anything since 1987, gave zir a jar of plum.

Will caught up mid-crowd. "Where. _When._ You know sword fighting. You know — that wasn't beginner's luck, that man teaches it and you _disarmed_ him, there was a — you did a _move_—"

"It's been a while," Kyu said. "Sloppy bind. Don't tell him."

"_Sloppy_ — Kyu. Decade. I've known you a decade. I know the jobs, I know the boxing, I've inventoried the entire — when in your life were there _swords?_"

Ze walked a few steps, considering the plum jar, and gave him the answer with a shrug: "The work city. There was a club near the office. I needed something after hours that wasn't a screen." A beat. "Tuesdays and Fridays. Four years."

"_Four years._ You trained four years and it never once came up—"

"It never came up," ze agreed, as if he'd solved it, and offered him the jar. "Plum?"

He took the plum. He ate the plum. He spent the walk home doing the math on how many other four-year-long entire disciplines might be filed away in there, unmentioned, waiting for a booth — and arrived, by the door, at the honest total, which was: unknowable. The inventory would never be complete. He'd married a person with no bottom to the filing cabinet, and every few years a drawer would just slide open in public.

"You're doing the face," Kyu said, unlocking the door.

"I'm updating projections."

"Mm." Ze let him in, and paused, and — because ten years had taught ze exactly what he needed after a drawer opened — added, flat, over zir shoulder: "There's nothing else with swords. That was the only sword thing."

A pause.

"There's an archery thing," ze said, "but it's small."

---

# The settling

The mirror thing had been going on for three weeks. Will thought it was private. He'd catch himself at the hallway glass in the mornings, one second too long, fingertip near the corner of his eye pretending to fix his hair, and then go to work and not mention it to anyone, least of all the person who never missed anything.

On a Tuesday evening he was at the bathroom sink and Kyu came in behind him, reached past his shoulder, and set a small jar on his shelf. Good brand. Eye cream.

Will looked at it. Then at zir, in the mirror.

"It's—" he started, and didn't have an ending. "I know it's stupid."

"It's not stupid." Ze was watching him in the glass, unhurried. "But you've got it wrong. You think the face is leaving. It isn't. It's just arriving slower than you'd like."

A pause.

"I like this one better than the one I met," Kyu said. "It's got more of you in it."

Will turned around, away from the mirror, to look at zir directly. "You bought this before you brought it up."

"Mm."

"You've known for weeks and you just — waited. With cream." He huffed, somewhere between a laugh and a protest. "I married surveillance."

"You married _maintenance._" Ze picked the jar back up off the shelf, cracked the seal, and turned his chin toward zir with two fingers — matter-of-fact, no negotiation on offer. "Hold still."

"I can apply my own—"

"You'll do it wrong. You'll rub. It's _tapping,_ there's a technique, I read the box." Ze was already working, thumb dotting the cream along his orbital bone with the exact unhurried precision ze brought to knife maintenance, eyes narrowed at his temple like a contractor assessing trim. Up close like this Will could see the smirk arriving — slow, one-cornered, entirely pleased with the situation. "Look at you. Sitting still. Very good."

"I'm being _handled._"

"You're being _serviced._ Different invoice." Ze tilted his head the other way, did the second eye, took zir time about it — longer than any box instructed, Will was fairly sure, thumb smoothing along the cheekbone once more than the technique could possibly require. Then ze capped the jar, set it back on the shelf, and looked at the result with open satisfaction.

"There. Sound." A tap to his sternum, flat, final. "Foundation's fine. It was always fine. Bed in ten — and stop auditing my favorite thing in the hallway, you're tying up the mirror."

Ze left. Will stood there a moment, face faintly cool where the cream sat, hearing _my favorite thing_ land about four seconds after ze was safely out of range of being thanked for it — which, he was certain, had been timed.

The jar refilled itself after that, quietly, on no schedule he ever caught. Some nights ze did the application zirself, unprompted, chin turned with two fingers, no negotiation on offer, and Will learned to sit still for it. He stopped lingering at the mirror around the same time. The lines went on deepening exactly as scheduled, and some mornings he'd catch the newest one in the hallway glass and hear it in zir voice without zir being anywhere nearby — _arriving, not leaving_ — and go to work.

---
# Addendum: the routine

It became a fixture inside a month, and the honest accounting — which Will kept privately, the way he kept everything — was that he'd stopped thinking of it as skincare almost immediately.

The mechanics were always the same. Some evening signal he never quite caught — ze just _knew_ when — and then Kyu would appear in the bathroom doorway, jar in hand, and tip zir chin at the edge of the tub: _sit._ Will sat. Two fingers turned his face up. And then a few minutes of something he had no dignified name for: zir thumbs working along his orbital bone, tap-tap-tapping with ridiculous care, the occasional adjustment of his chin like he was an instrument being tuned, zir face close and flat and absolutely absorbed in the work.

He'd stopped protesting somewhere around week two, because the protests had been a formality even to him.

The truth was it might have been his favorite part of any given day. Not the cream — he remained agnostic on the cream — but the _arrangement_ of it: sitting still with his eyes half-closed while the one person who touched nothing carelessly handled his face like a maintenance item ze had strong opinions about. It was the only context in existence where ze looked at him from four inches away for minutes at a time, methodical, unhurried, thumb smoothing along his cheekbone in passes that had long since exceeded any technique on any box. He got to just — be held still, and tended, and looked at. By ze. On schedule.

"You're purring," Kyu observed one night, mid-application, dry.

"I'm _breathing._"

"You're leaning into my hand like the bodega cat." The smirk arrived, one-cornered, close enough that he could watch it happen in high definition. Ze did another pass with the thumb — slower, watching him track it. "You don't even care about the wrinkles anymore. You haven't done the mirror thing in a month. You just like this."

Will considered denying it, and then considered who he'd be denying it to, and closed his eyes instead.

"Ten more minutes," he said, with the shameless serenity of a man who had located paradise in his own bathroom. "There's — settling. Around the other eye. Very serious. Needs the technique."

A pause. The thumb kept moving.

"Obviously," Kyu said — and if the applications ran longer after that, neither of them ever once mentioned it to the other, and the jar, mysteriously, began emptying twice as fast.

---
# The pattern

Somewhere in the third year of being married, Will did the audit properly, because a thing had become undeniable and he wanted it on the record.

The eye cream was not an anomaly. The eye cream was the _latest entry._

Because it had happened with everything. Every single thing ze did for him — or to him, the line was administratively blurry in this household — followed the same trajectory: he'd resist it, or tolerate it, or accept it with the mild condescension of a man indulging his spouse, and then some weeks later catch himself _scheduling around it,_ protecting it, quietly bereft on the days it didn't happen. His enjoyment always came in over projections. Massively. Every quarter.

The 6 a.m. gym sessions ze'd bullied him into — he'd endured them for a month as a tax on the relationship, and now woke _before_ the alarm on gym days with something horrifyingly close to enthusiasm. Being cooked for — he'd thought of it as a nice perk, and it had somehow become the axis the entire day turned on, his body now keeping time by zir kitchen. The blunt haircut assessments, the wardrobe vetoes delivered like building inspections — "not that shirt, it fights your face" — which should have stung and instead felt like being _known_ at a resolution nobody else had ever bothered with. The manhandling, frankly: the chin turned with two fingers, the collar straightened without permission, being steered through crowds by the shoulder like valuable freight. He was a CEO. Two hundred people arranged themselves around his preferences daily. And apparently what he'd wanted, all along, at depths he'd never had cause to sound, was one flat-faced person who arranged _him_ — who treated his whole existence as territory under active, opinionated, meticulous management.

He'd mentioned it to zir once, in approximately those words, lying on zir lap during a movie he'd stopped following.

"I keep enjoying things more than I'm supposed to," he said. "Everything you do to me. I run the projection, the projection says 'this will be nice,' and then it's — not nice. It's _load-bearing._ Every time. The cream was supposed to be skincare and it's the spine of my Tuesday. What is that."

Kyu didn't look away from the movie. One hand was in his hair, which was itself Exhibit K or thereabouts.

"That's underpricing," ze said. "You've been doing it your whole life. Someone takes care of you, you book it as small." A pass of the thumb along his hairline, absent, precise. "It was never small. You just never had the data."

Will lay there and absorbed the fact that ze had, in one sentence, explained thirty-nine years of him to himself, without pausing the film.

"And you?" he said, eventually. "Did _you_ project any of this?"

A long silence. On screen, something exploded, unwatched.

"No," Kyu said. Flat. And then, because the dark and the lap and the third year permitted it: "Came in over projections. Every quarter."

He turned his face into zir knee and stayed there, and the hand kept moving, and neither of them said anything else, because the books were open, the pattern was mutual, and there was nothing left to audit — just the long, compounding business of being each other's best investment, enjoyed, every single day, more than either of them had thought they would.

---
# The good nights

The kiss had been routine for years by the time Will thought to wonder when it started.

He genuinely couldn't place it. There was no first one on record — no scene, no threshold, no night either of them could have pointed to. It was simply true now, the way the coffee was true and the groceries were true: lights low, both of them settled, and Kyu would lean over, unhurried, and press one small kiss to whatever was nearest. Temple, cheekbone, the corner of his jaw. Brief. Almost administrative. Then ze'd burrow in, arrange him to zir satisfaction, and be asleep in four minutes — a person closing the day's last ticket.

It happened on the good nights. It happened on the nights they'd bickered, which had ruined several of Will's attempts to stay properly annoyed. It happened on the nights ze came home at two from a close, moving carefully in the dark, thinking him asleep — the kiss landed anyway, which was how Will knew it had never once been for his benefit. It wasn't a message. It was a signature. The day, signed off. _Confirmed. Yours. Resume tomorrow._
