He brought it up the way he brought up everything now — plainly, mid-moment, no production. The kiss had just landed on his temple; ze was mid-burrow.

"That's my favorite one, you know," he said, into the dark. "Of everything. The whole catalogue. It's the good-night one."

"Mm." Ze finished arranging his arm where ze wanted it. "I know."

"You _know?_"

"You hold your breath for it. Every night. Have for years." Ze said it the way ze reported all zir surveillance — flat, unbothered, a meter read. "Thought you were asleep the first while. Then I clocked the breathing. You wait for it like the mail."

Will absorbed the fact that his most carefully guarded reverence had been on zir instruments the entire time.

"And you kept doing it."

"It's not _for_ you." A pause — one of zir pauses, the kind with cargo in it. "It's the end of the day. That's where the day ends. On you. That's just where it goes now." Ze pulled the blanket over zir shoulder, done, and then added, already sinking, in the smallest denomination: "Best part of the ledger. Don't tell the coffee."

"Wouldn't dream of it."

"Mm." And then, four minutes out from sleep, the last transmission of the day, half into his shoulder: "Good night, husband."

"Good night," Will said, to the dark, to the weight against him, to the signature drying on one more day — and lay there compounding quietly for a while, in the black, next to the whole of it.

The kiss kept landing. Every night, at whatever hour the day ended, on whatever part of him was nearest — named now, known now, discussed exactly once and never taxed by it, because nothing in that house had ever gotten smaller from being said out loud. That had been Will's discovery, in the end, the one the whole marriage was built on. Ze'd made a different one — that the day needed somewhere to end, and that it could be a person — and neither of them called their discovery anything, and both of them knew, and the account never closed.

---
# The idle

Will noticed it because doing-nothing was not a state Kyu had.

There was resting, which had a book in it. There was recovering, which had the couch and a purpose. There was the balcony, which had the evening and a beer and the street to supervise. But this — ze on the couch in the lamplight, hands loose in zir lap, looking at nothing in the middle distance, no book, no phone, no project, the whole restless competence of zir just _parked_ — this had no file. Will stood in the kitchen doorway with a glass he'd forgotten he was holding and ran the diagnostics from habit: not upset, the shoulders were wrong for upset. Not tired, ze carried tired differently. Just — idle. A machine that ran every hour of its life, sitting in neutral with the engine on.

He didn't ask. That was the fluency, in the end — knowing which silences were doors and which were rooms. This one wasn't a door. There was nothing behind it to draw out, nothing wrong to fix, no question that would improve it. It was a room, and ze was in it, and the only real decision was whether to knock or come in.

He came in. Set the glass down, crossed the floor, and folded himself onto the couch behind zir — one leg on either side, chest to zir back, arms around zir middle, chin settling on zir shoulder. The full arrangement. Ze didn't startle; ze'd tracked him from the doorway, obviously, ze tracked everything. Ze just exhaled, minutely, and leaned back into him by a degree — permission, filed after the fact.

"I'm not doing anything," Kyu said. Not a warning. An inventory.

"I know." Will settled his arms. "Me neither."

And that was the entire negotiation. Ze put one hand over his forearm — not holding it, just resting there, confirming the railing was where ze'd left it — and went back to looking at the middle distance, and Will looked at it too, and found nothing there, which appeared to be the point.

They sat. The lamp did its one job. Somewhere below, the street made street sounds; somewhere through the wall, 3B's television murmured its underlined nothing. The day had ended a while ago and the night hadn't asked anything of them yet, and in the gap between, nothing happened, at length, thoroughly, to both of them at once.

Will's thoughts wandered off somewhere around the ten-minute mark and didn't come back. That was the discovery — that ze had a gear he'd never been given, and that riding along in it was better than anything it replaced. No audit ran. No audience convened. The auditor himself appeared to be off shift, gone wherever auditors go when a man is holding his whole portfolio in his arms with nothing pending against either of them.

"This is what it's like all the time?" he said, eventually, into zir shoulder. "In there. When you go quiet."

"Mostly." A pause, unhurried. "It's not empty. It's just not _doing._" Ze tipped zir head back against him a fraction, the gray catching the lamp. "You never sit in it. You're always mid-something. First time I've felt you actually stop."

"I stopped."

"Mm." Zir thumb moved once over his forearm — a single pass, logged and denied in the same motion. "Took you years. Good instincts. Slow, but good."

They stayed until the lamp was the only light left and the street had gone quiet and someone's television had signed off, and then a while longer after that, because neither of them moved and neither of them wanted the other to — two people doing nothing, together, at professional grade, until the day finally found its way to where it ended, and ze turned zir head, and signed it off where his jaw was nearest.

---
# The hazard

Peace, Will had learned, was not the same as safety.

The marriage had reached its settled era — the rhythms all established, the wars all ritual, the account running quietly in the black. Nothing was volatile anymore. Which made it deeply unfair that ze still went off like ordnance, at random intervals, without doing anything at all.

Because that was the thing: it was never the deliberate material. The bar-shift looks, the doorway leans, the aimed smug — Will had built tolerance to the aimed arsenal years ago, the way you acclimate to any weather you live in. What got him, what still got him, five-plus years married, was the _unintentional_ catalogue. Ze, doing completely normal things, with zir whole attention elsewhere, not performing for anyone — and Will's entire operating system going down like a struck tent.

The forearms were a known hazard. Sleeves shoved up, ze at the sink or the cutting board, tendons doing their quiet competent work — that one Will had made peace with, insofar as peace meant losing quickly and quietly. The concentrating face he'd disclosed years ago, under duress, and paid for ever since. But the list had _deep cuts_, entries ze didn't know were entries: the way ze read leaning against the kitchen counter, ankles crossed, absently eating an apple ze'd stolen off his plate. The two-second full-body stretch after fixing something low, shirt riding, spine cracking, entirely graceless and somehow catastrophic. The voice at seven a.m., lower by a register, issuing the day's first flat instruction. The reading glasses ze'd finally admitted to needing — _ze_ had glasses now too, the entropy was mutual, and ze wore them shoved into zir hair like ze was daring them to be a concession — and when ze pulled them down onto zir nose to squint at fine print, unhurried, jaw set—

"You're staring," Kyu said, on one such evening, not looking up from the warranty card ze was reading like it had personally wronged zir. Glasses down. Sleeves up. Apple — his apple — in the other hand.

"I'm present in the room."

"You're staring like the bodega cat stares at the deli slicer." Ze turned the card over, still not looking at him. "What did I do this time."

"Nothing. That's the — you're reading a _warranty card._" Will gestured at the entire situation, betrayed. "You're not even doing anything. This is a Tuesday. We've been married _six years._ I have seen you do literally everything a person can do and you're reading appliance paperwork in your glasses and I want to _eat_ you. It's a disorder. I'm asking the doctor about it."

Kyu lowered the card, slowly. Looked at him over the glasses — over the glasses, which was somehow _worse_, and ze registered him registering it, and the smug began its unhurried sunrise.

"Six years," ze said, "and the warranty card gets you."

"The warranty card doesn't _get_ me, the — it's the whole assembly, you can't isolate one—"

"Fascinating." Ze set the card down with the deliberate care of someone tabling evidence, took the glasses off, folded them, and bit the apple — his apple — watching him the entire time with open scientific pleasure. "The deliberate stuff stopped working years ago. I've noticed. Diminishing returns on the whole arsenal. But _paperwork_—"

"Don't weaponize it—"

"—_paperwork_ in _glasses_ is apparently a load-bearing event." Ze stood, unhurried, and stretched — the full graceless spine-cracking one, absolutely on purpose now, ruined forever as an accident, and Will made a sound of genuine grievance at the theft. "Relax, husband. I'm not weaponizing anything. I'm going to go read the _insurance renewal_—" ze was already moving toward the desk, insufferable, radiant, "—very thoroughly, in my glasses, with my sleeves up. Paperwork night. Very boring. No reason for you to follow."

He followed. Obviously he followed. The insurance renewal went unread, the warranty card was never seen again, and the floor — old now, the pool long since retired, the tally a historical document — noted only that the third floor still, after all these years, occasionally observed the classics.

"For the record," Kyu said afterward, much later, into the dark, drowsy and pleased and thoroughly done reading anything, "you're the only person alive who gets got by _administrative tasks._"

"You're the only person alive doing them like that."

"I was doing them _normally._"

"I know," Will said, with the weary contentment of a man diagnosing his own incurable condition. "That's the whole disorder. Good night."

A huff — the almost-laugh, the small one ze never bothered suppressing anymore. Then the kiss, landing where it always landed, signing off one more day in the settled era: ordinary, administrative, and his favorite thing in the file.

"Good night, husband."

---
# The vintage

It was, the floor agreed, deeply unfair, and the floor had years of comparative data.

Will had aged the way successful men age when they're loved properly: the gray arriving at the temples like it had been invited, the lines settling into the face of a man who laughed at home, the whole polished-CEO apparatus mellowing into something warmer and better-fitting, like a suit finally broken in. Distinguished. That was the word people used. Baristas flirted with him gently now, in the harmless way people flirt with a handsome married man radiating contentment, and Will — who had once combusted over being audible through a wall — had grown into it, wore it easily, even enjoyed it. Charisma, aged in oak. A very good result. Anyone would have said so.

And then ze would walk into the room and demonstrate the difference between a very good result and an _unfair_ one.

Because Kyu hadn't mellowed. Ze had _concentrated._ The gray had gone from scattered strands to real silver streaked through the black — the exact thing men paid stylists to fake — and the flat face had acquired, with age, a weight it never used to carry: the unhurried economy of someone who had never once rushed for anything and now visibly never would. The competence had ripened into something worse than competence — _authority without effort_, a person who fixed things and priced drinks and settled disputes with four words, and whose attention, when it landed on you, landed like a verdict you hoped went your way. Ze'd been magnetic at thirty in a loud, scandalous, thin-wall way. At thirty-something ze was magnetic the way deep water is — quieter, and much more dangerous, and everyone at the bar knew it.

"It's the stillness," Will complained, at drinks, to the old crowd, in the tone of a man filing a grievance he had no intention of resolving. "That's the mechanism. Everyone our age is trying — good haircuts, running programs, whatever Daniel's doing with the cold plunges — no offense, Daniel—"

"Some offense taken."

"—and ze just _sits_ there. Not trying. Never tried once. And the not-trying compounds. I've watched it compound for twenty years. I age like a person, ze ages like an _asset._" He gestured, aggrieved, toward the bar, where Kyu — silver-shot, sleeves up, reading glasses pushed into zir hair, sixty percent listening — was flatly declining to remake a cocktail for a man half zir age, and the man was somehow _enjoying being declined._ "Look at that. Look at it. That kid's going to think about this interaction for a week."

"You married it," Lena said. "You don't get to complain."

"I get to complain _most._ I have front-row exposure. Do you know what it's like living with a person who gets _hotter_ on a schedule? There's no adjusting. Every few years the whole thing revalues and I have to recalibrate from scratch. The glasses era nearly ended me. The silver is currently ending me. I don't know what's next and I'm afraid of it."

From the bar, without looking up, without any possible way of having heard at that distance: "The cane era."

The table lost it. Will pointed at zir across the room, betrayed. "See — _see_ — even the _hearing_ aged unfairly—"

Ze came over eventually, drink in hand, and settled into the chair beside him with the unhurried gravity that had become, over the decades, zir signature register — and the table watched it happen, the small unconscious reorientation of the whole group toward zir, the thing ze had always caused and now caused _more_, and ze noticed none of it, or all of it, which had always been the same thing with zir.

"They're talking about how you age," Lena informed zir.

"Mm. The wine thing." Ze took a sip. "Will does the wine speech every couple of years. It's in the file."

"It's not a _speech,_ it's a documented phenomenon—"

"It's genetics and sleep, and I own a bar so nobody ever watches me drink too much." Ze said it flatly, the standard deflection, the _melanin_ answer for a different question — and then glanced sideways at Will, and there it was, the thing that hadn't aged at all, hadn't changed one degree in twenty years: the smug arriving at the corner of the mouth, aimed, loaded, entirely for him. "Besides. He's biased. He got got by a warranty card once. His testimony's compromised."

"One time—"

"The witness will calm down." Ze put zir hand on the back of Will's neck as ze said it — absent, proprietary, thumb settling into the exact spot it had settled into for two decades — and Will, mid-protest, visibly lost his thread, which proved the entire phenomenon better than the speech ever had.

"Unfair," he said, to no one, to everyone, to the ceiling.

"Compounding," Kyu corrected, serene, and finished his drink for him.

---
# The third visit

By the third visit, Will's office had developed protocols.

Nobody had written them down — you don't document what you can't admit exists — but the floor knew them the way floors know things. Front desk had a signal now: Dana would message Will's assistant a single mandarin emoji, origin never explained, and the assistant would rise, quietly, and go stand near the glass office like a man positioning himself between his CEO and weather. The group chat would go still in the way water goes still before something surfaces. Forty desks would achieve, in unison, the least convincing display of working ever mounted by salaried professionals.

Because Kyu Valentine was in the building, and the first two visits had entered company legend, and nobody was going to miss the third.

Ze came up at 2 p.m. on a Thursday carrying a paper bag, dressed like a person who had never once considered what a corporate dress code was — sleeves rolled, shirt operating at maybe sixty percent capacity — and walked the floor with the unhurried gait of someone touring a property they were thinking about not buying. Ze nodded at Dana. Dana, a professional, nodded back and messaged the mandarin.

Will was mid-meeting. Glass conference room, six people, quarterly something, projector running — and he felt the floor's temperature change before he saw anything, the way he'd learned to. He looked up. Made eye contact through two panes of glass with his husband, who was now standing at his assistant's desk, setting down the paper bag, and saying something that made his assistant — a serious young man Will paid well for his composure — laugh so hard he had to sit down.

Will lost his sentence. Completely. Mid-slide, in front of six people.

"...As I was — the projections," he said, to a room that had already turned to look. "Which are. On the slide."

Kyu did not come into the meeting. That would have been amateur work, and this was a professional. Ze simply _stayed in view_ — settled against the assistant's desk, accepted a coffee someone brought without being asked (the floor had learned; the floor provisioned back now, it was a whole ecosystem), and proceeded to hold court, at a polite volume, in Will's direct line of sight, for the entire remaining duration of the quarterly something.

Through the glass, Will watched his own staff assemble around his husband in casual orbit, one by one, like a solar system forming. He watched Lena from marketing say something and Kyu respond and the whole cluster crack up. He watched — and this was the part that ended him — ze produce something from the paper bag and hand it to Priya from design, who put both hands over her mouth. He had no idea what it was. He had no way to find out. He had eleven slides left.

He got through them the way men get through things: badly, at speed.

"—and Q4 tracking to plan, any questions, no, great," and he was out of the conference room with his laptop still open in there, crossing his own floor with what he hoped read as executive purpose, to the desk where his entire department had unionized around his spouse.

"Don't stop on my account," Kyu said, without turning around. "You had two slides left. I counted through the glass. Sloppy finish."

"What are you _doing_ here."

"Errand." Ze held up the paper bag by way of testimony. "The good bakery's two blocks from here. It was on the way."

"Nothing is on the way to — we live in the _opposite direction_—"

"New route," ze said, serene, and the floor — openly listening now, all pretense of work abandoned, this was the content they came for — made a collective sound they disguised, poorly, as coughing. Ze reached into the bag, took out the last item, and set it on the nearest surface with the flat ceremony of a diplomatic gift. "Anyway. That's yours. The one Priya got was better, but you were in a meeting, so. Priorities got made."

Will looked at it. It was the pastry he liked. The specific one. From the bakery that sold out by ten a.m., which meant ze had gone at nine, which meant this errand had been _planned_, which meant the entire casual drop-in was a constructed event with a supply chain — and ze was watching him arrive at all of this with the smug rising like a slow tide, in front of forty witnesses, three of whom were visibly texting.

"You bought the whole floor pastries," Will said, "and gave mine away _by rank of availability._"

"Attendance matters. Tell your Q4." Ze pushed off the desk, unhurried, collection complete — the flustered CEO, the ruined meeting, the floor's total allegiance, one afternoon's haul. Then, at a volume calibrated precisely to reach the first row of desks and no further, which meant it reached the entire floor by four p.m., ze added: "Home by seven. I'm cooking the thing you like. Don't eat the pastry too late, it'll ruin your appetite, and I'll know."

And ze left. Nodded at Dana. Was gone.

The floor took approximately four seconds to detonate. Will stood at his assistant's desk holding a pastry like a man holding the evidence of his own defeat, aware that the group chat was already writing history, that Priya was already framing whatever she'd been given, that his authority in this building now had a documented ceiling and the ceiling wore rolled sleeves and shopped at the good bakery at nine a.m. for ambush logistics.

His assistant, recovered, leaned over. "Sir. For what it's worth. We'd all die for ze."

"I know," Will said, with the weary pride of a man whose kingdom had been conquered politely, in three visits, with baked goods. He picked up his pastry and went back to his glass office, and ate it at his desk at 4:30 exactly, appetite calculations already made — because ze would know. Ze always knew.

The mandarin emoji stayed in the protocol. Some legends you don't retire.

---
# The poacher

It happened at a gala, because of course it did — the natural habitat of people who try to acquire other people's husbands.

She was, to her credit, extremely good at it. Investor-adjacent, expensively casual, and she'd mapped Will inside ten minutes: handsome, rich, CEO, wedding ring worn like an afterthought on a man clearly attending alone — because Kyu was at the bar, out of uniform-recognition range, having declined to circulate on the grounds that circulating was Will's job and the open bar was zirs.

So she moved in. Laughed at the right density. Touched Will's forearm at the correct professional-deniable frequency. Will, who was fluent in being managed and could see the whole playbook from page one, did what any happily married man with a functioning survival instinct would do: glanced across the room at his husband for the rescue.

Kyu was watching. Had been, obviously, from the first forearm touch. Ze read the entire situation across the room with the speed ze read everything — and then raised zir glass a half-inch at Will. A toast. _Good luck out there._

And went back to zir drink.

Will excused himself twenty minutes later, extraction performed entirely solo, and arrived at the bar in a state.

"You _saw_ that."

"I saw it."

"She had a whole — there was a _campaign_, Kyu, she asked what I look for in a business partner and did the _eye thing_—"

"Mm. Decent technique. Sloppy target selection." Ze sipped. "You should be flattered. That's premium poaching. She's done it before."

"And you just — _watched?_ You toasted me. You _toasted._" Will's voice was climbing into a register the nearby glassware could feel. "Where's the — you're supposed to come over. There's supposed to be an _arrival._ A hand on the shoulder. A 'sweetheart, who's your friend.' It's _traditional._"

"Why would I do that." Ze said it with genuine, unhurried curiosity, the way ze asked all questions Will considered rhetorical. "She can try. Trying's free." The corner of the mouth began its ascent. "What's she going to do, out-cook me? Out-know you? She's working off ten minutes of intel and a forearm. I've got a decade and a file. It's not a contest, Will. I don't show up to contests I've already won."

"That's not—" Will pinched the bridge of his nose. "Okay. Listen. I need you to hear something, and I need you to receive it with compassion." A breath. The truth, deployed at cost, in public, quietly: "_Please be more jealous._ Just — a little. As a treat. I'm not asking for a scene. I'm asking for one possessive gesture per gala. One. I would _flourish._"

Kyu looked at him for a long moment. The smug reached altitude.

"You want me to perform jealousy," ze said, "about a man who checks where I am every eleven minutes in any room we're in. Who wears his ring to the _gym._ Who told the entire leadership team he'd been rejected for a _surname._" Ze set the glass down and stepped in — one step, unhurried, entering the exact radius where the flat voice dropped for an audience of one. "I don't get jealous, husband. Jealous is for people who think they can lose something. I did the math on you years ago. You're the safest asset I hold."

Will made a small sound. The register of the sound was not dignified.

"_That's_—" he began, and recalibrated, "—okay, that was possessive, that counts, I'll take it—"

"However," Kyu continued, picking the glass back up, ruling not yet complete, "since you asked so nicely." And ze looked past him, across the room, found the woman — who was, at that moment, glancing back at Will the way campaigners check on paused campaigns — and held her gaze, flat, for exactly three seconds. No expression. Just the full attention, delivered across a crowded gala like an invoice.

The woman found somewhere else to be with remarkable efficiency.

"There," ze said, returning to the drink. "One gesture. Per your specifications. Happy anniversary."

"It's not our anniversary."

"It's _an_ anniversary. Third gala someone's tried to poach you. I'm keeping a tally." Ze slid his water across the bar — provision reflex, mid-banter, uninterruptible — and delivered the closing line at the volume reserved for filings: "Let them try, Will. Every one of them makes the same mistake. They see a rich handsome CEO." A sip. "None of them do the _research._ You're not on the market. You've never been on the market. You were acquired before listing."

"...Pre-market acquisition," Will repeated, hand over his heart, a man receiving poetry. "Say that part again but slower."

"Drink your water," said Kyu, Valentine, holder of the asset, entirely unjealous and entirely certain — and Will drank his water, flourishing, exactly as forecast.

---
# The debrief

They got home from the gala at midnight, and Will made it exactly as far as the couch before the evening caught up with him — the circulating, the performing, the poaching attempt, the water he'd been rationed. He sat down to take his shoes off and did not complete the mission.

Kyu came out of the bedroom in home clothes, assessed the situation — husband beached on the couch, one shoe off, tie at half mast, staring at the middle distance with gala residue all over him — and sat down at the end of the couch with the wordless authority of established procedure.

"Down."

Will went down. Head in zir lap, the rest of him arranged along the cushions by feel, one shoe still on because some battles wait until morning.

And then the hand landed in his hair, and started moving, and Will's entire nervous system filed for leave.

Slow. That was the whole technology — nothing fancy, just zir fingers through his hair at the speed of someone with nowhere to be, occasional pass of the thumb at his temple, unhurried, thorough, the same care rating ze gave the good knives. Will made a sound into zir thigh that he would have denied under oath.

"There it is," Kyu said, dry, to the ceiling.

"I'm decompressing."

"You're purring. Bodega cat." The hand didn't stop — the commentary and the tenderness ran on separate channels, always had. "Big night for you. Got poached at. Got a possessive gesture. Got told you're a pre-market acquisition in front of a bartender. That's a full calendar."

"Mm." Will was descending through the layers now, eyes shut, gala evaporating off him a floor at a time. "The three seconds. The stare you did. She _left the postal code._"

"She left the _building._ I checked." A pass of the thumb, temple, slow. "Efficient. No scene. You're welcome."

"Do it again next time."

"Behave next time."

"I _was_ behaving, I was _poached at_, that's a passive — I was the _victim_—" but the protest was losing structural integrity, dissolving into zir lap, because the hand had found the spot behind his ear and the man had, at some point in the last decade, been fully domesticated and never once regretted it. "...Little to the left."

The hand went a little to the left. No commentary this time. That was the tell, Will thought, at the bottom layer, nearly gone — ze'd mock every sound he made and still take _requests_, still adjust course for him, unfailingly, like the teasing was the packaging and the compliance was the freight.

"Sleep," Kyu said, quietly, somewhere above him. "You're done for the day. I've got you."

And Will — one shoe on, tie surrendered, hair thoroughly governed — was asleep inside a minute, in his own living room, held by the safest hands in his portfolio, while ze sat there in the lamplight not moving, the hand still going long after it needed to, because the meter had run out and ze kept it running anyway, off the books, where all zir best entries lived.

---
# The compromise

The negotiation had taken years, and Will had technically won it, which was the worst part.

The original terms were simple: Kyu at home existed in a state of total wardrobe indifference — clothing was for jobs, for weather, for other people's buildings, and home was none of those things. Ze cooked in nothing, read in nothing, did the crossword in nothing, padded around the apartment with the unbothered ease of a person to whom the concept of being perceived had never applied. Will had spent the first years of cohabitation adapting to this the way one adapts to living somewhere scenic: you never fully stop looking, you just learn to function while looking.

But there were logistics. Guests. Deliveries. The sister's fifteenth-of-the-month invasions, which had produced at least two incidents. Mai's visits requiring advance wardrobe notice. And so Will had, over the course of several diplomatic summits, petitioned for what he called "a home minimum" — some baseline garment, anything, a standard.

Kyu had resisted on principle, then relented on practicality, and one day came home with the solution: a wrap. Large, silk, vaguely robe-adjacent, the kind of thing that tied at the waist and technically covered ze from shoulder to thigh.

It was also, Will discovered when ze put it on, almost completely transparent.

"That's—" he said, from the couch, and stopped, because his sentence had left without him.

"Clothes," Kyu said, tying the waist with the smug already fully deployed. "Per the treaty. Shoulders: covered. Everything: covered. You asked for a garment. This is a garment. I checked the definition."

"It's _see-through._"

"It's _silk._ Silk is breathable. You want me comfortable in my own home, don't you." Ze did a slow, deliberate turn, ostensibly to model the coverage, actually to demonstrate with full scientific rigor that the coverage was a rumor. "Look at that. Decent. Modest, even. The sister can visit. Deliveries can come. Society's standards: met."

And the diabolical thing — the part Will understood immediately and could do nothing about — was that ze was right, and it was worse. Naked Kyu had been, after years, a known quantity: scenic, yes, but ambient, the baseline of home. The wrap _changed the physics._ Now there was a layer — a shifting, translucent, catching-the-lamplight layer that moved when ze moved and clung when ze cooked and slid off one shoulder during the crossword with a timing that could not possibly be accidental — and Will's brain, which had fully acclimated to _nothing,_ had no defenses whatsoever against _almost nothing._ The garment didn't conceal. It _annotated._

"You did this on purpose," he said, on day four, hollow-eyed, a man who had lost a negotiation by winning it.

"You wrote the terms." Ze was at the stove, the silk doing things in the kitchen light that should have required a permit. "One garment, home minimum, your exact words. I complied in full. I'm very treaty-abiding." A glance over the shoulder, the smug at its cruising altitude. "Funny thing, though. You used to get _more_ done around here. Before the compromise. Productivity's really dropped since I started dressing modestly."

"I hate the treaty."

"Mm. Renegotiate anytime." Ze turned back to the stove, silk shifting, case closed. "But the wrap stays either way. Turns out I like it." A beat, and then, flat, the killing blow delivered to the sauce ze was stirring: "It's the first piece of clothing that works better than none. I'd have started wearing clothes years ago if I'd known they could be a _weapon._"

Will lay back on the couch and stared at the ceiling, defeated, married, and — this was the unreportable part, the line item he'd never say aloud with ze in earshot — enjoying the arms race more than any outcome it could possibly produce. The treaty held. The wrap stayed. Nobody ever renegotiated, because the current terms were, by mutual unspoken agreement, exactly as unbearable as both parties wanted them.

---
# The metrics

The data was undeniable, and both of them kept data, so both of them knew.

Since the wrap's arrival, one household statistic had climbed steadily and conspicuously — Klaus's successor generation could have charted it through the wall, the floor's oral tradition certainly noted it, and Will, a man who reviewed quarterly numbers for a living, was fully aware that the garment had paid for itself several hundred times over on a per-wear basis. As textiles went, it was the single highest-performing asset in the apartment.

The problem was the _distribution_ of the performance.

"I've run the numbers," Will announced one evening, from the couch, in the tone he used for board presentations. Kyu was in the kitchen, in the wrap, being the numbers. "And the garment is operating at two completely different efficiency ratings, depending on whose ledger you read."

"Mm."

"For _you_, it's a miracle of engineering. One piece of silk. Minimal maintenance. And it converts directly into—" he gestured at the general state of their marriage, "—outcomes. At a rate. Your cost-per-result is basically zero. You put it on and the _economy moves._"

"Sounds efficient." Ze didn't turn around. The shoulder did the thing. Will lost his place in his own presentation and found it again with visible effort.

"For _me_," he continued, hoarser, "it's a catastrophe. Do you know what my evenings used to contain? Reading. Correspondence. I had a _chess app._ I was ranked. Now my evenings contain one activity and a recovery period, because someone weaponized a treaty, and my productivity—" he pointed at ze, the whole silk-adjacent situation of ze, "—my productivity is _gone._ You've achieved peak efficiency by destroying mine. That's not a marriage, that's a hostile takeover of my calendar."

Kyu turned around, leaned back against the counter, and let the lamplight finish the argument for zir. The smug arrived at full altitude.

"File a complaint," ze said.

"With who? You're the whole department."

"Then the process is working." Ze crossed the kitchen, unhurried, silk announcing every step of the route, and stopped at the arm of the couch to look down at him — flat face, loaded eyes, the expression of a person reviewing extremely favorable quarterly results. "Look at you. Ranked chess player. Correspondence backlog. All those evenings you'll never get back." A pause, timed like an invoice. "You want to renegotiate the treaty? Say the word. I'll go back to the old dress code tomorrow."

Will looked at zir for a long moment. Did the math he always did. Arrived where he always arrived.

"...The treaty stands," he said, with dignity, already reaching for the tie at the waist. "But I want it noted in the minutes that I was ranked."

"Noted," said Kyu, gracious in total victory, and the household's leading statistic climbed again that evening — very efficient for the garment, very inefficient for Will, and neither party, per the minutes, requested any change whatsoever.

---
# The commission

It was past one, the apartment dark, the day signed off — the kiss had already landed, temple, administrative, final. Will was three breaths from sleep when ze said it.

"I like the silk."

"Mm." Half-conscious. "Evidence supports."

"Maybe you could get me more of it."

Will's eyes opened in the dark.

Because he heard it — heard the whole thing, every layer, with the fluency of a decade. This was not a comment about fabric. This was Kyu Valentine, the person with no slot for receiving, who returned coats and closed joint accounts and had made _wanting things_ a foreign language — voluntarily requesting a gift. Naming a want. Out loud. To him. The word _maybe_ was doing the deniability work, the timing was chosen for the dark where zir ears couldn't be observed, and the entire sentence was, by the grammar of this house, roughly equivalent to a proclamation with trumpets.

An invitation. Sealed, stamped, deniable, and absolutely real.

"Okay," Will said, with the flattest voice he could manufacture, matching zir register, because reacting at true scale would collapse the moment like a soufflé. "Noted."

"Mm." Ze settled. Four minutes later, asleep — a person who had handed over something enormous and correctly trusted it to be carried properly.

Will lay awake for another hour, incandescent, _planning._

Because this — this was the thing about being him, married to ze: he had spent ten years with a war chest and nowhere to spend it, a provider's income bricked up behind a spouse who wanted nothing, and ze had just, casually, at 1 a.m., handed him a _license._ A whole category, opened. Silk. Ze liked the silk. Ze wanted more of the silk. His mind was already moving through it like a man given the keys to a market he'd been window-shopping for a decade — weights, colors, that place in the fabric district Lena had mentioned, whether ze'd tolerate a robe or only wraps, what the dark charcoal would do against zir shoulders, whether this could be stretched to sheets, _it could absolutely be stretched to sheets,_ pillowcases, the whole bed, an entire silk _regime_—

He caught himself at the edge of the cliff. Breathed. Because he also knew the other rule, the one that governed all gifts in this house: overshoot and the door closes. Arrive with eleven boxes and a _presentation_ and ze would return ten of them on principle and possibly re-derive shame about the first. The license was for _more silk_, not for a campaign. The gift had to arrive the way everything arrived here — offhand, deniable, priced as nothing.

So he did it right. It took two weeks of covert operations he enjoyed more than any acquisition of his career: one wrap in charcoal, one in a green ze would never have chosen and was going to look criminal in, found "on the way" to a meeting that did not exist, left folded on the bed with no note, no occasion, no witnesses.

He was in the kitchen when ze found them. He heard the pause. He kept stirring, heroically, eyes forward, a man betting everything on not looking.

Ze came out wearing the green one. Said nothing. Took zir coffee. Sat down with the crossword.

"Had extra," Will said, to the pot.

A silence with cargo in it.

"...Obviously," said Kyu — and if the green wrap entered heavy rotation from that week on, and if a third one in a color _ze_ had somehow ordered appeared on _his_ side of the bed a month later, invoice-free, retaliation-grade, then the ledger was simply doing what the ledger did now: running forever, unbalanced, in silk.

---
# The bite

It happened without warning, which was the whole point.

Kyu was at the counter, reading something on zir phone, half-dressed in the transparent wrap, entirely unguarded — and Will, passing behind ze on his way to the fridge, simply leaned down and bit zir shoulder. Not hard. Just enough to register, teeth and all, gone before ze'd finished processing the sensation.

Ze went rigid for exactly one second.

"—_Will._"

"Mm?" He was already at the fridge, the picture of innocence, retrieving water like a man who had done nothing at all.

"You _bit_ me."

"Did I." He drank the water. "Strange."

Kyu turned around slowly, phone forgotten, zir hand going to the spot on zir shoulder like ze was checking for damage ze already knew wasn't there. The flat face was working through several settings at once — offense, confusion, something underneath both that hadn't fully surfaced yet.

"You've been doing this for _months._" Ze said it like ze was reading off a report ze'd finally compiled. "The wrist. The shoulder blade. Last week you bit my _collarbone_ mid-sentence, I lost the whole thought."

"You did seem distracted."

"_I_ was distracted. _You_ did it." Ze crossed zir arms, which did nothing to hide the fact that ze wasn't actually angry, just cataloguing. "Where does this even come from. You're a _CEO._ You wear a _tie._ You have an internal auditor with your father's voice. And every few days you just — _bite_ me. Like a golden retriever discovering teeth."

"I don't have an explanation." Will set the water down, entirely unbothered by the accusation, which was itself new information. "It's not planned. You're just standing there, and then you're not standing there anymore, you're bitten."

"That's not a defense, that's a _confession._"

"I'm aware."

Ze studied him for a long moment — the man who spent two weeks sourcing silk through covert channels, who drafted PowerPoints for sincerity, who needed physical intervention to stop rating himself against strangers — and arrived, visibly, at a working theory.

"You've got a whole catalogue," ze said slowly, "and this wasn't in it. This is a _new_ one. You've developed a new item completely off the books."

"Bold of you to think I disclosed the whole catalogue."

Something crossed the flat face that wasn't quite the smug yet — closer to genuine surprise, the rare kind, the kind that happened maybe twice a year. Ze uncrossed zir arms.

"You're supposed to tell me these things."

"You didn't tell me about the swords for a decade." Will came around the counter, entirely too pleased with himself. "I'm allowed exactly one undisclosed item. Consider us even."

Kyu opened zir mouth to argue the math, and Will, with the perfect timing of a man who'd clearly been planning this exact moment for exactly as long as ze'd suspected, leaned in and bit zir shoulder again — same spot, gentler, deliberate this time, no ambush left in it, just claim.

Ze went still. Didn't pull away.

"There," Will said, against zir skin. "Now it's disclosed."

"You're going to regret narrating that."

"I regret nothing. It's in the file." He straightened, smug in a register ze'd taught him without meaning to. "New rule. I make excellent rules."

"You stole that line."

"I learned from the best." He picked his water back up, victorious, and left ze standing at the counter with a hand still at zir shoulder and an expression that hadn't fully resolved — somewhere between outrage and something that looked, if you knew where to look, exactly like the smug ze usually kept for zirself, borrowed now, sitting strangely well on him.
